## Introduction


Mirosław Słowiński

Can there be a world without history? Can it be understood if one cannot tell of it? This book is an attempt to describe an ordinary world that great history destroyed. The small town of Zagórów, situated in eastern Wielkopolska, on the southern slope of the valley of the Warta, together with the surrounding settlements and villages was for centuries shaped by Poles, Jews and Germans. An emblematic reflection of this denominational and ethnic mixture were the houses of worship of Zagórów: the Catholic church of St Peter and Paul at the Mały Rynek, the synagogue on Średnia Street, and the Evangelical-Augsburg church on Konińska Street. The cataclysm of the Second World War brutally shattered this mosaic picture assembled over generations.

In the first half of the eighteenth century, after the hard years of 1708–1710, caused above all by the effects of the terrible onslaught of the Great Frost, which in 1709 gripped Europe from the Mediterranean to England and Scandinavia (one could cross the Baltic by sledge from Poland to Sweden), and by the struggle with unimaginable, total famine and the painful aftermath of successive epidemics of smallpox, cholera or typhus, Zagórów went through perhaps the heaviest period in its history. Universal looseness of morals, actions according to the law of the stronger, the helplessness of the owner of the town – that is, of the monastery in Ląd. From the books of the Zagórów parish preserved in the State Archive in Poznań we learn that "this sudden death was more merciless towards men than towards women, which is why after every epidemic in Zagórów and its surroundings quite a few children were born out of wedlock – as if to balance the state of the population thrown into disorder by the pestilence[^1].



[^1]: Słowiński Lech, Mniej i bardziej ciekawe przypadki mojego życia. (Wspomnienia). Poznań 2000. Lech Słowiński – born in Zagórów on 23.09.1922, died 29.07.2005 
<!-- s. 6 [PDF 6] -->

In the years 1735–1760 the abbots issue successive decrees intended to restore the functioning of elementary rights. Unfortunately, without greater effect. Order is finally introduced by Konstanty Iłowiecki, who on 25.03.1760 issues a decree annulling all seizures, exchanges and sales of land made after 1750. And he firmly enforces the new law. March 1768 goes down in history on account of a great fire in which 53 houses, the town hall and numerous craftsmen's workshops burned down. The successive actions of the owners and town councillors, of the royal Commission of Good Order, lead to the rebuilding of the town. The governors of the town and its owners were surely aware of the losses that successive epidemics brought upon the male part of the community. There simply had to begin to be a shortage of hands to work, of labour.

Perhaps it is precisely this many years' work on restoring the rule of law in the town, but also some kind of agitation effectively disseminated, together with the economic processes taking place beyond the western border, that cause new arrivals to flow into Zagórów and its surroundings from the second half of the eighteenth century onwards. These are German settlers. All of them are Protestants – members of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. After the year 1750 there also appear reports that Jews are reaching the town. Earlier already, in the year 1718, 14 German families arrive from Silesia, to whom the heir of Trąbczyn leases forest lands and fallow grounds in perpetuity. In time a village called Łazińsk arises there. The town grapples with its own problems, and the surrounding landed proprietors must think about who will till their difficult land. The owners of the estate in Trąbczyn, Paweł and Katarzyna Prusimscy, in 1746 designate places for the first two settlements. These were Trąbczyńskie Luźne Stare Olędry and Trąbczyńskie Luźne Olędry (today Łazińsk Pierwszy and Łazińsk Drugi). The settlers were German peasants of the Lutheran faith. Evidently the first experiences proved positive, for the Prusimscy locate a further settlement, called Łukomskie Olędry. The newcomers take over 

 in Poznań. Professor of the Adam Mickiewicz University in Poznań. I am publishing the memoirs thanks to the kind consent of the closest Family. The typescript is kept in the home archive.

<!-- s. 7 [PDF 7] -->
 the leased land according to Hauländer law (settlers on Dutch law). They are free people, with their own self-government, a cult of work and harsh customs. It may be an interesting fact that in the last of the settlements located by the heirs of Trąbczyn there was also a group of Polish peasants, Catholics. In the years 1746–1789 the heirs of Łukom create successive settlements – these are today's villages: Bukowe, Imielno, Huta Łukomska (called at that time Zagórowskie Olędry), Myszakówek.

The name Olędrzy, used for a long time in the Zagórów land, was not connected with the nationality of the settlers, but with the law on the basis of which the villages were located and relations with the landowners regulated. This law was brought into the Commonwealth by Mennonites arriving from the Netherlands, who were fleeing persecution in their own homeland. The Warsaw Confederation adopted by the Polish Sejm in January 1573 was, for its times, an exceptional act of religious tolerance, which guaranteed the nobility freedom of worship and ensured peace between the dissenters. The Mennonites were not a numerous group that reached the Vistula and the Żuławy, but the law proved very useful in organizing the settlement campaign and was applied for a long time – until the mid-nineteenth century. Zbigniew Chodyła, a meritorious researcher and expert on Hauländer settlement, estimates that Holendrzy and their descendants constituted barely less than one percent among the settlers located on Hauländer law. The others were, in the great majority, Germans – Evangelicals, and also Poles – Catholics and Protestants. "As a result of the second partition of Poland (the year 1793), Zagórów found itself, together with the entire Słupca Land, under Prussian rule. A year later it was engulfed by the Kościuszko Uprising. In the vicinity of Zagórów, Ląd, Lądek, Słupca and Gniezno, the corps of General Jan Henryk Dąbrowski, supported by local insurgents, fought bloody battles with the Prussians. The fall of the Commonwealth after the defeat at Maciejowice restored Prussian rule in the little town. Zagórów, Słupca and the adjacent areas entered the so-called South Prussia"[^2]. In this period, dramatic for the 


[^2]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

<!-- s. 8 [PDF 8] -->
 Polish state, when in 1795 the third partition takes place and the state finally falls, the Zagórów office issues a special decree regulating the presence and settlement of foreigners in the town.

In the year 1796 the Prussian prince Friedrich Ludwig Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen took over as his property the Zagórów estates hitherto belonging to the Cistercian monastery. In the year 1797 the Prussian King Friedrich Wilhelm II issued a Statute for the Jews of South Prussia and New East Prussia (the lands annexed after the partitions), which regulated the legal situation in those provinces. "From that time German colonists began to flow into the vicinity of Zagórów in great numbers. They founded their settlements mostly on former forest lands, on the infertile sands and wetlands of the Warta valley, unwillingly settled by the Polish population. In this way, with the passage of years, Zagórów was surrounded by a ring of numerous villages and settlements almost entirely German, such as: Olchowo, Tarszewo, Chróściki, Anielewo, Michalinów, Augustynów, Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry and others"[^3]. Schools (Kantoraty), houses of prayer and cemeteries came into being. On the territory of today's gmina of Zagórów, in 25 localities (out of a total of 52), Germans of the Evangelical faith lived for over 200 years as the dominant group. They formed the Zagórów Lutheran community, which came into being as a filial congregation in 1843 and became an independent parish in the year 1856. According to the research of the already-mentioned Zbigniew Chodyła, in the whole of Wielkopolska in the years 1721–1793/1795, 637 settlements were founded, of which 228 in the Kalisz voivodeship, to which Zagórów belonged[^4].

After the Congress of Vienna in 1815 Zagórów and its surroundings found themselves within the Kingdom of Poland subject to the Tsar of Russia. The town strives to attract craftsmen and merchants who could raise the town economically. These are, among others, the times of the building of numerous windmills. In the times when Zagórów belonged to the monastery in Ląd, Jews were not allowed to settle permanently in the church estates. The Prussian order changes this 


[^3]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

[^4]: Chodyła Zbigniew, *Zarys najstarszych dziejów osad olęderskich w Puszczy Pyzdrskiej (1746-1793)*, Poznań 2015.

<!-- s. 10 [PDF 10] -->
 situation. As early as the year 1808 (that is, in the times of the short life of the Duchy of Warsaw), 28 Jews permanently settled in Zagórów were recorded. Both the Ashkenazi Jews arriving in Zagórów and the settlers from Silesia, Prussia and Pomerania lived for long years in the world of their own language (Yiddish and German) as well as of religion. These were communities with their own self-government, deep autonomy and religious separateness. That was so above all in the times of the first Commonwealth. The partitioning powers regulated the social order according to their own jurisdiction – yet the cultural distinctness and the special legal status remained.


Until the mid-nineteenth century the contacts of Poles, Jews and Germans had an almost exclusively economic, commercial character. Also in later years one cannot really record (apart from incidental cases) mixed marriages: Polish-Jewish, Polish-German or Jewish-German. There are also no cases of conversion. Russian rule lasted more than a hundred years, but in none of the communities – Polish, Jewish or German – are there testimonies of conversion to the faith of the rulers – Orthodoxy. In the region, apart from officials, there are small groups of Rusins, and the nearest cathedral church (later Orthodox church) is in Kalisz. The first Jewish settlers were engaged in the production and trade of linen, in tailoring, hat-making, and later they in fact came to dominate commerce. Poles and Germans worked on the land and ran numerous craft workshops. It is worth mentioning an important communication undertaking. From the eighteen-twenties of the nineteenth century, along the main (Poznań) highway, a mail coach regularly travelled twice a week on the route Warsaw – Poznań – Berlin. It carried letters and parcels, but also passengers. It opened a window onto the world for the local people. But the possibility of contacts and of learning what others were doing was also created by another circumstance. After the defeat of the January Uprising, the Liquidation Commission for the Kalisz governorate, appointed in March 1864, singled out Zagórów, among 32 other towns, to be deprived of its town rights. On the basis of the act of 11 February 1869 this fact took place. It was never reconciled with this act, and successive boards managed for over 40 years to maintain the urban character of the proud town.

<!-- s. 11 [PDF 11] -->

Zagórów and the surrounding villages are, throughout that entire century, territories of the Russian-Prussian borderland. Not far away, along the Prosna, which flows into the Warta beyond Pyzdry, and further north on the eastern side of Strzałkowo, ran the border of these states. Across the green border, spirits and vodka were smuggled, and even whole herds of cattle and geese were driven over. The latter, on their short little legs, could cover as much as 60 kilometres. My grandfather, who as a young lad took part in these "operations", told how before setting out the geese were "shod", by being driven through warm tar and scattered chaff or fine sand. From Prussia there came mainly medicines, machines and other products of a developed industry. In nearby Słupca various companies were registered, which served as a cover for this widely practised contraband. This unlicensed trade undoubtedly had a positive influence on the economic revival of the region. It was also an important contributing cause of so widespread a raising of geese on the meadows of the Warta valley. This white bird is the traditional chief delicacy of the German festive cuisine…

In Zagórów the best testimony to the settling of the newcomers and their ever higher material status were the two houses of worship handed over to the faithful in that same year 1882: the synagogue on Średnia Street and the Evangelical-Augsburg church on Konińska Street. The revolution of 1905 in Russia awakens in the Poles the hope of a change of fate. In the last months of the year, after the masses in the church, patriotic demonstrations take place. Under banners with the White Eagle, the introduction of the Polish language in the schools and offices was demanded. The effect was the founding in 1906 of the Elementary School, in which teaching was in Polish. The Volunteer Fire Brigade, organized in 1899 by Dr Konstanty Lidmanowski, becomes an important centre of patriotic upbringing and the seed of many cultural initiatives.

Unfortunately, the world of which this small piece of earth is a part begins to be shaken by great passions and growing conflicts. War breaks out. As early as 4 August 1914 it dramatically reveals its face. German artillery bombards Kalisz, 50 kilometres away. The town 
<!-- s. 12 [PDF 12] -->
 burns, civilian inhabitants perish. The whole of eastern Wielkopolska, Zagórów included, found itself under German occupation. Requisitions of grain and foodstuffs, and, as war misery grows – the plundering of craftsmen's workshops, the tearing of copper sheeting from the roofs of churches and the requisition of church bells to be melted down for the needs of the army complete the picture.

The authorities do not yet segregate citizens according to nationality, race or religion. But the time for that, too, will come after the victory. Their view seems clearly to be expressed by the declaration of the chief quartermaster of the Tenth Army. "For the Supreme Commander the life of one German means more […] than the entire population of the occupied country. Colonel Thugott von Sauberzweig". This is a strong echo of an old political conception. The idea of Mitteleuropa had been developed in Germany for over half a century. It assumed a "march to the east", the conquest of the territories of Central Europe and their economic exploitation, while the Slavs and other nations inhabiting them, as lower in development, were to be subjected to Germanization and driven to slave labour. Such was the essential aim, on the eastern axis, sketched during the First World War by the German Empire. In the next war the thought of the Supreme Commander proclaimed by Col. von Sauberzweig will be developed – if one may put it that way – and will be realized on a mass scale with extraordinary brutality. But life must go on. The town councillors made good use of the front-line crises of the German and Austrian armies and, availing themselves of the declarations made by high politicians and of the proclaimed manifestos on the reconstruction of the Kingdom of Poland, they attended to their own affairs.

In this way, in 1916, Zagórów regains its town rights. The Zagórów scouts and members of the Polish Military Organization were preparing for the fight. In the year 1918, when it was already clear that the war was lost, the German plundering and removal of goods from the still occupied territories took place on a great scale. Whole transports flowed down the Warta on barges, towards the Oder. The fighters of the Zagórów POW, after an attack, captured one of such barges and then sank it. One of the guards perished 
<!-- s. 13 [PDF 13] -->
 in the fight. The other hid somewhere and remembered exactly who took part in this attack. This fact had its tragic consequences. What was, for the Jews and the Germans – or did it mean anything at all – the idea of a free Poland, nurtured through generations? Both the German and the Jewish children went through a short education strictly bound to the religion they professed. For the German Evangelicals it was the Bible. For the Jewish children – the Talmud. The mastery of the basic skills of reading and writing, of arithmetic and a knowledge of the holy books – these were the foundations of that education. Setting aside the harsh regime of the partitioners' law, from the sources mentioned, nor from their own teachers could they obtain the basic knowledge of the history of the country in which they lived.

In 1918, when independence was regained, wealthy Jewish and German families, as well as Protestant clergymen originating from the Reich, leave Poznań and Wielkopolska en masse for Berlin and other German cities. For a whole century the Jewish and Evangelical communities had been subject to the Tsarist administration. The experiences of ancestors who had crossed the borders of the plundered and disrespected noble Commonwealth were neither dwelt upon nor particularly cultivated. They had the opportunity and they functioned within the closed circles of their communities. The Jews, behind the tight veil of language and religion, conducted their business. The German settlers, likewise bound by language and religion, literally hewed out or dug out their place on this earth, in the forests beneath Zagórów and on the meadows of the Warta. When they travelled to Prussia, the majority considered that they were going on a visit. Their homeland was this scrap of earth, into which their sweat and tears had soaked. But the German Reich consistently conducts an intrusive agitation, and not only through the state administration.

Strong pressure is exerted on the clergy of the Protestant Church. How complicated and difficult these processes and individual dilemmas were is broadly illuminated by Rev. Waldemar Wunsz in the text "Kościół Ewangelicko-Augsburski w dwudziestoleciu międzywojennym". The Konin police report to their superiors that among the national minorities the attitude towards the new state is indifferent or neutral. Only the German 
<!-- s. 14 [PDF 14] -->
 Hakatists and the Jewish Bundists[^5] are decidedly hostile towards the Polish administration. Irrespective of nationality or religion, all become citizens of the Commonwealth. Rudolf Poschitzki of Olchowo has, in a document, his nationality recorded as German, and his language as Polish. His grandparents and father use the Polonized version of the surname, Poszycki. He served in the years 1931–1933 in the Polish Army; mobilized in August 1939, he writes a fine page as a soldier of the Poznań Army in the battles on the Bzura[^6]. Among the documents discovered in the house of the cantor in Łazińsk there is a letter of Gustav Tonna from Płatkownica to Bruno Mielke, which also shows a deeper reflection on the presence and life of the Germans, of the Evangelicals, on this land. From the letter one can infer that Bruno was rejected by the education authorities, or that the right to teach was refused him. In these few sentences we find "a piece of history", but also the life wisdom of an old teacher. "However, dear Bruno! Do not be too open, get to know the man before you undertake anything. Also, do not make too much show of your Germandom. In order to obtain a piece of bread and keep yourself, one must keep much to oneself. You need not disown anything, but neither need you profess to anything. And if Poland is to be our homeland, and it is, then we 


[^5]: Bundists – members of the General Jewish Workers' Bund in Poland, Russia and Lithuania (the Bund – the party operated in the years 1897-1948). They regarded the Jews as a separate nation, striving to create secular schooling and to develop culture in the Yiddish language, which was to strengthen their autonomy. They took part in various formations of the international movement of socialists and social democrats. They criticized the Bolshevik government of the USSR and at the same time urged cooperation with it. They were determined opponents of the Zionists and of the religious movement Agudas Israel. Representatives of both of these currents were present in Zagórów.

    Hakata – the League of the Eastern Marches (1894-1934), operating under Bismarck's patronage, regarded the Germanization of the Polish lands under Prussian partition as its principal aim. They had the means and a programme for driving the Poles out of their land by every possible method. After Hitler's seizure of power, most of the members joined the organization created by the NSDAP, the Bund Deutscher Osten (League of Germans in the East). HaKaTa – an abbreviation of the surnames of the organization's founders: Hansemann, Kenemann, Tiedemann.

[^6]: Michalski Bogdan, *Imiona zapomnianych mogił*. In: Zagórów – miejsce z historią, accessed 27.09.2023, 21:35.

<!-- s. 16 [PDF 16] -->
 must also be patriotically disposed towards Poland. In the times of Russian rule I worked for Russia, and now I do so still more willingly for Poland. ["Be ye therefore wise as serpents, and harmless as doves"]. But only do not give up; talk with the inspector and perhaps it will be better. The Lord will come to aid and help turn away from you this injustice. […] I, when I came from Russia, was also slandered as a great friend of Germany, but God sent me a faithful soul, who supported me, and everything turned out for the best. Now I hope that my direct school superior is satisfied with me. (Letter of 15.07.1936).


It is worth noting that Tonn writes these words at a time of aggressive nationalist and Nazi propaganda, widely circulated and conducted among the German minority. In the Jewish milieu the Polish State was, for the settlers who had been established there for years, an administration new in its very essence and foreign. Many extremely difficult matters had to be resolved, connected with the consolidation of lands on which, for 123 years, separate legal systems had been in force, different money and economic rules, customs and culture. The customs border between the former Russian and Prussian partitions was abolished in 1923. Only in the nineteen-twenties was the joining of the railway lines running from Warsaw to Poznań carried out. The nearest station was in Słupca, 15 km away. Bus transport also began to function, by which one could reach the unhappily chosen provincial office in Łódź. The distance from the main railway lines and trade routes did not make business easier. The war ended, but the problems did not end, and their scale corresponded to the mass trauma and the death of millions of victims of that tragic conflict. These were the times of workers' revolts, of the bloody revolution in Russia, and at the same time of the war of the young state against the army of the Bolsheviks, which was trying to conquer Poland and carry the revolution westwards. I have written about this already.

"What in the history of the town of that period especially commands admiration is that – in those extremely difficult times – they managed to undertake many investments 
<!-- s. 17 [PDF 17] -->
 which raise the urban character and the prestige of Zagórów"[^7]. All the streets are laid with a road surface; there is erected a representative fire-station building with an auditorium; the Cooperative Bank puts up the municipal slaughterhouse and the People's House (1931); the town contributes to the upkeep of the bathhouse; the community of Evangelicals first builds the cantorate house (1913–1917), and in the year 1938 completes the construction of a spacious rectory for the pastor. A municipal power plant comes into being. The town is a living organism and, on the scale of the region, one of the centres of trade. Admiration is aroused by "the effort of modernizing the town undertaken by the inhabitants. Institutions and buildings serving all citizens they built themselves, and they managed their affairs so that they were able to maintain them"[^8]. Yet something disquieting is happening.

Dangerous cracks appear in the social fabric of the town and the region. The undoubted success of surviving the hardest years of the crisis – which came about thanks to the collective effort of the inhabitants – instead of integrating the community of Poles, Jews and Germans more strongly and more deeply, reveals divisions and tensions unknown or deeply hidden. In the years 1935–1937 a wave of anti-Jewish riots passes through Poland. It is the effect of extreme nationalist propaganda spreading across the country, of the actions of activists of the National Party, and of the import of Nazi ideology. There have survived photographs of the march of a compact column of Zagórów nationalists carrying high a banner reading "Zagórów free of Jews". On 5 February 1936 these tensions came to a climax. A crowd of assailants, stirred up by some squire from Wyszyna, Wawrzyniec Silski, burst into the market square, attacking Jews and destroying their shops and property. The scale of the riots was such (some 2,000 individuals were counted) that the police, in defence of those attacked, had to use live ammunition. Three persons died and a dozen or so were wounded. Two days later, in Szetlewek, during an attempt to arrest the participants of the Zagórów assault, three more ruffians perished, and there were also many wounded. The court sentenced 38 participants to terms of imprisonment.



[^7]: Słowiński Mirosław, *Zagórów. Album miasta 1407-2007*, p. 27.

[^8]: Słowiński Mirosław, ibid., p. 28.

<!-- s. 18 [PDF 18] -->

Nationalist moods, however, pass not only through the heads of many Poles. They have long been present in the milieu of the Jewish community. Representatives of the Zionist party, organizing collections of money for the purchase of land in Palestine, operate legally, at times unofficially. In the council of the Jewish community the Zionists win ever more seats in the successive elections from 1924 onwards. There may have been no aggression in this against others, but the Zionists treated the country in which they had lived for centuries as a temporary place of residence. A foreign side… The community of Evangelicals in the last town board before the outbreak of the war was represented, as alderman, by Teodor Milicz. The state police also notice the growing activity of some German settlers intoxicated by Nazi ideology. In the house of the cantor in Łazińsk, Gustav Adolf Mielke – at the same time a teacher and the head of the German Evangelical school – there had been assembled a small library of "works" propagating the vision of a great Germany and of a happy nation of masters. Apparently, in this whole hot cauldron, which in the political sense Zagórów was in those times, Mielke's activity must already have overstepped the bounds of tolerance. He was forced to leave Łazińsk and, as a punishment, transferred somewhere to Galicia. Of course, he returned at once after the entry of the Wehrmacht. In Zagórów itself the well-known merchant Julius Disterheft organizes an underground cell of the NSDAP. Under the cover of commercial business he rides around the district, at home he organizes men's gatherings, creating a disciplined and hostile group – a fifth column. The shocked citizens of Zagórów would learn of this immediately after the occupation of the town by a detachment of the Wehrmacht. These nationalisms, manifesting themselves so variously in the small Zagórów milieus, are like rays focused in a small lens, showing the picture of a world falling apart, marching ever faster towards the edge, beyond which lies the black abyss and the death of the millions of victims of another war. That march towards perdition could not be stopped, for infinite space was needed for the building of the Thousand-Year Reich.

During the war, in the ranks of the Polish Army, there fight many, often anonymous, soldiers of German roots. In the history of the Beutler family, which 
<!-- s. 19 [PDF 19] -->
 Damian Kruczkowski describes in these pages, we see the dramatic quality of their fate. In a similar way the fate of Erik Starcki of Łazińsk would unfold; he broke through numerous cordons and took part in the bloody battle of the Polish corps at Monte Cassino.

From the first days after 20 September 1939, when the soldiers of the Wehrmacht occupy Zagórów, all the actions undertaken are directed in the first place against the Jews. One may have the impression that the German front-line soldiers, driving the terrified citizens into various humiliating tasks, cutting off the Jews' sidelocks, amuse themselves splendidly with their fear. It is a kind of release of the trauma brought back from the field of battle, on which at any moment you may perish. But there must be order. A new administration is quickly formed, at the head of which, as the new mayor, stands the aforementioned Disterheft. Now everything goes according to the plan long since settled in Berlin. The Polish schools and institutions are closed. All are obliged to bow to the uniformed occupiers. The behaviour of many German fellow-citizens, inhabitants of the surroundings and of Zagórów, undergoes a violent change. This is noticed not only by the Poles, but also by the astonished Pastor Gross, together with the dwindling group of the faithful, whom these newcomers and uniformed functionaries ostentatiously ignore. Christianity is a decadent religion of the lowly, the humble and the weak. Their faith is the Führer and the party. "Just behind the Evangelical cemetery, on the road to Imielno, there has survived a "cholera cemetery", overgrown with fine pines, in the midst of which stood a small brick "corpse house", a relic of the pre-funeral house from the times of the last pestilence. […] The German gendarmerie shut up for the night in that "corpse house" Poles caught out after the police curfew"[^9]. From 14 November the Jews are obliged to wear armbands with the Star of David, ten centimetres wide. All educational and cultural organizations and associations, and of course religious ones, are dissolved. The houses are to be marked with the German Jude. But this is only the beginning. There follows 


[^9]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

<!-- s. 20 [PDF 20] -->
 universal and systematic plundering of the shops, warehouses and private houses, from which their inhabitants are removed by force. The same applies to the Poles, into whose farms German settlers from Volhynia and the Reich are moved in.

Contempt for the human being, for the former neighbour, knows no bounds. The first Zagórów Jew to be killed is the well-known butcher Jakub Szafraniecki. Disterheft's gang and the soldiers organize for themselves an entertainment, setting fire to the synagogue. Later – since not everything burned down – they drive the Jews to demolish the remaining scorched walls. These are organized, methodical actions, designed to break these people and deprive them of every basis of existence. These actions were, however, a zealous implementation of the policy of the Third Reich. The local people and Disterheft's men had their own scores to settle here. The new mayor is said to have invited to his office the unsuspecting members of the POW who had taken part in the recapture and sinking of the barge in 1918, under the pretext of carrying out some repairs. All six were arrested and, together with four others detained for other reasons, taken away to the not distant forest near Grabina. There they were brutally murdered with axes, entrenching tools and spades. They were buried in that forest in a common grave. Zagórów never forgot this crime, and it had a fundamental influence on the attitude towards the Germans – long after the end of the war. It happened, after all, in a terrain in which the majority of the inhabitants were German settlers and their descendants, and it is not possible that they did not know of this crime, while some took part in it outright. Disterheft himself made a flourishing business out of the office of mayor. Rich Jews, well-to-do Poles, if they wanted to get something done, had to pay dearly. Most gladly in gold, in valuables. Despite the ransom paid, the matter did not always get moving, and the victim still remained in the ghetto or in the investigative custody.

How deeply this grim figure lodged itself in the memory of the inhabitants is testified by the tales of Disterheft's end. In one of them his eyes were gouged out and he was killed; in another his wife and daughter were raped and he himself was shot; in a further one he escaped safe and sound and then hid somewhere in America. None of these 
<!-- s. 21 [PDF 21] -->



Mayor Diesterheft (seated second from the left) and comrades, in front of the fire station. Zagórów 1939


Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat



The forest at Osiny. The place of the killing of the POW members, after the exhumation of the victims. Enclosed with a wooden fence and a cross erected.


Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat



In the solemn funeral of the murdered POW members, on 11 November 1945, the whole little town and the inhabitants of the surrounding villages took part.


Photo by Roman Matuszewski, from the archive of Andrzej Bernat

<!-- s. 22 [PDF 22] -->
 versions finds confirmation in the sources. The truth, for all the victims of this individual, is painful. He had gathered together enough "assets" and was sufficiently crafty to flee in good time and bury himself somewhere deep in Germany. He lived to a great age, and his family and descendants lived, and perhaps still live, in the conviction that he was not fleeing from the Poles, "whom he helped", but from the terrible Russians… From the spring of 1940 an open ghetto is organized in the town, whose principal seat was the People's House. Over the course of more than a year, 2,500 to 3,000 Jews were brought in. Some of the healthy and young men were sent to forced labour. The remaining group, of more than 1,500 persons, from September to 15 November 1941 was taken away to the forests near Kazimierz Biskupi and brutally murdered. Some were poisoned with exhaust fumes in trucks; others were thrown alive into pits with quicklime. This was a crime committed by the Konin branch of the Gestapo[^10]. The war in Zagórów showed its worst face. Among the numerous documents from the life of the German Evangelicals in the wartime years, which it proved possible to collect, only in one, a life story written by hand, does Gisela Schulz mention that she was a witness of how the Jews lined up for transport were brutally driven away. Apart from that, silence and the stillness of the dead.


In January 1945 the Russians enter the territories of the Zagórów land. Revenge has various faces, and this is a further act of the drama. The majority of the settlers are expelled. Their place is taken by the Polish farmers earlier thrown out, or by other families settled there. After months of uncertainty comes the decision to deport all of them to Germany. The winter removal of whole villages begins – most often of women, children and the old, for the men are being taken away to labour in Russia. Only a few manage to return. Heart-rending is the account of the tragedy of the families of Gerard Beutler and Leo Byczyński. In accordance with the order of the authorities, they were leaving with their wives and small children. In the village of Małe Cieśle, on 25 January 1946, they were attacked by unknown men, bandits. Gerard, a former soldier 


[^10]: *Dwa życia jak dwa psalmy. Historia mówiona Leona i Toshy Jedwab*. Edited by Krzysztof Makowski, Mirosław Słowiński. 2012.

<!-- s. 23 [PDF 23] -->
 of the Polish Army, died from the blows of a hatchet, and Leo Byczyński, shot, died after several days. These were not isolated cases. As Gerard's aunt Kuska from Lądek defined those years, with sorrow and simplicity: "Hitler came and spoilt everything." Time, as it flows, has its hard laws. But it also gives the possibility of looking into the past without the emotions that accompanied the dramas now gone.

My family home and the home of my grandparents stood on Konińska Street (Hindenburgstraße during the war) in Zagórów, in the closest neighbourhood of the Evangelical church, which we called the German church. As a very young person, in the second half of the nineteen-fifties, I attended a kindergarten which was housed in the former cantorate house, at No. 4 on the same street. I am unable to explain to myself a certain fear that always accompanied me when I passed (especially in the evening) by that house of worship. I am unable, because at home nobody frightened me either with that building or with those people who appeared once a month at the Sunday mass. A certain strangeness of the place was softened by the mighty chestnut trees, whose beautiful fruits, gleaming light brown, we gathered in racing competition. Over many years, perhaps once or twice, I managed to glimpse the inside, but the threshold of the church I never crossed. Nor, at that time, did I ever manage to free myself from the feeling of the strangeness of that place. Of the recent war I knew more from lessons at school or from the reading of books than from tales at home. Perhaps that is why those short stories, which my dear ones told, lodged themselves so firmly in my memory.

My mother's father, beloved Grandpa Bronisław Maciejewski, was a prisoner of the concentration camps in Oświęcim (No. 171 181), Brzezinka, Sachsenhausen and Buchenwald (No. 79 608). He landed in the camp for the killing of a pig. A neighbour – a Pole – denounced him to the police; to him, in confidence, Grandpa had sold two kilograms of ribs. In January 1945 the Russian soldiers entering the town were catching and killing on the spot marauders or over-zealous functionaries of the Third Reich. But those days of liberty regained – as I remember the accounts of the members of the household at that time – were not particularly celebrated. There was no news whatsoever of Grandpa (the father 
<!-- s. 24 [PDF 24] -->
 of four children); it was not known whether he was alive, where he was imprisoned. Grandma Salomea told of one incident. Intrigued by some commotion in the street, she looked out of the window and saw a patrol of Red Army men leading, under the muzzles of their rifles, the German gendarme, an inhabitant of Zagórów – Edwin Rydl, who had stayed behind and did not intend to flee anywhere. She was certain that they were leading him out to be shot. She ran out into the street, barred the way of the marching men and began to beg them to let Rydl go, that he was a decent man who had harmed no one. "Surely my protests would have achieved little – Grandma added – had not a Russian officer come up." He listened none too willingly to these protective assurances, until Salomea Maciejewska, with weeping, forced out of herself that her husband was in a German camp, that she did not know whether he was alive, but that this man had helped us. They let Rydl go. My grandfather was liberated by the Americans, who on 11 April 1945 occupied the Arbeitslager Dora in Nordhausen, where in an underground camp factory the V-1 and V-2 were produced. He returned home. This substantial man weighed 44 kilograms. (The denouncer, arrested together with Grandpa, died in one of the camps). Rydl was, across the street, the neighbour of my grandparents for the whole rest of their lives, and I saw him many times at their house. He came most often in the evening and always spoke in a whisper. He had his duties in the Evangelical church, but he was also a trusted man of Tytus Pieszak – a universally respected Zagórów trader, who entrusted him with responsible tasks requiring discretion.

From my father's wartime memories there lodged in my memory the story of the Herman family, who were installed in the house of my grandfather Tomasz Słowiński on Nowa Street (the Germans renamed it Manfred von Richthofen Straße, in honour of the Luftwaffe ace of the First World War). They took over the whole farmstead, the spacious house, leaving to the very numerous family one room and the kitchen. The father and the brothers – those who had not been taken away to labour or were languishing in captivity – had to work their land for the German master. But the daily relations did not give rise to any particular tensions between the families. Grandpa Tomasz Słowiński, for many terms of office – until the First World War – was elected mayor of Zagórów. He always gained many votes from the minorities: from the Jews and from the descendants 
<!-- s. 25 [PDF 25] -->
 of the German settlers. Might this have had some influence? Yet it was not this fact that lodged most firmly in my father's memory. When the Third Reich, in June 1941, attacked Stalin's Russia – its recent ally in the tearing of Poland apart – the Herman son received his call-up to the Wehrmacht and quickly found himself on the eastern front. His parents seemed proud of that fact, and often the father of the soldier, on a Sunday afternoon, would inform Grandpa Tomasz of his son's part in the victorious conquests. Until the time. One day a deputation of local notables strode into the yard. They brought the notification of the heroic death of the son fighting for the Führer and for a Greater Germany. Whole nights long there reached them from behind the wall the unending wailing and the loud weeping of the mother.

At the beginning of the nineteen-fifties the Office of Public Security controlled and intruded into all the areas of the social and economic life of our country, rebuilding itself from the wartime destruction. From the year 1949, after completing his military service, the Gminna Cooperative in Zagórów was directed by my father Zdzisław Słowiński. The style of governing of the authorities of those days was such that every undertaking was proclaimed an extraordinarily important campaign. And the propaganda of those days saw enemies and diversionists everywhere. One of the more important campaigns was the harvest. For the authorities it was an extraordinary event; for the majority of the people living on the land it was a natural duty of providing bread for themselves and for the country for the coming year. It had been so from time immemorial, and that is what my father thought – the son of a hard peasant. During the next harvest, officers from the county command of the UB entered my father's office with the information that there was a decision to designate the former German church in Zagórów as a grain warehouse. My dad never used strong or grand words. He calmly answered that there was no such need, because the warehouses for the reception of the grain were prepared. Unfortunately, this answer, instead of calming the envoys from Konin, positively irritated them. They declared that the decision existed and that the chairman was answerable for its execution. My father firmly refused. He asked one short question:

– Do you want to do the same thing the Hitlerites did with the Catholic church, turning it into a warehouse?!

<!-- s. 26 [PDF 26] -->

At that the conversation ended. They returned after a few days, only to hear the refusal once more. They left the office, threatening and intimidating.

– They heard good advice – my dad concluded – to occupy themselves with "their own job" and let me get on with my work.

The first mass after the war in the Evangelical church took place on 18 July 1954. My father directed the cooperative without interruption for 40 years.

To young people, fed on propaganda pap, the war appeared as an extraordinary adventure, in which victorious fighting came first. Of course, the blood that flowed was the blood of enemies. So naturally I very much wanted to hear the telling of some heroic deeds of my dear ones. Excited by another film or by a novel I had read, I would try to question my father or grandfather about that difficult time and about their wartime experiences. They could not be persuaded into longer reminiscences.

– God forbid a war – I heard many times…

Grandpa would dismiss various questions with the brief statement that whoever had not been there, had not survived the camp, the death march, hunger, would understand little. Nor did my father belong to the veteran storytellers. Apart from general sentences, very cautiously formulated, it was difficult to extract from him any precise description of an event, of human behaviour, especially that concerning the times directly after the liberation. He had many bad experiences in this, but he did not want to share them. The story of the harvest campaign was an exception.

In the corner house on Konińska Street, adjoining across Targowa Street the former cantorate house, there lived in the attic a woman whose dramatic fate I learned of only in adult life. Her attire – some rags, worn grey hair, a matte madness in the eyes and often a she-goat led on a string – it is a sight I cannot forget. And the hollow sound in the middle of the night – the sounds of wood being chopped on the corner stone adjoining the house in which shelter was given her. This was Janina Breede, née Stawicka, the wife of a well-known German doctor – one of the first ladies on the Volksliste. Her sad story is described by Katarzyna Połom in the text "Doktorowa. Świetność i upadek Janiny Breede". This tale 
<!-- s. 27 [PDF 27] -->
 speaks above all of this: that we should remember whence we came and from what source our roots drank water. In the history of human affairs there is one unchanging regularity. Those who depart soonest are its witnesses: politicians, creators, participants in events great and in those of every day, victors and vanquished – living people. What they accomplished, built or wrote remains far longer, but even that implacable time consumes and buries. There remains the memory of the succeeding generations and the sorrow that those who have departed we can ask about nothing any more.

In the course of the last years much has been achieved, so that this vanishing world should not be obscured by the "cloud of oblivion". There already exists a considerable collection of documents concerning the past history of the inhabitants of the Zagórów land. In the year 2007 there appeared the book which I entitled *Zagórów. Album miasta 1407-2007*, accompanying the celebrations of the 600th anniversary of the granting of town rights. It is a panoramic picture of the town and its citizens across the centuries. Five years later there appeared the book *Dwa życia jak dwa psalmy. Historia mówiona Leona i Toshy Jedwab*. Through the fate of a Jewish married couple we come to know the life of the local Jews and the drama of the Holocaust. It is worth mentioning the valuable output of the Zagórów historian Bogdan Grzonkowski, the collections of Andrzej Bernat, the publications of Jarosław Buziak. Katarzyna Połom's website: "Zagórów – miejsce z historią" (zagorowzhistoria.com) is an important address on the net, where successive texts appear about the documents that have been found, about people and events in former and recent times. It is also difficult to list all the exceedingly valuable initiatives popularizing "close" history, which are undertaken by the Municipal Centre of Culture, directed by Łukasz Parus, and by the Society of Friends of the Zagórów Land, with Grzegorz Cieślewicz at its head. The picture of the history of the town and of the Zagórów land is, however, still incomplete. It is extraordinarily difficult to reach the accounts and documents from the life of the Evangelicals of Zagórów and its surroundings, mostly Germans. The hard laws of the end of the war caused that after 1945 there remained here a handful of the formerly numerous, active and industrious community. Also in connection with this, the picture of the participation, lasting at the very least two centuries, of the German-Evangelical population in the life 
<!-- s. 28 [PDF 28] -->
 of the town and gmina of Zagórów is far from complete. If one leafs through the pages of the internet records, then in fact we have an insight only into the times of the Second World War. The scholarly literature is exceptionally modest. I thought, therefore, that we would never come closer to sketching a fuller picture of the history of the town and of this land, if we did not try to learn something more about those who lived here two hundred and fifty years ago and whose traces time is erasing. Despite the whole tragic period of the years 1939–1945, one must remember that this is only a part of the history of this land and of its many inhabitants, which one should attempt to tell.

This is that broken link in the chain of mutual connections, forming a kind of code of life and the picture of those generations. Of that strange community, living at once together and apart. Yet one must try. Perhaps we shall be able to gather into a coherent whole this scattered history. In August 2020 I met with Łukasz Parus – the director of the House of Culture in Zagórów – and shared with him the observations described above. He caught fire at the idea and, what is important, knew well whom to invite to cooperate. Nor did the idea have to be urged upon Rev. Waldemar Wunsz, the pastor of the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Konin, in whose territory the Evangelical church of Zagórów lies. The inaugural meeting took place on 10 September 2020. And so the idea of undertaking work on the preparation of a publication about the history of the Evangelical community (Hauländers and non-Hauländers, Germans, Poles) in the Zagórów land began to materialize. A few weeks later, at a meeting in the Gęsi Puch Guest House, the former cantorate house, the editorial committee was constituted, which undertook to bring the whole matter to a successful conclusion. After nearly three years of work there had been assembled a rich body of material in the form of studies, articles, interviews, memoirs (here the precious record of Wiesława Kowalska's conversations with Erich Kuhn, and the story of Eugenia Plitt recorded by Günter Hennings), documents and photographs, which we hand over to the readers in the form of the prepared book. We strove, as far as lay in our power, to realize the main task that guided us – that is, to look into the depth of history, into the tragic years of the war of 1939–1945 and into the most recent times.

<!-- s. 29 [PDF 29] -->

And fate – governing itself by its own singular and unpredictable logic – caused a collection of letters, documents and books, found in the house of the cantor Gustav Adolf Mielke of Łazińsk, to fall into our hands, revealing the picture of a family utterly intoxicated by Nazism, fervent, but also naïve in their blindness, admirers of the Führer. For the writer of these words it was not an easy read, but this work had to be done… Thus came into being the text "Listy żołnierza Wehrmachtu odkryte w ścianie domu kantora".

It is difficult to define the character of this volume, on account of the variety of the material placed in it. It is not a monographic study, but rather a book, which in its composition recalls the old domestic *Silva Rerum* (Las rzeczy). In such books there were written down accounts of important events, notes from journeys, diaries, receipts and household recipes, as well as legends preserved in memory. So too can our texts be read. From the first to the last page, or the chosen items, which have been grouped in three parts: Historia i pamięć, Wspomnienia i refleksje, Świadectwa. If we have succeeded in preserving in memory and telling even a small fragment of this two-hundred-year history, then perhaps it will be easier to understand.

That the work undertaken was recognized by the Zagórów community as needed is testified by many rather unusual incidents, which accompanied us. On the website of the Zagórów House of Culture there appeared a communiqué with a request for information or for the delivery of surviving documents, photographs, testimonies and writings. The announcement met with a quick response, and very interesting, source materials reached the House of Culture. The Society of Friends of the Zagórów Land announced a collection among internet users, to support the budget of the publisher.

An altogether extraordinary event, however, was the concert of Daniel Słowiński and friends, organized by Rev. Waldemar Wunsz on 23 September 2023 in the Evangelical church in Zagórów, combined with a collection of money for the needs of the publication. The church was filled with listeners, who rewarded the artists and the organizer with a standing ovation. The money collected by the Society and during the concert significantly supplement the means devoted to this purpose by the mayor Roman 
<!-- s. 30 [PDF 30] -->
 Kulterman. Also the starosta of Słupca, Dr Jacek Bartkowiak, gives assurance of support for the printing of the book with means from the budget of his Office. On a separate page we try to name, by name, all the donors and sponsors, thanks to whom the printing of the book becomes possible.

The volume, handed into the readers' hands, is the result of a collective work. To all the authors, for their reliable cooperation and patience, enormous thanks are due. I thank Dr Zbigniew Chodyła for the consultations and the critical reading of the texts. I consider it my duty to emphasize particularly the contribution of Katarzyna Połom, Łukasz Parus, Damian Kruczkowski, Günter Hennings, and the translators of the German language, with Jacek Kin, who did the greatest part of the translations and passed on many important materials. Without their creative participation the accomplishment of this work would have been impossible.

<!-- s. 31 [PDF 31] -->
## History and Memory


<!-- s. 32 [PDF 32] -->


<!-- s. 33 [PDF 33] -->

### The Protestants of the Zagórów Land (16th to 21st Centuries)[^11]


Bogdan Grzonkowski[^11] Text prepared and made ready for print by Katarzyna Połom

#### I. The Reformation Era



Drawing for the design of the stained-glass window The Coat of Arms of Zagórów, by Andrzej Żurek


A plaque on the wall of the Evangelical church in Zagórów commemorating the inhabitants of the Protestant faith who settled on the Zagórów land from the mid-18th century onward.


Photo from the archive: “Zagórów miejsce z historią”

Protestantism reached the vicinity of Zagórów, as it did the whole of Wielkopolska, in the years 1520–1525. In the second half of the sixteenth century the territory of Konin county, together with the estates of the Cistercian monastery in Ląd on the Warta, to which the little town and part of the surrounding villages belonged, had been seized by the Reformation, and the missionary centre of the religious “novelties” was precisely the Cistercian monastery, from which the clergy carried them to the surrounding localities.

The Evangelical nobility reduced the extent of corvée labour, the threshing quotas and other burdens of serfdom, winning adherents of the new faith 


[^11]: Bogdan Grzonkowski (born 1958 – died 2009) – a historian by education and vocation, a figure of great merit in the study and promotion of knowledge of the history of the Zagórów land. One of the fruits of his many years of searching and work was the text "Historia ewangelicyzmu w Zagórowie" [The History of Protestantism in Zagórów], written in 2002. It was published in part, in the form of articles, in the local press. The author's intention was to write a book on the history of the Zagórów Lutheran parish. His tragic death brutally thwarted these plans. Bogdan Grzonkowski was my teacher and mentor. It was he who instilled in me the bacillus of delving into history and of respect for the land on which I grew up. When word came of the beginning of work on "Zerwane ogniwo" [The Broken Link], Barbara Leśniewska, Bogdan Grzonkowski's sister, contacted me and handed into my keeping all the materials left behind by her late brother. Among them was, inter alia, the typescript of the "Historia ewangelicyzmu w Zagórowie". She also kindly consented to the article being worked up and used in the publication being prepared. I made minor corrections, supplemented the history of the Zagórów church after the year 2003, and gave the text a new title, footnotes and a bibliography. In one of our numerous conversations Bogdan Grzonkowski mentioned that perhaps one day we would write a book together… In a certain sense that succeeded.

<!-- s. 34 [PDF 34] -->
 among the peasants. Congregations were active in Golina, Myśliborz and Cienin – that is, directly in the vicinity of Zagórów – and also further afield: in Lisew, Nieświastów, Wyszyn, Wola Łaszczowa, Żychlin and so on. In keeping with the premise that the Reformation was a renewal of the Church, often entirely new congregations were not founded at all; rather, in existing churches services began to be held and the sacraments administered according to Reformation principles. In Konin, services were held in the castle. Even the parish priest Lutomierski favoured the religious novelties from Wittenberg. In turn, the inspection carried out by canon Wysocki in the year 1550 found that the monastery estates of Ląd were leased to dissenters, and even turned up correspondence with Wittenberg. In the second half of the sixteenth century, Lutherans also served as castellans of Ląd, among them Rafał Przyjemski.



Drawing for the design of the stained-glass window Luther's Rose, by Andrzej Żurek.

Indeed, Protestantism must have been very widespread in Zagórów and its surroundings, since:

– the monastery in Ląd, seized by the spirit of the Reformation, possessed three towns (Lądek, Kłodawa and Zagórów, with the parish churches located in them) as well as 63 villages;  
– the coat of arms of Zagórów is based on the so-called Luther Rose[^12];  
– under the pretext of national matters (the Cistercians hailed from Germany), in the autumn of 1553 the Cistercians were driven out to Silesia, to Henryków (Heinrichau).

After the election of Jan Wysocki as abbot in Ląd there began the period of the Counter-Reformation, which destroyed the Polish current of the Reformation in this area. The old teaching returned to the surrounding churches, yet in hearts and minds there remained the influence of the new way of thinking, which is visible to this day.

Nor can one forget the events of the year 1651, when Cossack emissaries appear among the peasants of the Ląd estates, at the head of whom stands Piotr Grzybowski. At that time the abbot of the monastery is 


[^12]: The Luther rose, or properly Luther's seal (German: *Luther rose*) – the symbol of the Protestant Reformation, designed and described by Martin Luther, and at the same time the emblem of most Churches of the Augsburg Confession (Evangelical).

<!-- s. 35 [PDF 35] -->
 Jan Zapolski, who, embarking on the construction of a new, great church beside the monastery, lays further burdens upon the surrounding peasants, mercilessly exacted by the leaseholders. The peasants of the Ląd estates remember, and perhaps even profess, Lutheranism. They therefore support the rising organised by Grzybowski. The abbot of Ląd, however, smashes the peasant grouping at Królikowo.

#### II. Evangelical-German Settlement in the 18th–19th Centuries

The spread and further development of Protestantism came about above all in the eighteenth century, when the settlers then arriving in great numbers, chiefly German, were in their overwhelming majority Lutherans. It appeared anew in the period of Hauländer settlement (by colonists of Dutch or Flemish origin, living under Hauländer law), whose beginnings in the vicinity of Zagórów reach back to the year 1746. It was then that the owner of Trąbczyn, Paweł z Kolna Prusimski, permitted the founding, in the forest, of the colony of Łazińsk, and also of Trąbczyńska Huta. After the second partition of Poland (1793), the Zagórów land fell to Prussia. After 1804 the following colonies came into being: Olchowo (Sophiental), Mały Las (Ludwigslust), Tarszewo (Friedlichsfeld), Michalinów Oleśnicki (Emilienheim), Zalesie, Augustynów (Augustinhof), Anielewo (Ingelfingen), Chruściki (Adelhof).

In that period settlers came wandering to these lands from Lower Silesia, from the valley joining the Warta, the Odra and the Noteć. In their overwhelming majority they were Protestants. The lands on which they founded their settlements were wasteland – either they had to clear the forests and establish villages, or they settled in the Warta valley, where flood waters visited them several times a year. These settlers came in the hope of bettering their hitherto lot, and they stayed on despite the harsh conditions offered by the local realities.

In 1835 the number of German colonists with their families, Protestants, living in twenty localities in nine of the gminas of that time, situated in the nearer and farther surroundings of Zagórów, was as follows:
