| Wrąbczyn | 76 | In the gmina of Koszuty | 40 |
|  |  | Róża Kolonia | 40 |
| In the gmina of Pietrzyków | 224 |  |  |
| Wrąbczynkowska Kolonia | 224 |  |  |

In all, 3245 such settlers lived in the vicinity of Zagórów at that time.

The arriving settlers cleared the forest, turning it into arable land, founded villages and built chapels – houses of prayer – with a school room and a dwelling for the cantor. The oldest cantorate in our area arose at Łazińsk (a new house of prayer was built in 1838 for 300 persons); further cantorates were built later in Olchowo, Michalinów Trąbczyński, Michalinów Oleśnicki, Anielewo and Tarszewo. In 1849 there existed a cantorate at Tarszewo, lying at a distance of five versts from Zagórów, and it numbered 150 of the faithful.

<!-- s. 37 [PDF 37] -->

#### III. The Founding and Growth of the Parish

##### 1. The Beginnings of Pastoral Care. The Erection of the Parish and the Building of the Church

Until 1817 pastoral service to the Protestant settlers was at first provided by pastors from Września, and later from Grodziec, Konin and Żychlin. Pastoral care demanded a great sacrifice of time and health on the part of the pastors, who had to travel by horse-drawn carriage for tens of kilometres to reach those chapels. After all, they had their own duties in their mother parishes. The growing number of Protestants made constant pastoral care a necessity, which involved the calling into being of a new parish and the building of a church.

In 1829 efforts were set in motion to build an Evangelical church at Kopojno, on the estate of Gottlieb Weigl. In accordance with the protocol of 7 December 1829, signed on 15 November 1833, Weigel established (for the future parish) an annual deputat consisting of 40 quarters of rye, 10 quarters of barley, 25 quarters of oats and 6 barrels of beer. In his letter of 22 March 1836, addressed to the superintendent[^13] of Kalisz, Karol Weigel wrote that he wished to support the Protestants (“it is my greatest wish”). Unfortunately, in that same year Karol died, and his son and heir Aleksander withdrew from his father's dispositions.

In 1836 the Provisional Church College (composed of Matthäus Luther, Ludwig Sperling, Johann Kelsch, Johann Gottlieb Kittel, Karl Heit) turned to the above-mentioned superintendent Modl[^14] with a request for help in building the church, and this was promised to them. After 1836 the Konin county commissioner approved the plan for the founding of an Evangelical church community and the building of a church in Zagórów. In the Evangelical church community 


[^13]: Superintendent – a Protestant clergyman exercising supervisory authority over the pastors of a given area.

[^14]: Ernst Johann August von Modl (1794-1860), parish priest in Kalisz, a Lutheran, superintendent of the General Consistory of Evangelical Denominations, and after 1849 Lutheran superintendent.

<!-- s. 38 [PDF 38] -->
 there were then 2937 souls. The cost of construction was to come to 1727 złoty, and the annual upkeep of the pastor to 253 zł 28 gr, 40 quarters of rye, 25 quarters of oats, 10 quarters of barley and 6 barrels of beer.

Unfortunately, it did not pass without further difficulties. On 9 February 1837 the Protestants of Lisew declared that they had their own house of prayer and pastors from Grodziec, and that consequently they did not wish to belong to the Zagórów parish. The faithful of Chruściki, Holendry Tomickie, Pyzdry and Ciemierów did likewise. Despite the schism, the Protestants of Zagórów itself and the nearer surroundings resolved to bring about the founding of a branch in Zagórów and the election of a church college. The final and legal establishment of the branch congregation did not come until 1843. In the meantime, services were held by pastor Scholz of Żychlin in rented premises. In 1841 the mayor of Zagórów declared that Zagórów possessed 3924 Evangelical souls and ought to be a parish with its own church. For the time being, it was decided to move the branch from Zagórów to Łazińsk, which had a newly built house of prayer (1838-1839) able to hold 300 persons.

The heiress of Trąbczyn, Michalina Mączyńska, declared to superintendent von Modl in 1846 that she undertook to render assistance in the form of grain and fuel, provided that a church arose at Łazińsk and a pastor exercised his ministry there. On 6 July 1846 von Modl came to Łazińsk and established that indeed the local Protestants wished to have their religious centre here. This time the faithful of Koszuty, Młodojewo and Kosewo decidedly opposed the location of the church at Łazińsk, and consequently the Consistory[^15] did not give its consent to the earlier proposal supported by von Modl. In 1850 a building was purchased at 54 Konińska Street, one part of which was destined for a house of prayer, and the other for a school and the cantor's dwelling. Until Christmas of 1856, Zagórów was administered by pastor Karl Friedrich Orive, parish priest of Grodziec.



[^15]: Consistory – the council standing at the head of a diocese or a Church.

<!-- s. 39 [PDF 39] -->



The building of the Evangelical-Augsburg church in Zagórów at the beginning of the 20th century.


Photo from the collection of Andrzej Bernat


Members of the parish choir and trombonists from Zagórów, Olchowo, Oleśnica, Michalinów, Mały Las and Augustynów, 1938. Seated in the middle of the lower row is pastor Aleksander Gross; beside him, the cantor Keiser.



Photo from the parish archive



The consecration of the parsonage of the Evangelical-Augsburg church in Zagórów in 1938.


Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat

<!-- s. 40 [PDF 40] -->

From Christmas of 1856, Zagórów became an independent parish, receiving as its first permanent pastor-parish priest Wilhelm Winkler. In the following year the parish purchased two houses at 51 and 52 Konińska Street, in which, after renovation, the parsonage was set up. In connection with the elevation of Zagórów to the rank of a parish, the young heir Weigel declared on 10 January 1858 that he would give 12 morgs of land and brick for the building of a church, if only the parish were transferred to Michalinów Kopoiński. The Consistory rejected this proposal, and on 28 February 1862 it finally approved the building of the church in Zagórów. In the following year, representatives of the Consistory went to Count Taczanowski, the owner of Zagórów, to obtain assistance for the building of the church. The request was granted. Help also came from the Ministry of the Interior, which in 1877 resolved to appropriate 3000 roubles for the building of the church. The successive pastors: Winkler, Eugeniusz Biedermann (in the years 1860-1865), Karol Edward Erdmann held office in Zagórów very briefly. The reasons for their departures were prosaic: an unhealthy and damp parsonage, low pay and unsettled parish affairs – the location of the church and the extent of the parish. Despite the short time of their service in Zagórów, they cannot be accused of idleness – quite the contrary. On 8 May 1878 pastor Karol Edward Erdmann consecrated the foundation stone for the building of the church. On that occasion a church building committee was elected, composed of Aleksander Arndt, Wilhelm Schmechel, Wilhelm Münh, Wilhelm Kuch, Morstin Ziegelchorf, Emmanuel Munk and the members of the Church College. The technical works fell to Przanowski of Słupca, and the master builder was Gustaw Jursch of Orlina Duża. The town of Zagórów contributed from its forest 110 pieces of timber for the building of the church. The construction of the church lasted four years and cost 13 131 roubles 78 kopecks. The solemn consecration took place on the first Sunday of Advent in 1882.

In the following year the Church College ordered an organ from the firm Schlag and Sons in Świdnica, and in 1884 the parish taxed itself for the purchase of two bells. In thanksgiving for the services rendered to the parish 
<!-- s. 41 [PDF 41] -->
 by pastor Karol Edward Erdmann, his memory was honoured with the setting in place of a marble memorial tablet. The building and consecration of the church brought to an end the earlier disputes connected with its location. Seen from the perspective of time, the proverbial finger of God is clearly visible in this: who among the faithful, and how, would have driven to mass at Łazińsk, Kopojno or Michalinów after the resumption of services following the Second World War? The roads to those localities were sandy, buses did not reach them, and the parishioners had been stripped of their property; they had neither cars nor even motorcycles. To reach any of those localities, one had to drive through Zagórów…

##### 2. Further Construction Work and Pastoral Activity

In the territorially vast and numerically large Zagórów parish, construction and repair work went on constantly. A few years after the construction, precisely in 1890, a mighty hurricane tore off the sheet-metal roofing of the roof and the tower. Repair work was necessary, at a cost of 760 roubles.

In 1898 a new house of prayer was built in the cantorate of Chruściki, connected with the Zagórów parish. In the same year the parish also suffered a loss – the house of prayer in Michalinów burned down. In Zagórów itself, the parishioners at their Assembly of 7 March 1913 resolved to begin as quickly as possible the construction of a parsonage, a cantor's house (cantorate) and a school (on a plot purchased from Jan Hilman for 311 roubles). For this purpose a building committee was appointed, composed of August Driesner, Wilhelm Neumann, Paweł Rentz, Ferdynard Schulz, Christian Münch, Friedrich Schütz, August Kintzel, Juliusz Schneider, August Gibel, Daniel Gliege, Edward Kuhn and Heinrich Kinast.

On 16 October 1913 the foundation stone was laid for the construction of the parsonage, the cantor's house and the school. Unfortunately, the construction work was interrupted by the outbreak of the First World War. Work was resumed only in 1931.

<!-- s. 42 [PDF 42] -->

In 1923 Aleksander Gross became pastor in Zagórów (he remained so until his death in 1945). In the same year the parish received one bell, in place of the previous one – requisitioned during the First World War by the occupation authorities. In the years 1926-1927 the organ was rebuilt. The clock for the church tower, for the sum of 5000 złoty, was in turn endowed in 1927 by the married couple Luiza and Ferdynand Krüger of Olchowo. In this way they wished to commemorate their son, who had gone missing during the war in 1920.

The parish was visited, among others, on 22 August 1877 by superintendent Teichmann of the Diocese of Kalisz, on 10 August 1884 by general superintendent Everth, on 10 May 1903 by general superintendent Manitius, on 23 May 1909 and on 6-7 July 1925 by bishop Juliusz Bursche. The church officials supervised and passed judgement on the expansion and the activity of the parish.

The construction of the parsonage was resumed in 1931, it was roofed over in 1934, and four years later the new building was solemnly consecrated. This was an important event for the whole community of the faithful of the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Zagórów. Pastoral work in the parish concentrated above all in the cantorates, where the cantors led Bible meetings, choir rehearsals and rehearsals of the trombone ensemble. As a rule, once a month the pastor came to the cantorate to administer to the faithful the sacrament of communion. On the more important feast days, the parishioners from the cantorates made their way to the church in Zagórów.

On 1-11 September 1927, pastor Gross organised an evangelization in the parish, in which the following pastors took part: Leffler of Gębin, Paweł Otto of Łódź, and the evangelist Mark of Lwówek. In that year the choir was reactivated and a youth circle was founded. In October 1933 the solemn observance of the 50th anniversary of the consecration of the church took place. Apart from Zagórów, the largest Lutheran parish was the parish at Łazińsk, numbering before 1937 more than 150 Protestant owners of farms living there to the number of 115, plus 15 farmers from Huta Trąbczyńska, 10 from Trąbczyn B, 5 from Trąbczyn C and about 12 farmers from Trąbczyn D.

<!-- s. 43 [PDF 43] -->

#### IV. The Period of the Second World War (1939-1945)

After Hitler's coming to power, fascism and German nationalism sought their supporters and henchmen not only in Germany but also in Poland. As a result of such activities, the Evangelical Church became an arena of propaganda warfare, especially after 1936. The propaganda conducted from the centre in Łódź also reached the vicinity of Zagórów. Its task was to stir up anti-Polish moods – and this with regard both to Protestants and to Catholics. With sorrow one must state that there were those who fell for the bait of this propaganda. The hitherto good relations between the Polish population, which for centuries had lived alongside the settlers who had come from Germany and Silesia and alongside their descendants, began to sour. The international situation and the propaganda activities stirred up dislike, and even hatred. The slogan: “A Protestant is a German”, and the growing psychosis, meant that many Poles – Catholics – saw in the Protestants Germans, representatives of a hostile nation.

It was precisely this view – apart from methods of intimidation and coercion – that greatly facilitated the task of the occupation authorities in persuading the descendants of the settlers that, despite their Polish citizenship, they belonged to the German nation. Even before the outbreak of the war, this led to many lynchings and tragedies. Among others, in the neighbouring Pyzdry, the cantor Adam was unjustly suspected of espionage and anti-Polish activity; he was bestially beaten and drowned in the Warta. All millers of the Protestant faith were suspected of sending spy reports; they were visited by the police and the army, and some of them were also arrested[^16]. In Szymanowice, on the first Sunday of September, directly after mass, three Protestants were murdered – farmers who had supplied wagons to the Polish Army[^17]. These tragedies, arising from the psychosis fanned by the hysterical proclamations of the populists, called forth further ones – a tit for a tat. Meanwhile, many Protestant parishioners took part in the September Campaign, serving in the Polish Army.



[^16]: According to the oral account of Helena Winter.

[^17]: Wojciechowska Bogna, *Pastor z Wierzchów* in „Gazeta Poznańska" no. 209 of 7.09.1990, p. 9.

<!-- s. 44 [PDF 44] -->

After the fall of independence, the drama began. The descendants of the settlers were forced to sign the Volksliste[^18]. Some did so of their own will, others with open reluctance. The arguments that convinced people to sign the nationality list were the events described above and others like them, the ban on the renovation of buildings, and worse living conditions for those unwilling to sign the list. After signing the German nationality list, the men were conscripted into the German army – and yet some of them had only just taken off the Polish uniform…

The Polish population, mostly Catholic, was in a wretched position: forced labour, deportations. The Catholic church was closed, and people could not lead a normal religious life. In an even worse situation were the Jews, who were first placed in a ghetto and later transported to the extermination camps. The parish itself and the Protestants were not, as one might have supposed, in the best of positions. Hitler, like Stalin, permitted the existence of the Church as a necessary evil and a temporary instrument for ruling over people. Already during the hostilities, many cantorates went unstaffed, for the cantors had been called up to the army. The Hitlerite authorities restricted the possibilities of the Church's activity and kept the clergy under surveillance. Such acts as baptism or marriage performed in church were not recognised by the Hitlerite authorities. Records were drawn up no longer at the parsonage but at the state office. Many men were conscripted into the army; some of them perished. Among them were those who 


[^18]: *Deutsche Volksliste*, VDL – the Volksliste (German Nationality List). The so-called signing of the Volksliste involved filling out a detailed questionnaire containing questions about religious affiliation, since when the family had lived in the given area, what schools had been completed, about relatives in Germany, etc. The questionnaire was sent to the home. Once it had been collected, by decision of an official, the persons surveyed were assigned to one of the four groups of the Volksliste.

<!-- s. 45 [PDF 45] -->
 did not want to go to the army. Such men, as follows from the account of Mr Edward Schwartz, “were robbed by the Hitlerites”. If, after such preparation, the man summoned refused to put on the uniform, he was murdered in a bestial manner. The Protestant parishioners carried on unofficial slaughtering and trade, and many gave their Catholic neighbours various kinds of support. Yet not all were willing, and some were simply afraid, for a general terror was raging. It was not known who would report whom to the Gestapo: a Pole or a German?

The events of the war, and the division into “equals and more equals” – that is, the nationality groups – affected the worsening of relations between Protestants and Catholics. For people, for their humanity, for Christian values, there came a time of heavy trial. Not everyone emerged victorious from that trial – both among the Protestants and among the Catholics. There were examples of self-sacrifice, of risking one's life for others, but there was also persecution. During the war, some Protestants made no secret of their anti-Polish sympathies and looked down with a sense of superiority on the Polish Catholic population. Some made themselves sorely felt by the Poles and contributed to their death, their deportation to concentration camps, or other forms of persecution. As early as November 1939, there came arrests, imprisonment and secret executions of members of the Polish Military Organisation. This was retribution for the “Barka” action of 1918. A group of Polish fighters, during an attempt to sink a barge carrying grain requisitioned from the local farmers, killed one of the bargemen. Both the Poles and the mortally wounded German knew one another; the victim, named Klemsztajn, came from Wrąbczynek. The second of the boatmen, the brother of the man shot dead, escaped and filed a report at the gendarmerie post of a robbery and murder. He, too, knew well the people who had set upon them. No arrests took place at the time; Poland regained her independence and chaos reigned at the gendarmerie posts. The family of the murdered man, however, promised vengeance, which was carried out only on the night of 22 November 1939. In the forest near Grabina, members of the POW were tortured to death: Karol Rychliński, Franciszek Pluciński, Jan Kmieciak, Feliks Molski, Antoni Płócienniczak and Stanisław Budaszewski. Together with them perished men who did not belong to the POW and had nothing to do with the “Barka” action: Tadeusz Płócienniczak, Mikołaj Smorawiński, Marek Paradziński and Józef Krata[^19].



[^19]: The irony of fate was that in 1918 it was Romuald Brzeg who fired from the barge – the only one of the "Peowiacy" who managed to escape and avoid death in 1939.

<!-- s. 46 [PDF 46] -->

The painful memories, cherished for long years after the war, engraved themselves permanently in the memory of the people of Zagórów. To this day, the image of the descendants of the settlers as vindictive and treacherous enemies lingers in the consciousness of many inhabitants of the little town.

#### V. After the Second World War

Both during the liberation and immediately after it, lynchings took place, carried out upon often innocent Protestants. So it was in Olchowo, where in January 1945 Soviet soldiers, set on by a certain Pole, murdered almost the entire Winterfeld family from Zagórów. These people were innocent. The Germans who had something on their conscience had long since managed to leave the neighbourhood – rightly foreseeing that the wronged Poles would seek revenge on their recent persecutors. After the liberation, the Protestants lost their farms, their workshops, their means of livelihood; they were driven out of their own premises. Most of them resolved to leave the Polish lands on which they had lived and worked for generations, for over 150 years. Many parishioners experienced deportation; they worked for the Russians in harsh conditions and on starvation rations of food. Among them was also the last pre-war pastor of the Zagórów parish, the Rev. Aleksander Gross, whose body did not endure the hardships of the work in the quarry. The pastor died in the camp hospital at Finolne (near Stalino) in November 1945[^20]. Many parishioners shared the fate of their pastor. Those who had the good fortune and survived went straight from Russian captivity to Germany, for fear of persecution at the hands of the Poles. The remaining Protestant population had to serve the Catholic farmers, or was deported to farms belonging to the state farms (PGR).

The church was closed; there were plans to turn it into a grain warehouse, which in the end did not come to pass. The organ vanished, however; in the windows 


[^20]: Kneifel, E., *Die Pastoren der Evangelisch-Augsburgischen Kirche in Polen*, pp. 95-96.

<!-- s. 47 [PDF 47] -->
 the panes were smashed and the crystal chandeliers were looted. The houses of prayer were converted into seats of the gmina authorities or into schools. The drama begun in 1939 went on: the Protestants, deprived of their property, of pastoral care, of the possibility of baptisms, funerals and church weddings, departed by degrees for Germany. For long years there remained in their hearts grief, sorrow, and here and there – hatred. The situation began to return to normal only after the lapse of many years. As a result of the state's legal regulations with regard to the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, the parish's real estate began to be returned. This real estate had already been settled upon, and often tenancy agreements had to be concluded. The tenants, in turn, did not always wish to pay, treating church property as former German property. It was this problem that had to be faced by the delegate of the Consistory for the territory of the parish – Bernard Otello – and later by the superior of the diocese, the Rev. senior Ryszard Trenkler of Toruń. The real estate at Wierchy was administered by the Church Property Fund (Fundusz Mienia Kościelnego) until 1997. As a result of the actions of bolder Protestants, among them Edwin Rydl, who gathered signatures under a petition for the opening of the church, the church building too was handed back. The parish, once numerous, became a diaspora parish and a very poor one, for such were its members. The first post-war administrator of the parish was the Rev. Karol Świtalski, who on 18 July 1954 celebrated the first service after the Second World War. The pastor also took over the management of the parish property. This caused many difficulties, yet it helped to cover the financial needs of the congregation only to a minimal degree. What mattered most, however, was the recovery of the church and the resumption of pastoral work – to which, after all, the Church of Christ on earth was called. Before the Rev. Świtalski and the members of the parish there stood the difficult task of rebuilding religious and church life, of overcoming hatred and dislike. It was also necessary to maintain the church, which after 1945 had been stripped of its chandeliers, bells, organ and liturgical furnishings. The archives, too, vanished somewhere, and among them – the registers of vital records. They have not been found to this day. Services were held once a month and only in Zagórów, for the remaining houses of prayer had been converted 
<!-- s. 48 [PDF 48] -->
 into state schools. The administrator himself lived in Poznań, from where he commuted to the parishes under his care.

In the so-called cantor's house, from the end of the Second World War until the first half of the 1960s, there was located the Municipal Kindergarten in Zagórów, and after it, until the mid-1980s, the Municipal Centre of Culture together with the “Ruch” Club and the Municipal Library. The ground floor of the parsonage, meanwhile, until the mid-1960s was the seat of the Municipal National Council, and afterwards it was leased to the Municipal Kindergarten in Zagórów, moved there from the cantor's house (I attended the kindergarten both in the cantor's house and at the parsonage; I remember those years well and recall them with nostalgia). The first floor of the parsonage was inhabited by the Bürger family. Mrs Ilona Bürger for many years was the caretaker of the local Evangelical church, tending with care the sanctuary entrusted to her keeping[^21].

In the year 1968, a reorganisation was carried out in the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, as a result of which the Zagórów parish lost its legal personality and became a branch of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin. After the Rev. Karol Świtalski, parish priest of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin, the ministry was exercised by the Rev. Rudolf Gajda, and at the turn of the 1960s and 1970s – by the Rev. Henryk Schneider. From 1976 the parish priest of the Konin parish was the Rev. Councillor Andrzej Mendrok, who was at the same time parish priest-administrator of the branch congregation in Zagórów[^22]. Thanks to his initiative and his devotion, in the 1980s the church was successfully renovated and the building regained at least some of its former charm.

#### VI. After the Period of Transformation (1989-2023)

In the 1990s, the most fruitful events and undertakings for the Protestants of Zagórów were those taken up 


[^21]: Mrs Ilona Bürger died in 2016.

[^22]: He served in his post until 2011, when he was succeeded by the Reverend Waldemar Wunsz.

<!-- s. 49 [PDF 49] -->
 by the then parish priest. It was precisely on the initiative of pastor Andrzej Mendrok that, on 15-16 August 1992, the second gathering of the parishioners of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish took place. The 110th anniversary of the consecration of the church in Zagórów and the 50th anniversary of confirmation were then celebrated. During the momentous ceremonies, a memorial tablet in honour of pastor Aleksander Gross, who had been parish priest of the Zagórów parish for over twenty years (1923-1945), was unveiled in the church. Apart from the local Protestants, the gathering was attended by the faithful who had once belonged to the community and now live beyond the borders of Poland. There came about 90 persons from Germany and two persons from the USA. Through the efforts of the parish priest, the Rev. Councillor Mendrok, the Evangelical Seniors' Home in Zagórów was opened on 16 June 1995. The act of consecration was performed by the superior of the Pomeranian-Wielkopolska Diocese, the Rev. bishop Michał Warczyński. In June 2000, the celebrations of the fifth anniversary of the Evangelical Seniors' Home took place. In 2002, it was inhabited by seven residents, among them Edward Szwarc, a former farmer from Stanisławów. He was often seen at the Evangelical cemetery in Zagórów, where he tended the graves with devotion. Nine years later, on 30 November 2009, with the death of the last resident, the EDS ended its activity[^23]. After 2000, a few families of German origin lived in Zagórów itself, and several dozen in the territory of the whole gmina – in their decisive majority Protestants. With each year, this number alas grows smaller: the older ones pass away, the young set out into the world. For those who remain, services are held on the first and third Sunday of each month at 12.00.

On 9 June 2001, during the celebrations of the jubilee of the ordination of the Rev. Councillor Andrzej Mendrok in Konin, the municipal authorities of Zagórów were represented by the mayor, Wiesław Radniecki, and the deputy mayor, Włodzimierz Chmielewski. During the ceremonies, there performed the choir Cantabile under the direction of Aleksandra Koronkowska, MA. In the following years, the two choirs: the Zagórów Cantabile with its conductor, Mrs Aleksandra Koronkowska, and 


[^23]: More on the activity of the EDS and the life of its residents in the later part of this publication.

<!-- s. 52 [PDF 52] -->
 the choir of the Evangelical parish in Detmold under the direction of Mr Johann Pöld, established not only fruitful cooperation but also personal friendships, organising joint concerts in Zagórów, Konin and Detmold. In 2001, the choir Cantabile went off on a several-day tour together with their German friends and had the opportunity to give several unforgettable concerts. The crowning of this musical adventure was the recording of a joint CD on 28 April 2001. The performances of the choir Cantabile accompany most of the August services, which are at the same time reunions of former and present parishioners and of their descendants.


On 18 August 2002, the inauguration of the 120th anniversary of the consecration of the Evangelical-Augsburg church and of the sixtieth anniversary of confirmation took place. This beautiful ceremony will long remain in the memory of the parishioners. In the years 2003-2013, the tradition of the August services is being continued[^24]. Every year a numerous delegation comes to Zagórów from Detmold to pray together with the local Protestants and, after the service, to take part in a parish picnic, to talk, to reminisce and to plan further meetings. In August 2008, the church building was fitted with a new electronic clock and bells, whose sound carries to this day through the little streets of the town and over the meadows along the Warta. In the morning, the carillon plays the hymn *Kiedy ranne wstają zorze*; in the evening, one hears the melody of the hymn *Wszystkie nasze dzienne sprawy*.

On 24 August 2014, the traditional service took place with the participation of former Protestant inhabitants of Zagórów and its surroundings. Taking part in it were guests from the partner parish in Detmold, parishioners of the Reformed parish of Żychlin, and parishioners of the Konin parish. The sermon for the Polish-German community present at the service was preached by pastor Burkhard Krebber of Detmold.

The musical setting was provided this time by the male choir Quarta of Zagórów. Thanks to a project of the “Echo Pyzdry” Association, represented 


[^24]: The account of the life of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in Zagórów after the year 2003 was written by Katarzyna Połom.

<!-- s. 53 [PDF 53] -->
 by Wiesława and Przemysław Kowalski, the film *Ludzie z żelaznych domów* was being made, telling the story of the Hauländers who inhabited the lands of the Pyzdry Forest for 200 years. One of its protagonists is Mr Erich Kuhn – the untiring initiator and organiser of the meetings of the former Protestant inhabitants of these lands in Zagórów. On Saturday, 18 August 2018, on the three disused Evangelical-Augsburg cemeteries in the gmina of Zagórów, ecumenical prayers took place, led by the Rev. Waldemar Wunsz of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin and the Rev. Jan Walczak of the parish of St Stanislaus Bishop and Martyr in Trąbczyn, with the participation of invited guests and the inhabitants of the surrounding villages. On the cemeteries in Michalinów Trąbczyński, Łazińsk Pierwszy and Łazińsk Huta, stones bearing plaques informing about the character of the place were set in the course of the summer. The founder and initiator of the placing of the plaques was Erich Kuhn. On the initiative of the Frydhof Association, thorough clearing work had already been carried out in the year 2017 on the cemeteries in Michalinów and at the Huta, thanks to which the memory of the deceased was restored. Help came from the inhabitants of the villages, from the Society of Friends of the Zagórów Land, from the pupils of the Gimnazjum in Trąbczyn, and from Jan Kloc – a Zagórów councillor, who with his own hands performed most of the heaviest work on all three cemeteries. The cemetery at Łazińsk Pierwszy is at present in private hands. A patch of ground was cleared there for the setting up of a cross, a stone and a symbolic lapidarium. The meeting ended in the hall of the Volunteer Fire Brigade (OSP) of Trąbczyn, where the guests could admire a collection of miniatures of works made by Jan Kloc, and likewise an exhibition, also his own work, connected with the centenary of the recovery of independence. On 19 August 2018, the traditional service took place. The musical setting was provided by the choir Cantabile of Zagórów, which for many years had been cooperating with the parish in Konin. In the following year, on 18 August, in the Evangelical church in Zagórów, an ecumenical service took place, combined with the unveiling of a plaque commemorating the former Protestant inhabitants. Taking part in the service were: the Rev. dean Adam Czechorowski, parish priest of the church of St Peter and St Paul in Zagórów; pastor 
<!-- s. 54 [PDF 54] -->
 Tadeusz Jelinek of the Evangelical-Reformed Parish in Żychlin; the starosta (county head) of Słupca, Jacek Bartkowiak; the mayor of Zagórów, Roman Kulterman; the councillors of Zagórów with their chairman Ryszard Sikorski; and many other invited guests. In the full church, the hymns sung by the male choir Quarta of Zagórów rang out beautifully. The group of former Protestant inhabitants was represented by Erich Kuhn, born at Łazińsk, of Detmold in Germany.

On 15 August 2021, after the pandemic break, the parishioners returned to meetings with the participation of guests from Germany. During the service, the hymn *Amazing Grace* was played on the saxophone by Jakub Wunsz, while Alan Woźniak accompanied him on the piano. After the service, the parish picnic took place, as usual. The collected offerings were handed over to aid the persons affected by the torrential rains and floods in Germany. On Sunday, 21 August 2022, the next Polish-German service took place in Zagórów, as is traditional. Taking part in it were inhabitants of Germany whose roots reach back to the vicinity of Zagórów. Our guests also included parishioners from Żychlin with the Rev. Tadeusz Jelinek. During the service, there performed the ecumenical choir operating at the parish in Konin. The third Sunday of August, the 20th day of the month – such was the date of the next meeting of former and present parishioners and their friends, at the service and the parish picnic in 2023. Among others, we remembered the late Edith Kuhn, the wife of Erich Kuhn. During the service, Daniel Słowiński played and sang. The cooperation with him bore fruit in the organisation of an extraordinary concert, the proceeds of which were destined to help in bringing out a book devoted to the history of the parish. This one-of-a-kind musical event took place on 23 September. At Daniel Słowiński's side there performed: Joanna Słowińska, Zuzanna Wilczyńska, Agnieszka Witkiewicz and Wojciech Nowakowski. The moving concert, maintained in an ecumenical spirit, gathered many listeners, who favoured the coming into being of the publication commemorating the history of the Protestants of Zagórów. The proceeds of the collection and of the sale of the ceramic fund-raising bricks made by Kinga Kujawska were credited to the account destined for the publishing work. We believe that many beautiful moments still lie before us. The Evangelical-Augsburg Parish

 in its ministry gathers around itself the faithful and a numerous company of friends who, in the spirit of ecumenism and of the ideas of respect and cooperation, wish to take part in manifold activities and to support them. In the community, we are ready for new challenges[^25].

### Hauländer settlement and Prussian colonization in the vicinity of Zagórów in the years 1746-1806


Przemysław Kowalski

Until the beginning of the 18th century the Puszcza Pyzdrska was a sparsely populated area, administratively divided into the counties of Kalisz, Konin and Pyzdry in the Kalisz voivodeship. The centre of the forest, with a predominance of poor soils in the dry places, was covered with extensive pine woods, while in the waterlogged places it was dominated by alder carrs with an admixture of oaks. This area was almost uninhabited. The few settlements were connected with forestry or smelting, e.g. Smoleniec and Huta. The situation was different on the outskirts of the Puszcza Pyzdrska, where villages had been founded since the early Middle Ages, among them Ruda Komorska, Szymanowice, Niniew, Piła, Brudzew, Lipe, Jarantów with its medieval fortified settlement, Kościelec, Kosmów, Przespolew Kościelny, Malanów, Grzymiszew, Dzierzbin, Dąbroszyn, Grabienice, Rzgów, Kopojno, Dolany, Ląd with its medieval fortified settlement and Cistercian abbey, Policko, Rataje and Dłusk, and the towns of Pyzdry, Chocz, Stawiszyn, Tuliszków, Rychwał, Zagórów, Lądek and Ciążeń, which have survived to this day (Lądek and Ciążeń lost their town rights at a later period). Settlement on the outskirts was favoured by better soils, the waterways of the Warta and Prosna, and the old trade route running from Kalisz to Konin. At that time, in the centre of the forest only the route connecting Stawiszyn with Ląd had been brought under cultivation, of which among other things the villages of Trąbczyn, Szetlewek and Królików with a church of Romanesque origin and a medieval fortified settlement are a reminder.

<!-- s. 58 [PDF 58] -->

#### The Hauländers' promised land

In the 18th century the landowners possessing forests and wastelands in the Puszcza Pyzdrska – such as the nobility, monastic orders, the state treasury, the Bishop of Gniezno and the towns of Pyzdry and Zagórów – gradually came to the conclusion that for these lands to begin to yield income, they had to be put to agricultural use in a form other than that practised hitherto. Farming based on corvée labour did not meet the needs of transforming difficult, unprofitable land (poor, dry, marshy) into fertile and profitable land. Newer technologies and hands to do the work were lacking. The best way of developing these areas was to establish Hauländer settlement on them. This settlement was one of the important forms of rural rent-paying settlement in early modern Europe.

Poland, within both its historical and its present-day borders, was one of the several areas where this settlement was developed broadly and intensively in the years 1547-1864, at first with the participation of the Dutch, and then of their descendants, though mainly by Germans and Poles. The name “Hauländer settlement” derives from Old Polish terms: its pioneers, the Dutch, were called “Olędrzy” (“Olendrzy”), and above all from the Dutch law they brought with them, known as “olęderski” (Hauländer), which in the 17th–18th centuries was commonly used by the settlers of other nationalities who by then already predominated numerically.

In accordance with the rules of Hauländer law (the law of settlers of Dutch/Flemish origin), as applied in the feudal charters concluded by the founders of settlements with entire communes, the Hauländers were people who were personally free and held their land on the terms of emphyteusis[^26], at first in the sense of a multi-year, long-term, renewable lease, and later for the most part already in perpetuity, after payment (as a lump sum or in instalments) of the so-called okupne (*grundgeld*), which gave them the possibility of selling the entire farm together with its buildings, 


Fragment of a map from the publication Breyer Albert, *Die deutschen ländlichen Siedlungen im mittelpolnischen Warthebruches*



[^26]: Emphyteusis – in Roman law, a long-term, hereditary and alienable lease of land.

<!-- s. 59 [PDF 59] -->



<!-- s. 60 [PDF 60] -->
 upon payment of a vadium against the sale sum. After the expiry of the period free of dues (usually lasting from six to seven years), intended for developing the holding, the settlers owed the lord only rents (in money and in kind) and labour services to a very limited extent. They possessed a limited freedom of land tenure, answered solidarily, as an entire commune, for the punctual fulfilment of their obligations towards the lord, and had an elected village self-government (with a village headman (sołtys) confirmed by the manor), which among other things supervised the fulfilment of these obligations, and they retained a limited judicial freedom, being subject to their own village court and only partially (in criminal matters and in appeal proceedings) to dominial jurisdiction, patrimonial in the royal and church domains. They also paid dues to the Catholic Church (until 1768 this included Protestants) and taxes to the state, as a rule possessed freedom of trade, and enjoyed freedom of religion (until 1768 partly restricted where public worship was concerned) and the right to establish their own schools, most often serving at the same time as houses of prayer. These rules of Dutch law were applied by the Dutch mainly on flood-prone and waterlogged areas, and subsequently by their descendants and continuators, mainly Germans and Poles, not only on such terrain.


The essence and distinctiveness of Hauländer colonization as against other forms of rent-paying settlement – sołtys (village-headman) settlement and emphyteutic settlement – were therefore decided not by the ethnic affiliation of the settlers, but by the following factors: 1) the collective solidarity of the commune for the dues of each individual Hauländer towards the lord, and the resulting equal standing of the settlers – householders within the commune, together with the associated organization of self-government and mutual neighbourly assistance, and the receiving of farms (in long-term or hereditary possession) on the basis of collective, commune-wide charters; 2) the economic function, which consisted above all in occupying areas often difficult to develop, through drainage and clearing or clearing alone, requiring in the case of flood-prone and waterlogged land a knowledge of hydrotechnics and collective 
<!-- s. 61 [PDF 61] -->
 cooperation; further, in an agricultural culture consisting in the cultivation of the land in a single block (making individual three-field rotation possible), with animal husbandry, especially dairy cattle, being of importance equal to the growing of cereals and other crops.

In the 16th–18th centuries, and to a lesser extent later, until about 1864, this settlement developed in various regions of Poland: in Żuławy, in the lower Vistula valley, in Mazovia, and from 1597 in Greater Poland, at first in its western part (the former voivodeship), and later (especially from the third, fourth decade of the 18th century) in the eastern part of this region (the Kalisz voivodeship and, from 1768, the Gniezno voivodeship created out of part of the latter).

In 1746 Hauländer settlement also began in the territory of the Puszcza Pyzdrska. The settlers arriving here were mostly free peasants of the Lutheran faith of German nationality, coming mainly from rural settlements in Silesia, Pomerania and Brandenburg, lying in what was then Prussia, but also from western Greater Poland, and to a lesser extent peasants of the Catholic faith, also of Polish nationality, coming most probably for the most part from Greater Poland. They usually received on lease one włóka (łan) of land to develop by drainage and/or clearing. The size of the włóka, amounting to 30 morgi, depended on the length of the rod, that is, the measure with which it was measured, and ranged from 17.95 ha to 24.20 ha.

Around Zagórów Hauländer settlement was carried on only in the noble, private estates. Its pioneers in the present-day Zagórów commune, and at the same time in the Puszcza Pyzdrska, were the owners of the Trąbczyn estate, the spouses Paweł Prusiński and Katarzyna née Rozdrażewska. In 1746 they located the first two villages under Hauländer law. These were the Trąbczyńskie Luźne Stare Olędry (today Łazińsk Pierwszy and Łazińsk Drugi) and the Trąbczyńskie Luśnie Olędry (today part of Łazińsk Pierwszy). The settlers were peasants of the Lutheran faith and German nationality. Both villages arose in the belt of forests and bogs stretching from Stawisko to Czarny Bród. The next village located by 
<!-- s. 62 [PDF 62] -->
 the spouses Prusiński was Trąbczyńskie Luźne Nowe Olędry, later called Łukomskie Olędry (today part of Łazińsk Pierwszy). The village was founded in the years 1754-1767, and was settled by settlers of the Lutheran and Catholic faith, that is, of German and Polish origin. Between 1746 and 1789 further villages were founded in the noble estate of Łukom: Bukowa, or the Łomowskie Olędry (today Bukowe), Imielno Olędry (today Imielno), Zagórowskie Olędry (today Huta Łukomska), Myszakowskie Olędry (today Myszakówek). The population of these villages was mixed in faith and ethnicity, of Polish and German origin. In a similar period, in the noble estate of Trąbczyn, the village of Łazy Olędry (today Łazy) was founded. The foundress of the village was the widow Katarzyna née Rozdrażewska Prusińska. The settlers were Polish peasants freed from corvée labour and transferred to Hauländer law.

The last village located under Hauländer law in the years 1794-1800 was Koszelewska Łąka, settled by German, Evangelical settlers from Western Pomerania. Later, until the enfranchisement of the peasants in 1864, no other Hauländer villages were founded, but the process of enlarging the already existing villages continued.

In the years 1789-1791, in the eight Hauländer settlements listed above: Bukowe/the Łomowskie Olędry, Imielno, Łazy, Myszakówek, Stara Huta Zagórowska and the Trąbczyńskie Stare and Nowe Olędry, and probably the Luśnie, counted together with them, there lived in total 503 people, of whom 256 were Catholics and 247 Evangelicals. A population that was exclusively Evangelical and German lived in Myszakówek, and an exclusively Catholic and probably exclusively Polish one in Bukowe, Łazy and Stara Huta Zagórowska. In the remaining four settlements lived inhabitants mixed in faith and ethnicity, with Protestants constituting a decided majority of them in Imielno and in the Trąbczyńskie Stare and Nowe Olędry and the Luśnie Olędry.

#### Under Prussian partition rule

Several further new villages were founded in the territory of the present-day Zagórów commune as part of the so-called Prussian colonization, developed especially in Greater Poland after its incorporation into Prussia following the Second Partition of Poland and its inclusion in the new province named South Prussia[^27].

This colonization was initiated in 1796, after the takeover, by decision of the Prussian king Fryderyk Wilhelm II, of the church estates and the expropriation of the leaseholders of the royal estates known as starosties. The confiscation covered the church estates, mainly the monastic ones, as well as the estates of the starosties whose leaseholders had taken part in the Kościuszko Uprising of 1794. In return for the lost landed estates, the Prussian state undertook to pay the monasteries annually 50 per cent of the lost income from the confiscated landed estates, and the leaseholders 25 per cent of their lost income. The aim of the colonization was the Germanization of the Polish lands and the raising of the income of the Prussian state. In November 1806, with the outbreak of the Franco-Prussian war, the colonization campaign in South Prussia came to an end.

Around Zagórów the estates of the Cistercian monastery in Ląd were subject to confiscation; these comprised among others the villages of Świątniki, Wardężyn, Sługocin, Sługocinek, Sławsk, Drzewce, Oleśnica, Wrąbczyn, Skokum, Kopojno, Koszuty, Wola Opacka, Ratyń, Myślibórz, Dolany, Jaroszyn, Rzgów, Świątniki, Szetlew, Grabienice, Kowalewo, Ląd Kolonia, Królików, and also the towns of Lądek and Zagórów. Most of these localities came into being thanks to a settlement campaign lasting hundreds of years, conducted by the Cistercians of Ląd. In addition, the monastery owned extensive forests and wastelands. In 1796 the new owner of these estates became the commander of the Prussian army, Prince Fryderyk Ludwik Hohenlohe Ingelfingen. The entire area of the domains of Prince Hohenlohe amounted to 250 łans. Of this area 


[^27]: South Prussia existed in the years 1793-1806, that is, from the Second Partition of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth until the creation of the Duchy of Warsaw in the period of the Napoleonic Wars.

<!-- s. 65 [PDF 65] -->
 measuring 100 łans was designated for the settling of colonists. The prince energetically set about bringing colonists from Germany (often from areas lying far from Poland) in order to settle the forests and wastelands taken from the Cistercians. In 1805, 18 families were brought in, who in turn assigned smaller forest areas to further settlers, also hired labourers. The initiative to recruit further settlers and to settle them near the larger colonies was left to colonists possessing considerable areas of land. For the most part these were forest lands and meadows situated on the Warta. In total, during the period of South Prussia, 8 new settlements were founded within the boundaries of the present-day Zagórów commune: Kolonia Klein Ingelfingen (today Anielewo), Emilienheim (today Michalinów Oleśnicki), Kolonia Sofiethal (today Olchowo), Kolonia Ludwigslust (today Mały Las), Kolonia Frydrychsfeld (today Tarszewo), Kolonia Adelshof (today Chruściki), Adolfsberg (today Zalesie) and Augustinenhof (today Augustynów).

In addition, the villages of Schwentz (Święcia) and the colony of Wrąbczyn were founded. From the contract of perpetual lease of land concluded with the settlers it followed that they were personally free and were obliged only to very limited duties towards the manor and to certain payments in kind. After the expiry of four years free of obligations, they were also obliged to pay rent in two instalments, at Christmas and on St John the Baptist's day (24 May) – in the amount of 30 thalers a year from each cleared and cultivated Magdeburg łan. For the schoolteacher, the village headman (sołtys) and for the cemetery, 21 morgi were assigned. The colonists came mostly from the Margraviate of Brandenburg and from Pomerania. Not all the settlers remained in the settlements mentioned. In 1806 two families from Mały Las asked to be allowed to leave it and move to East Prussia, and in 1818 forty families from the village of Świecia made the same request.

<!-- s. 66 [PDF 66] -->

#### Hauländers vs Prussian colonists

A characteristic feature of the Prussian colonization, in contrast to Hauländer settlement, was the creation in the colonies of farms of very varying size, on the assumption that the smallholders (who were the most numerous) would support themselves mainly from wage labour on the large farms. The colonists were peasants who had come from Germany. Most of them came from Württemberg; smaller groups arrived from other provinces. All the colonists were granted tax relief and exemption from compulsory military service. They were people who were personally free, and they received the land in perpetual lease. Something new was the material assistance granted by the Prussian state to the colonists in non-cash form (farm implements, livestock, building materials) or in cash form: a grant for setting up the farm, reimbursement of travel costs, and payment for clearing. The amount of the assistance depended on the financial situation of the colonists, who were for the most part poor people, and had come to Poland encouraged by the possibility of improving their condition. In South Prussia an effort was made to create fairly large farms, from 20 morgi to 6 łans.

The colonization of the years 1793-1806 was not, however, in the end a successful undertaking. The assignment to the newcomers of wastelands and land requiring clearing or drainage deterred many of them, who had expected to receive in this province land already prepared for cultivation and of a larger area. For this reason a large part of the colonists either returned to the Reich, or departed for Russia, where the conditions of settlement were more favourable. Comparing Hauländer settlement and Prussian colonization, it is nevertheless clearly visible that Hauländer settlement was conducted more rationally. The Hauländers came from nearby areas and knew the local conditions better than the colonists, who often came from distant regions of Germany. They were generally in a better financial situation. At the moment of settling they had to possess the funds to set up a farm and to pay the entry fee (wkupne). They were settled in groups, and for the management of the affairs

<!-- s. 69 [PDF 69] -->
 of the village they instituted a self-government arising from below. They did not require enormous state assistance in order to settle. Despite the lack of assistance they managed better on their new farms and abandoned them less often.


After the war lost by Prussia with France in 1806, Greater Poland found itself in the years 1807-1814 within the borders of the Duchy of Warsaw, and after the Congress of Vienna, in the years 1815-1832, the eastern part of Greater Poland found itself in the Kingdom of Poland (Congress Poland), while the western part was in Prussia. Zagórów and the Puszcza Pyzdrska found themselves in the Kingdom of Poland (Congress Poland). The Polish authorities of both the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland (Congress Poland), owing to the difficult situation of the public finances, were unable to give financial support to settlement in the Polish lands. In order to compensate for the obligations the Prussian government had entered into towards the colonists, the promised material or financial assistance had to be converted into an extension of the period free of lease rent and taxes.

For the new applicants wishing to settle in the Kingdom of Poland (Congress Poland), suitable places were designated in the state estates, with a period free of lease rent and taxes. In the Zagórów commune, colonization on the new terms was not carried out.
