<!-- s. 71 [PDF 71] -->
### The Evangelical-Augsburg Church in the interwar period (1918-1939)


Pastor Waldemar Wunsz

The influence of sixteenth-century Reformation thought on the territories of the then Republic of Poland is beyond doubt. This is clearly visible at the courts of the Wielkopolska and Lithuanian nobility and among the townsfolk, who embraced the new confession en masse. Clergy educated at the University of Wittenberg preached the reformed teaching, contributing to the growth of evangelical influence. The Counter-Reformation halted it, and later contributed to a marked shrinking of the Reformation's reach. The renewed influx of people of the evangelical confessions is connected with the settlement and colonization processes on Polish lands. These were connected with the deliberate bringing in of newcomers to settle the land, mainly from German-speaking territories, and later with the planned colonization campaigns of the partitioning powers. The principal areas of settlement for the evangelical population groups were the regions lying north and south of the Warta river, in the vicinity of Kalisz and Konin, the villages in the Vistula valley near Warsaw, and the heavily industrializing Łódź. Parish and diocesan structures of the evangelical Churches took shape, which was connected with the earlier attainment of religious tolerance by the dissidents. The Evangelical-Augsburg Church united in its parishes a small group of Polish-speaking evangelicals, descended from the sixteenth-century Reformation, decimated by the Counter-Reformation and the partitions, and a more numerous group descended from economic emigrants who had arrived since the close of the eighteenth century.

In the next period, in the area of the Russian partition, two tendencies can be observed among the descendants of the settlers and colonists. In the villages, owing to their homogeneous structures, the cultivation of tradition, 
<!-- s. 72 [PDF 72] -->
 confession and language of origin continued, oriented toward the immediate surroundings and a fencing-off from the outside world – and it was rather an expression of traditionalist consciousness than of national feeling. In the towns – multicultural clusters – a progressive Polonization was noticeable, up to decisions to introduce services in the Polish language, occurring from the end of the nineteenth century. Additionally, despite the efforts of the partitioning power and the designation of a university for Lutheran pastors in Dorpat, thus far removed from Polish influences, assimilation among the future clergy made headway, especially through the Konwent Polonia operating at the university.

Before the outbreak of the First World War, in the area of the Russian partition there was in essence no question of a sharpening of relations between the German- and Polish-speaking population – unlike in the Prussian partition. Prussian policy toward the eastern provinces and its anti-Polish character, however, intensified the processes of self-defense of Polish society, part of which were debates on the possible role of the German-speaking colonists as German outposts and instruments of Germanization. Whereas national origin or a sense of national identity did not carry much weight until the outbreak of the war in 1914, and the center of political conflicts focused on the Russification measures of the partitioning power, already in the period of the struggles over the future of the lands of the individual partitions, over their political and territorial shape and ethnic composition – a polarization of positions occurred; the problems of national identity and of national demarcation between the German- and Polish-speaking population revived. This theme also revived within the evangelical Churches. The occupation of the lands of the Russian partition by the Germans initiated a process of political transformations culminating in the rebuilding of the Polish state and the regaining of independence after 123 years. The Russian administration carried out – especially after the battle of Tarnów and Gorlice – the forced resettlement of tens of thousands of politically unreliable German-speaking inhabitants from the north-eastern territories of Congress Poland. The presence of the German troops, however, met with a reserved attitude among the German-speaking evangelical inhabitants of the former Russian partition.

<!-- s. 73 [PDF 73] -->



Confirmation certificate of Fiorentine Tichmann.


Photo from the parish archive.


Confirmation list from 1919. Found in the cantor's house in Łazińsk. Transl. Jacek Kin




Confirmation. In the middle sits Pastor Aleksander Gross.


Photo from the parish archive.

<!-- s. 74 [PDF 74] -->

War damage and the wrongs inflicted at the changing of armies shaped everyday life, and punitive actions – such as the artillery shelling of Kalisz by German units at the beginning of the war – contributed to a negative image of the German troops. In the nationally and denominationally homogeneous rural areas there were no outbursts of nationalist zeal. The advancing German army, the officials of the administration and the field clergy encountered a German-speaking population which, thanks to its way of life rooted in family and religiosity, seemed to match the image idealized by German propaganda, but it was a population "undefined" in its Germanness, beyond a sense of national identity shaped in the German Empire. The idea was therefore invoked of a special calling and of a God-given mission of the German nation, in order to set in motion processes of transforming consciousness and bring about a national awakening. Soon, despite the Reich's policy marked by many contradictions, the declared Polish question stood to a certain extent in the field of tensions concerning the German population. As a result of the changes at the front, the German population faced the problem of shaping relations in the newly created Polish State, at the expense of the German Empire. Most of the clergy of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, by reason of their social position, were open to the Polish efforts aimed at state independence. The clergy nevertheless found themselves in a situation of great pressures: as a result of the changes among the German population – whose driving force was the group of so-called Łódź activists linked with the Hakata – arguments were put forward about the German-speaking character of the majority of the Church's members, and consequently about the necessity of profiling the Evangelical-Augsburg Church unambiguously in the German-national spirit. Moreover, the German administration filled vacant church posts with clergymen-administrators of German orientation, in order to give the Evangelical-Augsburg Church a structure corresponding to the general evangelical and German interests. The task of the German administration was to see to it that the elements of German-evangelical culture existing 
<!-- s. 75 [PDF 75] -->
 in the parishes were strengthened. For this purpose, too, the necessary pastors were specially brought in. Until 1918, 45 pastors from Germany conducted pastoral work in the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. This caused shifts in the internal balance of forces of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in favor of the pro-German orientation. Nor did the draft of the new church order leave any illusions as to the direction in which the church reform planned by the pro-German camp was heading, together with the conception of separating the Polish and German parts of the Church and attaching the German part to one of the Lutheran Churches in the Reich. With the Wielkopolska Uprising, the creation of the People's Council in Poznań and the transfer of power in Warsaw to Józef Piłsudski, the German occupation came to an end. Together with the German troops, clergy of Reich origin and members of the church boards also left the country in part. Yet, as Governor Bruno Geissler wrote after returning to Germany: the activity of the German clergy from the Reich in occupied Congress Poland did not remain without national-political consequences. The emergence of a new, independent Polish state in 1918 required of the evangelical Churches – previously located on the territories of the three partitions – a regulation of their relationship to the state and to the emerging legal order, and an adaptation of church life to the new territorial and social conditions. This process was hindered by the varied confessional, linguistic and national traditions of the individual Churches – and it must be remembered that in the interwar period seven evangelical Churches functioned on the territories of the Second Polish Republic. The situation was further complicated by the fact that some of the evangelical Churches had their supreme authorities in Berlin or in Vienna. The political forces in the German Reich constantly questioned the existence of the new Poland – remaining in the hitherto legal union therefore meant for the Churches a questioning of Polish sovereignty. At the same time, the national tensions within the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, which escalated during the synod in Łódź in October 1917, long preserved the danger 
<!-- s. 76 [PDF 76] -->
 of a division of the Church into a German part, linked with one of the Lutheran Churches in the German Reich, and a Polish part with its authorities in Warsaw. General Superintendent Juliusz Bursche – the head of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, whose supreme authority was in Warsaw – took the side of the newly created Polish state. This was connected with the striving to liberate evangelicalism from the isolation conditioned by the Counter-Reformation and the German-speaking immigration, through an opening of the Church toward Polish culture and society. This, however, meant violating the existing stereotypes and the immutable boundaries of the relations between nation and confession. From the end of the nineteenth century, both in Poland divided by the partitions and in Bismarckian-Wilhelmine Germany, the nation was traditionally identified with religion, which produced a way of thinking about Polishness – Catholicism, and Germanness – evangelicalism. Other options aroused suspicions as to their national authenticity and required proofs of loyalty. Bishop Bursche, presenting an evangelical model of the Polish cause, balanced between dubious alliances with the Polish national-nationalist forces, thereby giving sustenance to the Germans, who considered him a traitor to German cultural dominance, from which the fruits of centuries of activity in the east had been taken away. On the other hand, Bishop Bursche's vision ran up against the image, common to the political camps, of an idealized reborn Poland – united linguistically, culturally and denominationally, and therefore able to exist only as a Polish national Catholic state. This meant that the national and religious minorities could at most count on limited rights, until they were assimilated, while the Roman Catholic confession acquired the rank of a state religion. Bishop Bursche advocated a model of relations on the nation–confession line that would accept diversity – that is, a true coexistence of confessions and nationalities. The tragedy of Bishop Bursche lay in the fact that, in his strivings to secure a future for evangelicalism within Polish society, he was forced to cooperate with political forces that were prepared to grant the minorities at most limited rights.

<!-- s. 77 [PDF 77] -->

In the intra-church discussion, Bishop Bursche repeatedly returned to the principle adopted at the general synod of pastors as early as 1906: *suum cuique*. Adopted unanimously, it concerned the various nationalities and language groups in the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. It read: The synod (of pastors) takes its stand on *suum cuique*: the Jew for the Jew, the Greek for the Greek. The pastor should stand above the nationalities; he is not a politician, but a pastor. His task is neither Germanization nor Polonization, but the faithful proclamation of the Gospel, whereby, as far as possible, he must prove just toward every nationality. However, during the occupation new political constellations arose, changing the coexistence of the Polish- and German-speaking population and making the application of this principle difficult. Clergy and laypeople who came under German-national influence placed the question of maintaining Germanness above the goals of the Church. On the other hand, the conception of a missionary Lutheranism, open to the Polish surroundings, promoted by Bishop Bursche, with the end of the war became a programmatic political option in favor of the new state – arousing distrust from the Polish as well as the German nationalist forces.

The maintenance of the Church's unity with strict observance of the principle of supranationality collided with the process of national awakening of the German-speaking members of the Church, which intensified in proportion to the growing tensions in German-Polish relations and the sharpening internal political situation in Poland. Bishop Juliusz Bursche said: "Bilingual is our Church, the Evangelical-Augsburg, in our country. There are German evangelicals, and there is also a considerable number of evangelicals of Polish speech. For above all, quite a few authentically Polish families sacredly preserve the faith handed down to them from their fathers and grandfathers from the times of the Reformation, but also among us, who bear German surnames, there are many who have grown into the local society." And how could it be otherwise? Having dwelt here for several generations, we have ceased to be alien newcomers; we have grown into the country, which has become our homeland, for whose good we live and work. For neither origin nor surname decides 
<!-- s. 78 [PDF 78] -->
 belonging to a nationality, but language and feelings. Yet Bishop Bursche did not hesitate to engage the Church in nationality actions in Masuria and Upper Silesia during the plebiscites, which for the German opposition constituted a point of criticism against the General Superintendent. Unfortunately, the provisions of the Treaty of Versailles meant a defeat for Bishop Bursche. An action was begun of creating Polish Evangelical Societies in Wielkopolska and Pomerania, but the effects of these actions were not impressive. The complexity of the nationality relations was influenced by the process of assimilation of the evangelicals into the Polish environment of the former Russian partition, and, on the other hand, by the growth of German national consciousness, strengthened by the influence of the events of the First World War, and later by the influence of contacts with the German evangelicals of the former Prussian partition, including those of the Evangelical Church of the Union, whose parishes bordered Congress Poland on the west. Aware of the threats to the position of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in Poland given its ambiguous national character, Bishop Bursche opposed the politicization of the Church and its being drawn into nationality games. He invoked the consensus worked out before the war as to the essence of church ministry and called upon the clergy and laypeople: "Whether one of my pastors is pro-Polish or pro-German in orientation is all the same to me. I demand, however, one thing: that he not allow national questions to influence his activity in office in a decisive way. In one word, his task is not to proclaim either Polishness or Germanness, but the Gospel, to Poles as well as to Germans, and also to Poles who were once Germans and bear German surnames." Were he to reproach the latter for their Polishness or consider it his task to restore them to Germanness – as is now demanded of us from all sides – he would only arouse resentment and bitterness, and moreover would achieve nothing.

Engaging in Polish nationality matters, he at the same time waged a battle with the state authorities for consent to conduct religious instruction for the children of the evangelical confession in the language of instruction 
<!-- s. 79 [PDF 79] -->



Easter, 23.04. 1935. Parishioners at a festive gathering at the cemetery in Olchów. In the top row, first from the right, stands the teacher Homt.


Photo from the collections of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin.



Games and pastimes.


Photo from the parish archive.



Trombone Choir of the Zagórów Parish.


Photo from the parish collections.

<!-- s. 80 [PDF 80] -->
 German – where this was necessary. He emphasized that a wrong was done to the children if they were forced to learn religion in Polish – comparable to the situation of the Polish children in the former Prussian partition. From 1918, work began on a new church order for the Church, connected with a draft act on the relationship of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church to the Polish state. The church order envisaged a synodal-consistorial structure of the Church, whereas the draft act on the State–Church relationship was to give the Evangelical-Augsburg Church the status of a free and autonomous national church, enjoying the care and assistance of the state authorities in return for the supervision granted to the state in accordance with the provisions of the law. The decree of 25 November 1936 did not meet the expectations of the Polish or the German Lutherans – above all, the supervisory powers of the state were expanded, not allowing the Church in practice to make independent decisions in personnel matters. In 1937, in the church elections, a sharp church-political struggle took place, in which the main opposition to Bishop Bursche was the Working Community of German Pastors, founded in 1935, with the support of the foreign affairs office of the Evangelical Church of Germany. The leading role in the structures of the opposition was played by Pastor Eduard Kneifel, a native of Władysławów near Konin. In the four dioceses with a German majority: Kalisz, Płock, Łódź and Volhynia, the elections of the senioral authorities did not come to pass. Finally, Bishop Bursche decided to appoint commissarial seniors in them.


In the Kalisz diocese, the most serious candidate for the office of senior of the diocese was Pastor Adolf Ulbrich of Grodziec, but Pastor Edward Wende was appointed commissarial senior. For the Church, an important matter was the training of cadres and church workers. Until then, in the various partitions, the clergy had been educated at various institutions: in Vienna, at the German universities, and, from the Russian partition – in Dorpat. It was understood how important it was to establish a faculty of evangelical theology at one of the Polish universities. The Church sent several clergy 
<!-- s. 81 [PDF 81] -->
 to Basel so that they might obtain the necessary academic degrees there, for, for lack of personnel, the faculty could not be erected in 1921.

On 19 April 1922, the Ministry of Religious Denominations and Public Education officially established the Faculty of Evangelical Theology at the University of Warsaw. The highest number of students was recorded in the academic year 1929/1930 – there were as many as 102 students, among whom women also studied. Bishop Bursche faced criticism concerning the established faculty both from the German side and from the priest-professors of the Faculty of Catholic Theology of the University of Warsaw. The Evangelical Military Chaplaincy had already functioned before the emergence of independent Poland. The first chaplain nominated by the Polish authorities was the Rev. Grycz from Cieszyn Silesia. On 4 November 1918, the Rev. Ryszard Paszko – before the war the parish priest in Koło – addressed the Ministry of Military Affairs with a memorandum on the appointment of chaplains in the Polish Army. First the Section of Foreign Denominations was created, later the Department of Non-Catholic Denominations, and finally, in 1927, the Bureau of Non-Catholic Denominations of the Ministry of Military Affairs, covering five denominations. On 25 September 1919, the Rev. Ryszard Paszko became chief chaplain of the Evangelical Military Chaplaincy with the rank of lieutenant colonel. The structures of the chaplaincy were systematically expanded, though there were difficulties on the part of the Church, which did not want to release clergy from parish service for the military chaplaincy, and on the part of the state, which sought savings in filling the chaplaincy posts. Another difficulty was the distrust and fears of the military authorities toward the soldiers coming from German families – these were most often assigned to the units stationed in the eastern districts, most often in the Border Protection Corps. A process of conversion to evangelicalism was also noticeable among the soldiers and officers of the Polish Army, perhaps inspired by the earlier conversion to Lutheranism of Marshal Józef Piłsudski.

The Kalisz diocese of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church numbered in the interwar period about 60 thousand faithful, of whom the decided 
<!-- s. 82 [PDF 82] -->
 majority declared themselves as Germans. The existing stereotype – that the evangelical inhabitants of the towns became Polonized, while the inhabitants of the villages remained invariably attached to the linguistic traditions of the earlier generations – is not necessarily confirmed. Services in Polish were held from the end of the nineteenth century in the parishes in Kalisz, in Koło – where until 1915 the parish priest was Pastor Ryszard Paszko, though in the parish he administered in Babiak and in the rural cantorates there were no such services. Once a month and on feast days, services in Polish were held in Konin, conducted by Pastor Karl Henkl, who was at the same time a teacher of German at the local lyceum – reportedly called by his pupils by the catchy name of *der*, *die*, *das*. Polish services took place in Turek, where the parish priest was Pastor Leon Sachs, related by marriage to the family of Bishop Juliusz Bursche. In the parish archive there are preserved letters from the consistory of the Church, calling for the holding of services or for the ringing of bells on patriotic and commemorative occasions: e.g. the funeral of Marshal Józef Piłsudski, the 3 May holiday, or the anniversaries of independence. Services for Poles also took place in the parishes in Koźminek, Prażuchy and Sobiesęki, and branch services in Przystajń, in Dąbie, Zduńska Wola and Sieradz, as well as in the parishes of Chodeń and Przedecz, lying already in Kujawy. A German character at that time marked the well-developed denominational elementary schooling, with nearly 200 educational establishments, often combined with the parish rural cantorates. The development of Polish schooling influenced, slowly but distinctly, the bilingualism among the evangelical youth.

During the German occupation in the First World War, part of the clergy joined the current of social causes, for which repressions befell them. Pastor R. Paszko of Koło – a member of the POW – was arrested and interned in the prison in Celle near Hanover. Pastor K. Henkel of Konin died during the entry of the German troops into Konin in 1915. Pastor A. Paschke of Chodeń was imprisoned, as was Pastor Friedenberg of Prażuchy; unpleasantness befell Pastor G. Manituis 
<!-- s. 83 [PDF 83] -->
 of Zduńska Wola. Pastor E. Wende in Kalisz for a time stood at the head of the City Council, and Pastor A. Rutkowski in Dąbie engaged strongly in the work of the City Council and in social activity. Pastor L. Sachs in Turek stood at the head of the Citizens' Committee and engaged in the rebuilding of Polish schooling. In Koło, Konin and Zagórów, by contrast, clergy from the Reich were installed, who did not know the Polish language. This occasionally led to conflicts in the parishes, e.g. in the mid-1920s in Konin, where Pastor Adolf Löffler arbitrarily abolished the Polish services and church functions; the case of the funeral of the Konin mayor and bookseller Ernst Paweł Michl, whose wish was a burial in Polish, resounded far and wide. Pastor A. Löffler refused his consent; the burial was finally performed by a pastor of the Reformed Parish from Żychlin. The outcome of the conflict was the transfer of Pastor A. Löffler to the parish in Chodeń, and his post was taken by the conciliatory Rev. Robert Badke. Conflicts were also waged on the diocesan plane – after the adoption of the new church act, in 1936 elections of the senior of the Kalisz diocese took place. Pastor Adolf Ulbrich of Grodziec, elected by the votes of the German majority, was rejected by the state authorities, whereupon a commissarial senior was established in the person of the hitherto superintendent, Pastor Edward Wende. In the parish in Grodziec in 1937, out of more than eight thousand members of the parish, two persons declared Polish nationality. In many places German political associations were organized, e.g. the Jungdeutsche Partei in Polen or the Deutsche Volksverband in Polen, and the German press, shaping social moods in the German national-socialist spirit, was distributed.

Among the evangelicals declaring themselves as Poles one can find many important persons, meritorious for the work of self-government and for politics, officers of the Polish Army, people of education and culture. Mentioning the figures connected with eastern Wielkopolska, one cannot omit the best known: the aforementioned Ernst Paweł Michl – the Konin mayor, bookseller and printer, whose sons perished in the Warsaw Uprising; Major Adolf Laube, pharmacist, member of the Church Council, 
<!-- s. 84 [PDF 84] -->
 chairman of the Circle of Reserve Officers; Stefania Esse née Petschke, owner of the largest tenement house in the town, teacher, member of the Polish Military Organisation, who in 1921 welcomed Marshal Józef Piłsudski in Konin on behalf of the inhabitants; the sons of the Konin entrepreneur Daniel Sztark: Witold Sztark became a colonel of the Polish Army, and the son Heliodor Sztark – a diplomat; Oskar Bartel, historian, pedagogue, professor of the Christian Theological Academy; Ignacy Boerner, engaged in the activity of the Polish Socialist Party, who fought in the Legions and became an officer of the Polish Army, later the minister of post and telegraphs; Juliusz Ulrych, legionnaire and Piłsudski man, who in 1918, as head of the POW, took over power in Kalisz from the hands of the Germans and organized the 29th Infantry Regiment of the Kalisz Land, contributed to the founding of the Academy of Physical Education in Warsaw, in 1925 became minister of communications, and after the war in England was, among other things, president of the Józef Piłsudski Institute; his brother Aleksander Emil Ulrych, born in Turek, was a merchant in Kalisz, a member of the city council and a deputy to the Sejm; during the occupation, for refusing to sign the Volksliste, he was dispossessed and sent to forced labor in Wrocław, and for his activity in the Home Army he was arrested at the end of 1944 and placed in the concentration camp in Buchenwald, where he died of exhaustion shortly after the liberation; those meritorious in Kłodawa: Edward Woltersdorf, an activist in the POW, in the Polish Army and in the Fire Brigade; his brother Aleksander Woltersdorf, also active in the POW and in the Polish Army; and, from another branch of the family, Karol Woltersdorf, a member of the underground scouting movement in Kłodawa and later in Warsaw; the sisters Danuta Gerke, born in Słupca, and Monika, who, after the death of their parents – Konin merchants – were raised by relatives; both entered the Polish deaconess community Eben-Ezer in Dzięgielów, and after the Second World War Sister Danuta for nearly 40 years was the mother superior of the deaconate, struggling with the adversities of the PRL's measures. Another branch of the Gerke family moved in the interwar period to Warsaw, where its members during the Second World War were actively engaged in the underground: secret teaching and aid to the Jews

<!-- s. 85 [PDF 85] -->



③

<!-- s. 86 [PDF 86] -->
 (the intelligence worker and Home Army courier Irena Łukomska), in the defense of Warsaw in 1939 (Waldemar Gerke), in the fight in the Home Army (Rudolf Gerke), during the Warsaw Uprising (the fallen Witold Gerke) and in the Army of Anders (Lech Gerke). Worth mentioning are also: the engineer Henryk Kliem, son of the pastor of Chodeń, builder of the port in Gdynia; Edward Hauptmann, son of the cantor of Sompolno, professor at the gymnasium and one of the founders of the Polish evangelical congregation in Poznań, engaged in the Home Army in the region of Koluszki and Brzeziny; as well as many significant Konin families: the Reymonts, the Trenklers, the Rosins, the Peczkes.


The growing awareness of the approaching armed conflict intensified the tensions and the distrust directed at persons of the evangelical confession. An interesting read are the memoirs of Pastor Richard Kneifel, a native of Sompolno, pastor in Izbica Kujawska – it must be remembered that these are postwar memoirs. As one conscious of his German nationality and transparent in his views on maintaining the distinctness of the German minority in Poland, Pastor R. Kneifel cites information about the difficulties made for him by the education authority (Kuratorium Oświaty) at the time when he was a teacher of religion at the gymnasium in Sompolno, about the surveillance in the period before the outbreak of the war, or about his arrest in September 1939, together with the pastors of Koło, Dąbie and Babiak, among other persons politically unreliable. Their internment in Bereza Kartuska prevented the destruction of the railway station in Koło, and the subsequent course of the September campaign allowed them to return to their parishes.

Before Pastor Krempin of Koło returned to the parish, the parsonage had been demolished and plundered. In these memoirs mention is made of many German evangelicals who were killed or interned in the first days of the war – there occurred gravestones bearing the information that the deceased had been bestially murdered by *polnische Banditen*. The family of Pastor Krempin found shelter with the Beutler family of Koło, as did Pastor Leon Sachs of Turek with his wife, who had been removed from his post by the new German administration. Pastor R. Kneifel rather quickly realized that the administration of the Kraj Warty was aiming, in the long run, at the secularization 
<!-- s. 87 [PDF 87] -->
 of society and at the elimination of the Church altogether, as had been done with the Catholic Church, by closing the churches and deporting the clergy to the camps. The evangelical-Lutheran Church in the Kraj Warty was given the legal status of a private association, donations and church taxes were prohibited, and the church cemeteries were expropriated. Pastors of Polish orientation were removed from their church offices; of the evangelical population the signing of the Volksliste was expected. In place of the Polish farmers murdered or resettled to the General Government, German settlers were brought in from Volhynia, Bessarabia and Galicia. The evangelicals also found themselves among the victims of the Soviet assault on Poland on 17 September 1939. Among those murdered in Katyn and the other places were Major Adolf Laube, the Konin pharmacist, and General Ryszard Paszko, pastor of Koło, chaplain of the Evangelical Military Chaplaincy, and many others.

The entry into Poland of the German troops and administration was not always accompanied by the euphoria of the evangelicals, even those of German orientation. The signing of the Volksliste, likewise, was not connected solely with evident descent – sometimes it gave a sense of security and peace, and sometimes – as in the case of the Konin entrepreneur Jerzy Trenkler – the signing of the Volksliste, consulted with his Polish employees, secured work precisely for them, and not deportation or a camp. It happened that persons who in September 1939 wore the uniform of the Polish Army, after signing the Volksliste, a few months later went to the front in the uniform of the Wehrmacht. An interesting counterweight to Bruno Mielke – a soldier of the Wehrmacht, the author of the letters found in Łazińsk – is the figure of Eryk Stark, likewise an inhabitant of Łazińsk, who, deported to forced labor in the Reich, fled across the Alps and in Italy joined the Polish Army as a rifleman in a sapper unit, went through Monte Cassino, and remained in Great Britain after the war.

Often the phrase recurs in the accounts: before the war everyone lived in harmony. All the more thought-provoking is the influence of ideology on the needs of human consciousness. Many accounts have been preserved of the disgraceful 
<!-- s. 88 [PDF 88] -->
 behavior of the German evangelical neighbors. We shall also find such accounts as appreciate the behavior of the evangelical neighbors – because thanks to them the war was survived, or their Polish neighbors were saved. It is hard to agree with the thought that the citizens of Poland of German nationality, even those absorbed in the propaganda demonstrating the superiority of German culture over Slavic culture, or asserting the superiority of the Aryan race over others – expected or anticipated actions as dramatic and criminal as those undertaken by Hitler's Germany during the occupation. Eloquent is the behavior during the war of the Zagórów pastor Aleksander Gross, who, according to oral tradition, walked about Zagórów and, meeting the inhabitants, repeated: "Forgive me, forgive me for what the Germans are doing…".

#### Bibliography

Kiec Olgierd, *Kościoły ewangelickie w Wielkopolsce w XX i XXI wieku*, edited by Jerzy Kołacki and Izabela Skórzyńska, Poznań, pp. 123-132.

<!-- s. 89 [PDF 89] -->
### Cantors and kantorents, or education the Lutheran way: denominational schools in the parish of Zagórów


Katarzyna Połom

> "What full-of-character, Bible-versed, respected personalities our Augsburg church had among its cantors! They were men with calloused hands and thick skin, yet often fervent in faith, with a strong desire and readiness to serve, men who sought their equals."[^28]

As a graduate of pedagogical studies, I certainly encountered the name "cantor" back in my university days, so it does not sound foreign to me in the context of schooling and the history of education. Yet if you were to ask a random person what a cantor was – we could expect bewilderment. In the best case – the interlocutor would think of the figure of Tadeusz Kantor[^29]. In the worse one – if we are not joking, that is – he or she would think, after all it is common knowledge that a cantor is nothing other than a currency exchange booth. What schools? What churches? And yet this ecclesiastical lay office still officially functions today – in the Catholic, Evangelical, Orthodox churches as well as in Judaism – although its role has been reduced largely to matters connected with the musical setting of services. Where, then, did this church office, little known in our country, come from? What did it contribute to the old educational system? And – most importantly – what did the history of the cantors, the kantorates and the cantorate schools associated with them look like in the lands closest to us: in the very Lutheran parish in Zagórów. I will try to answer these questions below.



[^28]: Kneifel Eduard, *Die Bedeutung des Kantors für Gemeinde und Kirche*, translated from German by Magdalena Hoffman, 2021.

[^29]: A distinguished director, painter, stage designer and avant-garde actor.

<!-- s. 90 [PDF 90] -->

#### The cantor, that is, the chief singer in the temple

In Latin the word *cantare* means to sing, and hence the name cantor was given precisely to the chief singer in the temple – the guide and teacher of liturgical singing. The term was already used in antiquity, and a mention of the role of singers can be found in the Bible: "He set singers before the altar, and their song was sweet in its sound" (Sir 47:9). From the Judaic tradition the office of cantor and the custom of liturgical singing passed into Christianity; the first mentions of their role appear as early as the third century AD. It was then that St. Hippolytus, speaking of the cantor, also points out that he need not – indeed, ought not necessarily – belong to the clergy. The term became more widespread only in the fifth century after Christ. The position of the cantor is dated from the moment of the founding of the *schola cantorum*.

According to the historical record, a definite hierarchical order prevailed among the cantors belonging to the *schola cantorum*. At the head of the ensemble of cantors stood the *primicerius*, also called the *magister scholae*, followed by the *secondarius*, the *tertiusscholae* and the *quartusscholae*, also known as the archiparafonista. Over time, schools of this kind began to be founded also in other cathedral and monastic centers (among others Metz, St. Gallen, Reichenau, Fulda), which led to the introduction of the office of cantor. The cantors fulfilled their duties primarily in the church, but they also taught in schools. In the later period the office of cantor spread through the monasteries and collegiate churches"[^30]. Over the centuries the scope of the cantor's duties expanded considerably. Until the sixteenth century the office of cantor was held essentially by clergymen, mainly because they were educated people. Only an experienced and educated singer could be a cantor. After the Council of Trent the office of cantor could also be entrusted to lay persons. Such a possibility is provided, among others, by the synod of Utrecht (1865), which, admitting lay 


[^30]: https://liturgia.wiara.pl/doc/420664.Kantor-animator-spiewu-w-zgromadzeniu-liturgicznym (accessed 10.11.2021, 11:50).

<!-- s. 91 [PDF 91] -->
 men to the function of cantor, laid down specific requirements: "Lay men who are chosen to sing the office in the church should be pious people leading a truly Christian life, namely such as believe in their hearts what they sing with their mouths and confirm it by their deeds"[^31]. After the split of the Church in the sixteenth century as a result of the Reformation, the office of cantor continued to function in both confessions, with the functions of cantors in the Catholic Church gradually being taken over by organists.

#### Cantors in the Polish lands

In the Lutheran Church the role of this office took on ever greater significance in the socio-cultural and educational sphere, especially with regard to the migrating population. For at the beginning of the nineteenth century there came an intensified influx of German settlers into the territories of the Kingdom of Poland, and among the inhabitants of the newly founded colonies there was a clear need for spiritual leadership.

#### "Where the pastor cannot go, he will send the cantor"

Unfortunately, not everywhere and not in every circumstance could the pastor reach all the faithful awaiting him. This concerned rural areas in particular, and often the centers in which the pastor was to exercise his ministry lay several dozen kilometers apart. Covering such a route on foot, by bicycle or even by horse-drawn cart was certainly not among the easiest of tasks; it cost a good deal of time, and in bad weather conditions it was no small feat. It happened not infrequently that pastors visited their faithful only once or twice a year. Likewise, the inhabitants of the villages appeared at services in the parish 


[^31]: Ibid.

<!-- s. 92 [PDF 92] -->
 church only on major feast days and on the occasions of confirmation or the harvest festival. In the localities where it was impossible to guarantee the German settlers steady pastoral care, it therefore became necessary to appoint a lay functionary who could replace a clergyman in certain duties. Such a person was precisely the cantor, obliged to hold services in the house of prayer every Sunday and feast day, to sing hymns, to read out the letters and to read the Bible. The lay functionary could also perform weddings and funerals, but he had no ability to administer baptisms, and he was not allowed to deliver any speeches while administering the sacraments[^32]. His duties also included work as a teacher, that is, teaching children writing, reading, arithmetic and – above all – religion. For an important obligation of the cantor was the preparation of the farmers' children for confirmation[^33].

#### Work neither easy nor lucrative

Initially cantors did not have to have pedagogical preparation. Often it sufficed merely that a candidate for this office could read and write. The cantor was chosen by the pastor or the parish college, paying for his services from parish funds. These were all too often modest, which explains the commonplace absence of a professional teacher in the settlements. In that period (just as earlier in Germany) functionaries for the post of cantor were recruited from among educated farmers, former soldiers or craftsmen. It happened that a weaver, a shoemaker or an innkeeper became a teacher. The profession of teacher was for them a supplementary job, since the income brought by the occupation of "guardians of schools and cantors" (even 


[^32]: Krajewska H., *Szkoły i nauka religii – udział łódzkich protestantów w procesie kształcenia młodzieży 1820-1914*. „Rozprawy z Dziejów Oświaty". 40, 2000. Warszawa: Instytut Historii Nauki PAN (Polish). [accessed 2021-04-04], p. 6.

[^33]: In Protestantism: the solemn admission of young people aged from 14 to 18 into the church congregation. It corresponds at once to First Communion and to Confirmation – Catholic sacraments – but it is not itself a sacrament.

<!-- s. 93 [PDF 93] -->



The German folk school in Zagórów, 1942.


Photo from the collection of Andrzej Bernat



Pupils of the school on Łazińsk. In the back row, in the middle, the tallest boy is Abram Kowalski – the son of the only Jewish family living in that village.


Photo from the archive of Erich Kuhn



An announcement in "Zwiastun Ewangeliczny" of 1903 about vacancies for qualified cantors and for a teacher in the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Zagórów.



Schoolchildren with the cantor Johan Milner.


Photo from the parish's collection

<!-- s. 94 [PDF 94] -->
 counting the proceeds from a piece of land and the salary of a church functionary) amounted in truth to a subsistence minimum. Holding the function of cantor more or less on the side, they occupied themselves on a daily basis with their ordinary gainful occupations. Besides the duty of teaching the children of the Hauländer farmers and performing specific religious offices, the cantor usually also served as the gmina secretary: he kept the gmina register, handled the gmina's correspondence, and presumably also the private affairs of the Hauländers[^34].


#### Cantors with qualifications wanted

Along with the professionalization of schooling and of the teaching profession, ever higher demands began to be placed on cantors, and after 1870 a requirement of formal education was introduced for persons wishing to serve as teachers in the schools. Candidates for teachers could train on specialized courses in theological and pedagogical knowledge, after which they sat for the state examination. From 1889 an annual cantorate conference was organized, one of whose aims was to provide cantors with courses in theological and pedagogical knowledge so that they could pass the state examinations qualifying them for this function. Cantors could also prepare for the profession of teacher at the German Evangelical Teachers' Seminary in Warsaw, transferred in 1911 to Łódź[^35].

In the middle of 1942 the occupying authorities in Poland deprived the cantors of the right to hold services. In a letter to the teacher Mielke of 13 October 1942, the Zagórów pastor Aleksander Gross complains of this state of affairs: "Thereby [one] deprives the council of cantors of the possibility of acting and of living through the readings during services every Sunday"[^36].



[^34]: Rusiński, *Osady tzw „olędrów" w dawnym województwie poznańskim*, Poznań 1939 – Kraków 1947, p. 115.

[^35]: Wikipedia, accessed 12.11.2021, 12:20.

[^36]: Letters found in the wall of the house in Łazińsk, letter no. 5, translated by Jolanta Łuczak.

<!-- s. 95 [PDF 95] -->

#### The right to education for boys and girls

Martin Luther, both in his sermons and in his writings, argued for the necessity and importance of education. He was perfectly aware that learning from one's parents and from one's own experience alone would not suffice for a person to understand the world around them. The aim of education according to Luther is the capacity to make independent choices, and the chief instrument – one's native, German language. The Reformer advocated the accessibility of schooling for children and young people of both sexes; he prepared for pupils the *Small Catechism* and the *Large Catechism*, which significantly facilitated the education of successive generations[^37].

When the colonists, called Hauländers by their Polish neighbors, arrived on the new lands in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, they first had to concentrate on the considerable challenge of providing a roof over their heads and preparing the poor soil for cultivation. This guaranteed a source of livelihood for the families and the chance of further expansion of the settlement. Only then could the newcomers attend to the second – equally important for them – aspect of life: building their cultural and religious identity. The denominational schools, organized in accordance with the suggestions of Martin Luther and his successors, supported them in this.

#### The organization of the kantorate schools

In fact, immediately upon arriving at the place where a colony was to be founded, a cantor was appointed and a plot was designated for the kantorat: the school and the house of prayer, located in one building. The settlers would then turn to the manor lord on whose domains the lands they inhabited lay, with a request for permission to establish a school and for its endowment. If the manor lord 


[^37]: Kowolik P., *Marcin Luter (1483-1546) – niemiecki teolog i reformator Kościoła. 500 lat Reformacji*

    https://docplayer.pl/70734225-Marcin-luter-niemiecki-teolog-i-reformator-kosciola-500-lat-reformacji.html accessed 12.11.2021, 18:00.

<!-- s. 96 [PDF 96] -->
 settled the Hauländers in a place where buildings remained from an earlier village, he would donate one of those buildings free of charge for the school. A piece of arable land, exempted by the lord from all dues, was assigned to the school, which made it possible to maintain the teacher. Besides the income from the land he cultivated, the teacher also received a salary from the contributions of the gmina. The term kantorat could, however, have a much broader meaning – as a territorial unit of the parish, and not only the Evangelical school or the house of prayer (chapel)[^38], which may at times introduce a slight confusion into the heads of persons attempting to explore the topic for the first time. Let us therefore try to make it precise: the kantorates as territorial units grouped the inhabitants of several localities within a radius of several to a dozen or so kilometers, and the seat of each individual kantorat was the building of the same name. It usually stood out slightly in architectural terms from the rest of the dwellings and simultaneously served as a chapel and a schoolroom. Usually one half of it constituted the prayer hall, while the other was the dwelling of the teacher and cantor.

#### The cantors' school

In the denominational school, called the *Kantoratschule* (sometimes: *Lektoratschule*), that is, the cantors' school, lessons began and ended with a prayer led by the cantor, and instruction took place in German, since those schools were most often attended only by the children of Evangelical settlers, both boys and girls. The Polish-Catholic peasant of the time remained in a state of bondage to his landlord and at a low cultural level. The German-Lutheran colonist, by contrast, under the influence of the ideas of the Reformation, was a free man, and in his education and more advanced farming methods he far surpassed his Catholic neighbor. Only in the towns – and even there to an insufficient degree – did the Polish 


[^38]: Wikipedia, accessed 04.08.2021.

<!-- s. 97 [PDF 97] -->
 population have schools[^39]. In the Catholic Hauländer settlements there were generally no schools, and the children of settlers of Polish nationality and of Catholics gained access to universal and compulsory education in their native language only after the First World War[^40].

#### Educating children was not to everyone's benefit…

Teaching in the cantors' schools initially (until the mid-nineteenth century) lasted two years, and the not-so-numerous pupils attended classes only in the period free from intensive farm work – that is, in winter and in late winter before spring. In the remaining months every pair of hands on the farm was worth its weight in gold, and from their earliest years children were trained for work in the farmyard and in the field. School education was not compulsory, and therefore not every parent was in a hurry to send their child "to school" – at any rate, not until the date of confirmation began to approach by leaps and bounds. This was somewhat at odds with the precepts of Martin Luther, who as early as his "Sermon on the duty of sending children to school" urged parents not to delay in securing access to schooling for their children. The level of teaching and its results were, however, rather unimpressive at the time, usually being the resultant both of the education and pedagogical abilities of the teacher and of the attitude toward the school of the parents and the children[^41]. Yet there were among the teachers those who, despite their own low level of education, strove constantly to fill the gaps in their own knowledge and encouraged their pupils to do the same, not only imparting knowledge but also influencing the shaping of their young characters.



[^39]: Knaifel E., *Die Bedeutung des Kantors für Gemeinde und Kirche*.

[^40]: It was only in 1919 that Józef Piłsudski issued a decree establishing compulsory schooling for children aged from 7 to 14. Earlier, instruction in the Polish language often took place in private homes or in illegal organizations.

[^41]: Rusiński, ibid., p. 116.

<!-- s. 98 [PDF 98] -->

The situation was changed by a tsarist decree of 11 September 1864 and by the regulation of the authorities of the Congress Kingdom of the same year, as a result of which the kantorate schools were subjected to partial state control, being divided into three categories:

- a house of prayer,
- a house of prayer with Sunday school instruction,
- an elementary school under state supervision.

As I mentioned earlier, requirements concerning the education of persons wishing to serve as teachers in the cantors' schools soon appeared. This guaranteed the pupils of the schools the care of a qualified pedagogue, able to provide access to a broader and verified knowledge of the world around them. In 1938 there appeared the Regulation of the Council of Ministers of 17 December 1936 on the Recognition of the Fundamental Internal Law of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in the Republic of Poland, in which it was laid down that:

> "To provide for the religious needs of their parishioners, the parishes (filial parishes) erect and maintain churches, schools, charitable institutions and parish houses – and also establish parish cemeteries. In the larger and more extensive parishes, preaching stations for the occasional holding of services in private premises, as well as houses of prayer, that is, chapels and congregation halls, that is, kantorate schools, may be established in individual localities, in which the cantor, that is, the lay assistant of the parish priest, in his stead baptizes children, buries the dead, holds read services, teaches the parishioners religious songs, and also teaches the children religion and prepares them for confirmation"[^42].



[^42]: Paragraph 16 point 1, https://sip.lex.pl/akty-prawne/dzu-dziennik-ustaw/uznanie-zasadniczego-prawa-wewnetrznego-kosciola-ewangelicko-16835480 (accessed 11.11.2021, 20:28).

<!-- s. 99 [PDF 99] -->

#### Kantorate schools and cantors' schools in the parish of Zagórów

At the outbreak of the Second World War, the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Zagórów administered the following kantorate schools:

Olchowo: cantor and teacher from 1924, Ferdinand Homt.

Tarszewo: cantor Adolf Münch.

Michalinów, gmina Oleśnica: cantor Ludwig Schulz.

Michalinów, gmina Trąbczyn: cantor Paul Schulz (farmer).

Łazińsk: cantor and teacher Gustav Adolf Mielke, from 1925.

Anielewo: cantor and teacher Bruno Mielke.

Schools for children of the Evangelical confession operated in the following localities:

Zagórów, Łazińsk, Olchowo, Anielewo, Michalinów Oleśnicki, Michalinów Trąbczyński, Wrąbczynkowska Kolonia (today: Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry).

The information we possess about them is, unfortunately, incomplete and at the present time largely impossible to supplement.

The fundamental document on which I base my study is the book by E. Kneifel entitled *Die evangelisch-augsburgischen Gemeinden der Kalischer Diözese* [The Evangelical-Augsburg Parishes of the Kalisz Diocese] of 1937, which constitutes the most reliable and most extensive source of knowledge on the subject that interests us. An interesting source also proved to be the Chronicle of the Primary School on Łazińsk, written down by the school's first post-war headmaster, Józef Magryta, and the Chronicle of the Public School in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry – notes kept by Olga Ritterówna, M. Wróblewski, Maria Gawrońska and Konrad Kryszak. The rest of the information comes from the recollections of former parishioners, former and present inhabitants of Zagórów and its surroundings. In the protocol of the visitation of the superintendent of the Kalisz diocese, Pastor Teichmann, of 22 August 1877, we find the information that under the administration of the parish of Zagórów there is one elementary school with some sixty children

Photo by Katarzyna Połom



The former denominational school on Łazińsk, today it houses private apartments.


Photo by Artur Paprzycki, 2021.



The building of the former cantors' school in Michalinów Trąbczyński.


Photo by Artur Paprzycki, 2021.

<!-- s. 102 [PDF 102] -->
 in Michalinów, and seven cantors' schools, attended by 230 pupils, boys and girls of the Evangelical confession.


In the following years the number of pupils in the schools increased considerably, and the German and Polish schools functioned in agreement side by side or shared buildings and staff for years – until September 1939. After the outbreak of the war, the occupier introduced the Regulation on Schooling in the General Government of 31 October 1939, which presupposed national segregation: children of German nationality could attend only German schools, Polish children only Polish schools, which from then on remained under the strict supervision of the occupying authorities. In March 1940 Polish children were deprived entirely of the possibility of education, whereas the children of German citizens attended the *Deutsche Volksschule*. After the Second World War nothing returned to the old order; the majority of the German settlers abandoned their homes, and the school buildings passed entirely into the hands of the new Polish authorities.

##### The school in Zagórów

From 1850 there functioned a schoolroom together with the cantor's dwelling, carved out of half of a building purchased for the sake of the then-forming parish community at 54 Konińska Street (in the other part of the building, in accordance with custom, the prayer hall was organized). Religion in the Evangelical school in Zagórów was taught by the pastor, and the remaining subjects – a qualified teacher (usually the cantor). In the interwar period there were already several primary schools operating in Zagórów, including private ones, and the seven-year public school on Kościelna Street was also attended by children of the national minorities. E. Kneifel confirms that before the outbreak of the Second World War nearly 20 children of the Evangelical confession were receiving instruction there. In 1835 the teacher was August Gabler. From 1940, when the Poles were deprived of access to education, the school was renamed Deutsche Volksschule im Hinterberg. The post of teacher was then held by Mr. Gulke.

<!-- s. 103 [PDF 103] -->

##### The cantors' school on Łazińsk

Łazińsk was the oldest Evangelical settlement and the largest kantorat in the parish of Zagórów, hence the impressive number of pupils of the cantors' school – around 160 children. It was the largest of the Evangelical schools in the territory of the parish, and its owner was the collective body of the cantorate, the inhabitants of the village of Łazińsk, German colonists. Like all the others, it was initially a denominational school with German as the language of instruction. The first school building was erected in the years 1838–1839 using bricks and a building material very characteristic of the Hauländer settlements – bog iron ore.

Wishing to attract and encourage the teachers to more intensive work, the inhabitants of the village placed at the teachers' disposal 20 morgi and 218 rods of land and built the farm buildings: a barn, a stable and a woodshed. The teacher's dwelling, consisting of two rooms and a kitchen, was likewise located in the building of the Evangelical chapel. There are no exact data on the development of the school, on the names of the teachers and their periods of service. The last teacher, Gustav Adolf Mielke, held the post of teacher and headmaster from 1932 – we learn this from the Chronicle of the Public School on Łazińsk, kept by Wincenty Magryta in 1946. Mielke also served in the settlement as cantor.

The erection of the next building, in which classes were held on the eve of the outbreak of the Second World War and during the occupation, the inhabitants of the village owe to the efforts of Gustav Mielke. The new building consisted of three classrooms and the headmaster's office. "The Evangelical religious brotherhoods contributed their funds to the building of the school […]" – writes Wincenty Magryta in 1946, who was then acting as the school's headmaster. In 1932 Gustav Mielke was stripped of the post of headmaster on account of "overzealousness in cultivating Germandom" and transferred to Galicia[^43]. He returned to his post after 1939 and taught until he was called up to the army 


[^43]: According to the memoirs of Eugenia Plitt.

<!-- s. 104 [PDF 104] -->
 after the defeat at Stalingrad. "From that time dates the decline of the school – writes Wincenty Magryta in the school chronicle in March 1946. – On the building, often unrepaired and neglected, one can see the lack of care of Mielke Gustav's successor, his sister Leokadia" and two young teachers from Thuringia.

##### The denominational school in the village of Anielewo

In this locality there existed a one-class school with German as the language of instruction, attended by a fairly numerous group of 60–70 children. The teacher at the institution was the cantor Bruno Mielke – the brother of Gustav, the teacher and headmaster of the school on Łazińsk. Shortly before the outbreak of the Second World War, Bruno Mielke was serving as a teacher in the locality of Maślaki near Wilczyn. It is difficult to determine whether he worked at both schools at once, or whether someone else, whose name we shall never know, replaced him in Anielewo. After the start of the military operations of the Third Reich, the young cantor, who dreamed of completing the seminary and of a pastoral ministry, was called up to serve in the Wehrmacht.

##### The cantors' school in Olchowo

In the beautifully situated settlement of Olchowo there operated a one-class school with German as the language of instruction. More than 40 children attended it. From 1924 the post of teacher was held by the then cantor, Ferdinand Homt. Today not even a trace remains of the school building; a small rise below the forest recalls its former location. Olchowo at present is a locality almost entirely depopulated; most of the buildings left by the pre-war inhabitants have fallen into ruin and been dismantled down to the last plank.

<!-- s. 105 [PDF 105] -->





A pupil's exercise book for learning to write. Auxiliary publications for the school teacher and cantor. Materials discovered in the wall of the cantor's house on Łazińsk.


Photo by Mirosław Słowiński


<!-- s. 106 [PDF 106] -->

##### Michalinów Oleśnicki, the cantors' school

In the settlement there functioned a one-class school with German as the language of instruction. It was housed in the house of prayer. It had approximately 60 pupils, who were under the care of a teacher named Julius Berendt. After the Second World War a primary school for the children of Michalinów was organized in the building. The building is the property of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin; at present there are apartments there rented by private individuals.

##### Michalinów Trąbczyński, the German denominational school

We know very little about this school. Nearly 70 pupils attended it. The language of instruction was German, and from 1923 the teacher was Friedrich Klingbeil. The function of cantor was performed by the farmer Paul Schulz, who lived near the cemetery. The building in which it was housed has survived to this day – it is now in private hands.

##### Wrąbczynkowska Kolonia (Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry)

The teacher Olga Ritterówna, who kept the chronicle of the Public School in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry in the school year 1938/1939, notes:

From time immemorial there has existed in the Colony of Wrąbczynkowo, also called the Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, a cantorate settlement […] comprising in a single parcel 10 morgi and 71 rods, of which 8 morgi are arable land. On this settlement there stood, and still stand to this day, the following buildings:

- the cantor's house, thatched with straw, consisting half of a schoolroom and half of a dwelling for the cantor;
- a house of clay and boards, roofed with tiles, in which there is one great hall of prayer;
- a barn of beams;
- a farm building of clay, for pigsties and a cowshed.

<!-- s. 107 [PDF 107] -->

All these buildings were maintained from the contributions of the faithful. The cantor, whenever he had free places in the school, also admitted children of the Roman Catholic confession to instruction – with the consent of the entire Evangelical community. The chronicle also lists several names of men who held the cantoral office. They were:

Michał Hertrog from 1842  
Krystian Kanwiszer from 1870  
Beutler  
Kleinert  
Cado  
Fryderyk Lejmann from 1902.  
Adolf Münch from 1909 until the First World War[^44].

During the First World War the cantors' school was transformed into a public elementary school for children of both confessions. In 1928 it was decided to build a new school building, and the former seat was converted into dwellings for the teachers. The German settlers were rather reluctant about this idea, wishing to preserve in the old building a separate one-class elementary public school with German as the language of instruction. They even submitted a petition to the school board, declaring categorically that until their application was considered favorably, they did not intend to send their children to the five-class public school[^45]. On 10 September 1931 the construction was completed and the new school building was solemnly consecrated. The five classes (8 divisions) were attended by 313 pupils, and they were taught by 4 teachers: Maria Gawrońska, Konrad Kryszak, Stanisław Trębaczkiewicz and J. Mikołajczykówna. The post of headmaster was taken up by M. Wróblewski. With the consent of the school board, from the school year 1932/1933 one class was to consist of the children 


[^44]: The names are listed by Olga Ritterówna.

[^45]: The school events of the years 1928-1935 are described in the Chronicle by M. Wróblewski, the headmaster of the school from 1931.

<!-- s. 108 [PDF 108] -->
 of Germans living in the vicinity of the school. The head of the division organized in a separate room, and at the same time the teacher leading it, was Juliusz Bauer, who had been teaching in the public school since 1928[^46].

"The school in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry until the war in 1939 was Polish-German – recalled Gustaw Kuske from Lądek in a conversation with Wiesława Kowalska, the author of the publication *Puszcza Pyzdrska. Podróż sentymentalna* – Up to the 4th grade the Polish and German children were taught separately. From the 4th to the 7th grade instruction was only in Polish, jointly for the Polish and German children." Every child of the settlers spoke both German and Polish fluently. The children were taught in two shifts (the older classes from 8 o'clock, and the younger ones from 11), and there were about 20 pupils in a class. The children attended religion classes separately[^47].

Looking through Frieda Banik's school report card for the school year 1933/1934, I learn that the children in the fourth division of the third cohort still attended classes: arithmetic with geometry, nature study, geography, history, drawing, handicrafts, singing and physical exercises. Frieda's report of 15 June 1934 was signed by the head of the division, Juliusz Bauer.

In 1935 the ailing Bauer was replaced by Olga Richterówna, a German teacher who had come from the Koło county. In beautiful Polish she keeps the entries in the school chronicle and prepares her pupils to take part in patriotic ceremonies: the successive anniversaries of the recovery of Poland's independence, on the occasion of the name day of the President, of Marshal Rydz-Śmigły, or of another anniversary of the death of Marshal Józef Piłsudski… As of 15 November 1937 the one-class German public school is renamed a public 


[^46]: Ibid.

[^47]: Kowalska Wiesława, *Puszcza Pyzdrska, Podróż sentymentalna*.

<!-- s. 109 [PDF 109] -->
 elementary school of the first grade, and a year later, in August, the board of the gmina of Dłusk decides to sell the remaining cantoral land (a large part of it had earlier been divided among the teachers of the public school), together with the buildings, to a private purchaser. After the outbreak of the war, Olga Richterówna left Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry; on 16 November 1939 Edmund Weiss was appointed headmaster of the school, and the former headmistress Maria Gawrońska, with the consent of the occupiers, tried to organize instruction in the school. German was to be the official language of instruction, and Polish – an additional subject. "Naturally this existed only on paper – none of the teachers knew German well enough, nor did anyone feel like teaching it"[^48]. On 1 March 1940 all the Polish teachers were dismissed, and the Polish children left the walls of the school.
