<!-- s. 111 [PDF 111] -->
### The Beutlers: Two Centuries in the History of a Family


Damian Kruczkowski

In telling the story of the Evangelical, Hauländer families of the Konin region, including the areas of the Zagórów municipality, it is necessary to speak of particular families and particular individuals descended from them. One of the many families and figures worth mentioning — because in the last few years of her life she was one of the main sources of knowledge about the settlers of the Konin and Zagórów area — is the Beutler family and Gertruda Schwemmer, who came from it. Mrs Schwemmer was a carrier of the experience and knowledge of several generations of the Beutler family, who settled, were born, worked, founded families and died within the borders of present-day Poland. Gertruda Adelheid Schwemmer née Beutler was a contemporary of independent Poland. She was born on 26 December 1918 and died in Berlin on 11 February 2018. I first met Mrs Gertruda Schwemmer in Zagórów, in the Evangelical church, during a Polish-German service attended by parishioners led by Fr Waldemar Wunsz, parish priest of the Evangelical-Augsburg Parish in Konin, and parishioners from Detmold led by their pastor. This is already a tradition of many years' standing, begun by Erich Kuhn, born in Łazińsk near Zagórów — his ancestors came to these lands as settlers nearly two centuries ago. Mrs Gertruda Schwemmer had snow-white hair and bright eyes looking out from behind her glasses. Her small figure gave her person a kind of ethereality, airiness — one could say fragility. That is how I came to know her. The gaze of her bright eyes, despite her age, was very lively, curious about the world, about events and people. I had the honour, together with her daughter, of escorting her to the altar so that she could receive communion. After the service we drove 
<!-- s. 112 [PDF 112] -->
 to the Evangelical cemetery in Zagórów, where with Mrs Gertruda and her daughter Lila we visited the grave of their relatives: Alfred, Emil and Emilia Beutler. It was then that we arranged to talk the next day.

The next day I drove to Skulsk, where after the war Mrs Gertruda and her husband Reinhard Schwemmer had bought a house. On a foundation of blue and red granite boulders, roofed with pantiles, several dozen metres from the gate, deep in an almost magical garden, rises a house of red brick. Mrs Gertruda was waiting for me behind the house, seated at a white table on an equally white garden chair. The red of the brick, the green of the trees and lawn, the magic of the garden — all of this made her seem even more ethereal than she was in reality. I sat down on the other side of the table and we began to talk about the past, about the history of the Beutler family.

Who was Mrs Gertruda Schwemmer of the Beutlers? Born in independent Poland, she came of age in the times of the Second Republic, married during the Second World War, already in the Warthegau, and bore her first children in that period as well, the later ones already in the People's Republic, in which it fell to her to raise and educate all of them. She spent the last twenty-five years of her life in Berlin, to which she moved on account of her age, to be closer to her daughters, who surrounded their 'Mutti' with solicitous care. A European, a Pole, a German, but above all a Hauländer — for that would probably be the most fitting description of the entire social group from which the Beutler family grew, a group that in the present day, as before the Second World War, was diverse, both in origin and in views and political opinions, as well as in identification with a particular nationality.

From Gertruda Schwemmer née Beutler, as she was leaving this world, back to her first ancestor known by name on Polish lands, there stretched 240 years. Andreas Beutler — for that was the name of the first Beutler, who began the family's history in Poland — was born in 1778 and was one of the many settlers whom Polish kings, church hierarchs and the Polish nobility had earlier invited. The so-called Hauländers were brought 
<!-- s. 113 [PDF 113] -->
 to lands depopulated by wars or epidemics, and above all to waterlogged, marshy areas and river floodplains, as people who knew perfectly well how to find their way in such conditions: to drain meadows and forests, dig ditches, take land from the water and the bogs and then cultivate it with success, land that brought profits both to them and to those who allowed them to farm these territories.

Hauländer settlement in the lands of Poland reaches back to the sixteenth century, when actual Dutchmen — or, as it is safer to say, inhabitants of the Netherlands, famed for their skill in combating the geographical depression of their country — arrived to transplant that skill onto the soil of the lands of Eastern Europe. In time the inhabitants of northern Germany, of Pomerania, but also of Mazovia and of Poland's northern lands acquired the same skill. All of them, dropping the mute 'h' in many languages and polonizing the name, came to be called Hauländers. It was to one of these settler groups that the aforementioned Andreas Beutler belonged, and presumably his wife Eva Rosine Schnell as well. Settlers were most often second, third or last sons in a family, for whom there was no longer enough of the paternal inheritance to divide, and who faced either hunger and vagabondage in their homeland or an uncertain tomorrow — yet with a chance of survival in a completely foreign, though neighbouring, country.

One can imagine the uncertainty with which Andreas Beutler set out into the unknown. Nothing good awaited him in the family home apart from the aforementioned hunger and vagabondage, seasonal work, the life of a hired hand, for he had no land of his own with which to feed himself and a numerous — as was often the case in those days — family. He packed up his modest belongings, perhaps on a wretched cart, or perhaps simply on his back, and set off eastward. What surely did not guide him was the idea of *drang nach Osten* that the propaganda of the Third Reich would exploit many years later, but simply the desire to survive and to secure a livelihood for his family. It is certain that in this journey — relatively short, but nonetheless terribly difficult and dangerous if one takes into account the times and the means of transport — many did not reach their destination; they remained somewhere at their halts, in forest or field graves, overcome by the hardships of travel and illness, 
<!-- s. 114 [PDF 114] -->
 often by the bands of marauders prowling the European routes of that era. For Andreas, however, it succeeded. He arrived safely in the lands lying a short distance east of Konin, in the vicinity of Kramsk in eastern Wielkopolska. In this region a new stage of his life began, a new chapter in the existence of the Beutler family. The villages of Bilczew and Klonowa, and then, a little further west of Konin, Święcia and Dąbrowica, are the places where the Beutlers would live, work and die for over two centuries.

It was the beginning of the nineteenth century. Living conditions in the new territory, though fertile and bountiful, were not easy at the time. Despite many exemptions from obligations owed to the manor or the Church — which was a privilege of the Hauländer settlers — their life was no fairy tale. Hauländer law, under which the settler villages were founded, guaranteed above all the personal freedom of the settlers, long-term and even perpetual leasehold of the land, with the possibility of buying it out — provided the manor or the Church, which was the founder of the village, agreed — as well as the passing of the land to one's heirs. Draining floodplains, bogs and waterlogged meadows, wresting from the water land that could be cultivated, clearing the forests and thickets that often covered large areas of the terrain given over to the settlers' use, building homesteads and farm buildings from scratch — these were the first and most fundamental tasks that the new inhabitants of these lands had to perform. Treacherous shifting sands and bogs, the depths of rivers and lakes, ice equally treacherous in winter, in summer swarms of mosquitoes and midges, which besides their painful stings often left their victims with various illnesses that decimated the population. But the will to fight, the will to survive, of these people who had covered hundreds of kilometres to find better conditions of existence for themselves and their families, a better world, was enormous. At the beginning of the nineteenth century Andreas Beutler built a house, put up farm buildings in the yard, cleared a stretch of forest, drained expanses of marshland, and as he finished one round of work he began the next, no less toilsome: tilling the soil, raising animals, grappling with a hard but prosperous life, ensuring the family's survival. Today we can imagine how heavy that labour was, for agricultural machinery was still a long way off. When he began to farm 
<!-- s. 115 [PDF 115] -->
 his new domain, Poland was by that time already partitioned among three powers, but on these lands, around the settler villages, there still existed old indigenous Polish villages with which the settlers came into contact through neighbourly relations and commercial exchange, quickly learning — for practical reasons — the language spoken by the majority of the local population, while at the same time preserving their own, German, at least in the first periods of settlement. With time it looked very different: some confined themselves to reading the Bible in the language of their old homeland and to inscriptions on gravestones, while at home or with neighbours they spoke Polish. There were families in which German was not used at all, and others in which an effort was made to remember the ancestors' homeland and the children had to learn it as a matter of course.

As I have already mentioned, the laborious work — and in the initial period surely also many problems of an organizational nature, as well as those arising from life on waterlogged terrain — was far from an idyll. The nineteenth century reaped an enormous harvest among the young, and especially among children, both newborns and those raised, as it used to be said. Andreas Beutler died on 23.11.1826 at the age of merely 48 — one might say almost in the prime of life. Five years earlier, at the age of 38, his wife Eva Rosine had died. A few years after the death of them both, in 1831, an epidemic of cholera swept through the territories where their descendants now lived. That one, and the one of 1852 — far more severe — the Beutler family survived. The second wave was so strong that, as sources from that period report, the local Catholic population made its way weeping and lamenting to the sanctuary in Licheń to beseech the Mother of God for grace and preservation from the pestilential air. It is certain that of all the children Andreas and Eva had, four reached adulthood: Johann (Jan), Martin (Marcin), Louise (Ludwika) and Andreas (Andrzej) junior. Andreas, the son of Andreas, was a key figure for this story, for it was with his descendant that I sat in the magic garden by the beautiful house and listened to tales of the family and of ancestors from long ago. In 1829 the nineteen-year-old Andreas, recorded in the registers of vital records 
<!-- s. 116 [PDF 116] -->
 as Andrzej, already a gospodarz — for that is the title the official gave him in the marriage record — residing in the village of Klonowa, married, as the marriage register states, his peer, Justyna Lehmann. Meanwhile Louise married a teacher, Fryderyk Wilhelm Schulz — their daughter Mathilde Schulz later married her cousin Ferdynand Beutler, who was the son of Johann. Martin married Ludwika Hirsekorn, and Johann Ernestine Steinborn. The farming family of Beutlers grew and slowly began to move about. One conclusion suggests itself: the lands their father had chosen as a place to live favoured the family and allowed both its rapid growth and its social advancement. The first and second generations of the Beutler family were above all farmers, ploughmen, husbandmen — one could say: the salt of the earth. To every piece of land, no matter in what corner of the world the little plot lies on which the farmer tills the soil, thereby sustaining himself and his family, while the surplus produced makes it possible to keep alive those who live in towns and small cities or in the great metropolises.

The Beutlers have strong genes; in every generation many children were born to them. Unfortunately, many died, but to say it outright — though it may seem brutal — such were the times. Knowledge of hygiene, medicine and so forth, especially in the villages, was scant, and so many tiny children died, and it was not rare for young mothers to die in childbirth as well. It was the sons who carried the name Beutler into the future and onto successive generations, and so the four sons of Andreas-Andrzej and Justyna — Ludwik, Andrzej, Gottlieb and Wilhelm — founded four further lines of the family. In the nineteenth century Europe experienced many upheavals: Napoleon's battles, the dissolution of the old Holy Roman Empire of the German Nation, the rise of the Austrian Empire, the November Uprising, the Spring of Nations, the January Uprising, the founding of the German Empire under the Hohenzollerns' sceptre, the industrial revolution. Momentous and fraught with consequence was, of course, the industrial revolution, but also many innovations in technology, medicine and other fields of science. The Beutler family, too, did not stand still; it grew, developed, evolved. To some descendants 
<!-- s. 117 [PDF 117] -->
 of Andreas and Eva Rosine tilling the land was not enough; they did not want to be farmers like their forebears, especially as the times favoured them, the family was becoming well-off and relatively, by village standards, propertied. And so two sons of Andreas-Andrzej and Justyna became teachers: Wilhelm in Nowy Czarków, Andrzej in Święcia.

When one analyses and traces the histories of settler families, phenomenal and worthy of admiration is how, from generation to generation, despite many hardships and obligations, and often disinclination, these families strove to raise their social status. And very often it was precisely through persevering labour that they succeeded. I think that the cult of work, which has been characteristic of Protestantism from its very beginning, that is, almost since 1517, is the source of their success. These people did not work merely in order to consume and stand still; by working they multiplied their goods and converted them into the success of the whole family and into a social status ever higher and better. The sons of Andreas-Andrzej and Justyna clearly subscribed to the ethos of work. Two became teachers, the others continued to till the land, but now as prosperous husbandmen, enjoying esteem and respect, which found reflection in the registers of vital records, where very often they appear in their villages as godparents or witnesses. The Beutlers married: Gottlieb Julianna Schiewe, Andrzej Henrietta Hahn, Wilhelm Emilia Fryderyka Lauf, Ludwik Emilia Hermann.

The four lines of the Beutler family, as they grew, put down roots in the surrounding land, but above all in society. Although they were aware of their different origin from that of the majority of the inhabitants of these lands, the matter did not preoccupy them and did not rob them of sleep. They worked, cared for their families, entered into relations with neighbours, pupils, workers, and led normal and reasonably peaceful lives. In 1862 Justyna née Lehmann Beutler died, and in 1879 Andreas-Andrzej Beutler, the head of the family, passed away. One generation was leaving the stage of history, and then the next generation assumed full rule over its domains.

<!-- s. 118 [PDF 118] -->

Ludwik Beutler, born on 29 May 1845 in Bilczew, is the progenitor of the line that shifted the centre of gravity of the Beutlers' family life to the south-west of Konin. In time this line emigrated almost entirely to the west, to Germany, for after the Second World War it could no longer find a place to live in post-war Poland. But that comes later. In the meantime, three sons were born to Ludwik and Emilia: Adolf, Julius and Ewald, along with five daughters. It is thus evident that Ludwik and Emilia upheld the family tradition of having a sizable flock of children. Large families were the norm in those days; people knew nothing of contraception, and modern medicine and the care of women in childbirth and of newborns were still in their infancy. The end of the nineteenth century, when Ludwik's children were being born, was a time of relative peace in Europe. The German Empire had consolidated itself and the thunder of cannon had ceased until the next great upheavals and revolutions — several decades of relative peace. The boys developed normally and most probably very quickly began to help their parents with the work on the family estate in Bilczew. But Bilczew was decidedly too little for healthy and strong, with time young, men, when the whole wide world stood open before them, and thanks to the solid and persevering labour of themselves, their parents and the generations before them, the family could afford to dispatch the boys into adult life beyond Bilczew. Ewald Beutler set out for Koło, where he settled and led the life of a townsman. His son Erwin Beutler became a pharmacist in Koło. He devoted himself to that profession without reserve, practising it even after leaving Poland, in Berlin, in Weissensee, where he settled. Julius went off to Łódź, where he opened and ran a butcher's shop — he was yet another of the Beutler clan to become a townsman. Julius's shop was said to be on Piotrkowska Street. Adolf, for his part, led the life of a farmer, in keeping with family tradition, but no longer in Bilczew — in Dąbrowica near Rzgów, not far from Zagórów. And it was he who, one December day in 1918, became the proud father of another daughter, who at her baptism was given the names Gertrud Adelheid — only after the war did the offices polonize the name to Gertruda.

<!-- s. 119 [PDF 119] -->

The fates of the sons of Ludwik and Emilia illustrate the family's next leap up the social ladder of the world of that day. From village to town, from a small farm to an almost considerable latifundium. Work, work and work again — that is the value that was unquestionably instilled in successive generations of Beutlers, and that they took deeply to heart. Wherever on the earthly globe they appeared, even if they had nothing in the way of material possessions with them, they made indisputable and salutary use of that treasure, faithful to the Protestant ethos of work and to the family tradition. The land in Dąbrowica near Rzgów, on which Adolf Beutler, born on 4 January 1874 in Bilczew, farmed into his late years — in practice, until the end of the Second World War — most probably belonged to the dowry of his first wife Amalie née Kinast, or was bought shortly after their wedding. The first daughter of that couple, Natalia, was born already in Dąbrowica in 1900, and in Dąbrowica, two years later, another daughter, Emma, came into the world. Adolf Beutler steadily expanded the farm and developed horse breeding. The farm lay a proverbial stone's throw from the Warta, though on a slight elevation of the terrain, which was very important in view of the flooding.

At the beginning of the twentieth century there were as yet no flood embankments along the Warta, and so the river, overflowing year after year, flooded the meadows, fields and farms lying closer to its banks. Mrs Gertruda still remembered the roar of terrified animals that the farmers of the flooded farms evacuated by boat to safer ground. Life in the countryside, as has been said earlier, is no idyll. The lack of proper health care, the lack of specialist medicines and equipment, continued to reap a sad harvest among the rural population. Emma and Natalia were still little girls when their mother, Adolf's first wife, Amalie Beutler, died. Life abhors a vacuum, especially in the countryside, with the work of a large farm being steadily developed, with small children to raise, and so Adolf entered into a second marriage. This time the choice fell on Emilia Münch from Wrąbczynek near Zagórów, daughter of Christian and Paulina. Deft, resourceful and hard-working, she quickly took charge of 
<!-- s. 120 [PDF 120] -->



Adolf Beutler with his wife Emilia.


Photo from the archive of Lili Vogt

Employees of the mill and the power plant in Zagórów; third from the left is Adolf Beutler, on the roof of the car his daughters, Irena and Aniela.

<!-- s. 121 [PDF 121] -->



Alfred Beutler, a name-day card.

Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat

<!-- s. 122 [PDF 122] -->
 the household, her stepdaughters, and in the following years further children began to arrive.


It is on precisely this branch of the Beutler family that I would like to focus attention now — first, because it is the one best known to me, and second, because the fates of the children of Adolf and Emilia, as well as their own, make up an international mosaic of which many citizens of Poland after the Second World War formed a part. As I mentioned, Adolf was occupied with tilling the land and breeding horses, and the horses that were born and raised on Adolf Beutler's farm in Dąbrowica were of exceptional beauty. Emilia, besides being an exceptionally resourceful housekeeper — with so many children it is hard to be anything else — was also a seamstress, by which she supplemented the household budget among the women of the surrounding villages and small towns. The beginning of the twentieth century was marked by many turbulent events that resounded widely across the world and did not remain without influence on the life of the Beutler family. In the early years of the new century conflicts flared up anew after years of relative peace. In 1901 the long and peaceful reign of the British Queen Victoria came to an end; in 1905 the Russo-Japanese War broke out, which in a short time laid bare the internal decay and instability of the Russian Empire; the revolution of 1905; unrest in Europe, and as its consequence the outbreak of the First World War, called the Great War, in 1914; the revolutions in Russia in 1917; the fall of the Austrian, German and Russian monarchies; the end of the war and Poland's recovery of independence in November 1918. All of this formed the background against which the next generation of the descendants of Andreas and Eva Rosine entered into life. From 1918 all of them became full-fledged citizens of the Second Polish Republic. And here I must list all the children of Adolf and his two wives, with a brief description of their fates, interweaving the narrative with mentions of events that steered the fates of the young Beutlers in this and no other way. Each of these stories is a separate tale that shows people, events, times, and above all points to the extraordinarily rich multicultural heritage of the territories of present-day Poland. Natalia Beutler was the first daughter 
<!-- s. 123 [PDF 123] -->
 and the first in the family born in Dąbrowica, in 1900. When she reached the so-called marriageable years — that is, when she could already marry — she wed Tadeusz Molicki, with whom she emigrated to Argentina, precisely to Buenos Aires. There, still before the war, they owned a grocery and general-goods shop. They sailed to Poland on a visit in 1939, and the outbreak of the Second World War overtook them there, which effectively made it impossible for them to return to Argentina, and so they decided to remain in Poland. During the war they settled in Silesia — they lived in Bytom, where Tadeusz Molicki worked as an accountant in a leather-glove factory in Świdnica (*Handschuhfabrik-Alfred Scholz – Schweidnitz*). The niece of his wife, and daughter of Mrs Gertruda, Lila, has preserved to this day a wall thermometer bearing the factory's name, and it still works. After Tadeusz's death Natalia lived with her sister — precisely Gertruda — in Skulsk. Through her niece she is remembered as an exceptionally kind and likeable aunt who, even in old age, disliked older people, but felt very much at ease in the company of those younger than herself.

Natalia Molicka née Beutler died in 1991 and was buried at the Evangelical cemetery in Konin. Emma Beutler, born in 1902, was the second daughter of Adolf and his first wife Amalie. The family associates Emma with a photograph, already as a middle-aged woman, living in Canada, to which she had gone with her husband Erwin Byczyński. In the photograph taken on the occasion of her son's wedding around 1967 one can see an elegant woman with decided features that betray a propensity for frequent smiles. Erwin Byczyński, her husband, came from the neighbouring municipality of Lądek, from beyond the river, and despite his Polish-sounding name also traces his line back to German settlers of the eighteenth century, who, perhaps on account of the cooper's trade they practised, were initially called Tonne[^49], a name that gradually underwent polonization and sounded Beczka, Beczyński, Bitschinski, up to the form Byczyński. We shall yet meet the Byczyński family in this story.



[^49]: *Tonne* (German) – barrel.

<!-- s. 124 [PDF 124] -->

Ella Beutler was born in 1908 in Dąbrowica as the first daughter and first child of Adolf Beutler and his second wife Emilia. She married Reinhold Hintz from Tarszewo, a village likewise situated in the Warta's floodplain, not far from Dąbrowica. Mrs Gertruda recalls that Reinhold Hintz met his future wife when she was in the first grade of primary school, that is, as a girl of about six or seven, and on seeing her is said to have declared that she must be his wife. At the turn of the 1920s and 1930s Reinhold left with his mother for the USA. After a few years he returned to Poland, bringing with him on the ship a large American automobile in order to impress Ella, for the visit, which was to last three months at most, had been arranged for the sole purpose of allowing him to propose and marry Ella — which in fact came to pass. The newlyweds departed for the USA. They proved a well-matched couple, as their relatives and descendants confirm, above all the daughters Alice and Shirley, who lived until recently. Reinhold died at the age of 88 in 1989, and in 2004 Ella died; she was 95.

Erwin Beutler, Adolf's first son, the future heir to the farm in Dąbrowica, which, because of the vicissitudes of war and the post-war period, he never managed to take over. In the photograph in which he poses with a horse bred on the family farm one can see a handsome young man, holding the horse confidently by its bridle, a born farmer and breeder. Erwin was born in 1910 in Dąbrowica. The fates of Erwin, as well as of his brother Gerhard, show what a cruel elemental force war is and how much evil ideologies of every kind, aimed at setting people against one another, can do. When the war broke out, Erwin was a young man. For tens — indeed almost hundreds — of years no one had asked his family of which country they wished to be citizens. They were simply Beutlers. In 1918, after Poland regained its independence, they were given Polish identity documents and recognized as citizens of Poland; in 1939, occupying Poland, the Germans recognized them as Germans, gave them German documents and made them citizens of the Third Reich. No one asked these young people whether they wanted this at all. A paradox it must be called that 
<!-- s. 125 [PDF 125] -->
 many of them, after all, fought in September 1939 in the Polish Army, because they were citizens of Poland, the country in which they had their lands, homes, families, their heritage, a country that was their homeland. In life there is not only black and white; there are, rather, many — even very many — shades of grey. Recognized as Germans, they had to submit to the realities prevailing in occupied Poland. During the Second World War Erwin worked in the German police in Łódź. There he met his wife Wanda. In the winter of 1945, like many Germans or those recognized as Germans or recognizing themselves as such, Erwin Beutler's family — consisting of himself, his wife, twin boys wrapped in blankets and a little daughter — set off westward, fleeing the Red Army, of whose cruelties legends circulated. During the flight one of the twins died of cold and hunger, which led the Beutlers to decide to return home. Only after the war did Erwin Beutler and his family leave for Germany, to Burgdorf in Niedersachsen, where Erwin died in March 1990.

Alfred Beutler, born in Dąbrowica in 1913 — son of Adolf, another victim of the elemental force of war. Alfred served in the Polish Army and as a Polish soldier was demobilized in 1939. One of the cards he sent to the family contains his photograph in a Polish uniform, and it was sent on the occasion of a name day, which shows the extent to which the minority of German origin had taken up Polish customs. In Germany name days are not observed — birthdays are. The irony of fate and the mad ideologies decreed that, in order to confirm his loyalty to the new, no longer Polish authority, Alfred was conscripted into the German army. The hell that broke loose at the end of the war claimed young Alfred's life, alas. Only in 1952 did the family learn what had become of him. A letter sent to Alfred's brother Arnulf informs that Alfred Beutler died on 17.04.1945 in Austria — the car in which he was travelling drove onto a mine, and Alfred was killed on the spot. His cousin, the grandson of Andreas the teacher from Święcia, also Alfred Beutler, likewise served in the Polish Army; he managed to leave the occupied territories, distinguished himself in valour at Monte Cassino, and for this was decorated with the order of Virtuti Militari.

<!-- s. 126 [PDF 126] -->

The example of the two Alfred Beutlers shows how convoluted the fates of members of one family can be when war breaks into a calm and settled life. Polish history, after all, knows many such examples. The next child of Adolf and Emilia Beutler was Gerhard Beutler, born in Dąbrowica in 1915. Gerhard, like many like him, was also a victim of the terrible Second World War. Called up to the Polish Army in 1939, he did not fight for long, if indeed he was called upon to fight at all. After the occupant had entrenched itself in Poland he had to demonstrate loyalty to the new authority, and as a man of German roots recognized as a German he was employed in the municipal office in nearby Rzgów. Gerhard concentrated on work and on supporting his family. He married Erna Byczyńska from Sługocin in the municipality of Lądek, and here once more the Byczyński family steps onto the stage.

Gerhard is probably the most tragic figure in the Beutler family. In 1945 he packs his belongings onto a cart — his family, his wife, his year-and-a-half-old little son Baldur, his nine-month-old little daughter Edith — and together with the family of his brother-in-law Leo Byczyński they set off westward. It was 25 January 1945, in the locality of Małe Cieśle, when their little caravan was stopped by men hostilely disposed towards them. Although everyone on the cart spoke Polish — Gerhard may even have mentioned his service in the Polish Army — the men attacked. Gerhard received a blow of a hand axe to the head and died on the spot; his brother-in-law Leo Byczyński was struck by two bullets in the larynx and died on 3 February in the hospital in Konin. The murderers, fortunately, did no harm to the women and children.

The women, in turn — as far as I know from the stories of the already almost legendary Erna of the Byczyńskis, as my aunt Aurelia of the Duszyńskis, Dogoda, used to say — it was under Erna's leadership that they turned the carts around and reached home. Gerhard's body was left in the field where he fell, lightly covered with snow. There was no room on the cart; it was taken up by the women with the children, and after the whole incident the gravely wounded Leo had joined them besides. Gerhard's body was never found; the locals told that it had been thrown into a great 
<!-- s. 127 [PDF 127] -->
 common grave in which the murdered and the fallen were buried. Erna née Byczyńska Beutler was a very resourceful woman. She had no choice; she had to ensure the family's survival. Cruel fate, in effect, shaped her itself. In the area, among the older members of the community, she was remembered as a woman who could arrange anything, obtain anything — an efficient organizer and trader. Parcels sent by relatives from the West made it considerably easier for Erna to support herself and her children.

At last it is time to turn to the next of the siblings, the peer of Polish independence, the person whom I had the pleasure of knowing and thanks to whom the history of the Beutler family can be told in this book, for the information contained here comes mainly from her and from her daughter Lili Vogt. Gertruda Beutler was born, like almost all of Adolf Beutler's children, in Dąbrowica, on 26.12.1918. Looking at one of the earliest photographs, in which Gertruda stands on a chair beside her elder sister Ella, on the occasion of Ella's confirmation in 1922, it is hard not to conclude that this is a joyful child, with lively eyes full of vigour and warmth. It is surely the Gertruda I had the occasion to know. Her exuberance, her will to live and to fight radiated already from that childish figure, as from the very mature woman I came to know. Gertruda grew up in Dąbrowica and, like most children from Evangelical families, was taught respect for work. She performed various tasks connected with work on the farm. As the last and youngest of the daughters still remaining at home, it was decided to educate her all the more carefully. At first she was sent to school in Barłogi, then in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry — for the duration of her schooling she lived with her grandfather Christian Münch — where the teacher was Julius Bauer from Łódź, who paid particular attention to the teaching of penmanship, the so-called Gothic script. From her mother she learned embroidery, sewing and good manners — as a girl from a respectable house she was to be a good match among the local youth. As a young woman, at a hunting party, she came to know Reinhard Schwemmer from Zarzewek, four years her senior, a young miller. The two fell in love and soon married, their wedding taking place in Zagórów in the Evangelical church on 12.10.1940. The bride's 
<!-- s. 128 [PDF 128] -->
 brother, Arwid, remembered that it was a large wedding — nineteen carriages drove from Dąbrowica to the church in Zagórów, and besides that a small bus carrying from Łódź the family of the bride's father. There were about a hundred wedding guests in Dąbrowica, and very fine cakes for the occasion were baked by the neighbour and friend of the Schwemmer family, Rudolf Tews. After the wedding the newlyweds settled in Zarzewek, where Reinhard took over the mill and the folwark from his father Edmund Schwemmer, who had died in 1939. Mrs Gertruda worked as a bookkeeper in the office at the mill. During the war, and already after its end, the children of Gertruda of the Beutlers and Reinhard of the Schwemmers were born: Richard, Edmund, Roman, Lila, Edwin and Renata. To this day Gertruda and her husband are remembered in Zarzewek and the vicinity very warmly. They harmed no one, and no neighbour harmed them — after all, they were their own; they were at home.

The Waleriańczyk family from Zastruże recalls that during the war, as children, they often received from Mrs Schwemmer flour and semolina beyond the ration-card allotment, by which the miller's wife exposed herself to consequences from the German authorities. When in 1945 Gertruda too was arrested and spent six months in prison in Konin, after which her trial as a "German woman" was to take place, Mrs Waleriańczyk testified in favour of Gertruda Schwemmer, deposing: Mrs Schwemmer saved my children from death by starvation. This testimony made it possible to release Gertruda from prison. Fate, at the beginning of the married life of the Schwemmer couple, painfully tested the couple, for their firstborn son Richard died at barely three months of age. The doctor brought from Zagórów kept watch over the dying child all night, but the boy could not be saved. Moments of terror were experienced by Gertruda Schwemmer in 1945, when Soviet soldiers burst into their house in Zarzewek, and in the flat there were only she and a neighbour. The fate of thousands of women, especially of German origin, who during the war were brutally raped and often murdered, would have awaited them. They were saved by a Soviet officer who took over part of the house for his quarters. He rescued them almost at the last moment. But already in January 1945 the whole Schwemmer family was 
<!-- s. 129 [PDF 129] -->
 evicted from the folwark in Zarzewek and, after several "halts" (for several nights they slept on the floor at the Lorentz family's in Zarzewo, until that family too was thrown out of its house, then in Grodziec, next in Dąbrówka near Świebodzin at the parents of Gertruda, Emilia and Adolf Beutler, to which they had been evicted from Dąbrowica), already in 1948 the Schwemmers settled in Skulsk. During the time of their wanderings they performed the forced labour imposed by the new masters of these lands, the communists — they worked for lodging and food. In Skulsk Reinhard Schwemmer, as a skilled craftsman, managed a mill for one Domiński, who together with the UB made the family's departure for Germany difficult, because Domiński needed a good craftsman at the mill, while the UB in turn took the stance: the Germans destroyed Poland, so now they must rebuild it.

In later years the Schwemmers applied for permission to leave Poland, but did not receive it. The authorities of the time refused Gertruda in 1972 even the journey to the funeral of her mother, Emilia Beutler, who since 1958 had been living with her husband in Burgdorf. Reinhard Schwemmer died in Skulsk in 1984 after a long illness, never again seeing many of his relatives and in-laws alive. Only in 1992 did Gertruda, already 74 years of age, herself decide to move to Berlin, where her two daughters had been living for years. The great courage of this frail woman, her incredible will to live and inexhaustible vitality allowed her to acclimatize quickly in a foreign country. An advantage impossible to overestimate, and one that facilitated her good integration in Berlin, was her excellent command of German, to a degree as good as her command of Polish. Polish, after all, must be regarded as her mother tongue, and when one conversed with Mrs Gertruda one could have the impression that she thought even in Polish, despite occasionally slipping in some German phrase. An example of her attachment to the language was, for instance, the fact that she replaced the unpronounceable Asian name of the caregiver at the senior citizens' home where Gertruda lived in the last months of her life with the homely Celinka, and addressed her so. The next son of Adolf and Emilia, Arnulf Beutler, was born in 1923, also in Dąbrowica. During 
<!-- s. 130 [PDF 130] -->
 the war he was a baker in Rzgów. After a fortunate departure from Poland, already after the end of the war, he reached Canada, where he founded and ran until his retirement a large bakery in Midland in Ontario, which was then taken over by his son. Arnulf and his wife Lydia had three sons. A few years ago a tornado severely damaged the bakery's buildings, and the enormous costs of repair meant that Gary Beutler had to turn to something entirely different. It was Arnulf who, right after the war, began the search for his brother Alfred, of whom the family had had no news from the moment he was taken to the front. Arnulf Beutler died at the age of 71 in 1994. His wife Lydia, born in 1926, lives in Canada to this day. The penultimate child of Adolf and Emilia was Arwid Beutler, born in Dąbrowica in 1925. In him, as in the next member of the Beutler clan, there awoke the need for a change of place, a change of surroundings. He left the family home at the age of fourteen; he stayed in Germany, the USA, then again in Germany and once more in the USA, where in Florida he was a pilot. He then worked in an aircraft factory, and there, as the result of an unfortunate accident, he lost one eye. Apparently unable to warm a lasting place for himself anywhere, he emigrated to Canada, where he settled near his brother Arnulf and where, not far from his sister-in-law Lydia, he lived until his death in February 2020. When he left the family home, he had no contact with the family — above all because the war had scattered them across the world, and he himself, too, travelled a great deal after it, seeking for himself a place on earth. Gertruda and he found each other through their sister-in-law Lydia on 2 January 2014, after 75 years. Arwid telephoned Gertruda and, after dozens of years, they spoke for the first time by phone. Enormous emotions accompanied it. From that time on the siblings spoke with each other regularly.

The youngest child of the farmer Adolf Beutler of Dąbrowica was Edmund Beutler, born, like his siblings, precisely on that beautifully situated farm in Dąbrowica, not far from the bank of the Warta, in 1929. In that same year Ludwik Beutler, Adolf's father, died; he had been spending the last years of his life with his son Ewald in Koło. There, too, ten years later, his wife Emilia Beutler née Herrmann died. But 
<!-- s. 131 [PDF 131] -->
 to return to Edmund. His sister Gertruda, eleven years older, very often looked after him, as the only daughter of the family still then living at home — the more so since Emilia Beutler née Münch, their mother, as an independent seamstress and besides that a housekeeper, was often busy with sewing, with the workers she employed, or with contacts with her clients. And so in his childhood Edmund thought that it was precisely Gertruda who was his mamma.

Warm and affectionate relations between the two lasted throughout Edmund's life; he died in Germany, in Mondorf, in 2013. In January 1945 it was not granted to the elder Beutlers, Adolf and Emilia, either to remain on the farm they had built with toil and sweat. Like the majority of citizens recognized by the German occupiers as Germans, and after the war once more recognized as such by the new people's authorities, they were dispossessed and evicted. Adolf was arrested for signing the Volksliste (the German Nationality List) and imprisoned in Warsaw, where he remained for three years. During that time his wife Emilia wandered from relative to relative; at first she lived with her daughter-in-law, Erna née Byczyńska Beutler, where eight persons had to fit into a single room. In 1948 Adolf was released from prison. Until 1958 they lived in Dąbrówka Mała in Lubusz Voivodeship, dwelling in a former manor house that had been converted into flats for the office workers of the PGR (state farm) created on the site of the former estate. In 1957 Adolf and Emilia still celebrated their golden wedding anniversary in Poland, and as early as the following year, together with their youngest son Edmund and his freshly wedded wife Selma, they left for Germany, settling in Burgdorf in Niedersachsen, where Adolf died in 1962, and Emilia ten years later, in 1972.

After twenty-five years spent in Germany, Gertruda all the while gladly returned to Poland. Here she visited her son's grave in Zarzewek, her husband's and her sister Natalia's at the Evangelical cemetery in Konin. Here, in this earth, lie the bones of many of her ancestors and relatives, and in this earth she herself at last came to rest. Polish habits, some sayings, diminutives, 
<!-- s. 132 [PDF 132] -->
 traditions, but above all tastes, remained with her forever. In 2017 Gertruda visited Poland for the last time, on account of her advanced age. It was the final journey of the peer of independent Poland to her homeland. Whenever her daughter Lila, living in Berlin, visited Poland, she invariably had to bring her mother some Polish delicacies: as a rule żur, grated beets, Polish apples, a bit of genuine sausage. Those were the tastes of Gertruda Schwemmer's childhood. Today, all over the world, further generations of the Beutler clan are being born and growing up; despite many storms and misfortunes, the family endures still. Gertruda could well be the personification of the history of the whole clan. As the words of the song in the opening credits of one Polish television series say: "every family is like a tree — it sways, it breaks, time changes it" — but in spite of everything it endures. Gertruda, like the Beutler family across the centuries, experienced years of plenty but also years of leanness, many successes and joys, but also many misfortunes and cares, and yet in spite of all she endured, not surrendering and showing inexhaustible energy and an appetite for life. With her a piece of the history of the Hauländers of the Konin and Zagórów region has passed away.

<!-- s. 133 [PDF 133] -->
### Letters of a Wehrmacht soldier discovered in the wall of the cantor's house


Mirosław Słowiński

German settlers admitted under Hauländer law in the villages and forest settlements surrounding the town of Zagórów formed a closed community which, despite two hundred years of dwelling on these lands, was not susceptible to assimilation. A strong and probably decisive bond was the Lutheran religion and the German language. They were all free people, whose relations with the landed proprietors who leased them poor grounds were regulated by separate laws, customs and self-government. That was so in the eighteenth-century Commonwealth, under the partitions and in reborn Poland. As one of the then inhabitants of Zagórów recalls – Lech Słowiński, a professor at the Poznań university: "In many villages right up to 1939 one heard only German. It must nevertheless be admitted that until then coexistence between the Polish and German populations was, on the whole, correct"[^50].

The largest settlement in this ring of homesteads was Łazińsk – situated eight kilometres south of Zagórów. To this day it is a place that perfectly illustrates the manner of organising, under Hauländer law, the space entrusted to the settlers: the siting of the farmsteads, the building of roads and drainage canals. As early as 1839 the first brick building stood here, in which the cantor resided and school lessons were held. A hundred 


[^50]: Słowiński Lech, Mniej i bardziej ciekawe przypadki mojego życia. (Wspomnienia). Poznań 2000 (typescript).

    Lech Słowiński – born in Zagórów on 23.09.1922 – died 29.07.2005 in Poznań. Professor of the Adam Mickiewicz University in Poznań. I am publishing the memoirs thanks to the kind consent of the closest Family. The typescript is kept in the home archive.

<!-- s. 134 [PDF 134] -->
 years later, through the efforts of the whole community of Łazińsk, whose works were directed from 1925 by the cantor Gustav Adolf Mielke, a new building was erected, housing classrooms, an office and the dwelling of the headmaster and cantor in one person. Yet Gustav Mielke's engagement did not concern only educational matters and religious duties. He was a highly active propagandist of German nationalists and of Nazi propaganda. This activity – even for such a multicultural region – must have aroused serious reservations on the part of the authorities, for Mielke was forced to leave Łazińsk in 1936 and settle somewhere in Galicia. He returned together with the Wehrmacht in 1939. Throughout the war the school, a greatly curtailed kantorate and the Mielke family were housed in this building, sizeable by village standards.

One must also record further sentences from the memoirs quoted above. "The aggressiveness of the local Germans towards the Poles and towards the third nation that had settled in large numbers in Zagórów since old times – the Jews – would reveal itself only with unprecedented cruelty in the period of the Hitlerite occupation"[^51]. The entry of the Russians on 18 January 1945 and the rebuilding of the Polish administration put an end to the long history of this kantorate. All the farmers were expropriated in April of that year. Eugenia Plitt, an inhabitant of Łazińsk, remembered that at first it was said that this was a temporary measure… Years passed and the former German school and kantorate were adapted into a residential building for teachers. Four flats were partitioned off. In 1993 the couple Dorota and Bogdan Karaszewski received the allocation of one of the premises. It was also the time when cars with German registration plates began to appear in Łazińsk and on the nearby Kopczyk, their passengers trying to document the former homesteads. Not all the locals liked these visits. Some would even call the police to check the identity of the visitors and ask what purpose their walks around the local farmsteads served.



[^51]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

<!-- s. 135 [PDF 135] -->

Bogdan Karaszewski decided with his wife that the flat allocated to them required a substantial renovation. Possessing numerous skills that come in useful in such work, he set about the job in 1995. The scope of the work must have been considerable, since the interior doors also qualified for replacement. Throwing out a door together with its frame – so that the ceiling did not come down on one's head – requires a certain experience. The operation succeeded, and the extracted, rotted frame revealed an unexpected sight. It turned out that this partition between the rooms, which everyone had taken to be a single, monolithic wall, was built of two walls, between which there was a considerable space. When the dust settled, there appeared before Bogdan Karaszewski a sizeable pile of papers sealed up tight. Someone had carefully secured them and cleverly hidden them. The first packet brought out contained many letters stamped with the field post of the Wehrmacht as well as a Book of Baptisms. Further on lay wrapped coverings of church altars and carpenter's tools. When the find had been taken out, books began to slide down from above through the narrow gap into the empty space… This collection of remarkable books "falling" downwards was opened by a biography of Joseph Goebbels. In all, thirty-one volumes came into Karaszewski's hands. Apart from three gnawed and probably mouse-damaged ones, most of the books survived in good condition, with the marks of time visible on the covers or pages.

The finder, in his spare moments, tried to interest various people and institutions in the discovered materials, but without much success. Great respect is due to the way Bogdan Karaszewski handled the find brought out from the wall. Everything was packed into a cardboard box and placed in a safe spot. Thus almost 25 years passed. In the meantime the family changed flats. An announcement placed on the internet seeking materials for a history of the Zagórów Evangelicals, the old settlers from Germany, did not escape Karaszewski's notice. One October day in 2020 he walked into the Zagórów House of Culture and handed over to its director, Łukasz Parus, this entire collection, untouched by the years.

<!-- s. 136 [PDF 136] -->

It is the end of 1944. As the east wind blows, one can hear the approaching front, the thunder of artillery salvoes. Despite the assurances coming from Berlin radio, it was known that the entry of the Russians was a matter of weeks. We do not know which member of the Mielke family was at home at that time (from various indirect pieces of information one may conjecture that it was one of the elder sisters, Leokadia). Preparing the cache in the gap between the walls required time, technical skill and ingenuity. The hiding place would surely never have been discovered had it not been for the decision to replace the doors. Most probably the objects being hidden were lowered in from above, from the attic, and then the whole gap was carefully plugged.

The packed collection consisted to a great extent of letters from the front written by the younger son of Julius Samuel Mielke – Bruno. But there were also letters and cards written to Bruno by the father, the brother Gustav, the sisters Leokadia (Kadzia), Anni and Olga. Besides this, many loose letters (63 in all), 43 postcards and 18 documents from the years 1936-1939, such as, for example, the permit of the Konin starosta of 26 September 1939 (!) for Gustav Adolf Mielke to move freely around Konin county. From the reading one may infer that the siblings exchanged photographs, including those from the front. None of the photographs mentioned was in the cache. More interesting is the question of how the letters of the father and the siblings sent to the Eastern Front came to be among these documents? Did Bruno leave them in Łazińsk while on leave? The surviving Book of Baptisms (*Taufe*) covers the period from 1912 to 1918 in Łazińsk. It was kept by the then cantor E. Cellmer.

The collection of books whose preservation was attempted may be helpful in describing the family that lived in the kantorate. The titles alone say a great deal. *Biographie Joseph Goebbels* by Wilfrid Bade, Lübeck 1938, *Die Nationalversammlung von Potsdam. Deutschlands große Tage. 21 bis 23 März 1933* by Hans Wendt, Berlin 1933 [The National Assembly in Potsdam. Germany's Great Days], *Deutsche Geschichte im 19. Jahrhundert* by Heinrich von Treitschke with an introduction by Alfred Rosenberg, Safari – Berlin 1937 [A History of Germany in the Nineteenth Century], *Der deutsche Aufbruch 9.11.1918* 
<!-- s. 137 [PDF 137] -->




Nazi publications discovered in the wall of the house of the cantor Gustav Mielke in Łazińsk.


Photo: Mirosław Słowiński



<!-- s. 138 [PDF 138] -->
 *bis 18.3.1938* by Walther Gehl [The German Awakening], *Deutsche Haltung vor Fremden* by Bruno Behm, Graz 1943 [The German Attitude towards Foreigners].


A few words about these items and their authors. *Deutschlands große Tage* is a little book describing the assembly convoked after the elections, which took place on 21 March 1933 in the garrison church in Potsdam, at which Adolf Hitler was entrusted with the formation of a government. Earlier, on 15 March, the victorious leader of the NSDAP had proclaimed the founding of the Third Reich and deprived his political opponents of their parliamentary mandates. Walther Gehl (1897-1942), one of the authors of books who fought with pen and weapon alike, was at the same time working "on the spiritual development of the Wehrmacht". Among other things he was the author of *Der nationalsozialistische Staat* (1933) and of numerous history textbooks for secondary school pupils. He fell in June 1942 on the Eastern Front. Bruno Behm (1892-1974), in turn, was an Austrian writer of extreme right-wing origins with a very eventful biography. He made his name with the statement that "If the Jews complain to the whole world of their lot, one must tell them that they brought that lot upon themselves". But the weightiest volume in genre terms is the book *Deutsche Geschichte im 19. Jahrhundert* by Heinrich von Treitschke (1834-1896), the author of a famous essay of 1879 which provoked a broad anti-Semitic response. It is in that text that the sentence appears: "*Die Juden sind unser Unglück*" (The Jews are our misfortune). The Nazis editing "Der Stürmer" took this sentence as the motto of the paper and… of their actions. The late author, however, had no influence on whom the publisher would invite to write the introduction to the next edition. It was Alfred Rosenberg, the creator of a criminal ideology, whose *Der Mythus des 20 Jahrhunderts* (The Myth of the Twentieth Century), alongside Adolf Hitler's *Mein Kampf*, played a decisive role in formatting German minds in the spirit of the racial superiority of the master race, for whom an appropriate living space was to be conquered. (By 1944 a million copies had been sold).

The library also contained books about important historical figures, whose selection – I believe – was not accidental. This includes the well-known study 
<!-- s. 139 [PDF 139] -->
 by the outstanding Belgian classical philologist Joseph Bidez, *Julian der Abtrünnige*, Munich 1940. Julian the Apostate was the emperor who strove to abolish Christianity and restore the old Roman religious cults. It would be hard to find a clearer analogy with the Nazi idea of Germania. The collection also included a work by Leopold von Ranke, *Geschichte Wallensteins*. The history of an outstanding commander of the Thirty Years' War. There is also a history of the King of Prussia, Frederick William IV. In the homestead tucked away in the woods there was the popular novel by Wilhelm Raabe, *Die Chronik der Sperlingsgasse*, 1857 (The Chronicle of Sparrow Lane). It is the story of a small-town loser who knows how to preserve his own personality in his oddities, enabling him to cope with an aggressive reality. This "hero of grey life" brings valuable humanist ideals into the cold, capitalist society…[^52].



[^52]: *Mały słownik pisarzy niemieckich, austriackich i szwajcarskich*. Warszawa 1973, p. 286.

[^53]: *Schlag nach über England* – a guide to England and the British Empire. On the back cover there is an announcement of a new title: *Großdeutschland–Polen–Westrußland*. ***Mit Demarkationsline vom 22.09.1939***. Maßstab 1:4 500 000. Format 60 × 47cm. Sechsfarbig. Diese neue Karte bringt in einem einzigartigen Ausschnitt den mitteleuropäischen 
<!-- s. 140 [PDF 140] -->

Among the books there are, of course, items such as the Holy Scripture of the Old and New Testament (*Die Bibel oder die ganze Heilige Schrift des Alten und Neuen Testaments*, Vienna 1886). The Bible – in accordance with old tradition – was a wedding present for Samuel Julius Mielke and Natalie Tonn in the year 1892. The volume contains, before the title page, pages for keeping a family chronicle with handwritten entries of the recipients, which I write about further on. Among the books are two collections of sermons, *Rozważania na każdy dzień roku* [Devotions for Every Day of the Year], a New Testament in a pocket edition and other mystical, religious works. There are guides to married and sexual life (*Liebe und Ehe. Ein Buch Für Eheleute* and *Das Liebes und Geschlechtsleben des Menschen*) and hymnals for festive occasions. There is also *Schlag nach über England* – a guide to England and the British Empire. Presumably published with an eye to compatriots preparing to visit the island after a victorious invasion…[^53] It is difficult to enumerate all these items in detail.

It is worth returning once more to the political part of the library collection and devoting a few words to Goebbels's biographer. Wilfrid Alfred Karl Bade (born 1906) belonged to the very active figures in Nazi propaganda. He was one of the leading lecturers and "creative interpreters" of the thoughts and ideas of the Führer and of his chief Goebbels. Besides the biography of Goebbels first published in 1933, he authored among other works such titles as *Die SA erobert Berlin* (The SA Conquers Berlin), *Geschichte des Dritten Reich 4 Bünde* (A History of the Third Reich in 4 Volumes), *Das Hohelied vom Dritten Reich* (The Song of Songs of the Third Reich) and *Tod Und Leben. Verse des Krieges* (Death and Life. Verses of the War). Bade died in December 1945 in Russian captivity in Kaunas. Yet more important than the author of the biography is its subject – Paul Joseph Goebbels himself. The doctorate in German philology which he received at the university in Heidelberg in 1922 was an academic confirmation of the intellectual prowess of the future minister of propaganda. His fanatical service to the Führer, his undoubted oratorical talent and his path to the summits of power must have made a very strong impression on these "well-read" inhabitants of the kantorate in Łazińsk.

If someone preparing for flight takes care of his little library, then it must have meant something to that family! Why was this cache created at all?! Out of fear lest the collection of letters from the front of a son and brother should fall into the hands of soldiers, thus giving a pretext for a harsh reckoning with the family? Was the Goebbels biography and the other books that the household regarded as important, because they constituted their national-socialist ideological identity, not put into the cache for the same reason? Were the remaining volumes hidden out of fear that the Russians or the Poles would burn them – just as the works cursed by that same Goebbels were burned? In one of the memoirs we read that the farmers being expelled in April were told that this was a temporary operation. The question is who said this: representatives of the new administration or local Nazi leaders who believed in the Führer's wonder weapon?


 Raum von Moskau bis Brüssel und von Helsingfors bis Bukarest. Preis 90 Pf.) As can be seen, the publisher had been well prepared by the propaganda ministry to illustrate the "implementation" of the secret provisions of the Ribbentrop–Molotov pact of 23 August 1939.

<!-- s. 141 [PDF 141] -->

If one leaves documents important to the family in a house one is forced to abandon, then presumably somewhere at the bottom of the soul a little flame of hope burns that perhaps this is only a painful moment, that soon we shall return to the family nest and to the land that fed us for decades. But – and this question also arises – does the protecting of the writings of Rosenberg, Goebbels or other authors like them not testify that the terrible defeat which the expellees and all of Germany are experiencing has taught them nothing? Are they unable, or do they not wish, to abandon the idea of "their" great Third Reich?

#### Shew me thy ways, O Lord (Ps 25,4)[^54]

The letters of Bruno and to Bruno found in the wall of the house in Łazińsk cover the period from 14 February 1940 to 14 May 1944. Bruno Edwin Mielke was born on 8 September 1911 in Białołęka[^55]. On 28 June 1932 he became a graduate of the State Teachers' Seminary with German as the language of instruction in Łódź. Mielke 


[^54]: All quotations from Holy Scripture come from: Pismo Święte Starego i Nowego Testamentu (Biblia Warszawska), Warsaw 1975.

[^55]: In the hiding place there was a picture postcard from pastor Stephen Glumpert addressed to Bruno Edwin Mielke, informing him that in Radzymin, in the official registers, the birth record 1911/120 of Bruno Edwin Mielke had been preserved, and that for a fee of 6.00 zł he could receive a copy. Bruno Mielke had numerous siblings. Preserved in the Holy Scripture (which the future parents Samuel Julius Mielke and Natalie Tonn received as a wedding present on 26 June 1892) is a list of brothers and sisters with dates of birth: Emil Leonhard – 1893, Leokadia – 1895, Florentina Amalie – 1897, Gustav Adolf – 1899, Olga Johanna – 1901, Apolonia Dorota – 1905, Hugo Georg – 1906, Oswald Julius – 1909, Bruno Edwin – 1911, Jerene Anna Natalie – 1914.

    *Die Bibel oder die ganze Heilige Schrift des Alten Und Neuen Testaments*, Wien. Verlag der britischen und ausländischen Bibel Geselschaft. The Bible volume, before the main text, contained the inscription *Zum Andenken ihrer ehelichen Verbindung den 14/26 Juni 1892 in Pułtusk Natalie und Samuel Julius verehelichten Mielke* and an entry concerning the bride's father. The following pages are the *Familien – Chronik* devoted to the grandparents, and the page *Kinder*, on which the above-cited list of the children of the Mielke couple is found.

<!-- s. 142 [PDF 142] -->
 writes in his letters of 1940 that it was the State German Teachers' Seminary in Łódź. This erasure of Poland from biographies and from pre-war times will be a characteristic lacuna in many records. The dream which runs through many of his surviving letters – including those from the front – was for Bruno to take up studies at the Evangelical-Theological Faculty of the University of Vienna. Worried whether he would manage on the demanding course of studies, he describes his preparation thus: "I have an education as a teacher (also in music), but unfortunately I know no foreign language (apart from Polish, which I have mastered well orally and in writing). But I suppose Greek and Latin cannot be done without […] As for abilities, I am indeed no genius, yet – as the former headmaster wrote on my certificate – I am diligent, because I, despite the lack of previous preparation (the headmaster's assessment), with God's help did not have to repeat a single subject. I am already 29 years old, still a bachelor, but I have a youthful appearance and disposition (so I am often judged)".

But 22 June 1941 came. Hitler opens a new front. He attacks his recent "ally" – the USSR. A letter of 26 June 1941 to Bruno's sister – Flora – from the Lehmann family of Dresden has survived. They thank her for the parcel with sausage and other foodstuffs. They mention in passing that the nearest meeting of the Women's League "wants to thank our dear Führer for the fact that he attacked at the right time, for one need only think what would have happened had it been otherwise. At the very beginning Russian hordes would have flooded over you, and last week we thought of Łazińsk very often". That the Russians might appear in Dresden was excluded in advance.

No one yet knows what terrible consequences Hitler's decision will bring. Bruno Mielke begins to realise that this war may freeze his plans and dreams for a long time. In a letter dated 8 July 1941 he informs his sisters Kadzia and Flora that he is cramming Latin "for Vienna". He holds to his intention, even if in August or later he goes into the Wehrmacht. That he cannot become a teacher, 
<!-- s. 143 [PDF 143] -->





Pages of the family chronicle before the title page of the Bible of Natalie and Samuel Mielke. A commemorative photograph of the wedding guests – the participants unknown. From the collection discovered in the wall of the cantor's house in Łazińsk.


Photo: Mirosław Słowiński


<!-- s. 144 [PDF 144] -->
 "the Antichrist is to blame". He also learned from his sisters that his comrade Rentz had fallen. "Again tears and suffering". He will remember him well also because he received from him a keepsake – a photograph of the Führer.


In a letter to the dean of the Evangelical-Theological Faculty of the University of Vienna he writes: "Dear Mister Dean! The time of the autumn semester will soon come. I would gladly begin my studies this autumn (for my intention of coming to Vienna has become a certainty). But unfortunately the Wehrmacht and the war will probably claim me. For the moment I have not yet been called up, but I have recently received a summons to be in readiness, so that I expect the order to report any day. As soon as I am released from military service after the end of the war, I shall want – should God preserve me in life and health – to lay down my office and come to Vienna. If, according to God's will, I were to fall in the war, then my brother will inform you of it". This letter, dated 10 October 1941, was written by Bruno Mielke in Konary, in the commune of Grodziec, where he worked as a teacher. Earlier, from 1940, there survives a document signed by him as teacher and lower school commissioner in Maślaki, in the commune of Wilczyn. It is a letter to the Konin starosta documenting the need to build new school buildings in Maślaki and Lipnica. The inhabitants – I quote: "with the wójt at their head consider it obvious to build a school worthy of the New Germany and are ready to give financial help".

It may be worth mentioning at this point that both Maślaki and Konary were, from the end of the eighteenth century, localities that came into being as a result of contracts concluded by the local Polish landowners with German settlers of the Protestant faith. Over more than a hundred years these localities became important regional centres of the Evangelical community. Earlier, Bruno Mielke had for five years been helping Aleksander Gross, the parish priest of the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Zagórów, and had worked as a teacher and cantor in nearby Anielewo. The last months of 1941 were a time of mounting expectation of call-up and departure for the front. In one of the cards we find this entry: "I would like to settle 
<!-- s. 145 [PDF 145] -->
 all my visits as far as possible now, because from August I must be on the alert: at any moment I could then be called up to the Wehrmacht, for I am recognised as 'indispensable' only until 31.7.41. In truth I am glad that in this great time I shall take part in the struggle.
I had already wanted to volunteer".

Bruno tries to put some of his affairs in order and writes an application for leave so as to be able to go to Konin and visit Łazińsk. In the letter of 10 October he shares with his brother Gustav the news he obtained during some course at an NSDAP school in Będlewo near Stęszew. It turns out that an official subordinate to the Reich Governor expounded at length before the course participants visions of allocating land to village schools, in this way to encourage teachers to settle in the countryside. Bruno Mielke considers that "it is obvious that proper schools must be built in the Wartheland and that land must not be stinted after the war with Russia". And here follows a longer exposition of the advantages of such a solution as opposed to taking over complete village farms, which would be subject to far more severe regulations and production obligations. Foreign (read: Polish) farmhands cause the farmers particularly many troubles. Hence he urgently urges his brother to take a prompt interest in the possibility of obtaining such a grant – preferably near Zagórów or Konin. He writes that Polish owners used to be there. "I hope that not everything has yet been divided up". In this entire – one of the longest – letters there is not a crumb of reflection that he is urging his brother to take part in the plunder of the land and houses of those who for years were their neighbours. Mielke is undoubtedly a religious man. Yet one gets the impression that in relations with Poles some of the Ten Commandments had been suspended.

The autumn months of 1941 were a terrible time on this land. From September to November the liquidation of the Zagórów ghetto was under way. The Jews are transported in lorries to the forests near Kazimierz Biskupi (the Wygoda reserve), vehicles in which they are suffocated with exhaust fumes. Those brought there alive are shot or thrown alive into pits with lime. The letters from this period are few and are the last before Bruno Mielke's 
<!-- s. 146 [PDF 146] -->
 conscription into the Wehrmacht. Of the tragedy taking place in those days in Zagórów we find not the slightest mention.

#### While I live will I praise the Lord (Ps 146,2)

The first of the surviving letters from the time of service in the Wehrmacht was written in Vienna on 30 March 1942. Called up to the army, he sets off with the other recruits on a long journey. First Chodzież, then Kutno, Poznań and on to Wrocław. From there, after eight hours, "we arrived safely in Vienna – the city of my dreams. […] Our last destination, where we shall be trained, is Klagenfurt, about 15 km from the border with Italy, in the south of Austria. It will surely be beautiful there in the Alps, it was already so during the journey. Service in the mountains can be exhausting, for we have been assigned to a unit of *Gebirgsjäger* [mountain rangers]. Everything was decided, there was no question of any choice; besides, every thing and every group has its good and bad sides. It is best to accept what has been given, then one need have no reproaches later". In the margin we read an addition: "In the end we arrived. Everything is very beautiful and proper. I feel as if I were in the dormitory of some seminary. The officers are kind. We are given everything". He need not have taken so many things. And further: "And now my comrades. Most of them are ethnic Germans (without citizenship) and *Bessaraber*, some are also Germans with citizenship. Most are weak, some quite incapable, and among them there are persons who exploit others. If they manage, then I surely will. That there are persons among them who exploit others is not consoling, for, as is known, the whole group suffers because of it".

Gustav wrote a long letter to his brother in training on 1 May. "During all the work one thought now wearies me – it appears since you have been far away. The will to work that I had when you were still here I no longer have. During the lessons you appear every moment in my thoughts. And when the break comes and I look through the window – then too I see you. You were always near, and now you are suddenly so far away – 


Letters of Bruno, Gustav and the Mielke sisters. A (probable) photograph from the wedding of Anni Mielke in Łazińsk. Found in the wall of the cantor's house in Łazińsk.


Photo: Aneta Węcławiak

<!-- s. 147 [PDF 147] -->



<!-- s. 148 [PDF 148] -->
 to this is added the worry where you will be in a few weeks. […] Apart from you I have no one. I have no friends at all, our girls themselves need support, and father… you know after all – he sleeps and eats, and reads all the time. But he is well. Without you I am rather lonely and would best like to leave here too – to where you are and where you expose yourself to dangers". If only he got a leave, he would set off immediately for Klagenfurt.


At present he is feverishly busy putting affairs in order. A few days ago they (the year-classes 1899 to 1895) received a summons to register for the duty of defence. "So on 28 April we were at the Commune Office and registered ourselves. I, Mundt, Grunwald Heinrich, Schmechel, Stolp and many others. This means that we too may be called up to the army in the coming days". In the closing sentences he writes that he does not understand those people who are "happy" when they try to take our faith from us. "I am a Christian and in recent days this has been made felt to me again and again". He could not bear it if some young man tried to arouse disgust for it or harass them – and then misfortune would happen. "I know – you can stomach something like that. But it cannot be tolerated for too long". (This fragment is precisely the contemptuous, indeed aggressive attitude of the adherents of the Nazi ideology towards the Evangelicals, generally Christians faithful to the Church, signalled outright).

After an account of the farm work and the neighbourly help of Stelter, Gustav declares to his brother that this year he has decided to marry. There is, however, one problem: "That Stell behaved earlier as if she did not want to – but now she does everything in that direction. Yet I cannot decide on her, because I believe she is such that discord might arise through her among my remaining siblings, and I do not want that at any price. Another will yet be found. I would most gladly like one with a farm. It grieves me most that you had to become a soldier and are not yet married". He is also very grateful that Bruno wrote a letter to Kadzia. She is at once different when she has read the letter. I tried to tell her so – but with words one cannot always achieve 
<!-- s. 149 [PDF 149] -->
 what one would like. Nor was it simple and pleasant. At the end, the good advice of the elder. He sends a parcel of food. "And may it arrive safely and may it taste good to you and serve your health. Above all you should not go hungry. Of the bread and bacon which I sent today one can cut two slices and put a piece of bacon in the middle, then hide it in a pocket and from time to time gnaw at it. I only fear – for I know your comradeliness and your good heart – that you will at once share everything and keep nothing for yourself. I do that too – but everything has its limits in this world".



Tactical insignia of the 2. Gebirgs-Division.

Bruno writes on 30 May from Innsbruck that they are finishing their training in the Alps and the next day set out for Lapland. (In Innsbruck was stationed the 2nd Mountain Division – 2. Gebirgs-Division – formed on 1 April 1938, and with this formation the war fortunes of the soldier Mielke will be bound up). At the Sunday service many high-ranking officers appeared, and he was also allowed to invite a few *Kameraden* to the church. (Perhaps such ostentatious conduct on the part of the officer corps was connected with the fact that the 2nd Mountain Division was formed from the earlier Austrian 6th Division, in which there were not so many adherents of the new "Germanic religion"). From the newspapers he learns that larger combat operations on the Murmansk front can be expected only in the middle of June. He assumes that they will not go straight to the front line, but will certainly take the place of those who go to the first line. He thinks that the time of an important decision "on the matter of Russia" is approaching. The next fragment, worth recording in full, is a kind of testament which he sends to the family:

"Dear Brother, there is one thing I should like to suggest to you, as man to man: let us not deceive ourselves and let us look the truth in the eye. It is clear (for me and for you) that there now comes for me a stage more or less threatening to life. (That the English have attacked German ships and will go on doing so is probably certain). Our transports are of course well protected. So if anything should happen to me, according to God's will, I beg you to deal in this way with my property in freedom: 1. That morga of meadow near Zagórów should belong to Kadzia; 
<!-- s. 150 [PDF 150] -->
 deal with it so that things may be as good as possible for Kadzia. 2. The money accumulated in the savings bank in my savings account is to be divided as follows: 30% for Kadzia; 20% for Anni; 25% for the church, home and foreign missions; 25% for you, at your free disposal. You will have the ways and means. Of clothes and things, take for yourself what you can use; the rest Kadzia may alter for Father. My 'large furniture' should still serve Father. Dearest Brother, I do not write the above in gloomy forebodings nor out of deep, dark despair and resignation; on the contrary, I feel freer when I know that everything is in order, and therefore I can be at peace".

He writes the letter on the way to the front "on the march" on 10 June 1942. "By God's grace we arrived safely in Finland. The journey by ship passed without problems. The sea was calm and majestic. […] We travelled for two days in Finland by train and found ourselves near a little town close to the Arctic Circle. Now we are to undertake a long march which is to last many weeks". You must understand that for reasons of security we cannot state anything precisely. If – God grant – victory is achieved, then I shall describe and tell you everything in detail". And further about his worries in service. "Some tasks are very difficult – e.g. the marching, for I have always been a stay-at-home and have not grown used to it. But since everyone wants to accomplish it, I too shall not give up.

I shall do everything within my power. Apart from my health and my life I can give nothing else. I shall keep my oath. […] May God grant victory". He cannot give the field post number, for they have not yet reached their destination.
