A letter recounting the company's arrival at its place has happily survived. "With God's help we survived the first stage of the great march to the training ground, where within 6 weeks we are to complete our training. First it was about 300 km, which we covered, doing up to 35 km a day. We usually marched at night, from 20 to 3 in the morning, that is, as the night hours are called here. For we are in such a wonderful place, 
<!-- s. 151 [PDF 151] -->
 where for months there is no night and no dusk. The sun sinks towards the horizon, remains there, to rise majestically again after midnight. All the time during the march you have the impression that you are travelling either in the evening hours or in the early morning. Only my watch told me whether it was evening or morning. Most of the days of the march were, thank God, fair and sunny, and so simply beautiful for walking. Our route led mostly through forest. In Finland there are chiefly forests, but small forests (bare firs, spruces, pines), with bogs and hillocks. One would gladly march if it were not so hard for someone who is not used to it. At the end I almost collapsed, rather my legs refused obedience: they ached, pricked and swelled. Therefore for the last 15 km I rode in a lorry with the other lame ones to the destination; that saved me, otherwise I would have harmed my legs. At the beginning, that is, during the first days of the march, there were no obstacles. Everyone, I too, then went his own way in the quiet night, and each wrestled with his own thoughts, his spirit was somewhere else. I was always with you, my dear ones. Enlivened, I would then picture your house in Łazińsk". In the margin he adds that it is good to know Russian; interpreters with the rank of officer have a much easier life…

He also reassures the family that they are well supplied. Recently they received a temporary borrowed postal code. "You may […] therefore also write to me. Since the code is borrowed, please do not forget to draw a circle (the size of 1 Pfennig) under the sender, without that the letter will not reach me. Address: Soldier Bruno Mielke, postal code: 39 801 Sender: Gustav Mielke. Łazińsk, post office Dromin (Trabczyn) via Konin Wartheland (Germany) and then the circle. Parcels may also be sent, unfortunately only up to 100 g. That is not much. Only what will keep a long time and is light counts"[^56]. Perhaps it is also worth quoting a rather peculiar request: "bake and dry little cakes of flour, which you can dry in a 


[^56]: The postal number denoted Feld-Kammandantur 578 or Ortskommandantur 578, that is, a territorial commandanture not assigned to any specific unit.

<!-- s. 152 [PDF 152] -->
 frying pan or on a baking tray, perhaps with fat. They are sometimes added to broth as well. I should like to dip them in tea or coffee too, and that will taste splendid". He writes that they should try to bake exactly such little cakes as he encloses as a model. "We were given a counted-out portion of these little cakes, of which I took 40 g to eat. Since it is forbidden, I afterwards had unpleasantness, and I should like you to send me some of them".

After the grievous experience of the winter war of 1939/1940 with the Red Army and the loss of 10 per cent of its territory – in October 1940 Finland concludes an agreement on military and economic cooperation with the Third Reich. From that time the Germans transfer their units to the territories of Lapland. In May 1941 Finland officially becomes an ally of the Third Reich. On 25 June it declares war on the USSR. The German-Finnish offensive in the north begins – just as the newspapers had earlier announced! – in June, precisely on 29 June. General Eduard Dietl, commanding the 20th Mountain Army (Armee Lappland), gives the order to attack. The campaign was given the codename *Silberfuchs* (Silver Fox). As the Finns themselves put it, for them it was a war of continuation – they wanted to regain the territories lost to the Russians.

The Germans wanted something more. The main objective of the operation was the port of Murmansk and, in the first place, the severing of the route between Murmansk and Petrozavodsk and Leningrad. For the Red Army the railway line by which military equipment arriving from the USA and England was transported to the port of Murmansk had key strategic importance. Finnish Petsamo, Russian Pieczenga, where large forces were concentrated, is a settlement lying on the river of the same name, which flows into a fjord whose beginning lies 5 km from the Barents Sea.

From later letters we learn that Mielke was stationed a few kilometres from Petsamo – in Parkkino. In this small locality, situated on the river Petsamojoki, sappers built a bridge through which the entire supply of the attacking troops passed. The task of the company in which Bruno Mielke served may have included, among other things, the guarding of this bridge. The attack of the combined Finnish and German formations did not succeed. Already on 17 July the Russians halted 
<!-- s. 153 [PDF 153] -->
 the whole advance. The terrain, too, proved a barrier impossible to overcome. Petsamo is separated from Murmansk by a mere 140 km. The Germans tried yet again to resume operations, but on 22 September the Führer's order came to break off combat operations. (The bridge at Parkkino was destroyed in a daring raid by a Russian aviator on 28 September, thereby cutting off from indispensable supplies the two divisions that remained fighting in the north-east). The front line on this section stabilised until September 1944.

The family followed and deeply experienced the events on the distant, northern wing of the Eastern Front. The scattering of July letters attests to this. Father, sister and brother independently of one another send post full of heartening words. The brother Gustav writes (22 July 1942): "Today I was again in Zagórów with Kadzia (sister Leokadia). Even if in Zagórów one sees and hears more, yet nothing could distract my thoughts and I thought of you all the time. […] How many hundreds of times the question arises how you are faring and why it was precisely you who were transferred so far away". […] The sister Anna with her fiancé Hans are at home in Łazińsk. Hans has two weeks' leave. In general many Łazińsk folk come home often on leave, chiefly those who are stationed in Germany or in France". It pains him that his brother is so far away that there can be no question of leave. On the southern front things go from victory to victory, so perhaps Bruno, crowned by this victory, will soon return home. He also has a question and at the same time a request there and then. "Have you not yet had the opportunity to have yourself photographed in your soldier's uniform? In a helmet and with the other things. We – and I most of all – would be very glad".

Two letters of the father – Samuel Julius Mielke – have survived. The one of 20 April was written on the Führer's birthday and is devoted above all to him. "May the Lord God preserve him in good health for a long time, until he overcomes all evil and we can sing with him the great Hallelujah of peace". He is also troubled by the fact that many of the locals avoid the church. In the second letter (28 July 1942) we read: "Dear Bruno, beloved son of my heart! May these lines find you in the best of health and in true soldierly courage. How gladly would I be there with you, to help you 
<!-- s. 154 [PDF 154] -->
 bear your burdens! But the distance is too great. Talk there with your commanders. I could be used as an interpreter of the Russian language and take upon myself the hardships of the journey". Further on there is already news of the much delayed, wet harvest. The letter ends with the words: "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, the love of God and the communion of the Holy Ghost be with us all. Amen. Jesus works miracles. *Heil Hitler*!".

In the letters from sister Olga and brother-in-law Erwin there are great thanks for the monetary gifts and for the fact that with his own body he shields them and their beloved homeland from the evil enemy. One of the letters opens with an exalted appeal for the help of Christ, through His wounds, passion and death, in that sudden need into which the brother has fallen. The sister believes she has the right to such words, because she and her husband want not only to wear the former *hakenkreuz* (swastika) but also to cultivate it in religion. "May God Almighty send down upon you grace and mercy. Our one most ardent wish is to see you alive and in health when this evil war is over". She briefly describes the christening of the little son, to whom they gave the name Adolf. "The holy baptism was modest, at home". Unfortunately they did not receive permission to kill a pig, but in the church the pastor arranged everything nicely and delivered a beautiful speech. The godparents were Gustav and Leokadia. "We liked it very much". She ends with the information that she has packed a bit of bacon and black bread.
Well baked.

Sister Anna, too, strives to support her brother strongly. "When at the cinema I watch the newsreels and the conquerors of mountains advance, I always think that you too are there. And then I am so proud of you. I know that being at the front does not belong to pleasures, but I would set off at once were I a man. After all, it must be an uplifting feeling, to defend the Fatherland, so that it does not bend before the wild hordes, and its inhabitants from mistreatment. We shall always be grateful to you for it […] we feel with our hearts everything that you physically must endure". And further: "In truth it is senseless for me to write this to you. You have never complained and you are certainly in this respect an exemplary soldier. You were taught obedience and were never spoilt. You also have 
<!-- s. 155 [PDF 155] -->
 a good and enthusiastic heart. That is in fact the only thing needed to receive everything with joy". At the end she writes that she is going with her fiancé Hans to Dresden – she wants to meet his parents and visit that beautiful city. (She is making use of an autumn leave).

A long letter of Bruno to his brother Gustav dated 2 August has survived. It is not a reply to the letters of the father, brother and sisters written in July. They probably crossed in the post. If they reached him at all… For how did they find their way back to Łazińsk? Returned – because there was no longer a unit at the old field post address – or did Bruno bring them back himself in his baggage? But what he writes is to some extent an answer to the family's cares and anxieties connected with the soldierly service of brother and son. He writes that on the 22nd of that month they are to set out for the front. He asks them to hold off sending parcels until they give the front field post number. He does not complain about the supply. "The food too is good. Every day there is meat, or something in the nature of meat. There is also more bread (750 g a day). On Sunday there is even 1/3 of a bar of chocolate and 1/5 l of schnapps. At the front itself the food will not be worse and even better". The soldier young in service ("until the 3rd year of the war I could have remained in the schoolroom") considers that he joined the Wehrmacht at the end – when the fighting is already reaching its culmination. He fears – of course apart from the fighting itself and its possible consequences – the coming winter. "The old front soldiers tell that in the north the hardest was the past winter. They had to freeze then, because there was no time for preparations. For this winter, however, the preparations are already being made in summer – if those men are still alive, then I hope they will not freeze. So it will probably be with us too, although by that time they may be transferred a few more times". Surely the memories of the front soldiers who survived the fighting in mighty frosts remained strongly imprinted in Bruno Mielke's memory. "My hope and my petition is to survive this one winter, and if God grants again the grace of victory, then my hope is not empty. In any case it is beautiful that one of us takes part in it, and for me it is an obvious duty, even if I, with my 31 years, do not entirely understand it! Would you believe that I already feel this?".

<!-- s. 156 [PDF 156] -->

It is worth pausing for a moment over this last sentence. How is it to be understood? As the pride of a soldier who is told that he is fighting for a homeland free of Jews and Russian hordes? Or perhaps in this "feeling", somewhere from the subconscious, there flows the question of what he is really taking part in? He does not write about combat operations – for it is obvious that the censor would never let such a letter pass. But he shows another enemy – nature, which in his account is more dangerous than the adversary. Or does he perhaps also indicate that that adversary has an extraordinarily dangerous ally? In reading letters from the front, the question will always arise. To what extent should they be taken literally, and to what extent should one read what is not there or what was said between the lines? In any case it is visible that the private Mielke has not yet come to know the true war machine grinding human flesh, and with growing apprehension awaits that encounter.

This letter is – in the language of war – loaded with emotions. Above all he writes of his great dream, of which he thinks unceasingly. "…if I return safe and in health, I shall prepare myself for the most beautiful of professions, in order to exercise it with the greatest blessing […] My heart is in any case full of longing for a life devoted by God – and as a pastor I can do that best of all". His thoughts also run to his future wife, who must have views such as his. That is the first condition about which he will first ask her. Does she belong to the Saviour and is she pious? Married life, as he imagines it, must be "a holiness of the Lord and blessed by the nearness of God and of the Spirit".

So too he advises the brother, who in a letter asks his opinion about Truthilde. "Observe her and talk with her thoroughly, how far she is sanctified by Christ". Gustav is 43 years old and, as one can gather from other letters, the search for a candidate for wife has been going on with varying success for some time. Yet the advice of the younger Bruno is invariable – he must know whether she is an inwardly searching, deeply believing soul. It would also be good if she were not ugly.

His thoughts return to the school years in Łazińsk, which were the most sunny and the happiest of all the periods of his life.

<!-- s. 157 [PDF 157] -->

He thinks with gratitude of his parents, who despite their weaknesses gave him what is most precious: "a healthy body and blood with the faith of our dear Evangelical Church", and finally of the wonderful siblings, from whose hearts beats only warm love. In the face of what is inexorably approaching, we read a letter like the life creed of Bruno Mielke and an attempt at an examination of conscience. It is also – or perhaps above all – an unspoken strong reaction to the news reaching them from soldiers returning from the first line.

Letters from the loved ones, such as those quoted earlier and the next one from Anna (of 22 September 1942), which I quote below, undoubtedly had their weight and significance in preserving the psychological stability and resilience of the private Mielke. "I am so proud that you are fighting in this decisive struggle. […] I believe that when a man such as you stands at the front and looks danger straight in the eye, small and insignificant things slip out of the soul, and it then cleanses itself, and afterwards becomes strengthened and proud. When you return, you will certainly be a different man. Take to yourself everything that is precious, and rid yourself of everything else. Conquer yourself; in that way you will return as a man of worth and nothing will be able to move you. I would so much like to shield you from all hardships, but such is the lot of man that he must pass through everything quite alone". Perhaps this girl, writing her letter just before dusk, intuitively felt a certain painful paradox which many soldiers experienced. They fought together, in a squad, in a company. On how they supported one another often depended their very lives, and on the other hand their equally dangerous enemy was the grim and oppressive feeling of loneliness.

Three weeks later the pastor Aleksander Gross writes from Zagórów (13 October 1942) about the service he celebrated on Sunday. "On 27 September we thought in Łazińsk of a prayer of supplication before God, and I recited to the congregation the verse of the Lord as a greeting from the distant front. All were moved at how a man in danger of death remains courageous, because he has entrusted his life to God. Many 
<!-- s. 158 [PDF 158] -->
 thought of their own family members, who likewise somewhere on this long and distant front are performing their heavy service. We pray every Sunday for our members of the armed forces, even if we do not name all of them by name and surname. Our dear brothers may be certain that the community of faith and love for the good Lord bind us to one another, even if thousands of kilometres divide us".

The letters dated December carry the addition: Eastern Front. "Which of us soldiers from the wilderness of Russia would not want to be with his nearest and dearest at Christmas!". "You surely think – he writes on 6 December – that I shall spend the festive days in sadness and dejection. It is the will of God and my superiors who sent me to the front. To oppose this in any form would be to oppose the will of God and of the powers above". Bruno treats himself as an envoy "for the family in the east, and for it and for my people I wish to offer this small sacrifice and be at the front for Christmas". He honestly admits that he has landed well – this is a fairly quiet sector. The Russians have so far attacked very rarely – almost not at all. His service consists in standing 6-8 hours of sentry duty (in pairs), bringing food and fuel and sometimes other things. Apart from that he sleeps, and when the light is good he writes letters and reads belated newspapers. Therefore he asks his brother not to submit an application for his release. "Firstly, it will achieve nothing; secondly, in winter it is better at the front. In the rear training, drill etc. are conducted. There is none of that here. It is indeed dangerous – but it is calm, and I firmly believe that nothing will happen to me".

Mielke had a great deal of luck… Under Stalingrad from the end of August a mortal struggle had been raging, entering its decisive phase in December 1942. But of this, silence in the Goebbels propaganda. Bruno asks his brother to send him the Führer's address of 1 October and pages torn from other newspapers with the speeches of Goebbels, Göring and Ribbentrop. In the battle lasting five months one and a half million German soldiers and their allies perished. Of the 91 thousand prisoners taken in the Stalingrad cauldron, about six thousand returned from captivity. But what news could this soldier read from the "belated newspapers"? Unaware of the turn of the wheel of history towards the west, 
<!-- s. 159 [PDF 159] -->
 he plans to establish numerous epistolary contacts with potential candidates for wife, so that after an unquiet life at the front, full of renunciations, he may look around for a cosy home. "It will be beautiful when I return home after the victory and in a sense of peace can lead a quiet life".

In December Bruno Mielke wrote a great deal. The approaching Christmas summoned memories of the family home, of the sisters, the brother, the father and the mother no longer alive (she died in 1933). "I want to do it [write] in such a way that you receive more post from me especially in the period of the Christmas feast". The first joy was the three-kilogram parcel received from home, and in it a fir wreath with the head of a little angel and tinsel. "I at once fastened it to my cupboard made from an empty grenade crate with two boards inserted. Several comrades who 'happened' to be nearby and were gazing with longing eyes at the Christmas cake being taken out got a morsel to taste. They pronounced hymns of praise as to the delicacy of the dainty".


In the letter of 14 December there is also the information that soldiers who have no families should send stamps to the local party wardens, and these will pass them on to the Women's Leagues, which will prepare Christmas parcels for the front soldiers. The Wehrmacht too saw to richly furnished parcels for every soldier, in which, alongside snuff, cigarettes, sweets, chocolate and wool for darning, there were also envelopes for letters, a comb, a bottle of vodka and of wine. Further on there is a paragraph of thanks for the fulfilment of his earlier request and ("I hope I am not betraying a secret") for the sending of an ointment against lice, which everyone has here. "On Saturday I washed myself, smeared myself all over and put on fresh linen. Now no louse bites, that is, the ointment works". The question is for how long. On the order of the Wehrmacht there was produced the preparation T-14 – a repellent powder against lice, fleas and mites etc. Each soldier was issued two such packets[^57]



[^57]: The preparation T-14 Ungeziefermittel gegen Läuse, Haarlinge, Flähe, Rausemilben usw. A repellent powder produced for the army in Munich, "against lice, fleas and mites, etc.". If Buno Mielke mentions an ointment, then he must have received from home an additional 
<!-- s. 160 [PDF 160] -->

In this letter there is also a description of the order of the day in force, or rather of the whole day and night. "We now have 6 hours of sentry duty within 24 hours – that can still be endured. Yes, here, dear life functions quite differently, also the times of meals. We eat dinner at 6 p.m., supper at 3 in the night, breakfast at 13 in the afternoon. Why so? Because at night we must be more on the alert and watchful than in the daytime. Whereas when day comes, there is nothing to fear, and then begins our time of sleeping". And the most important, joyful news. After a mere eight months of service, as of 1 December 1942, he was promoted to senior private. Captain Lebech with Lieutenant Hensler made their way in person to his sentry post and, in the face of the enemy, read out the order of promotion for exemplary service and a deep sense of duty.

To the soldiers present at the post the captain said that they should be proud to have such a comrade. "He would be pleased if we had only such soldiers". Curious that before reading out the order the commander asked Mielke to which class of nationality he belonged. The answer came that to the Volksliste. After a moment's reflection and pondering, the captain read out the order of promotion. Already in this same letter the senior private drifted into dreams about the chances of further promotions… "This I want to include in my prayer: that God grant victory to our dear fatherland and thereby peace to Europe and bread, that God grant my dear siblings a happy, blessed marriage; that God give me the strength to fulfil my soldierly duty in blessing and with honour".

Two letters written in December to Bruno were found in the cache. They probably reached him after the New Year – at that time a letter took a minimum of fifteen days to reach the Eastern Front. On 12 December Anna Steinbart writes from Konary, the woman standing in for Mielke in his work at the school. "Dear comrade in work in the grey uniform. You cannot imagine how happy the children were that their teacher had written to them. At once they warmed to the idea of answering 

 agent available for sale on the "civilian" market. I thank Mr Tadeusz Karpiński – the founder of the DDD Museum – for making the illustration available and for his valuable explanations (www.muzeumddd.pl).

<!-- s. 161 [PDF 161] -->
 you". In the following sentences we read her personal confession that work with small children gives her much pleasure, but the profession of teacher and educator is difficult and responsible. She feels that what she is doing is not succeeding, and without inner satisfaction she cannot go on doing it. She also lacks contact with the local people. "So I suppose I shall not be your successor much longer". Replying to the request for a photograph, she writes that she does not like being photographed and can send only one picture, and a somewhat blurred one at that. On the card there is Bruno's annotation that he replied on 31 January 1943.

The brother Gustav sends copies of documents confirming Bruno's professional position as a teacher and reassures him that everything is in order. He is also planning to go to Konin to check at the Sparkasse whether his teacher's salary, together with interest, has arrived in his account. He also tells of the two courses he attended: air-raid defence and the conducting of ceremonies. With bitterness he writes about the issued ban on baptisms, funerals and other church rites being conducted by lay preachers and cantors. The pastor Gross quoted earlier wrote about this. "Our neighbouring parishes have been experiencing tensions since the summer, because no cantor or, as it is called in the ordinance of the Reichsstatthalter, lay helper may any longer hold services; only pastors may hold them. Thereby depriving the kantorate councils of the possibility of acting and of experiencing the readings during the services every Sunday". The cantors, who were most often also administratively teachers, are as teachers separated from the Church. This decision can be interpreted as an effect of Nazi policy whose aim was to push the Church away from the teaching and upbringing of the young. This ban pained Gustav deeply. He greatly liked taking an active part in the service and always did so gladly. Christmas, New Year's Eve, the New Year therefore sadden him. Apart from that he has nothing special to say. "Yesterday [he writes on 19 December 1942] we had the pig slaughter".

Erich Kunitzer, director of the Teachers' Seminary in Łódź in the times when Bruno Mielke studied, rejoices at a letter from his former 
<!-- s. 162 [PDF 162] -->
 pupil. He asks on which sector of the front he has found himself, and boasts of his son Friedrich, who as a commander in a special unit is fighting somewhere in the Kalmyk steppe south-east of Rostov. Many former seminarians are at the front; of 120 Łódź teachers 50 have been called up to the army. "May God protect you all – writes Kunitzer – so that we may yet see one another. The struggle is hard, but we must carry it through to the end. I too would rather die at the front than find myself under the rule of the Jews, who have sworn a terrible vengeance upon us".

#### Thou hast given me the shield of thy salvation (Ps 18,36)

The short letter which Bruno writes on Sunday 20 December 1942 (it reaches Łazińsk on 5 January) announces the sending of a longer description of how he spent the Christmas feast and contains only one account: "During the hours of worship from 9 to 11 I stood sentry at my post, which is the more dangerous one (today we had to make the change). The main post consists of an oblong stone bunker. There I really ought to have been. But 'by mistake' I went to the side bunker. While I was just there giving myself to prayer, a shell of the Russian artillery struck the main post, broke a hole in the wall and threw many stones inside. What would have happened to me had I been just there?". And he ends: "Let God be praised and glorified".

To the account of the first day of the Feast Mielke sits just before midnight, straight after coming off the post on which between 21.00 and 23.00 he had stood with a good comrade Schwartz. He honestly admits that he had feared these Feasts and the onset of longing. But it was not so bad. In the forenoon the captain and the divisional chaplain came on a visit. They even came up to his post, and the chaplain was so kind as to ask about the family and gave him a bar of chocolate, wishing him a Happy New Year. It was not so bad also because "the joyful strength is given me above all by my faith in the living God, who is with me [...]". He could on that evening repeat from his little book, 
<!-- s. 163 [PDF 163] -->
 which he always has with him, the wonderful Christmas hymns, and "when I sing them softly, then the feast is around me". What did he read? Was it the passage of the Gospel according to St Luke on the birth of Jesus? "And Joseph also went up from Galilee, out of the city of Nazareth, into Judaea, unto the city of David, which is called Bethlehem; (because he was of the house and lineage of David:) To be taxed with Mary his espoused wife, being great with child" (Lk 2,4-5)[^58].

Did he sing the old thirteenth-century carol *Adeste fideles* (Let all the faithful sing, praising their God), or perhaps "Little children, little children, hasten now together // to the manger in the stable at this festive time"? It is an extraordinary image when we try to imagine a German soldier, standing on blood-stained Russian ground, at an advanced post, humming Silent Night. He saw in his mind's eye the family going to church, the praying father; he thanks his sisters for the presents sent in the parcels, the tasty cake, the warm leather gloves. Now he will no longer freeze – even if it were 50 degrees – as happens on the central sector of the front. "Here the temperature is such that there was even a thaw the day before, which the warm Gulf Stream current Golfstrom brought from the north". Although the command looks after them and even the food at the Feast is better, still they are at the front!

They had to be especially vigilant, for the Russians had attacked on the previous Christmas. They expected an attack yesterday too, but they did not come. "But today, on the first day of the Feast, from 9 to 11 they carried out an attack with the use of artillery and mortars – quite devilishly. Thank God there were no losses and no damage. In the afternoon, in answer, ours shelled the Russian bunkers – that is how it is at the front. The day before yesterday, just before midnight, blood flowed again – one of the comrades was hit in the face. Among our soldiers behind our walls and bunkers there happen only shots in the head with a fatal outcome". This is the cold account of a shelled soldier, who writes that danger here exists always. Therefore he asks for God's care and for the intercession of the family in prayer. Every 


[^58]: Quotations from the Gospel according to St Luke added – M.S.

<!-- s. 164 [PDF 164] -->
 one of the December letters written on the Eastern Front by Bruno Mielke ends with words of hope bound up with the swiftly approaching New Year. That it will be the year decisive for victory, that "from the hand of God the Father we shall receive everything that He wishes to give us – joy and suffering, happiness and unhappiness".

#### Hold up my steps in thy paths (Ps 17,5)

The first decade of January 1943 brings the letters of sister Anna to the brother at the front (7 January 1943) and a letter of Bruno (10 January 1943) to Anna, which is, however, not an answer. They must have crossed in the post. Anna thanks for the birthday chocolate sent from the front, which will be the main ornament of the table. Together with her sisters she prepared a parcel, into which they put socks knitted on needles between the Feast and the New Year; she made a head protector, Kadzia wrist protectors, and Fela knee pads. Anna's fiancé Hans added flash bulbs for the camera. And everyone rejoices that he can take pictures and cannot wait for them.

A completely different character and weight has the letter of the brother. Everyone lives with Anna's planned wedding. Bruno hopes that it will not end with this one wedding. Perhaps Gustav, perhaps Kadzia too will follow in her footsteps this year. He himself too greatly desires the moment when he will be able to present a sister-in-law to them. He sends a complete set of photographs with the request that Anna paste them into his album. Yet beyond the ritual assurances of remembering in prayer and the birthday wishes, Bruno believes that in the first days of this year so important for his sister he must share the gnawing anxiety he feels about the… spiritual safety of his sister. He reports that her fiancé Hans in his first letter to him writes clearly and openly that he "rejects the teaching from Scripture and wants to protect you from such things". And so "it is permitted to me to speak freely and openly to all people, even strangers, of the happiness of faith, only not to you, my dearest sister!". He considers, of course, and deeply laments, that this is some exaggeration. He does not yet condemn Hans, 
<!-- s. 165 [PDF 165] -->
 who does not know the splendours of Christ. Therefore he wants quietly to give them a Bible as a wedding present. He will not be able to come to the wedding and in this situation does not want to either. He knows that Hans loves her, that he is kind to the family and to him, and this is dreadful to him. He has only the hope that his sister does not share this severe prohibition and that they will be able to talk about everything. At the end he writes: "this is my request and wish for you – that you remain in your faith, which is clear and dear to you, steadfast and faithful. You might yet bring Hans to the faith. But that is not simple".

These most zealous cares for the state of his sister's soul and the attitude of her fiancé must have been repeated, for there has come to light a letter of Anna in which she presents her position, firmly grounded in reality. "I am sorry that I cause you restless hours, but believe me that there is no need for you to worry on my account. I can neither prove to you nor explain it, but I know that your cares are unnecessary, and they – on the other hand – unsettle me. If I were to leave Hans on your account or on yours, then after all I would gain nothing, since from his side no danger threatens me, if one may call it so. He loves me far too much to want to force me into anything I do not want. Am I to wait for a pastor who perhaps more than once was insincere? I do not want, like my sisters, to be 40 years old or more and then to suffer hysterical fits, because overloaded nerves lose hope. Those whom I have known were little different from Hans. He is at least loyal and worthy of trust. And in no case is he an unbeliever".

She reassures Bruno that there is nothing disquieting in the matter. On the other hand she considers it disadvantageous if he keeps brooding over it, for "at night and in a foreign land everything looks different". "We are all touched by sentimentality which we ourselves do not wish to see and do not wish to admit to. I believe we camouflage ourselves well". Anna, in turn, warns her brother not to fall into a peculiar religious mysticism. This Bruno certainly did not expect from his sister...

"I have no opinion of bare realism, but the opposite is also dangerous. You yourself consider that you are not a dreamer, but I think, 
<!-- s. 166 [PDF 166] -->
 that you are not very far from it and you must guard yourself against being one. Forgive me if I cause you pain by this, but I hope that you understand me well and do not take it amiss. I cannot and do not want to believe that you could turn away from me because I do not receive everything intensively. Yet it is true what I feel. I believe it is very disadvantageous to discuss this by letter, for it will lead only to misunderstandings. Remember one thing: that you need not be anxious on my account".

Two letters to the brother Gustav survive from January. On the twentieth he writes, deeply weighed down by illness, how he spent New Year's Eve. "At 22 I was able to vanish behind one of the snow-covered hills and, praying, receive the year 1943 from the hand of God". Bruno is troubled by the state of his brother's spirit, one who carries some great burden and who stands between him and God. Since that burden did not succeed in being drowned with the old year, let him heed a brother's advice and go to the pastor. "He will summon the wonderful, divine power (which Christ transmits to him together with his office) and will name the sin by its name. And then it will turn out that that sin, were it ever so great and filthy, will have to yield". An honest conversation is necessary, and the beginning is always heavy.

Just as in the earlier letter to his sister, so here too we see the great zeal of the candidate for a future pastor in the religious, spiritual formatting of his siblings. It is difficult to determine what to call Gustav's sin (if – as Bruno writes – it is a sin), but perhaps these travails of the brother may be an echo of the ban on administering baptisms, funerals or direct work at the services. "But there is an answer to it, which you will find in the Letter to the Romans 8,35". He does not, however, quote it. Let us recall: "Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation, or distress, or persecution"… (Rom 8,38) and the further verses which say that no powers "nor height, nor depth, nor any other creature, shall be able to separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord" (Rom 8,39)[^59]. The quoted epistle of St Paul is the confirmation of what 


[^59]: The Letter to the Romans, quotations added – M.S.

<!-- s. 167 [PDF 167] -->
 This drawing is a motif often exploited in propaganda and war publications. An oak on a hillock, helmets on the grave of the fallen and cranes returning to their native nests.



"Death for the fatherland is worthy of eternal honour". This is a quotation from the writer, poet and officer of the Prussian army Ewald Christian von Kleist, who died of wounds received in the battle of Kunowice.

<!-- s. 168 [PDF 168] -->

we may conjecture, and what Bruno Mielke for a long time did not say openly. Despite the many times repeated declarations of fidelity to the Führer and of subordination to the powers above, there stands over them a supreme authority to which this soldier gave himself entirely. It is God.

The description of everyday life at the front is written on 27 January. The end of the month brings hope of a winter growing milder. Surely the worst has already passed, and in any case, in comparison with other sectors of the front, things were milder with them – for temperatures reached at most minus 20-25 degrees. The worst in all this were the incredibly strong snowstorms, unknown in the Wartheland. He rejoices at the coming time of the withdrawal of their company a few kilometres to the rear. Surely there too there will be no "bread roll with butter", but at least they will be able to sleep more quietly, wash themselves well and get rid of the lice. After six weeks a return to the front line. "Perhaps then I shall have the luck that my leave begins, and when I return from leave, that is after your wedding, then it will already be warm and bright; then the great offensives will begin and the war – so may the grace of God ordain – will end. And how is your wedding? Have you already decided to go to Erika?".

It is hard to establish when the soldier Bruno received the letter written by his brother Gustav on 28 January 1943 (probably around the middle of February). The most important news is the information about the state of Bruno's savings recorded in his savings book (*Sparkassenbuch*). The deposits were regular and the interest was added. At the end of 1942 he has at his disposal the sum of 4871 marks. Gustav also reports that he was summoned to the Education Office in Konin. "There I was handed an act confirming the status of an official. I also had at once to take an oath. So I am an official for life – a teacher of the Führer". From Poznań the sister Anni brought the developed photographs of Bruno, which he had sent from Lapland to the family. Everyone admires how well he looks in uniform. And in the closing lines of the letter Gustav Adolf, sitting in the kantorate surrounded by forest, reports coolly and as if in passing: "Time and again news comes that some men from Łazińsk or from the neighbourhood have fallen on the Eastern Front. The fighting is after all not easy. Not a few of them perish".

<!-- s. 169 [PDF 169] -->

On 30 January 1943, from Berlin radio, in the time of the daily communiqués, there suddenly begin to flow the sounds of the dark adagio from Anton Bruckner's *Seventh Symphony*. It precedes the first official communiqué of this war about the lost battle of Stalingrad, the capitulation of the 6th Army and the capture of the thousands of exhausted and starving soldiers encircled in the cauldron. There is no possibility that this communiqué did not reach the front. They presumably also had some information channels of their own. Only speaking of this catastrophe threatened an accusation of spreading defeatism and of repeating the news from leaflets scattered on the Russian lines. With the consequences one can imagine.

What a strange letter, then, reaches the brother Gustav. About the planned marriage, about the fact that he will take his wedding vows only when he is released from service. For as a soldier he will get only a few days' leave. The betrothal and the wedding are to be the concluding festival of thanksgiving, a joyful beginning of private life. Bruno describes at length the girl from Trzemeszno, Elfride Fiedler: that she is housewifely, lively and talkative despite her low education. But that is good, for he does not want a wife with higher education who would lecture him unceasingly. There is, however, a certain catch – the girl, her parents, her siblings belong to the community of sabbatarians. But if she decides to marry him, she will have to convert to Lutheranism, and that before the betrothal. "I have not the slightest desire to go to church alone". If she does not do it, he will break with her at once. It is a long letter describing the multitude of thoughts that constantly accompany him when he dreams of the longed-for marriage. He asks how his brother is getting on with Erika, whether the sisters Anna and Kadzia will be taking their vows this year.
They are not to wait with that until his arrival on leave. That is all. About his service at the front he writes one sentence. "Things go variously with me". By the standards of wartime he receives quite a few letters from potential candidates. But the post brings in this matter more disappointments than hopes. The twenty-three-year-old maiden from Trzemeszno thanks for Bruno's photograph, on which he looks younger than in reality. In matters of religion she declares openly: "As for my faith and community, I shall tell you very plainly. As I already wrote to you earlier, I am a 
<!-- s. 170 [PDF 170] -->
 Seventh-day Adventist. The greatest privilege is that I sanctify the day which our Saviour also sanctified when he walked the ends of the earth, that is, Sunday. We call ourselves Adventists because we await the second coming of Christ and keep the seventh day". This declaration cuts short the further acquaintance.

In turn, a letter from Lipice reached Bruno from Ursula – the leader of a B.D.M. troop (Bund Deutscher Mädel – League of German Girls). This is the female section of the Nazi youth organisation Hitlerjugend. She is disappointed that the two previous letters she sent came back to her. She encloses them again in the envelope as proof. The girl writes that she has returned to her old path. Earlier her love for Bruno had blinded her! What does that mean today, when she ponders it? That it is for her the only path: "I believe in the almighty God, who will not allow the fall of Germany. Every faith that is directed against my beloved fatherland I reject from myself. Therefore I need no intermediary, those who so often see only their little "church" and do not perceive the highest aim for the Church, which ought to be Germany. Our Führer never intended to separate the Church from the state – quite the contrary! In the year 1933 services were instituted as an obligation for all organisations of the party. But the Church rejected that". Nor was it able to lead the German nation spiritually, and therefore the German youth cannot be entrusted to it. She judges such conduct of the hierarchy as acting together with the enemy states against the Third Reich. When victory has at last been achieved, she believes, the affairs with the Church will finally be set in order, for it cannot be that the Church acts against the state. Then too she will take decided steps. It will be either – or. And although she will not try to persuade her parents, she at least hopes that they will show understanding for her stance. Naturally she considers that "…among Christians faithful to their Church there are a few who stand up in defence of Germany and the Führer, but these are exceptions – among them are you. You have a sincere faith, but you have, after all, also reason, which allows one to discern what the future holds. Formerly you dwelt 
<!-- s. 171 [PDF 171] -->
 in that your village, cut off from the events of the world. Yet now you cannot close your eyes and ears to all this that does not accord with your unshaken views. Do you not see at all what is at stake in the struggle of nations?". She knows that Bruno will want to rebut all this. And she ends: "Believe me, were I not fond of you, your views would be a matter of indifference to me. You are such a dear man, so natural, that I have grown sincerely fond of you". There are no other letters or cards that would attest that this acquaintance was continued.

What happened at Stalingrad was a great shock for the Germans, a source of sharp tensions and discussions in the political and military leadership of the Third Reich. For not everyone agreed with the Führer, who called this spectacular defeat (of which the whole world was already speaking) a "heroic spectacle of German history". Generals Karl Strecker and Helmuth Groscurth did not end their report on the capitulation with the sacramental *Heil Hitler*, but with the traditional cry: "Long live Germany"... The news from the east also brought a spark of hope to the subjugated nations, an echo of which we shall soon see in the letters of the following months. But at the beginning of February it was not yet known how great was the significance of the lost battles on the Volga and the Don. The southern front, too, had not achieved its intended objectives (the seizure of the oil sources) and Army Group A had to withdraw from the Caucasus. Yet all the signs in heaven and on earth said that something important had happened.

In this sense Bruno's matrimonial exertions, his wedding plans, may be regarded as a kind of defensive reaction, a flight from the black everyday, a peculiar repression of what was reaching him. For Bruno Mielke was no fool. Two weeks later he sends a letter which is already considerably more open. "For a week now we have been here in reserve and are leading the barracks life of soldiers. Today is the first Sunday – to my and everyone's joy without duty. One can truly meditate spiritually and religiously. Spiritual is my thinking and my writing of letters to you. In the religious sense I am, as before, still lonely and in the desert. When the hours of holding the service come, I am always sorely tempted.

<!-- s. 172 [PDF 172] -->

Today too I was able to do it. […] I went out into the snow-covered mountains and found again an altar for prayer… How good it is then for a poor, lonely soul, when it finds itself near Him who said: 'I am with you all days'". But at once he qualifies: "I cannot tell everyone about it. The majority would take me for a dreamer". Yet for him this is a blissful and beatifying dreaming, a real happiness which the majority of pitiable people do not know. "Dreamer" is one of the milder names he could have been called. Many comrades steeped in the new faith often jeered coarsely at the humble adherents of the religion of the weak, the eternal penitents, as Christians were contemptuously called. Hence that search for a solitary place. It is a kind of escape, a hiding away, in order to give oneself to prayer in peace

In Berlin the ministry of propaganda is at work! On 18 February, a dozen or so days after the capitulation of Field Marshal Paulus's 6th Army, in the capital's *Sportpalast* Joseph Goebbels delivers the famous speech in which he announces the beginning of total war against Russia and the Allies. After the definitively lost battles of Moscow and Stalingrad, when the leading Nazis see the growing threat, above all a general mobilisation is proclaimed by Hitler. Goebbels admits publicly for the first time that the crisis on the Eastern Front has reached its peak, and that Stalingrad is a great alarm for the fate of the German nation. No German may think of compromise. The nation is called to hard war and is to be ready for any sacrifice, should the Führer give such an order. In the sports palace, under the slogan "Total war – the shortest war", only chosen and vetted guests were gathered. These are 14,000 fanatical Nazis, who sing and applaud after each of the sharper passages of this chiefly anti-Jewish philippic.
Goebbels calls with the words of the poet: Now, people, arise, and let the storm break loose ("*Nun, Volk steh auf, und Sturm brich los!*"). The whole staging of the rally is to serve, among other things, to rebut the Allied voices saying that Germany has lost faith in victory. But at the same time he wants to "feed" his ambitions, counting that Hitler will give him greater power.

<!-- s. 173 [PDF 173] -->





The photographs document German soldiers at Petsamo/Pieczenga. They come from publicly published archives of veterans from the Alpine countries fighting on this sector of the front. It was not possible to establish the authors of the photographs.


Map: source – Wikipedia, accessed 6.11.2023, 13:09


<!-- s. 174 [PDF 174] -->

On 4 March the "enjoying of quiet" in the rear comes to an end. An urgent order arrives rousing the Volksdeutsche (Germans from Poland, that is, the Wartheland) to report to the 2nd battalion. Straight after taking up their positions he sends the family a short message. At first they complained that, laden down, they had had to move in a blizzard. But the commander sent sledges for them, fed them well and at once distributed them to the advanced combat posts. After they had been disposed of in this way, one can see that they constituted a filling of the personnel shortfalls in the companies caused by the losses suffered. Bruno Mielke is assigned as an operator to man the heavy machine gun and is given as helper the private Atze. This is a weapon of the immediate field of combat.

There has survived the only description of a battle in the entire collection of letters. "From 12 at night (from Friday to Saturday) our heavy weapons were made ready (artillery, mortars, anti-aircraft guns). It is unbelievable how everything roars, rolls, seethes and bubbles, and when the shells whiz over to the other side, carrying death and destruction. Again – as always – there were many fallen and wounded on both sides (among the Russians far more). Were all ready for death? I think of it in my spirit and this is always for me the most important question. One more thing is astonishing, which I heard today: the Russian prisoners testified that the Russians in those same days were planning an attack on our positions, only we forestalled them. Yet one feels so wonderfully the care and love of God".

One thing is certain – Mielke was in that hell. On the Day of Remembrance, the first day of spring, 21 March, he writes that the lack of leave wearies him, as does the uncertainty whether the information passed on that he would get one will be confirmed. He wants to be with his family for the Feast and hopes that his brother will celebrate the wedding for which everyone is waiting. The day is splendid, sunny and comparatively quiet. The Russians have not yet answered their last attack of 15 March. But presumably they can no longer. Presumably it is as Hermann Göring announced, that the adversary is fighting with its last reserves. And we "through the totalisation of the war can field one more army. Therefore we are all firmly convinced that the Lord will send us victory this summer and will bring it about that the poor 
<!-- s. 175 [PDF 175] -->
 people on both sides attain peace". Who but the gunner of a heavy machine gun could see better what harvest the war reaps. He asks how "total war" has affected their life. Whether the brother wants to enlist – though he is so needed in Łazińsk, and there is the wedding in the plan too. (Soon the Wehrmacht itself will decide about that, without asking Gustav's opinion). At the end Bruno writes: "Two weeks ago we listened to the speech of the Führer. He spoke of how decisive a time our nation and fatherland are living through. On this special spring day, when we have happily survived the winter and the countless dangers, we ought to rejoice and be thankful that God, through our Führer, preserves us from the threatening evil, and how it strengthens us soldiers that we do not bear our sacrifices in vain, do not suffer in vain and – die in vain".

In many of the letters written in the coming months by the soldier Bruno we read with a certain astonishment that he believes more in the successive assurances of the leader and of the various conduct of politicians than in what he sees and must do at the front. How dangerous is the force of propaganda falling on ground ideologically well prepared. In quite a different tone is the letter written six days later. He already knows that he will not get leave for Easter; too many of those with longer service stand in the queue before him. "I already long for the quiet, for the blessed hours, and I recall the time I was able to live through in your house, dear brother. Łazińsk, Zagórów, also Anielewo, Białobłoty, Maślaki, Konary, Grodziec – all these are blessed places!". He will therefore not be at Anni's wedding, and Gustav's plans too may come to nothing. They may call him up to the army. What would be fatal! Evidently the Lord, who is in the heavens, so ordained everything. He believes, however, that "He, the faithful Saviour, will never leave me alone, and at Easter too – as every day – will comfort me with His nearness! This spiritual strengthening is particularly necessary for me now. For soon the decisive attacks must here, in the east, gather strength, and then various thoughts come. It will be as God wills". He advises his loved ones to hear and proclaim the holy word at the time of Passover. That which he now so lacks. In this way they will best arm themselves for the times of drought that may come.

<!-- s. 176 [PDF 176] -->

Nothing happens without the will and permission of the Lord. These are decisive and grave months; one must be able to sacrifice one's own wishes.

This is the first letter in which Mielke allows himself a certain generalisation of the situation on his sector of the front. Until now these were "narrow plans", showing the individual threats and dangers menacing him directly. Now he says: prepare yourselves for a hard time, for things do not look well. Whatever he had in mind and whatever his knowledge really was, in saying that the fighting is entering its decisive phase he was not mistaken. The next letter preserved in the cache is dated Eastern Front 27 July 1943. At the beginning of the month – on 5 July – the German attack on the Kursk salient begins. The struggle lasted until the end of August. The Russians knew the enemy's plans, code-named "Citadel", very well and adopted the defensive strategy proposed by Zhukov. When the forces of the attacking troops, bogged in the mud, dying on mines and under the murderous fire of the artillery, were exhausted, they moved to a counter-attack. It was the greatest tank battle in history. The lost battle caused the German command to be unable to seize the strategic initiative on the Eastern Front for the rest of the war. "With us it is comparatively quiet" – he writes. "A confirmation of the fact that the Russians are using all their forces in the south in order to reconquer the central areas. Here it is often gloomy and misty. Then it is dangerous, for the Russians readily make attacks then, but we are already watchful and our machines do not fail".

The thoughts of Bruno Mielke run to Łazińsk, where people can calmly gather the grain from the fields and run over the stubble fields. Here, on the deserted and harsh north, one feels nothing of what people in the countryside are living by now. And the harvests are important for the war and for the nation. He thanks his sister Anna for the parcel and in passing conveys to her the information that he is no longer writing to Elfride. And it is not recklessness or indifference, but rather responsibility. Apart from that he is healthy, and although they feed him well here, lately he has lost his appetite. "Would that the dear God were only with His grace near our people, so that everything ends well and mankind may again know peace".

<!-- s. 177 [PDF 177] -->

On Sunday 8 August he sends a letter written in some religious exaltation, at eleven o'clock, in the blessed hour of the services. "In my spirit I see in all countries the temples, I see in them those who through the blood and salvation of Christ have become my siblings". And he sees himself in the pulpit – in the most splendid of all official vestments, proclaiming the gospel. Instead of greetings he asks them to read Psalm 23.

Psalm 23  
*The Lord is my shepherd*…

*A Psalm of David.*  
*The Lord is my shepherd; I shall not want*.  
*He maketh me to lie down in green pastures*.  
*He leadeth me beside the still waters*.  
*He restoreth my soul*.  
*He leadeth me in the paths of righteousness*  
*for his name's sake*.  
*Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death*,  
*I will fear no evil: for thou art with me*,  
*thy rod and thy staff they comfort me*.  
*Thou preparest a table before me in the presence of mine enemies*,  
*thou anointest my head with oil; my cup runneth over*.  
*Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me*  
*all the days of my life*.  
*and I will dwell in the house of the Lord for ever*[^60].

#### In thee, O Lord, do I put my trust (Ps 71,1)

The dating of the letter of September (16 September 1943) carries a new addition: "in the field". Above all it is an extensive text on the occasion of Gustav's 44th birthday. We read a long exposition of the virtues of the 36-year-old Elza – the girl, 


[^60]: Psalm 23, quotation added – M.S.

<!-- s. 178 [PDF 178] -->
 to whom he wants to draw his brother's attention. He knows her only from the account of an acquaintance, but what decides her worth (in accordance with Bruno's system of values, which we have already come to know) is the fact that she is pure in faith, character and virtue, that is, she possesses all those most beautiful values which qualify her to be his wife. "Of course you must judge for yourself whether she pleases you […] but you must not set your judgements and requirements too high – more important than outward beauty remains, after all, inner worth". As for children – despite her age – she may still give birth to a few, and surely he does not want a large brood…? The letter also brings thanks to the family for the birthday wishes sent and the assurances of prayers to God for intercession on his behalf. He writes all this in the field and explains where such an address comes from. "Being in good health, I am at present helping with the building of roads. It is not easy to work physically 9-10 hours with pick and spade, but everything will pass; I am grateful that the food is good (better than last year) and that I can work for Germany and not like a prisoner of war in Russia – as has befallen many".

Where does this mood come from? Bad news after the heavy defeat at Kursk comes not only from the Eastern Front. On 10 July the British and Americans land in Sicily. On 25 July the Grand Fascist Council carries out a surprising coup, depriving Benito Mussolini of power. A few days later – on 3 September – Italy (the government of Marshal Badoglio) capitulates! On that same day the Allied troops land in southern Italy. The arrested Duce is freed by German commandos on Hitler's orders. And this event arouses the admiration of Bruno Mielke. "The fidelity of our Führer pleases me especially. This matter in Italy will become at least clear, and certainly further steps will soon be taken". Under the Germans' umbrella Mussolini creates in northern Italy the puppet Republic of Salò. This is another letter in which Mielke allows himself a commentary on broader events concerning the war and politics.

But that is not all. From the advice he gives his brother we may infer that Gustav, sitting in Łazińsk, notices the change in the behaviour of the Poles, who in various ways signal to the occupiers that they know more, 
<!-- s. 179 [PDF 179] -->
 than the organs of Goebbels write and proclaim. "One could rage that the Poles are becoming audacious; nevertheless I should like to ask you to confine yourself to quiet observing and reporting; provocative intruding breeds hatred, and hatred is capable of many things. That the saboteurs disappear – let the gendarmerie occupy itself with that, it is called to it. The Poles rejoice in vain for political freedom. The Germans are in form!". As a birthday present he sends his brother a goblet made from the casing of a bullet fired at the Eastern Front in Russia. Let it be a small keepsake from the front and always accompany you on your journeys.

Perhaps this "lifting of heads" of the Poles resulted not only from the news from the front, but also from the attitude of the elder of the brothers to the former neighbours. In a considerably later letter of Gustav Adolf to the sister managing the household (12 December 1944, field post number 33 977), when he is already serving as an artilleryman in the naval infantry in Hamburg, he encloses advice on how to behave with the slave woman allocated to the farm. "Like with a Polish girl. To begin with you must take her firmly by the scruff, so that she knows where her place is. You must lock everything up and portion everything out to her. After all, it is not known whether she does not steal and is not in league with the farmhand". Further on he inserts an extensive instruction on how to run the pig breeding. And he adds a warning not to delay the pig slaughter, for they (the girl and the farmhand) might steal the pig from the shed.

There has survived in the cache in Łazińsk an interesting example of the various forms of a peculiar undermining of the morale of the German rear. It is a planted forgery of a document appointing the addressee a "reproducer", who is to "service 9 women and 18 girls" for the raising of the birth statistics. (It was known in the neighbourhood that Gustav had long been looking around for a candidate for wife). The "document" states that this honourable task may not be protested against by wives and will not be recognised as grounds for divorce. The "official paper", **a copy of which we reproduce in Testimonies pp. 307-308**, is an example of the fictitious, misleading information and ordinances produced underground and circulated among the Germans. A special unit in the intelligence section of the AK occupied itself with this. It must 
<!-- s. 180 [PDF 180] -->
 be added that the officials of the Main Race and Settlement Office (RuSHA) located within the structures of the SS, responsible for the purity and propagation of the Germanic race, carried out a peculiar selection among the slaves directed to labour in Germany,
qualifying the chosen men as so-called racially valuable men.

Bruno's letters of November come already from Finland. Addressed in Parkkino (the region of Petsamo) in the far north. The field post number 31 059 indicates that Bruno served in the 136th Mountain Ranger Regiment. He sends his sister the money saved from his pay as a present on the occasion of her approaching birthday. In the parcel there is also a jar, into which he put a few little bunches of berries found between the snow and the ice. On 8 November, when he writes these words, the 10th anniversary of his mother's death falls. He was at a Catholic field mass. "There, too, one can hear a worthwhile word". Later, as was his custom, he went into the forest to pray and to read the Gospel of St Matthew appointed for that Sunday: "For many are called, but few are chosen" (Mt 22,14)[^61]. These are great and grave words, which he passes on to his sister for another, new year of life.

A few days later he writes to his brother. He knows that thanks to his letters to the sisters and the father Gustav is up to date with the news from Finland, but he prefers it when they write to one another directly. Above all the news of the next deferral rejoices him. He considers that Gustav can do more for the community on the spot than as a recruit. That he is a teacher would have no significance at the beginning of service. Like Bruno he would be a "Barras" and treated accordingly. He himself recalls that time as very hard, and everyone must pass through it. He survived that hard time; now he already has the status of an older soldier, and his high education serves him well. "From the commander I received appropriate functions; above all I give lessons (twice a week) at the level of primary school and teach all those who are weak in the German language". He also runs a lending library (of 500! volumes).



[^61]: The Gospel according to St Matthew, quotation added – M.S.

<!-- s. 181 [PDF 181] -->

Many German writers worked on the creation of whole series of books intended for the soldiers of the Wehrmacht. These were the so-called Feldpost-Bücher. "Anthologies were published containing a fascist-nationalist mixture intended to provide entertainment, some form of spiritual nourishment and, above all, ideological, Nazi support".[^62] What could one read in these works? Well, the individual is nothing! He is worth as much as he subordinates himself to the "voice of the blood", to the "holy fatherland", to the sense of duty or to the order of the leader. "Since the leader sacrifices himself for all of us and for Germany, the sacrificing of oneself for Germany and the blind devotion to the leader is likewise necessary". This entire literature instils in the Germans, on countless pages, the supreme principle: in the consciousness of a historical mission there holds for you "obedience and fidelity unto death"[^63].

(In the Russian Pieczenga, the former Finnish Petsamo, where Bruno Mielke was stationed and fought, there is a cemetery where "Our 12,000 Fallen Comrades" ("Unseren 12000 Toten Kameraden") lie at rest)[^64].

It is not difficult to find in the soldiers' letters quoted here those Nazi canons, stated outright and repeated many times. At times, however, one may gain the impression that it is done mechanically, without any, even hidden, reflection. Mielke volunteers for a medic's course. He does so, as he writes, for two reasons: such skills may prove useful for a whole life; secondly, he hopes that in this field it will be easier for him to win promotion. Here the Latin will come in useful, which he constantly 


[^62]: Orłowski Hubert. *Literatura w III Rzeszy*. Wydawnictwo Poznańskie 1979, p. 43.

[^63]: Ibid., p. 196.

[^64]: Today, on the territory of Russian Pechenga [formerly Finnish Petsamo], where the XIX Mountain Corps fought its battles, there stands a new cemetery (Friedhof), built in the years 2000/2001, for the twelve thousand mountain riflemen, airmen and sailors who fell in the fighting. **The bilingual inscription on one of the tablets states that the memorial cemetery came into being thanks to the efforts of the veterans from the Alpine countries, who thank the Russian veterans for their help and are grateful to them**. A separate tablet commemorates EISMEERFRONT 1941-1944 [the fighting on the Arctic Ocean front]. On a nearby hill the Russians erected a dominant monument dedicated to their soldiers who fought in defence of the Fatherland on the subpolar, Arctic lands.

<!-- s. 182 [PDF 182] -->
 learns in his spare moments with his thoughts on studies in Vienna. He is also fully aware that "medical service is not easy and no less dangerous during the fighting, for it must be in the forefront and dress the wounded – but it is all the more beautiful. […] If the war were to last until next summer, I shall be able to prove myself in action, but I am not afraid. I have a very strong faith". For the present – in comparison with the past winter – this winter, should nothing extraordinary happen, will be lighter.
Naturally, if the Russians do not push too hard forward, then all those stationed in the rear will be called up to help.

So for the time being he is doing well. Sleeping, clothing and food are in order. He is positively astonished "how they transport all this so far away into this wild region". And one more astonishing request. Someone stole from his medical bag 25 safety pins. He asks – if they can – to send them to him. He will put them back in, and in this way will not have to report the theft.

It is worth noting the dense correspondence which Bruno Mielke conducts. And it is not only his "privilege". The OKW – the High Command of the Wehrmacht – attached great weight to the smooth functioning of the field post. It was held that frequent contact of the soldiers with home and with their loved ones raises morale in the army, and also positively influences the mood in the families, that is, in the rear of the front. No matter where he was stationed on the far Eastern Front, in Finland or in Norway, the letters circulated smoothly and as a rule reached the addressee. From their reading one may infer that there were no special restrictions as to the number of cards or letters sent. Weight and formats, however, were strictly observed. Post reaching the front arrived at the battalion, where it was distributed to the companies, for those units were best informed about the addressee's whereabouts.

Almost two weeks before Christmas, on 12 December 1943, on yet another Sunday, Mielke writes from "his cosy little medical room". At this time, when services are held in all the churches, he can again lead his soul to where it 
<!-- s. 183 [PDF 183] -->
 feels best and where it will find untroubled peace. "How it fills me with strength and joy, this certainty of the deep grace of God". There is no good news for home. He cannot count on leave at Christmas. The long queue of those with longer service still stands before him. Perhaps they will allow him in January, "which may mean a long cold journey, but others too travel in winter alive there and back, so I suppose I shall endure it too". He is constantly corresponding with candidates for wife. At present with two: one – a neighbour from Konary – pretty and intelligent, but not a believer; the other *Volksdeutsche* (an ethnic German). The first one began writing to him from Wallensen. A little lacking in intelligence. "But one thing is certain for me: I shall not marry before I find a believing girl". She must guarantee a deep communion – especially since he is to become a pastor in the future.

Apart from that all is well – also from the soldierly point of view. When he compares the lot of the fighting comrades in the south or his own past winter, he is so far 50 per cent better off and it is finer. But the beautiful time may end any day. "So our commander said recently – the front from the Arctic Ocean [Eismeer] to Italy can at any moment become open". As a Christmas present he sends the family the splendid little book received from Hamburg, *Der Weg des Glaubens* [The Way of Faith]. "Write often". Their commander knew what he was saying. Already in January 1944 the resolute advance of the Red Army began, whose aim was to lift the blockade of Leningrad – which took place on 27 January 1944. The Russian attacks forced Army Group North to retreat. They withdrew to the region of Lake Peipus (now on the border of Estonia and Russia). Bruno Mielke's unit belonged to that grouping. In the south, at the same time, there began the advance which in the following five months will bring the liberation of Ukraine,
Volhynia and the Crimea. Still at the end of 1943, in the days from 28 November to 1 December, there takes place in Tehran the meeting of the leaders of the great coalition, Churchill, Roosevelt and Stalin.

The next surviving letter is dated 2 March 1944. Bruno reassures his nearest that they need not be afraid unnecessarily. "After a heavy week at the post we have, thanks to God's graciousness, quiet days so far.

<!-- s. 184 [PDF 184] -->

Every day is like a feast, like a present. And if the Lord sends heavy days again, He will also graciously help to bear their weight. Only to hold out until Easter, and we shall have the winter behind us. Perhaps next one we shall already have peace. But since that hour has not yet come, we wish to continue faithfully performing our duty, in order to help bring about victory". On the seventh of March the writing was interrupted by an alarm. "We must set out, hard days will come again". (The letter reached Łazińsk on 23 March 1944, from field post 31 059E – the number assigned to the 136th regiment of the 2nd Mountain Division, 10th company).

The letter written before Easter, on 12 March 1944, reaches Łazińsk already on 17 March! Within five days the post arrives from the distant Finnish north to the village hidden in the forests below Zagórów. He writes on a Sunday in the Passiontide. "How again the kindness of God crowned me, that He granted me (us) a free and quiet Sunday, so that in its holy forenoon hours I could read His holy gospel and then find a quiet place in the sunny, snowy solitude and join the invisible community of prayer in heaven and on earth". Further on he thanks the Lord that He caused such wonderful dear people to appear in his life: father, mother, brother, sisters. "It is the cross of Golgotha that in this special time allows us – sinners – to lay down all burdens and become clean and without blemish". This is an important and strong sentence. The soldier Mielke grants himself full absolution.

From everyday life he can report that he is healthy, enjoys a good appetite and sends up stamps for parcels in which he hopes to find "pączken" (pączki). Although he knows that in the fifth year of the war there may be difficulties and one must husband what one has, if it should be possible he asks them to send five-fingered gloves. He has two-fingered ones – so his hands do not freeze – but the many-fingered ones are more useful.

From the next surviving letter we learn that this exchange of correspondence was extraordinarily intensive. If things are comparatively quiet, he writes once a week; if the situation is more tense, twice a week! He asks his brother not to burden himself with writing to 
<!-- s. 185 [PDF 185] -->
 him every day. A week earlier they were withdrawn to the rear – 70 km from the front. "Here it is nicer and more comfortable than in the previous place. The barracks are spacious and airy. For a whole week now we have been papering the walls and working on their beautification. The cold and the darkness no longer weary one so. A little heating suffices and at 23 o'clock it is still grey. Soon there will be no night. Outdoors there is a thaw, and in some places last year's moss is showing". In the next paragraph he shares with his brother a joyful piece of news. Namely, on 20 April, the Führer's birthday, he was promoted to the rank of corporal (Obergefreiter). True, after two years of service that is nothing great, but dear to the heart all the same. He regards it also as a testimony of the grace of God, which has blessed his service and above all preserved him from all obstacles. As a corporal he also held his first service of worship in the melting snow. A clump of moss served him as an altar. "This hour in solitude allows one to see one's weaknesses". The sight of comrades drinking and carousing all night also fills him with sadness. He is almost ashamed that he sits here in the north listening to the rustle of the pines, while his *Kameraden* (comrades) must wage heavy fighting on the southern front. "In comparison with them – we are here on furlough", and at once he adds – "may it not get worse". He also has luck with his superiors. The commander of his company is Oberleutnant Hengl. He is the son of the Generalleutnant Ritter von Hengl commanding the 2nd Mountain Division[^65].

Bruno has luck with his commanders, but has no luck with girls. He writes that he "foundered" with Anna Steinbart and now links his hopes with Elizabeth Kaschuba, who is trying to creep into his heart. "Not ugly, educated, an excellent housekeeper". (Where did he have this information?) Naturally – as always 


[^65]: General of mountain troops Georg Ritter von Hengl was a distinguished professional officer. During the First World War he fought, among other places, at Verdun and in Russia. Later, as an aviator, he shot down 10 English aircraft. For his war merits in his native kingdom of Bavaria he was granted a title of nobility. On the Arctic front in Russia, in the year 1943, he commanded the 2nd Mountain Division, and in the year 1944 – the period from which Bruno Mielke's letters survive – he commanded the XIX Mountain Corps. They were stationed in the Petsamo/Pechenga area. He was taken prisoner in May 1945 in Norway. Wikipedia. Accessed 23.02.2023, 23:40.

<!-- s. 186 [PDF 186] -->
 in his case – there remains the matter of testing her religiosity and faith… He spurs his brother on not to lag in his advances to Erika, for by such indecision he may lose her. Happily, the letter of 4 May 1944 has survived. It came by air. (The field post number 48 341 relates to the 67th Medical Company of the 2nd Mountain Division). Here we learn that Bruno Mielke was on leave in Łazińsk. He could therefore have left behind part of the letters which had reached him at the front.

He writes to his brother Gustav. A great joy for him is the information that at Easter his brother partook of the Lord's Supper[^66], that he prevailed upon himself to a strong fidelity to his God. That he did so after a long time, overcame the inhibition and opened himself to "the forgiving grace". He had long awaited such a letter and thanks for the trust and sincerity shown him. "It makes me happy also because I can well empathise with your situation and understand you and your needs". There was a time when his brother's attitude – the leaving of the Church – caused him great, oppressive worry. During the leave they talked about it with the father, who spoke of the elder son with concern and in "inexpressible sadness". "Papa, despite his many errors and weaknesses, is a dear soul! I am so grateful to my Lord that I still have him". Almost always, when the younger son mentions the father or asks that greetings be passed on to him, he does not fail to mention the errors which the father committed and commits. There is in these remarks a somewhat condescending note.
