Now, when Gustav has returned to the Church, Bruno has one wish – that this renewed partaking of the Lord's Supper "may let your soul blossom anew and strengthen your spiritual hunger all the more". Today that oppressive worry, which tormented him because of Gustav's conduct, begins to accompany him 


[^66]: The Lord's Supper. In the tradition of the Evangelical Churches, the Eucharist – the Lord's Supper is, next to Holy Baptism, a Sacrament. In accordance with the words of its institution by Jesus Christ (Mt 26,26-28; Łk 22,19-20; 1Kor 11,23-26) and with the exposition of the Books of Concord, the Lord's Supper is the true body and true blood of Jesus Christ, truly present, and is administered to believers under the forms of bread and wine. That is why the faithful receive the Eucharist under both kinds: bread and wine.

<!-- s. 187 [PDF 187] -->
 because of his sister Anni, who, it is said, has not been seen in church for a long time. There falls the important question, which says a great deal about Bruno's spiritual formation. "Is this not a sign of her doubtful inner state? Is this not the first sad fruit of 'Germania' with the spirit and 'religion' taken over from Hans?" A few words about what so disquiets the writer.

In the thirties Nazism became a kind of political religion. The majority of German society, unfortunately, believed in these dogmas proclaimed with extraordinary effectiveness in the Goebbels propaganda. It was an ominous mixture of myth and anti-humanism. After the First World War the crisis expressed itself not only in the economic and social sphere, of which the erupting revolutions and revolts were the proof. The crisis and a clear weakening of influence also struck Christianity. Into its place there strode with growing force the idea of nationalism, which in Germany took the form of völkism, from the word *Volk* – nation. The primacy of "race", "society", "nation" over the rights of the individual held sway. The strongest race – the Aryan race – had the right to precedence, even one won by force. In this mixture of Nazi and chauvinist moods, of various forms of occultism, there also ran a strong criticism of Christianity. It was resolved that this religion stands in opposition to the national-socialist ideology, since "it was not a religion of the sword"[^67]. There is no place in it for heroes, for the "Übermensch", the overman. Christians are people guided by the principles of mercy, of love of neighbour, penitents... It is the religion of weak people, whose canon of laws cannot concern the true Germanic man. As one could read outright in the letter quoted above of one of the girls to Bruno Mielke, the Church acts against the Reich and the Führer. The realities of everyday life in the Nazi world forced Christians into behaviours that allowed survival. But is that all? There was founded, for example, the organisation Deutsche Christen [German Christians] – who accepted the hints of the Nazi ideologues 


[^67]: Natalia Stawarz, *Aryjski Chrystus i germańscy bogowie – nazistowska wizja wiary*. Wikipedia, text published 21.07.2018, 13:42; accessed 25.02.2023, 12:07.

<!-- s. 188 [PDF 188] -->
 about the chosen character of the German nation. There also appeared experts on the Bible proving that Christ was an Aryan...

In the letter of Gustav of 1 May 1942 one can find the "fruit" of these quite widely proclaimed teachings. He writes about the times of Christ: "Should wise people then have lived at all and held back God, and said: what are you doing, choose the Saviour from the Germanic people, and not from among the Jews. But it pleased God so". In May 1942 in Zagórów and the neighbourhood there was no longer a single Jew. But Gustav Adolf looks further with concern and, on the occasion of an occasion, encodes the question of what the authorities intend to undertake g. J. – this is the abbreviation of the words *gegen Juden* – against the Jews[^68]. "The scale of irrationalism, brutality, servility and cultism can be seen in the poetry of the brown poets" – writes Hubert Orłowski[^69]. In presenting the leader, words are chosen from the New Testament; Hitler is God's anointed, chosen by God or by other, vaguely defined powers. He is the saviour of the German nation. And all those who die faithful to their leader are to become martyrs. This is only a very brief reminder of what the idea of Germania was.

We return to Bruno's letter. He fears whether his sister has not taken over from her husband the faith in the new religion. In this "religion" even the conception of the soul was distorted in such a way as to serve as yet another element of propaganda[^70]. In the thought of national Christianity the soul was to strive to fulfil the divine plan, in which Germany – the nation chosen by God – is to heal and beatify the world. This deep disquiet at the behaviour of the sister, who has long not been seen in church, is also a clear declaration, though not expressed outright. Eternal damnation threatens the sister if she does not turn back in time. Bruno Mielke rejects the new religion with all the strength of a believing Christian. He considers it the road to man's perdition. A betrayal of God. If in the series of letters available to us he always renews the declaration of fidelity and faith in the Führer, of readiness to serve 


[^68]: This is the interpretation of the letter by the translator, Jacek Kin, which I consider justified in the light of the collection of books discovered in the wall of the house in Łazińsk and of other utterances of the cantor.

[^69]: Hubert Orłowski, ibid., p. 196.

[^70]: Natalia Stawarz, ibid., accessed 25.02.2023, 13:30.

<!-- s. 189 [PDF 189] -->
 the nation and of the greatest sacrifices, so also in each of these letters there will be found many, sometimes very emotional, assurances of submission to God.
He writes: "Gladly we wish to do everything for the Führer and the fatherland, but to become a traitor and a Judas to ourselves and to our conscience, and to the Lord of all lords – that is something no one may demand, and which a reasonable man (certainly also the Führer) will not demand. If they (those overzealous improvers of the world) do not want us as we are, do not want to have need of us, then let them 'dismiss' us. Better to follow this simple, ordinary life than to remain with a broken conscience and without moral support in this bloody – grave period"!

He therefore congratulates his brother that, despite being honoured with important offices, he wants to remain faithful to his faith, that he does not act like the many who for a miserable Judas's pay sold their Lord. "It may displease someone that in accordance with our conscience we seek in our dear, unsullied Church inner care and strength". We do not have to choose (presumably it was expected of the brother that, together with taking up the office, he would sign? some ideological? declarations), "we already fought in the hard period of Poland, and woe to us if we depart from our, so clearly marked, path. The decision, therefore, lies on the other side: whether they will allow believing people to work, or whether they will want to get rid of them". (Presumably the second option was chosen, for from another letter it is known that Gustav was soon called up to service in the navy.) "Now, when the time is dangerous, we must remain with the Lord. Our lot and that of our people is in His hands. Before the war, 'in the hard period of Poland', we abode in the faith, whereas in the young Wartheland everyone intoxicated themselves with 'the new fame'. They abandoned it and fell away far". This "weakening" in participation in the services was also described in his letter by the pastor Gross. Bruno confesses that he too was not free of that sin. Yet at this moment, when the Lord has granted them the grace of conversion and forbearance, "they wish to fight knightly, unto blood", so that nothing evil befalls them.

The letter is written by Bruno Mielke in the first days of May 1944. It is puzzling that the remarks concerning the course of the war were not questioned 
<!-- s. 190 [PDF 190] -->
 by the censor. Is this "loosening" of control perhaps a testimony to broader moods in the army? Never before in the writings available to us had he noted reflections of this kind. Although he does not say it outright, he nevertheless tries to prepare his nearest "for the worst" – that is, for the occupation of the Wartheland by the troops advancing from the east. "The war situation gives me much to think about. We want to fight and struggle to the last – but must we not look at the dangers – precisely as realistically and soberly looking Germans? I do not wish to see the lamentation, tears, suffering with which our nation is visited, should the threatening dangers materialise! How they will then treat you, the poorest of all, the liberated Poles! May God give us the strength to endure and preserve us from misery! But perhaps God wishes already to bring all matters to an end, then this is by no means excluded. And therefore we ought to persevere in the faith; then even the greatest cares will not overtake us. There is also another thought-provoking sentence. "We wish also to forgive those and to ask (for grace) for them whom we by our conduct have hurt and in this way have threatened their souls". The letter ends with the honest confession that shivers run through him at the thought that once he urged his brother to move to Konin! "It is the grace of God that in this time moving towards destruction we have this quiet island". He wishes them "to live through in Łazińsk many sunny and happy days...".

This and other letters show to what an extent the war destroyed, and at the same time laid bare, the complicated sense of national identity, the consciousness of one's own roots, but also of simple human decency, of many German settlers. For we often read of the "wonderfully simple and therefore beautiful childhood", of the joyful days lived through in Łazińsk, while at the same time Mielke writes that before the war this was for them "the hard period of Poland". In these accounts there is a peculiar alienation, a kind of extraterritoriality of the land that fed them. The system of "values" which the Nazi ideologues had for years been instilling into the Germans and which came to reign in the conquered Wartheland shakes, or rather suppresses in these pious people the age-old moral order built on the biblical Ten Commandments. There is no word of protest 
<!-- s. 191 [PDF 191] -->
 at what the authority does, whose orders are diligently carried out. They made slaves of their neighbours, they killed the patriots, they plundered their houses and land, and they still believe that "in this time moving towards destruction", on the ruins of the old ideals, they will preserve their sunny land.

This is a very important and dramatic picture of a man fitted, through a cruel and ruthless system, into ultimate situations, a man cornered by great history. For the members of the family to whom he wrote, such repeated declarations of a man who fought for many months in the front line and claimed that everything that befell him happened "by the grace of God" must have had a weight and force of arguments not to be shaken. For this is perceptible – even in this comparatively modest group of letters – that regardless of whether he is marching to the East to build the promised paradise, or retreating under the growing pressure of the adversary, he never gives a sign that he is going through a crisis of faith. Still his chief dream is to become a pastor, to celebrate the services and other rites and to proclaim the word of God. "It is not excluded that God will have to lead us all through very hard roads". He did not abandon his God for the new "filthy" faith. But – and this remark is worth raising – the image of the Christian:
the tiny, humble "servant of God" which the Nazi propaganda derided, does not entirely accord with what we perceive in the attitude of this soldier. He fights in the front line, kills enemies and lice, is tough and does not want to be worse, nor weaker, than his comrades. He differs, perhaps, from some of them in that he sees, or only has a presentiment of, the coming days of defeat. In this sense one may risk the remark that he did not walk those evil paths in darkness. Another matter is whether the light that shone for him was clear to the end.

In reading Bruno Mielke's letters, one gains the impression that they were written in two languages. Personal experiences, care for the family, confessional avowals – this is vocabulary straight out of the Bible, religious writings and sentimental ballads about love. Here even the descriptions of the side against which he is fighting do not "reek" of hatred. These are Russians, adversaries, in the sharpest version the enemy. Whereas the pre-war times are an idyllic picture of "blessed hours" and of the happy 
<!-- s. 192 [PDF 192] -->
 inhabitants of Łazińsk. Quite different phrases flow when he declares his fidelity and obedience to the Führer, his readiness for every sacrifice for the sake of the great Germany repairing the world. This is a set of ready-made clichés from the language of the Third Reich (LTI – Lingua Tertii Imperii).

The last of the surviving letters is dated 14 May 1944 in Norway. They must therefore have been transferred fairly quickly to an entirely new place. The field post number 48 341 remained the same. The letter travelled by air, as the *Luftfeldpost* stamps stuck on the envelope attest. This is interesting information in the context of what was to happen three months later. Namely, on 4 September the Finns halted their participation in the fighting with the Red Army, and on the 19th of that month an armistice was signed between the USSR and Finland. One of its conditions was the participation of the Finnish troops in an attack on the German troops, which were withdrawing from Lapland to Norway. And so it happened. Already from 15 September the Finns are fighting on the side of the Allies, together with their former and quite recent enemy: the Red Army. In October there will set out with full force the operation of the Red Army – as it was named, Petsamo-Kirkenes – as a result of which the German 20th Mountain Army was pushed out of the northern territories of Norway and Finland. Bruno writes: "How it will be for me here and to what extent my wishes will be fulfilled here remains in the hands of God. His will has led me here, to His guidance I wish to continue entrusting myself".

How does he understand his part in this war? As an act of the divine will. Is this war, then, also the work of God?

From the company in which he had been until recently he was suddenly transferred to a medical company. He writes the letter, as usual, on Sunday afternoon. In the holy forenoon hours they had known no peace. They had to scrub the rooms and clean the weapons. Just before dinner he managed to slip away to a quiet place for "the eternal word". The last sentences of this letter, the last sentences of the 33-year-old Bruno Mielke that have come down to us, are the words of a man who seems to perceive the whole tragedy of the war. In them there is also a muted trace that something is beginning to stir in his conscience. "When I returned, two pretty, little fair Norwegian girls came up shyly to our soldiers' kitchen with a vessel, so as to be given something. To my joy 
<!-- s. 193 [PDF 193] -->
 I still had with me a roll of sugar sweets (*Zuckerchen*). I gave them to the smaller of them, who, like her sister, had on her neck a little casket with a Christ's cross. A quiet 'thank you very much' and the bright gaze of the blue children's eyes was for me the thank-offering. I should not like to see that child again! (Quietly I prayed about it). This, together with the previous community of prayer, brings peace into the heart which the world does not know and cannot give".

\*

A broad search in the Polish and German sources available on the internet, and enquiries into the further fate of Bruno Mielke, brought no results. The letter of 14 May 1944 is the last material trace he left behind.

*You walk in darkness, and your ignorance is like the cloak of night*[^71].

In the last year of the war the 2. Gebirgs Division is transferred from Norway to Denmark. Later it took part in heavy fighting against the Western Allies at Trier and Philippsburg – Heilbronn. Part of the division ends the war in Württemberg; the remaining units capitulated in the Tyrol[^72].

**Acknowledgements**

Work difficult to overpraise was done by the translators from German. Without their creative, patient and responsible work it would not have been possible to come to know the thoughts, avowals and accounts of their senders recorded in the letters and cards. In alphabetical order I note the translators whose renderings were used in the present text:



[^71]: The rabbi's words to Father Józef Suryn in Jarosław Iwaszkiewicz's story *Matka Joanna od Aniołów*.

[^72]: After: Wikipedia, accessed 19.11.2023, 18:06.

<!-- s. 194 [PDF 194] -->

**Aneta Gorzelańczyk** – letters dated: 02.02.42, 30.04.42, 21.10.42.  
**Jacek Kin** – letters dated: 15.07.36, 01.10.36, 09.03.37, 24.07.40, 29.06.41, 08.07.41, 22.10.41, 01.05.42, 17.06.42, 12.07.42, 16.07.42, 01.08.42, 13.09.42, 22.10.42, 23.10.42, 28.10.42, 10.11.42, 12.11.42, 17.11.42, 20.12.42, 05.03.43, 17.01.43, 28.01.43, 30.01.43, 28.03.43, 02.09.43, 07.03.44, 14.05.44.
