<!-- s. 195 [PDF 195] -->
### The Doctor's wife: the splendour and fall of Janina Breede


Katarzyna Połom



Janina Breede at the age of 38

They say that wherever she appeared, she instantly drew attention. There was something about her – whether in her bearing or in her gaze – that made it impossible to pass her by with indifference. Attractive, with a tall yet graceful figure, she would stroll the streets of Zagórów, and the heels of her elegant shoes beat out a measured rhythm of footsteps. That distinctive sound echoed among the buildings of the pre-war little town.

Her outfits – as if straight out of a fashion magazine – cut by a skilled tailor's hand, fit perfectly.

– Oh, look, there goes Mrs Breede… – people would say, following the woman with their eyes, and in those voices admiration was sometimes mixed with a certain measure of envy.

The wife of the popular doctor Hugo Breede kept her head high, carefully coiffed and topped with a fashionable little hat. Her hand, clad in a glove suited to the season, gripped the handle of her handbag. Right beside her strutted a stately manservant, carrying his mistress's purchases. The elegant lady was recognised and greeted from afar. She would bow slightly; she was famous for her impeccable manners. Among the people captured in a photograph taken in 1938 to commemorate the ceremony of the consecration of the rectory of the Lutheran church in Zagórów, I recognise her unmistakably: she sits second from the right in the front row, at her husband's side. Hugo Breede is quite a distinctive figure: a stout, rather bald man with protruding ears, in round spectacles on a nose of considerable size, and with a moustache fashionable in the 1930s. I note that the Doctor's wife is one of only four women in this picture.

<!-- s. 196 [PDF 196] -->



Dr Hugo Breede, Janina Breede's husband.


Photo from the archive of Bogdan Grzonkowski.

She has an upright figure, her legs gracefully crossed at the ankles, and in her hands, folded on her knees, she holds gloves. She looks into the lens and smiles faintly; she appears somewhat weary. Here she is exactly my age – she is 39. Over her shoulders she has thrown an impressive stole of natural fur.

– She and my mother-in-law had identical furs, – recalls Mrs Halina Cieślewicz, whom one day I take in for a good grilling. – As soon as my father-in-law returned from America, he gave his wife such a gift. He travelled to Warsaw especially for that fur. Barely a few days had passed, and Mrs Breede had one just the same!

Not only in matters of fashion did she like to keep up with the times – I think she liked to stand out, to dazzle, to shine in company. It is possible she had grown used to this as early as childhood, being, after all, a daughter of one of the finest Zagórów families. She came into the world in 1899 as Janina Franciszka Stawicka, daughter of Michał and Joanna née Synoracki. Her father held the office of mayor of Zagórów in the years 1919–1921. There is no doubt that young Janina received a careful upbringing at home, and later on – having first completed the appropriate schools – she herself began work as a teacher. Brilliant, elegant, not bad-looking – she was an excellent match and must certainly have aroused the interest of men. Little wonder that young Hugo Breede, a medical student staying in Zagórów on an internship (earlier as an interpreter with the War Commandant), lost his head over her and proposed even before finishing his studies. Their wedding took place on 29 July 1922 in the Catholic church under the invocation of Sts. Peter and Paul. Janina was of the Catholic faith, her chosen one was an Evangelical. In accordance with the prevailing custom they raised their two sons in this way, Georg and Hugo: one in the Catholic faith, the other as an Evangelical. At first the newlyweds lived in the in-laws' house at Kościelna Street 12. There too the young doctor ran his medical practice. Before long the Breedes moved to Berdychów, where they occupied in turn several different lodgings. Eventually they settled in a spacious and beautiful house with a garden, built at the turn of 
<!-- s. 197 [PDF 197] -->
 1931 and 1932. In it the doctor opened a clinic, and in one of the previous houses, after prior reconstruction, he organised a small hospital.

In the doctor's surgery, splendidly equipped for those times, there was even the most modern X-ray apparatus! Breede was therefore praised as a specialist, people gladly turned to him for advice, and long after the war he was remembered as a kind and devoted physician who often helped the poorest free of charge. Good renown was paired with social prestige, which the Doctor's wife prized so highly. In the interwar period, like her husband, Janina Breede sat on the town council, among the most important personages of the Zagórów of that day. She took part willingly in social organisations, including those of a patriotic character. She was active in the ranks of the Polska Organizacja Wojskowa (the Polish Military Organisation), and was even among the members of the delegation taking part in Marshal Piłsudski's funeral. Her husband Hugo was chairman of the Union of Reservists of the Polish-Bolshevik War and a man engaged in the social life of Zagórów.

– Even in her maiden days Mrs Breede ran a Sokolic group[^73] – recalls Mrs Barbara Nowakowska, whose mother belonged to this organisation.

The Doctor's wife would travel away with the girls to camps in the vicinity of Warsaw and in the capital itself. Sports competitions, games and movement exercises, learning national songs – the cultivation of a love of sport and of the Homeland – all this was for the members of the group not only a patriotic duty but also an undoubted adventure. They were also impressed by their leader: an elegant, intelligent young lady from a well-to-do, educated family of patriotic views was no small authority for the girls. Janina continued her activity even after marriage.

After the war some will begin to relate that the closer to the year 1939, the more doubts was roused by Janina Breede's overzealousness in performing 


[^73]: The women's section of the Sokół Gymnastic Society.

<!-- s. 198 [PDF 198] -->
 functions in such organisations. It is said that the inhabitants of Zagórów active in nationalist groups could not shake off the impression that Breede wanted to know too much about "certain matters" – as my interlocutors put it enigmatically. In September 1939 the situation was already clear: she and her husband signed the Volksliste, proclaiming their belonging to the German nation. I am not surprised that Hugo Breede did so – he considered himself a German. But she – a Pole from a worthy family? Today it is truly hard to grasp. Why did she let herself be seduced by German propaganda? What enthralled her in the words and gestures of the Führer? How did it come about that she believed the tales of a master race told by an ungrown bawler with a tiny moustache? Was she driven by fear of losing her hitherto social status? Did she fear poverty, humiliation? Did she see in the Hitlerite expansion a chance to improve her hitherto existence? Or perhaps – quite simply – she was faithful to the marriage vow she made to Hugo on the day of the wedding: "I vow to you love, fidelity and conjugal honesty, and that I will not leave you until death…"? We shall not learn this today; we cannot ask her about it – the dead will not defend their own case today. They say she was already mad then. Then, when she eagerly sent her sons to the Hitlerjugend, where the boys quickly learned to treat Poles with contempt. Then, when in black officer's coats and a fitted costume she drilled the firefighters in front of the seat of the Zagórów volunteer fire brigade…

– I remember from childhood how Breede, in black officer's coats, shouted in German at our lads from the fire brigade – Tadeusz Molski relates in a telephone conversation. – It was something nasty. Why in German? After all, she knew Polish perfectly… She consistently and in unison with her husband assured the families of the Peowiaks arrested in 1939 that the men were alive, albeit sent to forced labour. She told everyone far and wide that on the radio Hitler had promised that there would be no revenge for the previous war on the Poles, so there was nothing to worry about. There exists the account of a witness of those events, from which it follows that it was precisely Breede who warned 
<!-- s. 199 [PDF 199] -->
 Romuald Brzeg[^74] of the arrest. Can we take this as a certainty? Did the Breede couple really know all along what fate the mayor Juliusz Diesterheft had in store for the participants of the "Barka" action? Or did they too deceive themselves to the end that the tortured arrestees would be taken away by the gendarmes to forced labour? We do not know that… We can only surmise. Yet if they really did know, more than one person could have envied them nerves of steel. For that is what would have been needed, to assure the families of the victims with a stone face that their loved ones were not threatened with execution.

During the successive days of the occupation, a frequent guest in the doctor couple's home was Walery Sławek, a doctor from Rzgów. It is said the Breedes secured him and his family safety from deportation – so, among other things, Mrs Wanda Jurkiewiczowa told me in a telephone conversation. And Dr Sławek additionally learned a great deal from his more experienced colleague. It is said that even before the war the couples of doctors knew each other and were friends. At the beginning of the war Henryk Pluciński and his mother also often stayed at the Breedes'. Not as guests, of course – the doctor employed them to work around the house. Franciszek Pluciński, Henryk's father, was one of the victims of the German revenge for the "Barka". The wife and son of the murdered man avoided deportation to the Protectorate only thanks to the intercession of the German doctor.

– Pluciński would not suffer a bad word about Dr Breede. About her he did not much feel like talking – says Andrzej Bernat, a well-known regionalist. My interlocutor repeats to me the story he heard from Henryk Pluciński about the causes of the doctor's premature death. It is said Breede with full awareness infected the wound on his leg, broken once again in an accident. He wanted to be unfit for military service and not to end up on the Eastern Front. He was convinced that this war was already lost for the Germans, because no one had yet defeated the Soviets on their own land. And the German army needed doctors.



[^74]: Jarecki, M., *Peowiacy*. *Pierwsze ofiary okupacji niemieckiej w Zagórowie*. Zagórów 2001, p. 135.

<!-- s. 200 [PDF 200] -->

– He had a premonition that if he ended up in the East, the Russians would kill him there. And if they came here, to Zagórów, he would lose his life that way too. So if he can still choose, he prefers to die in his own home – Pluciński related to Andrzej Bernat.

Hugo Breede, however, probably did not suspect that death would outpace the Soviets and pay him a final visit in the very same year in which the conscious infection took place. The Doctor's wife was widowed in the winter of 1941. From then on she managed the house on Berdychów Street alone. Once again I give the floor to Halina Cieślewiczowa:

– The school they attended stood on Kościelna Street, where the primary school was. And my neighbour, Mrs Matelska, recounted that once, as she was walking past that school, one of the German pupils ran up and knocked the beret off her head.

I can guess what the aforementioned Matelska may have felt. Humiliation, anger at the cub's prank, and at the same time fear, effectively stifling the reflex of protest in defence of her own dignity. And suddenly something happens that she does not expect:

– One of the Breede brothers, who saw this, seized his schoolmate firmly by the nape of the neck and ordered: "Pick it up and apologise to her! She is my neighbour and you must show her respect. It does no harm that she is a Pole!".

A brave gesture, I think to myself. The boys, indoctrinated in the Hitlerjugend, had nevertheless retained some human reflexes, contrary to the principles then drummed into them.

– All in all, they were not bad boys… – Mrs Halina agrees with me. – It was only the times that were bad for everyone…

Unfortunately, I did not hear more testimonies of good deeds towards Poles on the part of the young men. I only know that both sons of the doctor couple struggled to the end of their lives with the memories and pangs of conscience caused by their membership of the aforementioned youth organisation. People used to relate that Janina Breede loyally cooperated with the occupier throughout the war. At its very end she came by bicycle to one homestead in Anielewo, accompanied by some 
<!-- s. 201 [PDF 201] -->
 German. Probably someone had informed that the householders were helping partisans, hence this unexpected visit. The woman had a basket of victuals with her, the man was dressed in civilian clothes. They sat down with their companion, to the terror of the householders, calmly waiting for the hungry men to appear. Meanwhile the householder, old Kazimierski, called his then ten-year-old son Jan and instructed him: "Listen, Feluś, run and tell them [the partisans] not to come for dinner today, because there are Germans here". The quick-witted boy slipped out unnoticed and warned the fighters. So says one of the local stories, repeated when Mrs the Doctor's wife is being recalled. What only slightly astonishes me is the fact that only two people took part in the ambush on the armed soldiers of the underground, one of them a woman. Or perhaps there is something we do not know?

In any case, in 1945 the ground began to slip from under proud Breede's feet. And when the Soviet troops came to liberate Zagórów, the inhabitants of the little town knew perfectly well whom to point out to the Soviet soldiers as an enemy of the allied nation. Among these persons was also the hated Doctor's wife.

– Fleeing from the Red Army men, she hid in our yard… – relates Halina Cieślewiczowa on the basis of her mother-in-law's stories. – There she jumped into the well. It was January… And once those Russians had searched the whole house, the whole homestead, the cellars, the outbuildings and had gone away, only then did she begin to call for help. Someone hauled her out of the well, turning the crank, and she held onto that chain and stood in the bucket. When she came out she was all soaked and terrified. On the other side of the street was the bakery of the Matelskis; she fled there and undressed there. She climbed up onto the bread oven; Matelska gave her some blanket, in which Breede wrapped herself, and her clothes dried by the oven. She herself told me about that incident, of course already after the war.

Mrs Halina relates it all so vividly that with my mind's eye I already see that dramatic picture: a shivering, humiliated, terrified woman huddles under the blanket handed over by the baker's wife, swallowing tears – in a whisper she gives thanks for the rescue. And she listens tensely to whether somewhere a foreign voice with its characteristic singsong accent does not yet 
<!-- s. 202 [PDF 202] -->
 speak up. The Matelskis' dwelling, abandoned today, and the aforementioned bread oven intrigue me more and more… Fear of the Poles' revenge accompanied Janina Breede for a long time yet. When Dr Sławek settled in Zagórów, he was assigned the house and surgery of the German doctor. Driven out of her own home, Breede took shelter for a time with her sister in Łódź. The sons left for Germany, where at first they hired themselves out to work in a carpentry shop. They worked hard; after a few years they managed to improve their living conditions and gain an education. But Breede did not go to the West. She stayed. Mrs Halina relates:

– She was so afraid of the Poles, of the people of Zagórów, that when they came to her and her sister in Łódź, fleeing, she jumped out of a first-floor window.

What, then, prompted her to return to the little town? I do not know. But she returned. Perhaps she had hopes of regaining the house, calling to mind the good relations with the Sławek family? She must have been bitterly disappointed, for the new occupants would not even let her into her former home. Cieślewiczowa recalls:

– Up there, in the attic, lived Dr Sławek's mother. I myself heard more than once how Breede called out: "Wanda, Wanda, come to me! For I cannot go in there, he will not let me in…".

Someone else told me that the desperate woman would sometimes climb onto the roof and cling to the chimney; she had to be taken off it by force. The more incidents of this kind there were, the more firmly the conviction began to seep into the consciousness of the inhabitants of Zagórów that the Doctor's wife had "gone off her head", yet few pitied her. Mad, then mad – said some – she had it coming… Mad? Was she really, though? Perhaps, at first, feigning madness secured her a measure of inviolability? Despised and pushed away by the majority, but with time increasingly regarded as a harmless madwoman, she slowly ceased to be associated with the dangerous "German woman". Halina Cieślewiczowa seems to think likewise:

– Oh, she was a very complicated personality… She was always interested in everything and would discreetly enquire how things were going with her husband at the mine, 
<!-- s. 203 [PDF 203] -->



A commemorative photograph on the steps in front of the Common Room of the State Military Organisation and the Union of Reservists of the Polish-Bolshevik War. Dr Breede (the bald man in spectacles) stands in the second row, third from the left.


Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat.



The consecration of the rectory of the Evangelical-Augsburg church in Zagórów, 1938. Janina Breede sits in the front row, second from the right. Beside her, first from the right – Dr Hugo Breede.


Photo from the archive of Andrzej Bernat.



The grave of the Breede family at the Zagórów parish cemetery, in the Evangelical section.


Photo by Katarzyna Połom.

<!-- s. 204 [PDF 204] -->
 and what news there was. She was not, however, intrusive. Perhaps it was mere curiosity? I do remember, though, that once, at a session of the gromada council in Oleśnica, Mr Janicki came and said that Mrs Breede was a German spy! That she comes to the forest at twelve o'clock at night, that she has a radio station and is in contact with her son, the one in the army. I do not believe it – the Doctor's wife almost a Mata Hari – the grotesque association flits through my mind. Somewhat amused, I enquire whether that was long after the war and where the certainty came from in the first place that some radio station existed at all? I learn that this story broke in the 1960s, that the Russians located and confiscated a radio station at one of the farms in Imielno. At the same time it turned out that in Ląd one of the priests (who was not a priest at all, but only passed himself off as one – Mrs Halina explains to me) was also a German informant in the monastery. Both he and Breede were, according to my interlocutor, caught out. Interesting. Did the doctor's widow really "broadcast" for the needs of the German army, or did she simply, as a mother, want to make contact with her son? Why, after the radio station was confiscated, did she not bear the consequences of the alleged spying? Today it will be hard to verify.


– I remember Mrs Breede… Though by the time I saw her more often, there was no longer any real contact with her. After what she had been through, she was closed in on herself and distrustful. She lived in the attic of the house on Konińska Street. Though ill, she always tried to dress tastefully and neatly. Her faded and unfashionable clothes must once have been elegant and attested to the class of their owner. I remember she wore a little hat without a brim – old-fashioned, but very chic. On her hands she wore summer gloves… – Małgorzata Kasprzak tells me. Barbara Karczmarek, née Kasprzak, an inhabitant of Zagórów from birth, echoes her:

– Yes, I remember too. Poor, thin and stooped, once certainly a blonde. She would sort of slink along the walls as if she did not want to be noticed. My mama was always enchanted by her way of speaking; one could see at once that she was a highly educated woman. But when mama wanted to help her out, giving her new clothes – Breede would politely but firmly refuse.

<!-- s. 205 [PDF 205] -->

Even so, one can see that she was unable to bear the weight of human pity. Though with each year things must have grown harder for her. She lived in extreme poverty, suffered cold and hunger, and was buffeted at almost every step.

– I think Breede's life ended with the end of the war, when the sons fled Poland, the doctor was already dead, and she stayed here and had to come face to face with the inhabitants of Zagórów. That was the end of her life… – said one of my interlocutors. – After that, it seems, she was doing penance on earth for her sins as if in purgatory…

Barbara Nowakowska tells me how, soon after the war – despite her own difficult situation – the widow of Dr Breede helped my interlocutor's mother in caring for the children.

– She used to visit our house very often and was treated almost like a member of the family, like a friend. When mama sometimes had to go away, Mrs Breede would look after us, help with homework. It was not constant, but it happened fairly often. That is precisely how I remember her. I have the most delightful memories of her from my childhood. She was always perfectly proper towards me and my mama. I remember how beautifully she dressed me in a little dress for the nativity play, when I took part in the performance…

Barbara's mother remembered Breede from the time when the latter ran the Sokolic group and held her in respect, so there was no question of treating her badly in her home, even though she knew what was said in Zagórów about the Doctor's wife.

– She was no monster, although many may have been put off by her grand manner from pre-war times and her habit of expecting obedience from others – says Mrs Nowakowska. – But even if some of what she was later accused of was true, she paid a very high price for it all.

Despised by society, she lived in the attic, slept in a heap of rags and cardboard, together with the mice – so I was told. All the same she tried to keep up appearances to the last moment, and for kindness shown her, she repaid in kind – with a small favour, help with studies, a kind word. She gave the children tuition at Mrs Marczukowa's, and sometimes 
<!-- s. 206 [PDF 206] -->
 she would sit on a bench in the park and help the children solve their mathematics problems.

– She taught my youngsters too. Before they went to school, she would come to them – Cieślewiczowa recalls. – By way of thanks I always fed her. If she came on a Sunday, I gave her dinner; she warmed herself at our place, because at home she was cold.

I ask my interlocutor how she recalls her acquaintance with the Doctor's wife.

– I knew her from this side, as she came along so hungry, so frozen… I did not look at her through the prism of her past; I came to know her as a very cultured, warm lady. What weighed on her conscience from the time of the occupation, I do not judge. Perhaps she had no choice but to behave as she did. It is hard for us to judge now… The young do not understand what times those were and what situations. – Mrs Halina smiles at the memories. – My husband always used to tease me, saying: "Oh, look, your friend is coming!" And I would say to him: "Don't mock! You never know what life will bring to anyone! You may be someone great, and later you are nothing…".

Most of the inhabitants of Zagórów of the post-war generation remembered her as an eccentric, tragicomic figure, pinning on her for good the label of a pauper – a madwoman. In the 1970s she trotted along the little town's streets, startled-looking, gaunt, with two goats on a lead. In no way did she resemble the pre-war elegant lady with the proudly raised brow. She pastured her animals either by the narrow-gauge railway or by the windmill on Lidmanowska Street. The owner of the windmill, Mr Chmielewski, often chased off the boys playing football with the words: "Do not destroy my grass here, it has to be nice for the goats!". Andrzej Bernat, who came to Zagórów in 1972, had not known this figure, exotic to him, before. Once, during a game of football by the aforementioned windmill, he asked one of his pals who that was.

– I heard in reply, literally, that she was the wife of the old German doctor who had made a Holocaust of the local Jews. As a teenager I could not quite fit it into my head; I thought to myself: what a woman that must have been!

<!-- s. 207 [PDF 207] -->

Only years later, already as a regionalist researching the history of our little town, did my interlocutor put the facts together into a whole…

Mirosław Słowiński remembered Breede from childhood:

– More than once I saw how an old, gaunt woman in a nightshirt, with grey windswept hair, chopped wood on the block at midnight. That was on Konińska Street, exactly opposite the site of the kantorówka, where we now have "Gęsi Puch". That was precisely Janina Breede of the Stawicki family. That image cannot be forgotten. Death claimed Janina Breede in August of the year one thousand nine hundred and seventy-five. She had just been visiting friends, the Szymkowiaks, to whom she was showing the wedding photographs of her sons, sent from abroad. The great emotion evidently caused her tired heart to refuse obedience. Breede collapsed limply to the ground. An ambulance was called, but the woman died on the way to the Słupca hospital, never regaining consciousness. Her body was handed over to the town authorities, and the burial was carried out as quickly as possible. The then head of the town did not permit a Catholic funeral, so in enormous haste the deceased's body was driven to the cemetery and buried in the Evangelical section, as if ridding oneself of a shameful burden. Not one of the deceased's close acquaintances accompanied the coffin. Her old and still well-disposed friends did not manage to bid Mrs Breede farewell: Cieślewiczowa and Marczukowa, as well as her cousin Tenia[^75].

– There was no way of catching up with the coffin being carried. Before we reached the grave, it was all over. – Mrs Halina is bitter about this to this day.

It is said the curious gravedigger did not fail to peek into the coffin before covering it with earth: the greatest elegant lady of pre-war Zagórów was buried just as she had died – in poor clothing fastened with a safety pin and in worn-out shoes.

One of the sons of the Breedes, Georg, came to Zagórów in 1993. He met with members of his mother's family and with persons connected with her. One of them was my interlocutor, Wanda Jurkiewiczowa, 


[^75]: Tekla Frankiewicz, née Stawicka.

<!-- s. 208 [PDF 208] -->
 who invited Georg and his relative to dinner and accompanied him on a sentimental stroll around Berdychów. The man could view the family home only from the street; its then inhabitants did not agree to let him inside. Georg Breede followed in his father's footsteps and himself became a doctor, and his brother – an architect. Both missed their mother and took the news of her death hard. Yet it took fully twenty years before Georg decided to travel to his mother's home town. Hugo, tormented by memories from the war period, never managed to cope with his pangs of conscience and could not bring himself to come with his brother. He was ashamed because of the errors of his youth.

Wanda Jurkiewiczowa says:

– You see, madam, Dr Breede, father and son, were in my opinion honest people. Georg did not know how to reward all those who looked after his mother after the war. It pained him greatly that she was left alone in Poland. We were together at the grave of his parents and grandmother.

Standing at the grave of the Breede spouses, resting together in the Zagórów cemetery, I cannot resist pondering their story, above all the fate of the tough Doctor's wife. The words of one of Coma's songs appear in my head of their own accord and crawl onto my lips in a whisper: "Only now do I know how low falls he who did not fulfil his time in humility…"[^76].

I carried out most of the conversations that enabled me to attempt a portrait of so ambiguous a figure as Janina Breede over the span of two years (2020–2022). I met my interlocutors in person or conducted telephone conversations. A great help was also the article by Bogdan Grzonkowski entitled "In Memory of Dr Hugo Breede", published in Gazeta Słupecka no. 22(145) of 3. 06. 1993.



[^76]: Coma. *Zaprzepaszczone siły wielkiej armii świętych znaków*, from the album of the same title, 2006.

<!-- s. 209 [PDF 209] -->

I never had the opportunity to meet Janina Breede – I came into the world almost 10 years after her death. After many years I came to know the wider story. Being aware that human memories are always subjective, I tried to listen attentively to every account and, from the information conveyed, to put together a portrait as close to the real one as possible. But who the Doctor's wife Breede really was, probably only she herself knew.

For help in gathering information about Janina Breede I warmly thank:

Halina Cieślewicz, Barbara Nowakowska, Piotr Staszak, Wanda Jurkiewiczowa, Józef Jurkiewicz, Renata and Krzysztof Nowinowski, Andrzej Bernat, Małgorzata Kasprzak, Barbara Karczmarek, Tadeusz Molski

<!-- s. 210 [PDF 210] -->

<!-- s. 211 [PDF 211] -->
## Memories and Reflections


<!-- s. 212 [PDF 212] -->

<!-- s. 213 [PDF 213] -->

### Welcoming the Reverend Shepherd


Professor Lech Słowiński about his home town of Zagórów

The little town's cowherd, "Mr Matuszak", was an intriguing figure and a joker of a kind you rarely meet. He grazed the town's cattle for many years and all the farmers were happy with him. He knew perfectly well where, on the vast meadows along the Warta, the grass had already been grazed down, where it was flooded with water, scorched by the sun, and where it had already grown back. Besides, "Mr Matuszak" was a good diplomat and never came into conflict with the Zagórów gooseherds, always finding some kind of compromise in the dispute between cattle and geese. And there were far more geese on the Zagórów meadows than cows. Their numbers, at times, ran into the thousands. Hence the constant quarrels between the gooseherds and the owners of cows, whose enormous flocks of geese ate and fouled the grass with dung. It was thanks to "Mr Matuszak's" wise manoeuvring that open war between the gooseherds and the farmers never broke out. He proceeded on the assumption that there was grass enough for both the cows and the geese; one only had to move the herds of these creatures about in a reasonably sensible way, so that the one did not get in the way of the other. I have already mentioned above that "Mr Matuszak" had an incredible sense of humour, and it was this that won everyone over to him.

I remember that in the early 1930s the bishop of Włocławek, Father Karol Maria Radoński, announced a pastoral visit to Zagórów. The town did its utmost to receive the distinguished guest worthily. All the streets were swept, every blade of grass was plucked from the cobbles, the pavements were washed, and the window panes, gleaming with cleanliness, were adorned with holy pictures. Even the Jews and the Protestants joined in these general preparations. The Father Bishop was to be welcomed at the entrance to the town coming from the direction of Słupca and Ląd. The fire brigade and other organizations prepared, right next to 
<!-- s. 214 [PDF 214] -->
 the Jewish cheder, a triumphal gate entwined with laurel wreaths. At the top of the gate there was an inscription: "We Welcome the Reverend Shepherd". As it happened, the Father Bishop's entry into the town fell at the time when the cattle were returning from pasture. Beside the triumphal gate a crowd had gathered, headed by the mayor of the town and the town councillors, the parish priest, and a good number of local priests. Next to the mayor and the parish priest stood also, with bread and salt on a tray, the local rabbi, and beside him the Evangelical pastor. Behind their backs, in ranks, this time in agreement, stood two rival bands – the fire brigade's and that of the "Sokół" Gymnastic Society. Further along the road were drawn up ranks of scouts, Sokół members, riflemen and firemen, and a little ahead, on either side of the Słupca road, schoolchildren with little flags. To meet the bishop there set out a mounted unit hastily mobilized from among the local youth, splendidly adorned with ribbons. In short – a parade such as the town had not seen for a long time. Unfortunately, one mistake was made, for the whole assembly was located on the route along which the cattle returned from pasture, and the cattle, being cattle, did not consider it necessary to behave any differently than on any other day: it liked to gambol about, nibble something on the way, and relieve itself wherever it pleased. "Mr Matuszak" sensed the scandal hanging in the air, and wanting to curb the more aggressive cows, moved on foot to the head of the frisky herd. The cows slowed their pace, astonished by a crowd of people such as they had never encountered before, and our cowherd, with a dignified countenance, passed under the triumphal gate with the inscription: "We Welcome the Reverend Shepherd", bowed to the mayor, the councillors, the priests and the other dignitaries of the town gathered there, took the bread from the astonished rabbi's hands and uttered with dignity the following words:

– Thank you all most heartily for this solemn welcome. At least once the town has recognized my work. God reward you for it.

A small commotion ensued, and the cowherd, with his herd and his loaf of bread, escorted by the laughter of the gathered crowd, marched off down the sand-strewn streets of the little town. Unfortunately, shortly after he had passed, the limousine with the bishop arrived, and there was no time left to clear away the mess left 
<!-- s. 215 [PDF 215] -->
 behind by the unruly cattle. All that could be done was to cover the more conspicuous traces with twigs of the little birches planted beside the gate or to sprinkle them lightly with sand. The limousine of the distinguished guest therefore had to drive not only over the flowers scattered by the children.

"Mr Matuszak" also had a nice son, Kazimierz, who in his mature years served for many years as the local gravedigger. People valued this man's services more than those of the two feuding local doctors – Breede and Lidmanowski. For Mr Kazimierz had the habit of looking in on a house where someone was ill; he liked to have a chat with the sick person, comfort him, tell him a joke, and by way of farewell would, just in case, take the measurements for his grave. After such a visit the patient would usually leap out of bed and quickly recover, without any need of calling the doctor and taking medicines[^77].



[^77]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

<!-- s. 216 [PDF 216] -->

<!-- s. 217 [PDF 217] -->

### Where there is a border, there is also a road. A conversation with Erich Kuhn


Wiesława Kowalska

Erich Kuhn, elder of the Evangelical-Augsburg congregation in Detmold in Germany, a former resident of Łazińsk in the Zagórów municipality. For his services to cooperation between Poles and Germans, in 2017 he received from the German state authorities the "Medal of Merit for Work towards International Understanding". Erich Kuhn is a descendant of the Hauländers who founded the village of Łazińsk in 1746 on the lands of the Trąbczyn estate, belonging to the spouses Paweł and Katarzyna Prusińska née Rozdrażewska. Erich is not sure where his ancestors came from. It could have been Pomerania, Brandenburg or Silesia. They were certainly Protestants and used German in their everyday life. They were free peasants with great skill in farming difficult terrain: dry or waterlogged, which is why the landowners were happy to bring in a group of Hauländers, granting them the hereditary lease of unused land. Erich Kuhn's family lived in Łazińsk without interruption for about 200 years. When the Russians entered in 1945, the men from the Hauländer families were deported to Russian labour camps, and by decision of the new communist authorities the Hauländer families, after the confiscation of all their possessions, became homeless, without means of subsistence and without any rights. A similar situation befell all the Hauländers of German origin of the Pyzdry Forest who remained in their homeland after 1945. Today Erich tells his story.

<!-- s. 218 [PDF 218] -->

Erich Kuhn, 2014

> *The father's home is a true paradise for the Heavenly Father.*  

> *Though you roamed the whole world, you would find no more beautiful one*…[^78]

#### Łazińsk – my family home…

I was born in Łazińsk in 1932. Nothing is left of our homestead on Łazińsk Drugi… Our house was made of iron ore. We had eight hectares of land. When my parents married, they received dowries from their parents. Before his wedding my father had also worked abroad. With the money they had saved they bought this land. My father, besides working on the farm, also did bricklaying and carpentry. He built houses and made furniture. That is mainly what we lived on. We had no horses, maybe two or three cows and some other animals… sheep, goats, poultry. Mama's name was Emma and Papa's was Adolf. Mama did all sorts of work in the house and on the farm, and she could also sew. She sewed everything for us. When I was five, I went to live with my father's mother, Loisa Kuhn née Fandrich, also in Łazińsk, not far from our house. My parents sent me there because there were rather a lot of us at home, nine children. They could not cope.

At Grandma's I had it very good. I was given sweets… I remember the Easter nests filled with sweets. At home things were not so rich. My older brothers worked far more in the field than I did. I, as the little one, gathered potatoes with them. We weeded the field together. Those were our basic tasks.



[^78]: This is a fragment of a song performed by the Zagórów choir "Quarta" during the annual reunion of members of the former Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Zagórów in the year 2014. To the meeting, combined with a solemn service, there came from Germany a large group of former Hauländers (settlers of Dutch law) originating from the Zagórów area, among them Erich Kuhn, who organized the group and led it to his family's homeland.

<!-- s. 219 [PDF 219] -->

#### School

To get to school I went through the forest to Huta. I had about two kilometres to walk one way… I took the shortcut to school across that hill of sand…

There was once no division of Łazińsk into Łazińsk Pierwszy and Łazińsk Drugi. Łazińsk was a very large Hauländer village. I remember that hill of white sand. It was not overgrown as it is today. It could be seen from the windows of our house. We used to come here to play. I loved coming here… I took the shortcut to school across that hill of sand, to the school on Łazińsk, today called Łazińsk Pierwszy. On the way I passed the village of Huta. I went there for at most two years. I started when the war broke out, for in 1939 I had just turned seven. Every day I crossed the high hill on which a windmill stood. At that time the school was also the house of prayer.

Next to the school there was a cemetery. The second cemetery for the inhabitants of Łazińsk was closer to Huta, on the way to school. The school building had two classrooms, with two separate entrances to them. One entered from both sides. There was one more building there, a smaller one, the so-called old school, but I no longer went there. I remember that as a child I very much wanted to learn to play the violin; I kept dreaming about it, talking about it, but life did not allow it. I finished only five grades. When the war ended I was eleven, and very quickly I had to become an adult. In post-war Poland I could no longer study. As a Hauländer of German origin I had to work in order to eat and live… We, the Hauländers, were blamed collectively for the Second World War.

#### They resettled us to Łazy…

When the Germans resettled us to Łazy at the beginning of 1940, I started going to school in Michalinów Trąbczyński. I remember that the shortcut to school went across the yard of a farmer whose name was Schulz. I was always afraid of his dogs, but the shortcut was tempting. The Germans sent a new teacher to the school in Michalinów, a young German woman whose task was 
<!-- s. 220 [PDF 220] -->
 to teach us in German. I remember that we once had an inter-school match in Orlina. A new teacher from Germany had come to the school in Orlina as well. I recognized her thanks to the film *People from the Iron Houses*, which was made in 2014 by Wika and Przemek Kowalski of Pyzdry. Her name was Margeritte Todt. At the end of the war she managed to flee from the Russians, and after the war, in Germany, she organized regular meetings with her former pupils, who came from these villages. Young German women who had completed their pedagogical studies were given work assignments during the war. They were sent precisely to villages like ours.

#### Treibball…

And the match in Orlina was won by our Trąbczyn school! It was not football, it was Treibball. A kind of team game with a smaller ball, like a volleyball. You had to be able to catch it well, throw it far and run fast in order to win. I was short, so nobody really counted on me. I was sent in last, but it was I who threw the ball the farthest behind the opponents' line and ran very well. We won! I remember we walked to Orlina and back on foot, there was no transport of any kind. It was quite a long way, some 5–6 kilometres. In Orlina Duża there was a narrow-gauge railway station; we did not have far to go to it, but the school only used it when the destination of the school trip was somewhere farther away, e.g. in Poznań. We would take the little railway to Jarocin and from there an ordinary train.

#### War

When the war broke out I was still a child. I did not really know what was happening, but fear appeared… The Germans pressed on, the Poles defended themselves. There were killings too. I once heard that somewhere nearby a German had been killed, and then a Pole. I was afraid… sometimes I slept away from home, somewhere out in the field… Everyone was afraid. My uncle was in the Polish Army. Then, when the Germans came in, they forced all Hauländers of 
<!-- s. 221 [PDF 221] -->
 German origin to take German citizenship and to join the German army, the Wehrmacht. Refusal was threatened with a camp or death. My two older brothers were killed, and we do not even know exactly where, or where their burial place is. Bruno was killed somewhere in the Carpathians during the fighting with the Russians in 1944, and Gustav came home at the end of the war. You could say he was a straggler, because he had left his unit on his own. The Russians were already coming. When Gustav arrived, you could hear the Russian tanks coming down the road. He thought he would be able to stay at home, that the war was over for him. He thought everything would be as it was before… He was glad he no longer had to serve in the Wehrmacht. Yet the day after his return the Russians took him away, and he never came back. He vanished without a trace…

#### Resettlement without return

In Łazy the Germans assigned us a Polish farmstead which was larger than ours. Such were the customs they introduced then. The owners of the farmstead in Łazy, an older couple with a son, were moved into our farmstead on Łazińsk. Their son commuted to work somewhere nearby. The whole family survived the war there in peace. After the war they returned to Łazy, to their farmstead. But the new Polish authorities did not allow us to return to ours.

#### Abram

I remember an episode like this. During the war, things left behind by Jews were brought into the village. My mother got very upset. She said she did not want any other people's things. Probably the adults guessed what was going on. Officially it was said that the Jews had been moved out and all their things were there for the taking. There was one Jewish family in the village, called Kowalski. They ran a shop on Łazińsk. I remember that even at the beginning of the war that shop was still operating, so this family must have been taken away sometime in the middle of the war… I remember that at one point Mr Kowalski had a star on his clothing, front and back, but the shop was still open…

<!-- s. 222 [PDF 222] -->

I was friends with his son Abram when I went to school on Łazińsk. Abram was very thin and very tall. It was the only Jewish family in our village.

#### After the war

We became homeless. A Polish family moved back into the farmstead in Łazy. Mama and I went to our grandma Loisa on Łazińsk. From the farmstead in Łazy we were not allowed to take all our things and our animals. We could take with us what we could carry in our hands. There were seven of us then. Father was no longer with us, for in 1945 the Russians had taken him away for forced labour in Russia. The youngest sister was still a baby. Secretly, at night, we still carried over some small things. Everything else stayed behind. At Grandma's there was not much either. In winter it was sometimes so cold that our bread froze. In Łazy there were people who helped us as much as they could.

#### The Nowicki family

Then, right after the war, somehow one had to live… We depended on what other people were willing to give us. We had no rights at all. Everyone looked out for themselves, for how they would survive. As children we had to look for people who would help us. Like our mother, up to her illness and death in 1949. There was a family called Nowicki, farmers from Łazy. They were very good people. When we were thrown out of our house, our farmstead, they kept me for two years. They had five children of their own, but they took me in as well. I lived and worked with them. I was thirteen. I worked for lodgings and food. I also received bread, milk, potatoes, so that my siblings and Mama would have something to eat… It is hard for me to talk about it… If you had something to eat, you had it, but if not, you were condemned to begging or hunger. To this day I remember the smell and the taste of dry, smoked salt bacon. Once I got such a piece straight from the chimney from Mr Nowicki. My siblings went to work for farmers in Szetlew.

<!-- s. 223 [PDF 223] -->

During the occupation my mother employed Irenka Nowicka to help her, and after the war she searched for us through the Red Cross, even in Geneva. She received word that the Kuhn family was not in the register. She worried greatly about our fate. She also searched for us through the municipality, but received no information. Nobody knew anything. We met many years after the war in Zagórów, thanks to the help of Pastor Andrzej Mendrok of Konin.

#### Mama

Mama was forty-nine when she died of typhus. Our youngest sister Frieda was four then. Mama was taken to the infectious diseases hospital, and we could no longer have any contact with her. We received only a letter saying that she was no longer alive. She was buried in Konin. That was 1949.

#### Papa

The Germans did not take Papa into the Wehrmacht, because he was the sole breadwinner of a large family. We children were nine, including seven brothers. It seems that the number of sons in a family mattered here. Father had a reputation as a good carpenter. He was respected, often helped others and got on well with his neighbours. Right after the Russians entered in 1945 they took father, like all the men from our village. They gathered them in Grabina, and then ordered them to walk on foot in the direction of Pyzdry. More than one of them died on the way. From there they took them all to Russia, to a labour camp. In cattle wagons. They were given nothing to eat or drink. Many did not survive that journey. Our father died in the camp probably in 1946. I do not remember the name of that place. We do not know where he is buried. From the moment the Russians took him we had no contact with him whatsoever. The news that he was no longer alive was passed on to us by the few who had the strength and the luck to return from the camp in Russia. They said that there was no trace of the camp any more. The Russians liquidated it after about two years, in 1947 or 1948. There they destroyed very many people…

<!-- s. 224 [PDF 224] -->

#### A moment of stability

In the end we ended up at a state agricultural farm (PGR) in Działyn near Poznań. Our aunt Emilia, father's sister, was already there. We reached Jarocin on the little railway, and then by an ordinary train to Poznań and Działyn. After a few months in Działyn we were moved to a state farm somewhere near Gniezno. A bus from Gniezno ran there. My aunt was arranging papers in Gniezno for departure to Germany. Her husband had gone there earlier, and she was able to apply on the grounds of family reunification. Then we, as close relatives, had a chance to travel to join them. Neither Papa nor Mama was with us. Leaving was not easy. Sometimes the applications had to be submitted many times over. A family we knew, the family of Armin Muench, applied to leave twenty times. Several times they failed to leave because their permission came unexpectedly and too late for them to reach Konin. They could leave Poland on condition that within six hours the whole family would be on a train in Konin. Their family numbered twenty people. Everyone worked somewhere. By the time they received the information and got together, they no longer had any chance of reaching Konin – besides, they had no means to do it. And there were no telephones then. We wanted to travel to our uncle in West Germany, but the pressure was to keep us in the GDR, in communist East Germany. GDR officials even tried to pull us out of the train and not let us past the communist cordon. After the experience of the war and of the passing of the Russian front we were afraid to stay in their zone of influence. In the end we managed it.

#### Departure

We managed to leave Poland in the spring of 1950 thanks to the help of Aunt Emilia. We were orphans. We did not know what future awaited us there, what might become of us… It was very hard for us… After reaching Germany it was not easy either. After all, we were some strange newcomers – neither Germans nor Poles. There, too, nobody rejoiced at our arrival. We were on foreign soil. I had no education, I knew no trade.

<!-- s. 225 [PDF 225] -->



I took the shortcut to school across that hill of sand…, Łazińsk Drugi, 2016



Erich Kuhn at the exhibition The Pyzdry Forest and the Hauländers, Pyzdry 2016



Erich Kuhn and Wiesława Kowalska at the memorial oak planted on the site of Erich Kuhn's family home, Łazińsk Drugi, 2016

<!-- s. 226 [PDF 226] -->

In the spring of 1950 we were sent to the Friedland transit camp in Lower Saxony, in West Germany. From there we children were distributed among relatives who were already in Germany. My sister Frieda and I ended up in Ostharingen in the Harz mountains, with our aunt Emilia. I began work there as a farmhand. I worked with a horse and a plough. The experience of working with horses at the state farm (PGR) in Poland came in useful. I liked this work, and the farmer was happy that he had "got" me. But in time I decided to leave. I went to Detmold in North Rhine-Westphalia to see my brother Helmut. He helped me with the formalities, and I was given a guardian, since I was not yet twenty-one. I started working. With the money I earned I paid for courses, so as to further my education. I learned cabinetmaking. My training and apprenticeship lasted three years. I then added a specialist course in furniture making. I could earn more. And I was good at it. I had more and more design ideas of my own and more and more clients.

#### Edith

I was lucky to have found precisely that guardian, because later I married his daughter Edith, and to this day I am growing old happily with her. (Edith Kuhn, b. 10.12.1934, passed to the Lord on 21.08.2022, after the work on the article had been completed).

#### Returns

When I left Poland I knew Polish well, but in time I forgot almost everything. Years later, when I returned to my home region, I could manage only a few simple sentences. They helped me in enquiring about the places of my childhood. Home, the real one, the most enduring one, remained in Poland, on Łazińsk, among my trees, dunes, tastes and smells… Already as an adult I enrolled in Detmold in a Polish language course. I attended the course for two years, twice a month for two hours.

<!-- s. 227 [PDF 227] -->

My wife Edith also enrolled, but after a few months she gave up. It was too difficult a language for her. It was much easier for me. I took my son and my grandson to Łazińsk, to show them where I was born. True, there is no house any more, but we easily found the place by the little lake and the old willow that remained. A ditch with water led to the lake. I remember an anecdote: one day Mama dressed our sister Irka in a pretty dress. We brothers were seven. We assured Mama that yeees! We would look after Irka! But, being boys, we egged her on to jump over the ditch. She jumped, but right into the middle of the water. Oh, did we catch it from Mama! I have not forgotten that, those happy pre-war years… Now I show everyone that famous ditch.

#### The film in Berlin

The film *People from the Iron Houses* made an impression. The cinema was full. After the screening many people wanted to know where that place was, where those people from the iron houses had lived. Many people were moved by the film. It sparked a discussion about what the war unleashed by Hitler had done. Words of support were voiced for a dialogue that would lead to the mutual forgiveness of wrongs, to the release of this wartime and post-war trauma. The film *People from the Iron Houses* helped me greatly. If I had doubts about whether I was doing the right thing, returning to my home region and opening up the memory of times past, this film gave me wings. My reason and my heart told me not to think too much, but to go on and act for the sake of the future! I commanded myself: "Go there and talk to people. That is the most important thing!" This film says beautifully that we must talk, that we must reach one another, so that what the war did may never happen again… So that war may never happen again. When German friends watched this film, they stressed how apt were the words at its end: "Where there is a border, there is also a road"… That is what we must hold on to and act on! This film is therapy for many people…

<!-- s. 228 [PDF 228] -->

#### Who am I?

It is not easy for me to answer, although people often ask me this question… To answer so simply, where is my Homeland?… I do not know… I cannot answer unambiguously… I have spent most of my life there, in Germany. My children and grandchildren were born there. I probably feel more at home there… But my roots are in Poland… my childhood. Everything connected with those places and those memories is very important to me. It is my identity. I gladly come here.

#### My Poland in need

What I went through in post-war Poland made me think that perhaps I would not be able to face the past. But when I finally did, when I came to Poland for the first time after the war, I was very glad that it had happened. That I came to this land where I was born. Solidarność gave me the reason, and then martial law. I began to collect gifts, to prepare transports of them, in order to help those in need in my Zagórów region. I did everything that was within the reach of my modest means… I gained new friends and the joy of life. For in giving there is much joy… Years later I received for this activity a federal medal in Germany, as well as a beautiful letter of thanks from the Zagórów authorities, of which I shall quote a fragment: "We remember and appreciate your commitment to delivering gifts for poor families from our area, your help in seeking work beyond the western border in a time so difficult for Poland as the fall of the communist system and the beginnings of capitalism. Particularly valuable and significant for us remains the fact that the aid which flowed in thanks to your efforts was not intended only for Protestants, but extended simply to people in need" (Zagórów–Detmold 2017).

<!-- s. 229 [PDF 229] -->

#### The Oak

On the site of our family home on Łazińsk Drugi I planted in October 2015 a little oak tree. On a wooden board I wrote the initials of my siblings' names: Gustav, Bruno (they were killed), Helmut, Erich, Erhard, Irka (she bathed in the ditch), Horst, Harry (the youngest brother), Frieda (the youngest sister).

Before I planted the oak I had a few sleepless nights. I did not know whether it would be received well by the Poles who live here now. My siblings are glad that I come to my home region, that I do not let the memory of the family home die… But the war changed so much… I would not want anyone in Poland to judge these actions of mine badly, to see something bad in my intentions. It turned out that I need not have worried. The family who now live on the site of our farmstead not only received me very kindly, but also took care of my little oak, watering it and protecting it from wild animals. Such moments heal old wounds. The way I feel it, this oak is a joy not only for me but also for its Polish caretakers. For after all, where there is a border, there is also a road.

> *Interview conducted and edited by Wiesława Kowalska*,

*Pyzdry – Detmold, 2014–2021*

<!-- s. 230 [PDF 230] -->

<!-- s. 231 [PDF 231] -->

### We knew we would not return here just yet…


Told by Eugenie Plitt, written down by Günter Hennings



Florentine Schmechel (1918).


Photo from the collection of Günter Hennings

In the Konin voivodeship, about eighty kilometres south-east of Poznań, there is a small scattered settlement called Łazińsk. It was here that I grew up together with my sister and two brothers. Our ancestors came to Poland from eastern Brandenburg more than two hundred and twenty years ago. There is no record of whether they were recruited to settle on favourable terms by Polish recruiters, or on the prompting of the Prussian state. And so our ancestors, on foreign soil, with great zeal and with the help of their families, began the cultivation of the fields and meadows assigned to them and the building of the house and the farm buildings. On one of the beams in the room the date 1778, the year the construction was completed, was carved. This house, like many others in this region, was built of bog iron ore (Raseneisenerz, Raseneisengestein, Rasenerz, Sumpferz, Wiesenerz), while the roof was covered with straw. During the Second World War the exploitation of the ore began. It was transported on a narrow-gauge railway which was built for that purpose.

According to my grandfather's accounts, the Schmechel family was not the first to settle in Łazińsk. My grandfather's grandfather was the one who settled here with his family. This must have been shortly before the year 1778. The immediate neighbourhood from that time until the beginning of 1945 was predominantly German. We felt at ease in this region, and surely hardly any German from Poland who before the year 1939 visited Germany as a German from abroad would have said that he was travelling to his old homeland. We felt we were Germans, but our true homeland lay in Poland, in the region in which we could live and feel at ease as Germans.

In the closer neighbourhood lived two Polish families. We had good relations with the Sapikowski family. We played with their children, and the adults 
<!-- s. 232 [PDF 232] -->
 visited one another. Of all of us my mother spoke Polish best. So in our contacts there were no problems with understanding one another. These mutual good relations helped us overcome some of the difficulties in the last months of the war. We were able to keep with them, among other things, clothes or food, because at that time there was always the danger that our house, as the house of Germans, would be plundered. Our whole cultural life, apart from school, centred on the church.

Almost every Sunday morning we went to the service at the House of Prayer, which stood next to our school. The sermon was usually given by the teacher, and during the war also by my uncle Rytthammer.

Grandfather devoted one hour every Sunday afternoon to reading the Bible. He knew the Bible by heart. It was important that my parents and their forebears preserved the German language and their faith. And so for a long time the Bible, the hymn book and the prayer book served as the textbook for their children, before German teachers began to teach them and the later generations in German schools. The proper holidays were in summer. They lasted from mid-June to the beginning of September, so about ten weeks. Even if many did not travel anywhere then, it was still a wonderful time for us. There was no radio or television, and we made our toys ourselves. We only had to set our imagination going. Even if we had to help our parents in the house, in the field or in the meadow, we still had time for games and pranks. During the harvest we played hide-and-seek in the stacks. When the grain had ripened, the men cut it with scythes, and the women bound it into sheaves. In the evening the sheaves were set up in stacks. In this way the straw, the ears and the grain were protected from dew or rain. The wind blowing through the empty spaces in the stacks hastened the drying of the sheaves. Winter played a special role for us. The winters were frosty and very long. I still remember exactly how, on 7 October – it was my mother's birthday – we were digging the last potatoes and sowing the winter grain when the first frost appeared, heralding the approaching winter. From the end of October to March we had snow almost all the time. The windows were 
<!-- s. 233 [PDF 233] -->



From the left: Traute Kissler, Florentine Schmechel, Willi Schmechel, Eugenie Plitt.


Photograph obtained from Günter Hennings


*road*

From the collection of Günter Hennings

<!-- s. 234 [PDF 234] -->
 covered with a thick layer of frost when one got up in the morning. During the day the frost melted, and in the evening one could admire the beautiful ice flowers which formed anew on the panes. A chest was placed on the runners of a sleigh, and a horse was hitched to it. In such a vehicle one drove around the neighbourhood and through the snow-covered forests. But in the end spring came. It took another four weeks or so until the last snow melted. It was a very wet period. Once that was over, summer was not far off. As a rule the summer was very warm and dry.


Our farmstead covered 20 hectares. We raised a few pigs for our own needs; for milk production there were at most 12 cows in the byre. Mama kept about 100 hens. Most of the eggs, the milk or the heifer were sold. Thanks to this an important regular source of earning money was assured. Three horses did the heavy work. During the war we kept sheep and geese. In times of general want and restrictions they provided wool and meat. Thus, for example, our geese were taken away to Dortmund in exchange for textiles, or father cycled to Kalisz, some 40 (50 in the previous edition) kilometres away, in order to exchange meat there for other everyday items. Above all it was important to obtain the materials from which Mama then sewed clothes. For most of the war the Łazińsk region was spared all the horrors of the war – apart from the letters, which one feared and which contained the news of the "heroic" death of a son, a brother, a husband or a father. Not a single bomb fell here, and at the beginning of 1945 the front was still far away, even if with the east wind one could hear the sounds of the cannonade. Until the arrival of the first Russians on 18 January we knew the war only from afar.

"It will be harder for us than during the war, with all its restrictions," Mama used to say. "The Russians and the Poles will take revenge on us." I could not imagine that. Had we done anyone wrong? After all, Germans and Poles had lived for a long time peacefully side by side, without major conflicts. For me the whole order collapsed. What remained was a dull fear. In that uncertain time even rumours demanded extraordinary caution. When 
<!-- s. 235 [PDF 235] -->
 Russian soldiers or other strangers appeared, we hid from them as a precaution, because it was not known whether these strangers had come with bad or good intentions. We were not spared looting.

On 20 February 1945 our father was taken away. He shared the fate of the other German men. It was said that these men had been taken to road works in the Konin area. Shortly afterwards they were transported to the then Soviet Union. There they were held in captivity near Moscow and worked at peat extraction. The conditions in the camp were catastrophic. A dysentery epidemic broke out there, and many of them died, among them my father. Willi Wall, an acquaintance from Łazińsk, survived the forced labour in Russia and returned to Germany at the end of 1949. He recounted that my father died in the night of 10 April 1945. In April 1945 we were expropriated. At first it was said that this was a temporary measure. Our farmstead was taken over by the Męch family, which had five children. Their eldest son Marian later inherited the farmstead. From then on my mother, my sister, Aunt Lidia and I lived in the part in which grandfather had his life tenancy (grandfather died on 30.1.1945). And so, from one day to the next, on our own land we were merely tolerated and dependent on the whims of the new owner. We fed ourselves, among other things, on products we had buried in the ground. Milk was given to us by the new owner.

In the village (Grabina) there was now a Polish municipality. We were living in Poland again. The official decrees now bore the coat of arms and the name of the new Polish state and were addressed to us, the German population living in the area. There, in harsh terms, they enumerated what we were not allowed to do. From June 1945 until the expulsion I lived with the Pietrala family. (Maria and Józef Pietrala in the village of Osiny – translator's note J.K.). There I made friends with their daughter (Teresa Krystyna – note by J.K.), who was younger than me. Later, too, I kept in touch with her by letter. She married Mr Langwiński (Wacław – note by J.K.), bore five daughters, and ran with her husband a farmstead which had earlier belonged to my uncle Emil (on Łazińsk; before the Langwińskis the Fagasiński family lived there – note by J.K.).

<!-- s. 236 [PDF 236] -->

My sister Traute lived from May 1945 until the deportation in the neighbouring Grunwald farmstead. At weekends we met at my grandfather's life-tenancy quarters. In December 1945 the date of the expulsion was announced. Until then the farewell to our land had been taking place in instalments, through the weekend visits. Now it was to be the last time.

I remember that last time very precisely. As I left, I kept looking back every so often, so as to remember everything for the indefinite time of absence. There was a sad and depressing mood. We knew that we would not return just yet, and perhaps never again. It was a conscious farewell, above all a very painful one for Mama, for the years spent on Łazińsk were her whole life. But more strongly than the sorrow for what we had lost and left behind, I felt a full readiness to set out on an unknown, new path of life. I turned round once more; snow was falling; it was 11 December 1945. Only after tens of years was I granted to be here again. On that December day all the inhabitants of Łazińsk had to report to the Municipality Office in Grabina. Most of the belongings we had with us were taken from us. It took a long time, so that not everyone was processed on the same day. About half of the families left on the first day on the narrow-gauge railway, the rest on the following day. It happened that relatives who wanted to remain together were separated. We went by the narrow-gauge railway – my mother, my sister, my cousins Anna Hohm and Hilde Haupt with their little children and their mother (my aunt Rytthammer, née Schmechel), Aunt Lidia, the Runge family and I – on the first day from Grabina to Witaszyce. After a stay there of many hours we travelled in closed goods wagons through Poznań to Szczecin. Szczecin was one great heap of rubble. We arrived at the station of one of Szczecin's suburbs, probably Kreckow (Krzekowo). Then came a march along a gravel road to the sugar refinery in Szczecin Gumieńce (Stettin-Scheune). We spent the night at the sugar refinery. In the morning we went on to another goods station. Then we made a week-long journey in a closed goods wagon. The journey was often interrupted. We were also exposed to looting. After a week of dreadful travel we reached, shortly 
<!-- s. 237 [PDF 237] -->
 before Christmas 1945, the town of Demmin (Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania). The exact route between Szczecin and Demmin is unknown to me. Closed transports in railway goods wagons at that time went via Szczecin or via Kohlfurt south-east of Görlitz to the territories lying west of the Oder and Neisse. The next day we were quartered on an estate in Poggendorf. Here I went to school until the end of January 1946. A few days later, probably on 4 February, we travelled by train through Dresden (Dresden, because of the hostilities, lay entirely in ruins) to Nordhausen in the southern Harz. After a night spent in a camp situated outside the town we made a day-long march across the border which then existed, from the Russian zone into the British zone. There the English troops were waiting for us. We went by lorry to the Friedland camp, and then by train, via Hanover and Hamburg, we reached Bad Segeberg. After a day's stay in the Bad Segeberg camp and registration we arrived in Rendsburg. From then on Rendsburg was to become, for us and for the Runge family, our home for the years to come.

*Translated by Jacek Kin*

<!-- s. 238 [PDF 238] -->
### First Visit to My Parents' Homeland in 1981


Günter Hennings

For many years after the war, the Polish border was closed to Germans who wanted to see their homeland again. The breakthrough came with the change in the eastern policy of the Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany, Willy Brandt, and the signing of the Treaty of Warsaw[^79].

Now it was easier to travel to Poland as a German tourist and visit places that had until then been off limits. Over time my interest in visiting and getting to know the birthplace of my ancestors grew, especially as my parents often talked about the old days and thus stirred my curiosity. At Pentecost in 1981 my wife Charlotte and I – we were then teachers in Uelzen – travelled to Poland with my parents [Ella and Willi Schmechel – added by the translator]. Two decades have passed since then. Full of apprehension, we set out in our VW Polo early in the morning on the Saturday before Pentecost from Seelze towards Poznań. In those days the journey still went with difficulties. After an hour on the motorway heading east came the first stop. The onward journey led through the former GDR with all the attendant inconveniences which, at the time, though in a weaker form, also applied to us, Germans from the West. We already knew the motorway up to the junction towards West Berlin from several earlier trips, and the carriageway, thanks to subsidies from the West, was 


[^79]: Układ między Polską Rzecząpospolita Ludową a Republiką Federalną Niemiec o podstawach normalizacji ich wzajemnych stosunków [The Treaty between the Polish People's Republic and the Federal Republic of Germany on the Foundations of the Normalization of Their Mutual Relations], signed by Józef Cyrankiewicz and Willy Brandt in the then Palace of the Council of Ministers (today the Presidential Palace) in Warsaw on 7 December 1970 – translator's note.

<!-- s. 239 [PDF 239] -->
 in relatively good condition. Then the picture changed abruptly. The density of traffic diminished quickly because of the lack of travellers going only as far as West Berlin, and the road conditions deteriorated so badly that shortly before the Polish border part of the exhaust pipe came loose, and in a rattling car we reached our first destination. The border formalities were quickly settled, and the rest of the drive passed without major problems. After about nine hours of driving and 540 km covered, we reached our goal. Until the Tuesday after Pentecost we stayed at the "Bazar" hotel in the centre of Poznań. The day of our arrival was a lovely spring day, and in the afternoon we delighted in the beautiful old town of Poznań. But the damaged exhaust pipe gave us no peace. We dealt with this problem quickly as well. The kind receptionist from the hotel activated his acquaintances, and towards evening a young woman appeared at the hotel who, riding with us in our car, took us to an estate of single-family houses on the outskirts. There, in one of the houses, a perfectly equipped car workshop was to be found in the garage. All that was missing was the right exhaust pipe. But even that was no problem. After all, what are neighbours for! Before long a matching part was fetched and welded on. In the meantime we were entertained in the house by the owner, who in return received tights; for the repair, Western currency was gladly accepted, and we too were pleased to have a fully functioning means of transport again. For the first time in my life, after all these impressions, I drank Polish beer. It was good, but the next day beer could be bought neither in the town centre nor at the hotel. Today that is unimaginable.

On Sunday morning, on the day of Pentecost, full of tension we set out for our actual destination[^80]. We drove along the outbound road in the direction of Warsaw, and then along a two-lane road to Słupca (Grenzhausen). In Słupca we turned off the national road in the direction of Zagórów (Hinterberg). We made our first stop in Zagórów. Zagórów once formed 


[^80]: Łazińsk – supplemented by the translator.

<!-- s. 240 [PDF 240] -->
 a small centre for the inhabitants of the nearer surroundings. A weekly market was held here, bigger shopping was done, confirmation classes were attended, and news was exchanged. We went to the market square to park there. We walked to the church in which my parents were confirmed in 1940 and 1942. The church was open, so we were able to stay there for a longer while. Then we went to the building in which the Rau/Janke family once lived. We lingered there for a moment, and passing the church once more, we returned to the market square. On the site of the former warriors' monument (Kriegerdenkmal) there was a square with flowers.

After a short coffee break we drove to Łazińsk, a few kilometres away to the south. As far as Grabina the road was still in good condition, but beyond it only sandy tracks led on. We drove through a countryside whose landscape had changed greatly since my parents' departure in 1947. Small trees had grown up; where large ones had been cut down, the next generation had sprung up. The whole landscape seemed altered and in different dimensions. But appearances were deceptive. Nothing here had become smaller or larger. Driving along the sandy track that ran through a field overgrown with rye, we caught sight of the farmstead on which my grandmother and grandfather farmed. We turned off the sandy track and, along a grass-grown way, reached my grandparents' former property. We parked the car in the entrance to the farmyard.

At last the moment had come of which I had constantly thought and of which I had hoped that one day it would come. My wish to see the homeland of my ancestors was fulfilled. Across the grass-grown farmyard we approached the entrance and knocked at the door. But there was no one there. The door was not locked. We wondered whether to go in, but in the end we did not. The owners were absent. We only walked around the house and looked inside through the windows. At the kitchen window stood the coffee grinder with which my grandmother ground coffee. It had survived all these years and surely still worked. After photographing the rear of the house facing the well-kept orchard, we walked back onto the farmyard in order to view the barn and the pigsties and to capture the impressions in photographs.

<!-- s. 241 [PDF 241] -->

By car we went to the vicinity of several neighbouring farmsteads – my parents recalled names I had once heard. Some of the farmsteads no longer existed. Presumably no one wanted to live in them, or they were inhabited only for a short period, then fell into ruin and were dismantled. My aunt Eugenie Plitt, who kept up a written correspondence with the Langwiński family, informed them of our visit to Łazińsk. We drove back in the direction of Grabina.

On the way there, we met Mr Langwiński, who farmed the farmstead of my grandfather's brother Emil Schmechel. Looking for us, he had driven out to meet us. He had probably learned of our visit to Łazińsk from two of his guests, whom we met on the way to my grandparents' former farm, because that day their youngest daughter was receiving her First Communion and a Communion celebration was taking place at the Langwińskis' home. There we were warmly received and entertained. One of the older guests still remembered the families of Gustav and Emil Schmechel exactly. He was interested in what had become of them. He probably knew what had happened to the men in the last months of the war. At the end of the visit we arranged to meet their daughter Wanda in Poznań, we took photographs, and we exchanged small gifts as well as addresses. After the visit to the Langwiński family we drove on to the next destination of our journey, to the place where my mother spent her childhood and youth[^81].

The loud barking of a dog announced our arrival. Before we entered the farmyard, the residents present, of the surname Parusz, came up to the entrance gate. The conversation between my parents and Mrs Parusz proceeded rather matter-of-factly and with reserve. Her son and daughter-in-law, like Charlotte and me, merely watched the people talking. How good it would be to know at least a little of the language. Then we paid a short visit to the neighbour Barcok[^82].



[^81]: Włodzimirów – supplemented by the translator.

[^82]: Perhaps the surname in question here is Bartczak – translator's note.

<!-- s. 242 [PDF 242] -->

After a short while we drove to Trąbczyn, to the place where, after losing their family homes, my parents worked and lived with a Polish family until their departure for Germany[^83]. Towards evening we set off in the direction of Poznań, crossing the lovely Warta valley over the then still existing wooden bridge next to the monastery at Ląd. At the petrol station before the town there was a long queue of cars. We had no choice but to wait, since we held petrol vouchers which we had to redeem. At that time in Poland there was no other way of obtaining petrol or diesel.
