The next day we toured the centre of Poznań and enjoyed the wonderful, almost summery weather, in order to set out towards Hanover the following day. The drive to the border passed without problems. At about two o'clock we arrived at the border with the former GDR at Świecko near Frankfurt an der Oder. After the Polish border guards had stamped our passports, nothing happened for a long time. On the opposite side several Polish cars were waiting, returning to Poland from the Federal Republic of Germany packed full with bags. One of the cars was towing a trailer loaded with tyres. It was calm at the border. The car standing before us was quickly cleared. Just as I thought that, since the check was proceeding so smoothly, we would soon be home, a Polish customs officer approached us. After returning our passports, we were subjected to a thorough inspection; every piece of our luggage was checked very carefully. After all these "formalities" we could finally continue our journey home with relief.

*Translated by Jacek Kin*



[^83]: In the case of the Modelski family – I know this from the accounts of Günter's father, whom I met in person in Germany in the year 1989 – translator's note.

<!-- s. 243 [PDF 243] -->

### The Evangelical Senior Home in Zagórów and Its Residents in the Recollections of Rev. Katarzyna Kowalska


Recorded by Katarzyna Połom

The chronicle of the senior home records that in the pre-war cantor's house next to the evangelical church, a kindergarten was organised after 1945. Later the public library and the community centre found their seat in it. After it was vacated by the above-mentioned institutions, the building was designated for lease – shops operated in it, while two rooms facing the garden were occupied as a dwelling by the sexton, Mr Edward Rydel. With the passage of time the buildings fell into decay and required ever greater intervention. Before it was decided to carry out a major renovation, Mr Edward Kuhn convinced the Parish Council in Detmold of the need to take solitary, elderly, less well-off Protestants under care and to use the said building for this purpose. The parish in Konin accepted this idea, and thanks to joint financing of the project by both parishes and by individuals, a major renovation of the building was carried out in 1994-1995.

When all the construction work had come to an end, the time arrived for the solemn dedication of the building. In the notes from the chronicle we can read:

> "On 16 June 1996, during the Mission Festival, celebrated for many years in the Konin parish on the Catholic feast of Corpus Christi and held that year in Zagórów, the Superior of the Diocese, Rev. Bishop Michał Warczyński, after a solemn service, in the presence of Rev. Roman Lipiński from the Evangelical-Reformed Parish of Żychlin, Rev. Piotr Styks from Kępno, the local parish priest, Rev. Andrzej Mendrok, Pastor Burkhard Krebber from Detmold together with a delegation 
<!-- s. 244 [PDF 244] -->
 of the local Parish Council, the Mayor of Zagórów Wiesław Radniecki, the Head of the Lądek Commune Jerzy Kaszna, the Commune Secretary Mrs Alina Kmieć, the Director of the PDPS in Zagórów, Mr Marian Parus, the Parish Council from Konin, the Fire Brigade Band from Zagórów and many fellow believers from neighbouring parishes, performed the act of its dedication".

The first residents moved into the senior home even before its official opening. They were:

- Aldegunda Kinast b. 20.09.1926 in Bielawy, moved in on 9.06.1995.
- Marta Deitner b. 11.11.1922 in Stara Paprotnia, moved in on 8.06.1995.
- Edward Szwarc b. 4.09.1926 in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, moved in on 8.06.1995.
- Helena Wegner b. 6.01.1920 in the village of Dąbie, came to the senior home in July 1995.
- Genowefa Dalke b. 11.10.1911 in Linden, took up residence in the senior home on 1.10.1996.
- Erna Woltman b. 7.07.1923 in Bielawy – at the senior home in Zagórów since 21.10.1995.
- Lidia Zach b. 11.11.1912 in the village of Młynek, moved in on 15.10.1996.

Initially the residents of the senior home were looked after by Mrs Ilona Bürger; soon afterwards, from 12 June 1995, social care was taken over by Mrs Erika Fandrich, supported by her husband Artur. In January 1997 support was also received from the Municipal Social Welfare Centre (Mrs Kazimiera Marczak); nursing care was provided by Mrs Gabriela Okseniuk, and medical care by the physician Mariusz Eicke. The bookkeeping at the care home was kept by Mrs Teresa Kierzek, and the centre was run by Rev. Andrzej Mendrok.

<!-- s. 245 [PDF 245] -->

Through all the years of the Evangelical Senior Home's existence, its residents took an active part, as far as possible, in meetings, cultural and parish events; they also visited the Zagórów Social Welfare Home and its inmates on the occasion of holidays, parties and artistic reviews. The closeness of the large market square, the park, the shops and the traditional Wednesday market made it easy for the residents of the senior home to go out for a walk, to do their shopping and to keep in constant contact with the inhabitants of Zagórów and with visitors. The Protestant seniors were also often visited, as witnessed by the numerous, cordial entries preserved in the chronicle. Constant care over the seniors was given by Rev. Andrzej Mendrok together with his wife Rozwita (usually called in the chronicle the 'Pastor's wife') and Mr and Mrs Erika and Artur Fandrich. On the first and third Sunday of each month the residents attended services held in the church standing a few steps from the senior home, which was a great convenience for elderly people. As the years passed, the oldest residents declined in health and departed – as it is beautifully put in the chronicle – to the Lord, and others came in their place. Among them were also young people, students of theology on diaconal placements, who appeared among the seniors and grew close to them. Among them was the catechist employed in the parish, Mrs Katarzyna Cieślak, today Rev. Katarzyna Kowalska, one of nine women priests in Poland[^84].



Rev. Katarzyna Kowalska.


Photo from a private archive

I had the pleasure of meeting Rev. Katarzyna Kowalska and her husband Rev. Eneasz Kowalski in person, during their stay in Zagórów. Today the two of them serve as clergy in a completely different part of Poland, but I managed to get in touch with the former resident of the EDS and ask about her recollections of the time when she lived in Zagórów (the period from 1 September 2006 to January 2008). We had a truly interesting conversation, full of positive energy, during which I learned many facts 


[^84]: On 16 October 2021, the Synod of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in Poland voted by secret ballot in favour of introducing the ordination of women as priests. On 7 May 2022, Bishop Jerzy Samiec ordained nine deaconesses as priests in the Lutheran Church.

<!-- s. 246 [PDF 246] -->
 concerning everyday life in the care home – on the whole quite calm, but not lacking either comical or sad occurrences. Rev. Kowalska remembers well that the senior home was created to provide elderly people with care and access to participation in services, but at the same time – a certain independence in everyday existence. In connection with this, there was no round-the-clock care in the EDS. My interlocutor explains:

– Mrs Erika Fandrich came for four hours during the day; in that time she cleaned the corridors and did the residents' shopping according to a list they had prepared, and she brought dinners from the Social Welfare Home. The house had seven rooms; in six of them our seniors lived, and I occupied the seventh. Each of us had her own kitchenette (consisting of a fridge, an electric cooker, a sink and a cupboard), thanks to which each resident prepared her own breakfast and supper.

If need be, the carer could assist them in this. The shopping was done by Mrs Erika Fandrich out of the residents' own funds and settled according to receipts. The residents had complete freedom as to when they got up, when they ate their meals, what shopping they commissioned from Mrs Erika. Wednesday, in turn, was the day of Rev. Andrzej Mendrok's official duty; at that time he occupied an office in the attic, where he kept the institution's documentation.

– The one year I spent in the EDS was a turning point, for the residents were already beginning to age to such a degree that they were gradually ceasing to be self-reliant – says Rev. Kasia Kowalska. – I remember the time when dementia began to enter the senior home ever more boldly, and I had six baby monitors set on my windowsill, listening at night for whether something troubling was happening in any of the rooms.

Sometimes the carer's intervention was necessary, as for instance when one of the ladies, accustomed to the layout of her former flat and no longer able to assimilate the new arrangement, would go out at night to the bathroom and on the way back habitually end up in another resident's room and grow angry that someone was occupying her bed. Colourful footprints stuck on the floor, leading from the bathroom 
<!-- s. 247 [PDF 247] -->



The Evangelical Senior Home in Zagórów, 1995.


Photo from the EDS chronicle



The dedication of the Evangelical Senior Home.


Photo from the EDS chronicle



The residents of the senior home. Standing from the left: Edward Szwarc, Aldegunda Kinast, Genowefa Dalke, Erna Woltman, NN. Seated from the left: Marta Deitner, Lidia Zach.


Photo from the EDS chronicle

<!-- s. 248 [PDF 248] -->
 to the right room – says Rev. Kowalska – did not help; this lady's perceptive abilities were already so limited that she was unable to assimilate the new arrangement of the rooms. In connection with similar situations and the residents' self-reliance diminishing with every month, it became necessary to employ two more carers. The residents of the EDS were calm, warm-hearted people. They had their individual likings and habits, but the ritual common to all was… watching *Mody na sukces*. A moment before broadcast time all the seniors would sit down together in the dining room in front of the television, awaiting the next episode of their favourite telenovela in great suspense. At such moments, any attempts to engage them in some other activity were doomed to failure from the start! – Rev. Katarzyna tells me.


I mentioned earlier that Wednesday was the day of Rev. Mendrok's visits. His wife Rozwita would accompany him then, always arriving with a cake she had freshly baked. The residents of the house made coffee and sat down together with their pastor and his wife in the dining room. It was a time of Bible reflections, of singing hymns together and of celebrating the next meeting. The last Wednesday before Christmas was, by custom, the "Zagórów Christmas Eve", when all the residents, the carers, the residents' relatives and other invited guests met at the supper.

Warm, almost family-like relations developed between the seniors and the young inhabitants of the Evangelical Senior Home. My interlocutor recalled with particular emotion the moment when one of the residents, Mrs Ruth Miska, appeared unexpectedly at Kasia and Eneasz's wedding as far away as Brzeg, even though she had to hire a car with a driver to do so. On returning to Zagórów, another surprise awaited the newly-weds – the seniors, after prior deliberation, unanimously ceded one of the toilets to the young couple, having decided that the young marriage should have more space. Rev. Andrzej Mendrok – not wishing to be left behind – decided to place his office at Kasia and Eneasz's disposal as a bedroom, with the sole proviso that he would still like to be able to use 
<!-- s. 249 [PDF 249] -->
 it on Wednesday mornings. Rev. Kowalska recalled one more event that had stuck in her memory. One day one of the neighbours came to the senior home on some errand. My interlocutor opened the door for him, and the man began to explain in German what had brought him. At first she was somewhat stunned, listening to this speech, but after a moment she quipped that since she was a Pole and he a Pole, they would get along splendidly in their native tongue. The man was greatly surprised, just as she had been a moment before. After a while he confessed that all along he had been convinced that, since all the residents of the senior home belonged to the "German church", they were surely Germans and did not speak Polish. It is quite a telling picture of how little awareness of the identity of our neighbours of the Protestant faith a part of the inhabitants of Zagórów had, and still has to this day. Let us hope that reading the publication we have prepared will contribute to changing this state of affairs.

On 31 December 2007 Mrs Erika Fandrich retired. She had worked at the care home for almost 13 years. The entry in the chronicle under that date read: "For the years of work, care and sacrifices, on behalf of the residents we offer Mrs Erika our most heartfelt thanks with the words 'God repay you'".

On 6 April 2011 Mr Edward Szwarc died, the first and last resident of the Evangelical Senior Home. His passing was at the same time the end of the institution's activity, present in the landscape of the little town of Zagórów for sixteen years (1995-2011). The building remained empty for almost ten years, despite several ideas for its use being considered. None of the projects, however, came to fruition. Fortunately, since May 2021, after a thorough modernisation, a small, cosy guesthouse with the charming name of "Gęsi Puch" has been housed in it.

<!-- s. 250 [PDF 250] -->

<!-- s. 251 [PDF 251] -->
### History enchanted in photography – the Brudler family


Damian Kruczkowski

Two young people gaze calmly into the lens with the seriousness that was customary in those days. The couple is dressed modestly but elegantly. She is wearing a long dark gown that conceals almost her entire body. Delicate jewellery betokening a certain prosperity, above all a long chain, as well as her hair done up in the fashion of the early twentieth century, lend the woman in the photograph a certain dignity. The man has his arm around the woman, and on his handsome face a carefully trimmed moustache is visible. The young man is dressed in a long dark frock coat, a white shirt and a light-coloured cravat. Either of them could pass for passengers aboard the greatest and supposedly unsinkable transatlantic liner of its day, the RMS Titanic. But the photograph dates from 1913, when the floating colossus Titanic had already been resting at the bottom of the ocean for nearly a year. The photograph, meanwhile, shows inhabitants of the Łazińsk in the municipality of Zagórów, Ferdinand and Pauline née Haupt Brudler.

When work on this book began, materials began arriving from many quarters and from various people: documents, photographs, notes, finished texts. One of those people was Daniel Brudler, who sent the photograph described above depicting his relatives. When Katarzyna Połom and I saw this couple, we thought at once that it had to find its way into the book, as yet another testimony about those who once settled the Zagórów land, on which they were born, lived, worked and died. We asked Daniel Brudler for permission to publish the photograph and for more information about the Brudler family. He gladly agreed and shared what he had managed to gather about his family. The life story of Ferdinand Brudler is no less tragic than the story of the Titanic, but before we tell it 
<!-- s. 252 [PDF 252] -->
 it is worth recounting the history of the family and telling how and when the Brudlers came to the vicinity of Zagórów. Now, the Brudler family was connected with Zagórów and its surroundings from the mid-nineteenth century, when Johann Christian Brudler, a blacksmith, came to these parts. Johann Christian was born in 1795 in the village of Strzyżewo (German: Strehse) in the municipality of Zbąszyń (German: Bentschen).

At the end of the nineteenth century Strzyżewo belonged to Międzyrzecz county and numbered 100 households and 781 inhabitants. Protestants (605) predominated over Catholics (165) and Jews (11). Johann's parents were Christian Brudler and Dorothea Elisabeth née Mikke. The boy learned the blacksmith's trade from his father, who practised it precisely in Strzyżewo. Originally the boy was to take over his father's smithy, but because of his father's early death those plans were never realized. The changing economic conditions presumably caused the young man to long to seek his fortune in another place, and he set off eastward. The route of his wanderings can be traced from the birth records of his children and the death records of his wives and of himself. He came to the municipality of Krzymów near Konin, where in Roztoka he met his first wife, Anna Rosine Rostock. The couple had eleven children, which was nothing extraordinary in those times. Of all those children, for the purposes of this story the most important will be Christian Ferdinand, born in 1826. In the municipality of Krzymów Johann Christian was engaged in tilling the land. At a certain stage of the family's history they moved to Władysławów, where Anna Rosine died in 1851. Frequent moving about was within the standards of the life of farmers or small craftsmen of those days, dictated by changing fortunes and the need to find a better place to live and work, one that would guarantee a living for the family. Johann Christian contracted his next three marriages already in the Protestant church in Zagórów. At that time he lived, as one can read in the marriage record (1855) with his second wife, Kristine Schultz, in Huta Trąbczyńska, where he was a shoemaker. Kristine died after four years of marriage, and still in that same year, 1859, Johann Christian married once more. His chosen one was 35-year-old Wilhelmine née Krakowski. Nine years of shared life were ended in 1868 by 
<!-- s. 253 [PDF 253] -->
 the death of Johann's third wife. A quick-tempered man, as one may surmise, he decided to marry again. His fourth and last wife was Ernestine née Runge, who outlived her husband by twenty-one years. Johann Christian Brudler died in 1881 in Michalinów Oleśnicki (German: Emilienheim), and his fourth wife in 1902, also in Emilienheim. There, too, three of their children were born: August (1869), Heinrich (1873) and Pauline (1876). The already-mentioned Christian Ferdinand Brudler took Anna Julianna Franzke as his wife in 1844 in Żychlin; she bore him, among others, a son, Adam. It was most probably Adam Brudler with his wife Elisabeth Theofila née Pisching who set out on the road, and it was their son, whose likeness can be seen in the photograph described at the beginning, Ferdinand Brudler, who was born in 1886 in Bobrów (German: Boberfeld). In terms of fecundity the Brudler family did not stand out against the background of other families of that period. Ferdinand had six brothers: Wilhelm, Karl Adam, Friedrich, Theodor, Ludwig and Adolf, and three sisters: Olga, Maria Magdalena and Amanda. The family supported itself from farming in Bobrów.

The year 1913 arrived. The year in which the photograph sent to the book's editors was taken. It was then that in Zagórów Ferdinand Brudler married Pauline née Haupt, the one standing beside him in the photograph, the daughter of August and Ernestine. The young couple settled on the Łazińsk, where still that same year their firstborn son Johann came into the world. Unfortunately, the boy lived only twelve weeks. After the death of their firstborn the spouses moved to Brudzew (German: Brückenau) near Września (German: Wrenschen). There, too, three daughters came into the world:



Ferdinand Brudler and Pauline Haupt.


Photo from the family album of Daniel Brudler

Elly Paula (1915), Erna Frieda (1916) and Hildegard Dorothea (1917).

Ferdinand, like his father and grandfather, likewise supported his family from tilling the land. It would seem that nothing would ever disturb their family happiness. Unfortunately, on 20 January 1920 
<!-- s. 254 [PDF 254] -->
 Ferdinand's wife, Pauline, died suddenly. The young widower with three little daughters returned to the vicinity of Zagórów and settled in the village of Anielewo (Ingelfingen). There, too, he came to know the daughter of Gottlieb and Ernestine Drewitsch, the next Pauline of his life. Pauline Drewitsch accepted Ferdinand's proposal and became the next Mrs Brudler. She brought into the marriage her own daughter, Lene Drewitsch, who from then on was raised together with Ferdinand's daughters by his first wife. Further Brudler children began to come into the world. In January 1923 the spouses became the parents of twins: Otto Richard and Erwin Waldemar. The boys, unfortunately, died soon after birth. The following year Ferdinand and Pauline became the parents of a little daughter, whom they named Martha, and in the following years Gertrud (1927) and Lydia (1929) came into the world as well. But fickle fate did not allow the spouses to enjoy their happiness for long. Suddenly and unexpectedly Pauline Brudler died in 1930, and Ferdinand was once again a widower with a brood of under-age daughters. Unable to look after such a large brood, he entrusted the youngest little daughter, Lydia, to his brother Theodor and his wife Berta. A year after the death of his second wife Ferdinand Brudler married for the third time. He was still a relatively young man, and his little daughters needed a mother. His chosen one was a widow – Emilie Dahlke née Kalisch. The family continued to live in the village of Anielewo (Ingelfingen) until Ferdinand's sudden death, which took him in June 1933. Ferdinand and each of his wives gave a splendid example of unity and of the enduring strength of the family. Despite many upheavals, successive deaths and bereavements, Ferdinand was able to keep the family together with him, making use of his brother's help only in the case of the youngest little daughter. Yet the Brudlers never lost touch with one another. In the photograph from around 1990 of the reunion of Ferdinand's descendants, one can see six cheerful, elegant elderly ladies. They are the daughters of Ferdinand and his wives: Gertrud, Martha, Erna, Elly, Lene and Lydia. It was precisely this clan solidarity that often allowed the settlers, the Protestants, to survive the hardships of fate and the upheavals of history. It was the strength slumbering in the family that gave the people of those days the motivation to act and allowed them effectively to build their position.

<!-- s. 255 [PDF 255] -->

### Where the lilies of the valley grow[^85]


The novelist Zdzisław Kulawinek[^85] was interviewed by Łukasz Parus

The novel is about people from our parts. Poles, Jews, Germans living in eastern Wielkopolska. Where does that subject come from?

In the mid-1950s I often went hunting with my father in the forests between Stawiszyn and Grodziec. The sandy forest roads and tracks did not allow the pleasure of a longer ride, whether by motorcycle or by car. It usually ended at the first small hills. We covered the rest of the way on foot. In time I came to love those walks. I had the feeling that thanks to them I was discovering a completely new world, unknown to me until then. We would enter one village, then the next. We knew all the peasants who lived here with their families: the poor Poles and the even poorer Germans, whom nobody wanted, hidden deep in the forest in their cottages. Here and there one could come upon traces left by the Jews. Even then I had a fair amount of knowledge about the centuries-old, difficult and not painless neighbourhood of three nations. The years went by. Time did not manage to erase the memories of my youth. Both the people and the places sank deep into my heart and gave me no peace. My wanderings through the villages founded by Hauländers drew ever wider circles. I visited places lying ever farther from the surroundings of Grodziec, Stawiszyn or Zbiersk.



[^85]: Zdzisław Kulawinek, a Kalisz native and a passionate student of local history, is the author of the novel *Tam, gdzie rosną konwalie*, which he published at his own expense in the year 2007. The publication reminded the readers of Eastern Greater Poland (including Borowiec, Grodziec and Zagórów) of the multicultural pre-war society, that is, of the German and Jewish neighbours who had inhabited Poland for centuries.

<!-- s. 256 [PDF 256] -->

Zagórów was not far away, a proverbial stone's throw. And in these regions too I found many forgotten cemeteries, houses built of bog iron, churches and chapels. And here, too, the descendants of the old settlers told of their complicated fates. Zagórów – a small, quiet town – charmed me with its beauty and history. There are many monuments here connected not only with the settlers. Exquisite is the Warta valley with its oxbow lakes, dunes, meadows and all manner of bird species. I watched how its image changed in the different seasons. It is a place one can fall in love with. Over the years of wandering I accumulated a great many tales and stories. I wanted to fix them in the form of a book that would at the same time be a lesson in history. I wanted to preserve the tangled, complicated fates of the settlers, especially in the wartime and post-war periods. But I am not a historian. I don't know whether I would be capable of writing a popular-science book. That is why I needed such “warm characters”, who would introduce the reader to the world of the German settlers, the Jews and the Poles. Characters who would be guides through that world. Hence there appear Edward, Zosia and Grandma Eryka – though invented, they have certain traits drawn from real life stories. Thanks to the real Edward and a few other people I made contact with the inhabitants of the nearby areas. Long, interesting conversations with them “helped build” my protagonists. In my novel I wanted to include various threads connected with the Germans, the Jews and the Poles living in these areas. I needed facts, authentic stories.

Very important to me was the meeting with Bogdan Grzonkowski, a historian from Zagórów, a man of passion, who for years researched the history of his little homeland. He let me make use of his rich archive. It was he who introduced me to the history of Leon Jedwab, the fate of the Zagórów Jews and the annihilation of that community in the Kazimierzowskie Forests. He also told local anecdotes. I made use of that in the pages of my book. We should remember that the history of the Hauländer settlers, in their great majority Germans, is a part of our history, an important chapter in the history of Poland. Brought in from the mid-eighteenth century onwards, they settled the lands 
<!-- s. 257 [PDF 257] -->
 of Wielkopolska. They were given the worst land to cultivate, that is, areas lying in the flood zone of the Warta or sandy wastelands. They managed to bring them under cultivation. They built houses, kantorats, churches, marked out cemeteries. They blended in and grew into Polish society. They kept the faith of their forefathers, though in everyday life they often used the Polish language. The war and the post-war years changed everything. Many descendants of the settlers left for the West, often against their own will. For a large part of them, as for Johann – one of the protagonists of my book – Poland remained their homeland. So, at the end, let me allow myself a quotation: “The last traces of the history of these lands are crumbling churches, old cemeteries, collapsing cottages and the memory of old people. Before long time will erase it all. Then tell everyone about it. That there, deep in the forest, on the small hills overgrown with lilies of the valley, there are cemeteries in which lie the ashes of people who once came to these lands from far away. They spoke a different language. Here they lived for hundreds of years, worked hard. They came to love this land.”

<!-- s. 258 [PDF 258] -->

<!-- s. 259 [PDF 259] -->

### Out of oblivion I call to you… A short history of Ida Disterheft


Damian Kruczkowski

In the order of the world that it fell to all of us to come to know, as the approved and acceptable one, it is usually the case that every story has its beginning, development and end. We tell each other and pass on the stories of people and events that begin at some point on the timeline and end in another place, more or less distant. The story I am telling disrupts this accustomed order, but the disruption is the result of earlier catastrophic and cruel events, from the not so distant past, namely from the 1930s. My story begins at the end. From an end far more definitive than death – from oblivion after death. The theory of small miracles is a thing about the attraction of people, events, situations, but usually it is we ourselves who bring about that attraction. Yet it may happen that precisely out of oblivion, out of the depths of history, we will “hear” a call for remembrance, for being remembered, and in part – for being readmitted into the circle of life. I believe that such a situation occurred in the case I describe here.

Several years ago, as I was wandering the paths of the Municipal Cemetery in Konin, I approached the ossuary, that is, the large tomb into which are placed the exhumed remains of persons whose graves have not been paid for for another twenty years. On the outer walls of the tomb, faced in black granite, plaques are placed with the first name, surname and year of death of the persons interred within. I ran my eyes over all the plaques, and suddenly my attention was caught by a surname that was familiar to me. For on that one plaque there was the inscription: Ida Disterheft † 1988. I knew that surname, because it cropped up in the history of Zagórów in the time of the Second World War, the home town 
<!-- s. 260 [PDF 260] -->
 of my father. It did not figure there honourably at the time; quite the opposite, in fact. I became curious at once as to who Ida was, whether she could have come from Zagórów or the surrounding area, and whether she was related to the infamous occupation mayor of Zagórów. The feeling of curiosity that was born in me then was so strong that it was as if someone had commanded me and, impatient, was urging me on to uncover her story. I immediately grabbed my phone and began my search.



The plaque with the name of Ida Disterheft.


Photo: Damian Kruczkowski

This is precisely why I wrote above that this story begins from a definitive end. In 1988 Ida Disterheft was buried, her grave was paid for for the next twenty years, only to be forgotten in the end. When no one came forward any more who would have wanted to let the woman rest in peace, paying the administrative fee for another two decades, her remains were removed from the grave and placed in the ossuary. Perhaps it was meant to be just so, so that Ida could appear in the pages of this book. In this way she has been commemorated, for times and people will pass, and the book will tell of a forgotten woman, wandering even after death, from the Zagórów area, a descendant of a family that had chosen the municipality of Zagórów as its home more than a hundred years before Ida was born. Ida Disterheft died on 7 May 1988 in the Social Welfare Home in Ślesin, where she had been staying since 1983. The home in Ślesin was at that time a branch of the Konin facility. As follows from the death certificate I managed to obtain, the death was reported by Irena Wisznarewska, an employee of the home in Ślesin. When I received Ida's death certificate, I did not yet know that the last address of residence listed in the document was the address of the aforementioned facility, nor that Mrs Wisznarewska was employed there.

The record confirmed my surmises – Ida Disterheft was born in the municipality of Zagórów, precisely in the village of Łazy, on 27 March 1913. Her parents were Gottfryd and Natalia née Helwing. Today I already know that there was no direct kinship between Ida and the occupation mayor of Zagórów, and if there even was, it was very distant. There were several families named Disterheft in the vicinity of Zagórów, in the settlement villages, 
<!-- s. 261 [PDF 261] -->
 and all of them were of the Protestant faith. Protestant settlement in the municipality of Zagórów developed particularly in the first decade of the nineteenth century thanks to Prince Friedrich Wilhelm von Hohenlohe-Ingelfingen, who on the Zagórów lands granted to him by the King of Prussia founded new villages and settled colonists from Germany in them. It was most probably at that time that Ida's ancestors also came to these parts, as the entries in the registers of vital records confirm. I had the death certificate, the place of birth and the name of the reporting person. The search then stalled for a good few years. Still, Ida Disterheft kept returning to my thoughts – now on the occasion of visits to the cemetery and further visits to the ossuary, now during walks through the Protestant cemetery in Zagórów – or she would appear quite without any apparent cause, as if she were determined to remind me of herself and to motivate me to further searching. Ida's efforts proved effective. I also learned that the name of the street of Miss Disterheft's last place of residence had been changed, the numbering had changed too, and that was probably what had discouraged me earlier from continuing the search.

Very often, however, it is the case that the most obvious and simplest solutions are within arm's reach. So it was this time as well, for one day, as I was idly browsing Facebook pages, the name of a group I regularly look through struck me: Historia Gminy Ślesin (The History of the Ślesin Municipality). Of course! It dawned on me that after all I could ask the group's members about the address and about Irena Wisznarewska, and knowing already the potential of the Internet and Facebook, I could be sure the action would end in success. As I thought, so I did, and at express speed I had a lead on Magdalena Wisznarewska, who turned out to be Irena's daughter. Here enormous thanks are due precisely to Magdalena Wisznarewska, who took an interest in the story of Ida Disterheft, all the more so as her mother could not remember such a person. It is hard to blame Mrs Irena, if over the years of her work at the care home dozens of people came under her care. Almost everything we know today about Ida is the merit of Magdalena Wisznarewska.

<!-- s. 262 [PDF 262] -->

I mentioned that Ida Disterheft took up residence in the Ślesin facility in 1983. The life of this woman cannot have been easy; her fate was certainly not positively affected by the fact that she bore the same surname as the aforementioned mayor, who enjoyed no good repute. The harshness of her lot may be underscored by the fact that only in 1977 did she receive an identity card. Earlier it had presumably not been necessary for her, and perhaps she did not even know that she could apply for one, for her entire adult life – as she herself wrote in one of the applications I managed to obtain – she lived and worked for farmers in the nearby villages. She went into service after the death of her father, who was the last surviving member of the family in Poland. In the post-war years the sisters left for Germany, and Ida was left alone without any means of support. The people I managed to reach, who still remember her from the times when she worked for the local farmers, recall that she was called Lida. Ida alias Lida lives in memory as a tall woman with a slightly red face (perhaps acne or a disease of the blood vessels), who wore long, sleeked-down hair. What lodged in the memory of her contemporaries was an element of her attire – Ida always wore long dresses.

These are mainly the recollections of Mrs Wanda Rydlichowska (née Kin) of Szetlewek in the municipality of Zagórów, at whose parents' home Ida worked in the 1950s. Mrs Wanda tried to recall Ida and the details of her life, but she herself was a little girl when Ida worked for her parents. Mrs Wanda did say, however, that when she was preparing for the conversation with me, she had the impression that she could see Ida before her, so deeply had Ida's appearance lodged in her memory. Mrs Rydlichowska mentioned that in her opinion Ida was a person with an intellectual disability, as her behaviour and the way she spoke would indicate, but those were times when nobody particularly concerned themselves with it, diagnosed it, or provided rehabilitation. Wanda Rydlichowska recounted an incident that might point to Ida's mental state. Namely, one evening, when everyone was already to go to bed, Mrs Wanda's mother noticed that Ida was not with them, and it was already late 
<!-- s. 263 [PDF 263] -->
 and dark. Suddenly the household heard some sounds coming from the yard, as if a metal pail were being struck against a pail. Mrs Wanda's mother went out in front of the house and saw Ida. She asked her where she had been, and received the answer: “I was down at the stream and wanted to drown myself. But I jumped in and I jumped in and I couldn't.” The stream is a small river flowing not far from the home of Mrs Wanda Rydlichowska's parents, in which the water level, unless the river is dammed up in places, does not come higher than the knees. So much from Mrs Wanda's recollections. The residence registers further inform us that Ida Disterheft de-registered and moved away to Koło. Nothing is known at the present moment about her stay in Koło and the surrounding area. This scrap of memory is all that could be obtained. The family of the last farmer for whom Ida Disterheft worked evidently, after returning to Szetlewek from the vicinity of Koło, decided against a conversation, claiming that they remember nothing about her and know nothing.

Ida's fate is the frequent fate of women and men of German roots who for various reasons did not leave for Germany after the Second World War. One can only surmise why Ida did not do so, for what the real reason was we will probably never learn. The life of Polish citizens of German origin in the times of the People's Republic was not easy, of which Ida herself is an example. Though born and raised in a municipality where Poles made up the overwhelming majority, though recognized as a Polish citizen when Poland regained its independence in 1918, though she might have felt a Pole, in 1939 the occupation authorities classified her as a German, and a German she remained for many, in the eyes of many, long after the war. To this was added the shared surname with a war criminal, and the recipe for a lousy life was ready. As I mentioned above, in the 1950s she was in Szetlewek, then she spent some time in Koło or the surrounding area. In 1971 she came to Szetlewek, to a farmer who gave her work and a roof over her head for over a dozen years, for it was with him that she remained until she received a place in the Social Welfare Home in Ślesin. She knew farm work; after all, she was born in the countryside, and in Łazy her parents had their own farm. She helped them as all village children do.

<!-- s. 264 [PDF 264] -->

As she grew older, Ida began to decline in health. The community interview from the time when she was applying for a place in the care home showed that she lived in a little room in the attic, which lacked basic household equipment, and that she herself suffered from the ailments of old age and lapses of memory. Ida was certainly no longer capable of work on the farm. It was determined at the time that she required the care of a second person.

In the course of writing this story I showed a draft of the text to an acquaintance. And here again we are dealing with a small miracle, or perhaps precisely with Ida's overriding desire to have her story told, for it turned out that my acquaintance, as a teenager, had probably come across Ida several times at the care home in Ślesin. My acquaintance often visited the home to see her grandfather, and it was from that very time – the second half of the 1980s – that she remembered the woman, of whom she speaks as follows: “The Ida described in the text I connected with a lady I often saw at the care home. She caught my attention because she made a rather unpleasant impression (at least on me as a teenager), particularly her black clothes, her red face and her greasy hair. Somehow I have a strange association that they spoke of her as ‘that German woman’ and did not address her by her first name. I remember that my grandfather treated her with dislike, the other residents too. I felt sorry for her.” Could this have been Ida? Highly probable, since in the municipality of Zagórów the same features of appearance were remembered, which later caught the eye of the teenager visiting the care home and were remembered for years. Ida Disterheft remained unmarried all her life. Perhaps it was her conscious choice, or perhaps factors such as those I wrote about above combined to bring about that state. Her origins, her own and others' wartime experiences, the life experiences of the post-war years, the presumed disability, the loss of contact with her sisters in Germany, perhaps also the shared surname with the mayor meant that even if she might have appealed to someone, unfortunately it would have been socially unacceptable to ask for her hand. With time, even had such a bold man been found, perhaps Ida herself would have sent him packing, not wanting alms from fate in any form. She had 
<!-- s. 265 [PDF 265] -->
 the misfortune to be born, come of age and live in times uncertain, then wild and cruel, and then grey and cold.

She did not live to see calmer times. She died without family, without kin, without friends. She was buried at the state's expense. At the same expense, twenty years later, she was exhumed, and her remains were placed in a collective grave with those who had been forgotten. But in the shared history of the universe and of humankind they all, though often nameless, still exist and call to us from oblivion, from being forgotten. I think it was precisely such a call that reached me as I walked the cemetery paths. I did not come to the ossuary by chance, nor was Ida's surname there by chance. She passed through life, presumably often unnoticed, needed when something had to be done. She departed just as quietly, but I have an irresistible impression that after years she decided to remind us of herself. Someone might ask: why now, why only now? And perhaps earlier, aware of the burden that the world and people had laid upon her, she had resigned herself to her fate. Now Ida Disterheft is a kind of monument to all those Polish citizens of German origin whom the cruel world entangled in nationalisms, wars and restless, chaotic times of peace. Ida's fate is also a kind of warning. A warning whose sounds, clothed in Ida's voice, arranging themselves into rows of syllables, could ultimately take the form of a text reading: never again discord, division and war.

<!-- s. 266 [PDF 266] -->

<!-- s. 267 [PDF 267] -->

### Mrs Dolkowa


Damian Kruczkowski

The figure I would like to tell about appeared often in the recollections of my grandmother, Zdzisława Powała née Duszyńska, born in 1933 in Dolany. From childhood I liked listening, and Grandma did not need to be asked twice before she would begin to spin tales from the times of her childhood. Mrs Dolkowa, who was in fact Genowefa Dalke, née Zeigner, was my grandmother's teacher. She taught, among other things, German at the school in Lądek during the Second World War. Grandma remembered only the surname, moreover given in dialect form, a few situations, the general positive impression Mrs Dalke made on her, but she was unable to recall her first name. When the idea arose of gathering material for a book about the Protestants, I thought that Grandma's Mrs Dolkowa could be commemorated in it. But one had to find out a little more about her. And so one afternoon I wanted to share with Grandma the dark beer I happened to be drinking. I offered to pour her a little of it into a glass.

– Oh no, no, – Grandma replied, – but when we were kids and walked to the bar in Lądek for beer, the German, besides pouring into our little pails exactly as much as we had bought, would sometimes, when it was hot, add a small glass of dark beer for us. It was good. We always drank it, and on the way out we would say, each as best we could, *auf wiedersehen*. The German would laugh, because we didn't necessarily say it correctly.

– Mum, you should have said *danke* – my mother, Grandma's daughter, would prompt.

– What for? When we didn't have to, we didn't go around chattering German, and he did nothing to us. And once Mrs Dolkowa (Dalke) taught us, then we spoke it. Mrs Dalke taught us German. It went variously for us. We still 
<!-- s. 268 [PDF 268] -->
 managed to catch a bit of it. To this day I remember how Dolkowa taught us: *Bitte, Frau, kann ich raus gehen*? That means “please, Miss, may I go out?”, when we wanted to go to the toilet, – Grandma recounted. – But Klaudia (Grandma's later sister-in-law) caught nothing, couldn't learn. Dolkowa would always say to her: “you're the biggest one, and you can't do anything”. Maybe that's why she dug her heels in, because it was her mother who was taken to dig trenches, her father was deported, and Pałucka (Klaudia's aunt) had been assigned to look after them. Dalke. Mrs Dalke, but when our parents said “Dolkowa”, then I said it too.

There were many such conversations. We returned to the subject of Mrs Dalke several, if not a dozen or so, times. I very much wanted to learn her first name. By pure chance – although the cemetery in Lądek is small, I had nevertheless never ventured into that part of it – I came upon the grave of Genowefa Dalke. Thanks to this I had several new pieces of information, but confirmation was still needed that this was indeed the person Grandma recalls. Here again Grandma came to the rescue during a further conversation. Grandma said about her teacher as much as she knew, but she often added new facts, such as the story of how, as children, they went to the park adjoining the palace in Lądek to gather chestnuts, which Mrs Dolkowa would then pour into sacks. The sacks were carted off somewhere so that someone else could grind them into meal, and from it produce glue. The last thing Grandma remembers is the location of the house in which Mrs Dalke lived during the war, a house that still stands.

– You must ask Bronia when she comes, because they lived on the Wielodwór, right next door, after all.

One day I came into Grandma's and found Mrs Bronia sitting at the table drinking coffee. Ten years younger than Grandma, Bronisława Kowalska, a friend of Grandma's born during the war. It was in her grandparents' house, the Sieczkarkis', that the Dalke family was billeted after the war.

– Mrs Bronia, does the surname Dalke mean anything to you? Genowefa Dalke. – Does it mean anything? Of course, – an animated Mrs Bronia replies. – She lived later with the children in that old house that belonged to my grandparents 
<!-- s. 269 [PDF 269] -->
 in our homestead. But that was already after the war. She had two sons and a daughter, and it was with the daughter that I played, though she was a bit older than me. The kids left later, but her son does live in Słupca, after all. And besides, you must talk to Kuskowa, she'll know more!

Some time ago, much earlier, I talked with Mrs Kuske from Lądek, who comes from Piotrowo. I deeply regret not having asked her about more details. At the time Mrs Kuske recalled that the teacher would sometimes give her some little notes, which she carried “to the boys in the forest”. Possibly some partisans or fugitives were stationed for a time in the vicinity of Lądek with whom Mrs Dalke was in contact. That would make sense in the light of the account of her son Jan Dalke, who recalled that after the war the militia, although it often settled accounts with people for wartime transgressions, never touched Mrs Dalke's family, indeed it protected it.

So I began searching for that very son whom Bronisława Kowalska had mentioned. In old telephone directories I even found a telephone number and an approximate address, but the months were passing and I had neither the occasion nor the courage to contact Mr Jan. Facebook came to the rescue. In various circumstances I made contact with Mr Jan's daughter-in-law, his daughter, his niece and his brother's daughter. Suddenly and unexpectedly Mrs Dolkowa's family grew. Already then I knew that the story of Genowefa Dalke would be told. I met with Mr Jan Dalke on 7 September 2022 in his flat in Słupca. Mr Jan readily spoke about his mother and her story.

Genowefa Zeigner came into the world on 11 October 1911 in Linden in Wolfenbüttel as the daughter of Julius and Berta née Petrich. Her parents had gone to Linden for work, which in those times was a common phenomenon. The marriage was contracted in 1906 in Konin. When Genowefa was half a year old, the Zeigners returned to the Konin land, to Nowy Czarków in the municipality of Kramsk. Besides Genowefa, the couple had two more daughters and a son: Berta, Helena and Otto. Genowefa Zeigner, on 17 November 1934, again in Konin, married Reinhold Dahlke, born in Lutnia in the municipality of Kramsk. From her union with Reinhold a 
<!-- s. 270 [PDF 270] -->
 daughter, Łucja/Lucyna (1935-2008), and a son, Jan (1944), were born. After the outbreak of the Second World War the Dalke family was resettled to Lądek. Genowefa, as I have already mentioned, besides looking after the children, was a teacher. To this day she is remembered fondly by her surviving pupils. After the war the family was resettled to Niska Street, into a large house into which the Woźniak, Czarnecki and Falkenberg families were also billeted. The latter, with their sons Bronek and Adolf, left for Germany in 1956. In the summer of 1948 Reinhold Dalke was raking hay on a meadow by the Warta. Suddenly he heard the voice of a girl drowning in the river, coming from the direction of Zagórów. He was an excellent swimmer. The son recalls that his father crossed the river five times in one go. Reinhold Dalke threw himself to the rescue without a moment's thought. Unfortunately, exhaustion, the high temperature, the cold water and most probably the whirlpools brought about the tragedy. Mr Dalke's body was found after five hours. The funeral took place on 1 August 1948. After her husband's premature death Genowefa became attached to Julius Lim from Białobłoty, with whom she had another son, Zygfryd Lim (1949-2021). Genowefa and Julius never entered into marriage. In the turmoil of war Mr Lim's wife and two sons had gone missing. They were sought through the Polish Red Cross, but without result. Mrs Genowefa's family lived on Niska Street until 1964, when the house they occupied, like all the others on that street, was consumed by a great fire.

Significant from the point of view of the post-war fates of Polish Protestants is the anecdote Mr Jan told me about his aunts, his mother's sisters. Berta and Helena Zeigner left for Germany during the Second World War. After the war they remained there, trying somehow to arrange their lives for themselves. Similar recollections appear in many accounts of members of Hauländer families from Poland living in Germany. Although both ladies had by then lived in Germany for years and spoke German fluently, the Germans would often call them Polish swine.

Around the year 2000 Genowefa Dalke moved to the Protestant Senior Citizens' Home in Zagórów. There, surrounded by professional care, visited by her dear ones, she died in 2007 and was buried beside 
<!-- s. 271 [PDF 271] -->




Mrs Dolkowa, that is Genowefa Dalke.


Photo from the archive of the Dalke family


One of the men is the then bosun Jan Dalke, and the other… my grandfather, Walenty Powała.


Photo from the family archive of Damian Kruczkowski

<!-- s. 272 [PDF 272] -->
 Julius Lim in the parish cemetery in Lądek. The son of Genowefa and Reinhold Dalke, Jan, is a sailor by education. During my meeting with him I looked through a family album, turned the pages, often gazed at the face of Grandma's teacher looking out of the photographs. Suddenly I froze. In Mr Jan's album I found a small photograph I knew well from childhood. An identical one is in Grandma's album. Two men are standing on a jetty; on the right one can see part of a boat with the name Chłopi. One of the men is the then bosun Jan Dalke, and the other… my grandfather, Walenty Powała.


Grandpa sailed barges on the Warta. So it must have been. The stories of my family and of Genowefa Dalke's family perhaps intertwined precisely so that this tale could come into being and commemorate Mrs Dolkowa.

<!-- s. 273 [PDF 273] -->

### From memory, or spoken history


Compiled by Katarzyna Połom

The scraps of memories we note down on these pages are above all subjective, emotion-filled utterances of direct participants in the events described, as well as of persons closely connected with them. The written-down memories sometimes illuminate the same events from different perspectives. At times they exclude one another. Human memory is impermanent; some facts remain with us, others become deformed or perish in time. It is worth citing here an important remark of Professor Krzysztof Makowski: “Attempts to verify accounts […] are nevertheless beside the point. The researcher (apart from the possibility of correcting individual facts) is here almost entirely helpless. After all, it is impossible to establish to what degree memory underwent retouching and verification in the course of a lifetime, the awareness of which has been effaced. But that subjectivism of oral sources also has its advantages. It enables, namely, the analysis of human experience in a broader context. For we can learn not only what the author did, but what he wanted to do and believed he had done, as well as how he thinks about it today”. In oral history, diaries and memoirs – Professor Makowski notes further – “far more important than objective truth (if such a thing exists at all) is emotional truth”[^86].

All this makes us aware, once again, that the truth about a human being and about history is never one-dimensional.



[^86]: *Dwa życia jak dwa psalmy. Historia mówiona Leona i Toshy Jedwab opowiedziana Evie Urbach*, Poznań 2012. Here: Introduction by Krzysztof Makowski, p. 61.

<!-- s. 274 [PDF 274] -->

#### “Once a month the pastor came from Grodziec…”

##### Reminiscences of Eugenia Wysokowska (1900-1975)

The solemn service was on, and heaps of Germans came in all sorts of village attire. Weddings began in the carnival season, and the priest announced the banns from the pulpit. A dozen or so carts, horses decked out, ribbons on the whips, a band, the whole street full of carts and carriages. We'd ask the lads, and they'd drive us about the town and round the marketplace and back in front of the German church, and the lad was glad he was driving girls from town, and best of all – the ride was the best part. From the Oleśnica side the young couple came to the wedding in carts, always with the witnesses in one carriage, then the other carriages and carts. They invited a lot of guests. The musicians rode with drums on one cart, the young people behind them. They would stand up, shout, wave their ribbons about. The horses had ribbons on their harness, and up at the top of the whip too. When they drove back from the wedding, everyone came out to have a look.

#### We were at a wedding in Olchowo…

Zygmunt Wolf was getting married, and the bride was from Olchowo. He lived across from the Jareckis, with his mother. The Jareckis too were at the wedding with the lads. Lots of carriages and carts, a band – they drove through the streets, through Oleśnica and along the canal. It was lovely. A clearing among the trees, a house, a farmstead. Supper – there was plenty of food, but whenever they set out a platter of meat, half of it was gone. The next one, we said, will be all ours! They told jokes and anecdotes. Then they held the capping ceremony: they take off the veil and pin a little flower in the hair. A little basket went round the tables and they sang, and everyone had to drop some cash into the basket for the young couple. Then someone crawled under the table and pulled off the bride's shoe; she had to buy it back. Afterwards everyone returned to dancing; they began to dance till morning on the clearing, some dozed off on the chairs, some went to the barn, to the benches, and some in front of the house. The young couple went to sleep in the barn towards morning. There were beds for them separately. The Germans, their friends, sneaked in quietly and tickled the soles of their feet, playing tricks on them, and there was laughter! In the morning, whoever wanted went at sunrise out to the boats: the river was flowing, trees on the hill, 
<!-- s. 275 [PDF 275] -->
 the sun was shining – a beautiful sight. At noon the next day everyone drove off home.

#### I danced with the pastor, too

We were also at the wedding at Genia Najman's. She asked only me and a friend. I made all the cakes for her and baked them together with her. First we were at the wedding in the German church, we offered our congratulations. Then Genia sent two relatives to fetch me from home; supper was already being served. Mrs Nowak, Kazimiera, was there too, it seems with her father. I came in and bowed in every direction. The pastor was sitting at the table and at once asked me to sit beside him. I introduced myself. He served me food and did not stir a step from me. They played forfeits, and I bustled about so that I wouldn't have a forfeit, and the pastor kept stopping me so that I wouldn't manage it. And at last I ended up with one! I danced with the pastor, too. He was very handsome, merry, witty, kind, and I think he rather took a fancy to me as well. And when I was to walk home from the wedding, he said he would see me home. But since Dryzner and several Germans were walking that way, I thanked him and took my leave. On the way we also saw Mrs Nowak home.

#### We went to German weddings and funerals

The Helman, the cooper, from whom we bought shavings for kindling next door, wanted to take Grandma as godmother, but the pastor did not agree to it. But we did go to German weddings and funerals. The pastor always gave a speech over the grave, in Polish too when there were many Poles at the funeral.

◼

#### “We express our piety in everyday work and in treating people decently”

##### Halina Zalewska remembers

My family, the Webers, were formally Catholics, but ones who in their lives never set foot in a Catholic church. Kazimierz Weber's daughter – and my aunt Helena – when a priest burst into their home with his aspergillum, 
<!-- s. 276 [PDF 276] -->
 snapped back at the reproach that he could see no cross anywhere: “We express our piety in everyday work and in treating people decently.” I always considered that very Zagórów-like, and I was proud of it.

#### “For my mother's funeral I invited the female pastor”

For the funeral of my mother, Irena née Weber, I did not invite a Catholic priest, but the female pastor Halina Radacz, closest in outlook to my mother. The grave of Anna Maria Grymin, one of our ancestresses (Grandma was a Grymin by birth), lay right by the wall of the Protestant cemetery. We used to place little lights there for as long as that grave of green-tinged sandstone stood. Two years ago we could no longer find it…

◼

#### The Beutlers always felt and were Poles

##### From a letter by Alicja Beutler of 2009

My father Alfred Beutler was the second youngest of the seven children of Emilia and Emil Beutler. Four of the older ones emigrated in 1911-1912 to the United States. Only Regina, my father and the youngest, Adolf, remained in Poland. Both brothers took an active part in the Volunteer Fire Brigade in Zagórów. They all felt and were Poles. My father was in the POW, fought in the Legions; from his stories I remember that he was probably in the II Brigade. He was discharged from the army only after the Polish-Bolshevik war, in 1922. My father was in the signals, had the rank of corporal; in his platoon he had above all volunteer students from Warsaw. I know that he received the “Medal for Lida”; I saw the award certificate. (…)

#### My father had to undertake to raise the children in the Catholic faith

My parents were married during one of his leaves from the army, on 18 November 1919. At the wedding in Lądek Father made a “declaration of faith” – he was a Protestant, but he undertook to raise the children in the Catholic faith.

<!-- s. 277 [PDF 277] -->

#### Evenings with Legion songs

I remember the evenings when the former POW men from his group gathered at our place, but I remember only one surname – Roman Brzeg. At those gatherings Legion songs were sung. I remember almost all of them to this day. Father recited beautifully; I remember a very long poem beginning with the words: “In the hospital, on the ward, an unknown soldier is dying, his breast heaves in his last agony…” – I don't remember the rest. I was thirteen when Father left for Łódź to work for the railways (PKP). The evenings with songs came to an end…

I explain my father's acceptance of the Volksliste imposed on him by the fact that he had to hide his membership of the POW and, at all costs, protect the four children aged between 11 and 16.

He died at the age of 66 and was buried in Zagórów in the grave of his parents.

#### My brother deserted from the German army

As a result of the hostilities my brother ended up in the West; there he probably deserted from the labour service units[^87] or from the German army. He was in the Polish army under his mother's maiden name – as Leon Majewski. He never returned to Poland; he settled in England. He worked as a car-body designer for General Motors. He died at the age of 65.

◼

#### The Beutlers were always decent

##### Tadeusz Molski (b. 1920) remembers

Unfortunately, I have the impression that in the 1930s most of the Protestants were supporters of Hitler. And the worst was Mayor Diesterheft.



[^87]: Reichsarbeitsdienst (RAD; Reich Labour Service) – a mass organization for the civil and military preparation of youth in the Third Reich, which during the Second World War performed auxiliary functions on behalf of the Wehrmacht.

<!-- s. 278 [PDF 278] -->

But the Beutlers were always decent. One of them, a very upright man, worked with my uncle at the mill in Zagórów. It was a mill, a sawmill and a repair shop.

#### I cannot forgive

I still feel resentment towards the Germans living in Zagórów. Because of Diesterheft, in revenge for taking part in the “Barka” action, my father, Feliks Molski, was murdered together with his friends from the Polish Military Organisation.

◼

#### “The Diesterhefts kept the stolen monstrance under the floor”

##### Janina Woźniak (b. 13.01.1929) remembers

I remember Diesterheft (the mayor of Zagórów during the occupation) – a terrible man. Mrs Diesterheft was also an awful woman. She hurled abuse at Poles. Goods were bought in their shop on ration cards. Under the floor of their flat they had hidden the monstrance plundered from our parish church. Dr Breede's sons were “Hitlerites”. They struck my grandfather so hard on the head that his cap fell off. He was 62 years old.

◼

##### Helena Nadolna (10.01.1932 – 2.01.2013) remembers

Diesterheft was said to be a decent enough man before the war, but under the occupation he turned into a devil. He extorted gold from the Jews and gave away the POW men. After the war he was said to have fled from Zagórów by bicycle, and then he left for America. Diesterheft's wife went to Germany, and their sons probably died in the war. The younger was active in the Hitlerjugend, the elder fought at the front.

<!-- s. 279 [PDF 279] -->

#### Janke went to war but never reached the front

Not all the local Germans behaved badly towards Poles during the war. Janke was a blacksmith and a driver; he lived near the shrine on Konińska Street. Before the war he was good; he had a son, Hubert. Together with the German Henke he took part in the deportation of the Jews. Then he went to war, but he never reached the front. He was killed when partisans in the Lublin region blew up the railway tracks and German soldiers died.

#### Rydel and Krygier always helped us

Rydel, on the other hand, a Zagórów man of German origin, saw what he wanted to see – he truly favoured the Poles. He worked at the gendarmerie post, and when someone sent a parcel to a person under arrest, all it took was for Rydel to be there. The sexton Albert Krygier was also a good man. With some German families during the occupation the Poles had good relations. Once I went to the Rydels, the butchers, and played with their children, Irenka and Hans. I could always fill myself up at their place.

#### Dr Breede never refused help to the poor

Once we got fume-poisoned, and we went to that doctor for help. He was good; he never asked about money. His wife, née Stawicka, was a teacher by profession. The Breede sons belonged to the Hitlerjugend. That organization had its seat in what is now the building of the Social Welfare Home. Before the war it was Dr Lidmanowski's hospital. The juvenile fascists once entered the crypt beneath the Catholic church, searched for treasures, opened coffins and desecrated the place.

◼

<!-- s. 280 [PDF 280] -->

#### “I gave them all the gold…”

##### Wanda Jurkiewicz, née Andrzejewska (1923-2022) remembers

When the war was on, I was fifteen. One day my father took my brother to the dentist, for his teeth hurt. The dentist was a Jew; he asked Father to wait or to come later, because Mayor Diesterheft and a gendarme were with him just then. When those two had gone, the dentist said to my father: “All the gold I had, I gave them, and they promised me they wouldn't deport me.” Then he had a little peace, but in the very end they deported him all the same. A vile man was that Diesterheft.

When the war ended, the mayor fled with his son on a motorcycle. In the forest the Russians caught up with them; Diesterheft said they should take him to his flat, to Zagórów, and he would reward them for letting him go. He gave them all that gold he had got from the Jew, but they took him to the forest and did to him what he had done to the others: they killed him, took the gold and drove off[^88].

#### Revenge also reached the mayor's wife and daughter

The inhabitants of Zagórów organized a “defilade” straight after the war, during which they made Mrs Diesterheft walk along ringing a little bell and telling everyone what she had been. It was a great humiliation. However, she and her daughter later managed to escape from Poland.

#### There were also noble Germans during the war

Some Germans, as soon as they knew something was afoot, would give warning so that the Poles could flee in time. And one of the Beutler brothers knew everything about Diesterheft, about what was going on there. When he learned that Poles were to be deported to a camp, and that Cieluba would be deported too, he rushed off at once to warn him to flee and hide. But Cieluba, instead of fleeing straight away, wanted to have a shave first… and he didn't make it. One of the Beutlers' daughters, 


[^88]: The people of Zagórów were mistaken. Julius Diesterheft and his son Bruno survived the war and managed to make their way to Germany.

<!-- s. 281 [PDF 281] -->
 Ala, after the war helped me a great deal, thanks to which I was able to educate my sons.

Mr Rydel, too, was a noble man. After the war he looked after the graves in the Protestant part of the cemetery. I held him in great esteem, because he helped us a lot during the war. When he came to the house to inspect during the occupation, he would close his eyes and pretend not to see certain things. My mother always said that under the Germans she could hide something, but under the commies – nothing could be done. I was at his funeral, and whenever I came to Zagórów I would go to the cemetery to light a candle on his grave.

◼

#### Pastor Gross apologized.

##### Fr. Mieczysław Rydecki (b. 1932) recalled

I am the son of Edmund Rydecki, a member of the POW from the interwar period. In 1940 my father was arrested and deported. He was imprisoned in the jail in Sieradz. Help came to us from my mother's sister-in-law, Auntie Klara Wilantowa, who was of German origin. She gathered whatever gold jewellery there was in the family and set off with it for Sieradz. I don't know the details, but after a year and eight months had passed Father returned home. A few months later, sometime towards the end of 1943, he was summoned to the gendarmerie. The summons must have been brought by the pastor Aleksander Gross, who was well disposed towards the Poles; he apologized from the doorway for having had to come. Father was sent to work in the basket-making workshop in Pyzdry.

#### Auntie Wilantowa comes to the rescue a second time…

When the Germans rounded up me, together with a dozen or so other lads, and took us to Słupca to the *Arbeitsamt*, to assign us to work on German farms, my mother, Auntie Wilantowa and Mrs Ligocka followed us in a carriage and haggled with the officials for so long that, supposedly, the quota 
<!-- s. 282 [PDF 282] -->
 had run out for Romek Ligocki and me, and they sent us back home[^89].

◼



Nela Szumańska of Milicze.


Photo from the archive of Bronisława Wrzesińska

#### “…a Polish officer and a Hitler woman”.

##### Bronisława Wrzesińska, née Szumańska, remembers

My uncle Czesław married a German woman already during the occupation. Slogans, it was said, began to appear on the walls: “An equal married an equal – a Polish officer and a Hitler woman”. She was called Nela Milicz; she came from Zagórów. She worked at the bank, and whatever funds she could, she saved for the Poles. Unfortunately, my mother's dowry she did not manage to save. Auntie Nela had a camera and liked taking photographs. Thanks to this I have family keepsakes from those times. After the war, in 1949, auntie had a case in court in which it was demonstrated that she had acted to Poland's benefit, and she escaped punishment.

◼

#### From the recollections written down by Jarosław Wiśniewski

##### (a typescript from the archive of Bogdan Grzonkowski)

#### The Home Army billet in our house

From February 1940 the Home Army billet for the Słupca-Zagórów District was housed in our home[^90]. Meetings were held here, oaths were taken by the newly admitted, and documents were kept.

#### A hair's breadth from death…

In August 1943 the Germans, aided by the traitor Łodziejowski, got on the trail of the organization and carried out a search of our house. The documents 


[^89]: Jarecki M., *Peowiacy. Pierwsze ofiary okupacji niemieckiej w Zagórowie*, p. 131.

[^90]: Duży Rynek 15.

<!-- s. 283 [PDF 283] -->
 were hidden in the attic, in the water pipe. We were saved from certain misfortune by a gendarme, a local German. When the remaining gendarmes went up to search the attic, Father, pale with fear, turned to Rydel and asked them to end the search already. “There's nothing there…” – he said. Rydel understood at once and quickly called off the searchers. We were saved.

◼

##### Marta Janas (b. 1955) remembers

My grandparents, Hulda and Emil Banik of Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, before the war had quite a lot of land, a whole eight hectares, and then they kept buying more land. At home Grandma Hulda kept the documents certifying their holdings; they had been drawn up still in the Russian language and bear the tsarist official seal. Grandma guarded them like a treasure – she and Grandpa had the hope that they would return one day… They did not return. To this day my brother and I keep those documents as a keepsake.

#### It was a Protestant teacher who taught my mother the Polish patriotic songs

My mother Elza Banik attended the third grade still at the Protestant school in Wrąbczynkowska Kolonia (Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry), where Mr Munch and Mr Kuske from Lądek taught… It was located in that house to the left of the chapel, right by the road. It was her Protestant teacher who taught her the most beautiful Polish patriotic songs and religious songs. It was from Mum that I learned them.

And in connection with their origin they too went through their ordeal after the war. My mother, as a little girl, was separated from her mother; she was made to tear up weeds – thistles – with her bare hands. In dybki (wooden clogs)[^91] she and Grandma walked to Pyzdry, because Grandma was in service with the Skorupkas in Zapowiednia…



[^91]: Dybki – wooden clogs.

<!-- s. 284 [PDF 284] -->

#### Hitler destroyed us all

Mum always told me that the whole family considered it unjust, and took it very hard, that during the occupation poorer German peasants were taking over the farms of wealthier Polish farmers, and hatred was deepening between Poles and Germans. And yet before the war Grandpa and his neighbour, a Pole, would team up their horses so that ploughing would be easier and quicker; they always lived in accord. My aunt from Lądek always said: “And what did that Hitler go and do? He took my brothers from me (they died in the war at the ages of sixteen and twenty-two) and set us against our neighbours… He destroyed us all.”

#### They called me “that German”…

Mum married a Catholic and therefore had to sign a document in which she undertook that the children born of the marriage would be raised in the Catholic faith. That was the requirement for the priest to agree to the wedding. My brother and I were therefore baptized and raised as Catholics, although I always felt closer to Protestantism. But at school they always called me “that German”. Today, both of us, my husband and I, although we live in Kostrzyn, look after the cemetery in Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry; we care for the graves, for the memory…

◼

#### Dr Hugo Breede in the recollections of Prof. Lech Słowiński[^92]

[Hugo Breede] He was a Latvian German, married to a local Polish woman, a Stawicka.

We even considered him a Polish patriot. He held the honourable position of president of the Reservists' Association in the town – he took part in the Bolshevik war as a medic. He died at the beginning of the occupation, in the year 


[^92]: Słowiński Lech, ibid.

<!-- s. 285 [PDF 285] -->
 1940, at the age of only 44. He drank a bit too much, and perhaps that harmed him. His wife was the commandant of the local women's “Strzelec” unit, connected with Piłsudski's Legions. During the Second World War she accepted German citizenship, and after the war she had trouble on that account. She nevertheless remained in Zagórów until the end of her life, abandoned by her two sons, who after service in the Wehrmacht settled for good in West Germany. Once the first lady of the town – she lived after the war in great poverty, grazing her goat on the fallow strips and keeping a few hens.

#### A meeting in flooded Olchowo

Grzegorz Szumański, one of the last goose breeders in Zagórów, recalls how in the times of his youth, in the mid-1960s, the waters of the Warta once again flooded the whole valley. His elder brother Janek had some business with one of the farmers at Olchowo. The only way that allowed reaching the flooded village, cut off completely from the world, was to get there by boat. At that time, in most of the Zagórów houses located on the southern slope of the valley, whose gardens ran down to the meadows, they kept simply built boats, popularly called troughs. That is what the Szumańskis did too. They took an oar and a punting pole and set off across the floodwaters. What they found on the spot they never forgot. They could sail into the farmyard; only the well and the barn stood above the water line. They sailed the boat right into the house, straight into the entrance hall and the kitchen. The household had set all the furniture and beds up on hollow blocks, on which they had laid boards, and in this way they moved about the house. Nobody was thinking of fleeing or of a temporary evacuation. On a rise some 50 m away stood a little herd of cows, and a bit farther on, higher up, the cemetery, which – as the locals remembered – the water had never flooded. Out of the whole populous village of former times (before 1939 more than 60 families were settled here) three farmsteads remained…

*Recorded by Mirosław Słowiński*
